Initial Generals
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
//////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////// [☠.11]

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Explain these elements:
	Etiology
	Pathogenesis
	Physiology
	Pathophysiology
	Clinical manifestations
	Diagnosis
	Treatment
	Prognosis/Prognostication
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Etiology:
Definition: Etiology refers tthe cause or origin of a disease.
Significance: Understanding the etiology of a disease is crucial for effective treatment and prevention strategies.
Example: In Meniere's disease, the etiology involves multifactorial inheritance with factors like genetic anomalies, vasodilation, allergy, viral infection, and autoimmunity contributing tthe development of endolymphatic hydrops.

Pathology:
Definition: Studies the nature and effects of diseases, including their causes, development, and consequences on the body's tissues and organs.
Significance: Pathology plays a crucial role in diagnosing diseases, understanding their underlying mechanisms, and guiding treatment decisions. It involves examining tissues, cells, and bodily fluids tidentify abnormalities and provide insights intthe nature of diseases.
Example: In Meniere's disease, pathology studies have revealed key mechanisms like fibrosis of the endolymphatic sac and vestibular epithelia, altered glycoprotein metabolism, and immune-mediated processes that contribute tthe development of symptoms like vertigand hearing loss. Understanding these pathological features is essential for accurate diagnosis and targeted treatment strategies in individuals with Meniere's disease.
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
	What is the difference between Pathogenesis and Pathology when classifying diseases?
		When classifying diseases, the difference between Pathogenesis and Pathology lies in their focus within the disease process:

		Pathogenesis: Pathogenesis refers tthe process by which a disease or disorder develops, encompassing factors contributing tthe onset, progression, and maintenance of the condition. It delves intunderstanding how diseases originate and evolve within the body, including mechanisms like microbial infection, inflammation, malignancy, and tissue breakdown. Pathogenesis explores the journey of a disease from its initial triggers tits full manifestation and impact on the body's systems.

		Pathology: On the other hand, Pathology is the study of diseases themselves, examining their nature, effects on tissues and organs, and the structural changes they induce. It focuses on identifying abnormalities at a cellular or tissue level tdiagnose and characterize diseases. Pathology involves analyzing diseased tissues, cells, or bodily fluids tunderstand the underlying mechanisms of diseases and their impact on the body's structure and function.

		In summary, while Pathogenesis looks at how diseases develop and progress within the body, Pathology is concerned with studying the nature of diseases themselves, including their effects on tissues and organs.

Pathophysiology:
Definition: Pathophysiology examines the functional changes associated with a disease and how these alterations cause symptoms.
Significance: It provides insights intthe mechanisms underlying disease manifestations and guides treatment approaches.
Example: Endolymphatic hydrops is the pathologic feature associated with Meniere's disease, where alterations in endolymphatic homeostasis lead tsymptoms like vertigand hearing loss.

Clinical Manifestations:
Definition: Clinical manifestations are the signs and symptoms that characterize a disease or condition.
Significance: Recognizing clinical manifestations aids in diagnosing diseases and monitoring treatment effectiveness.
Example: In COVID-19, common clinical manifestations include fever, dry cough, shortness of breath, fatigue, nausea/vomiting, myalgia, and even nonclassical symptoms like gastrointestinal issues.

Diagnosis:
Definition: Diagnosis involves identifying a disease based on signs, symptoms, medical history, physical examination, and diagnostic tests.
Significance: Accurate diagnosis is essential for appropriate treatment planning and management.
Example: Diagnosis of Meniere's disease may involve assessing symptoms like vertigo, hearing loss, tinnitus along with vestibular function tests like audiometry or electronystagmography.

Treatment:
Definition: Treatment encompasses interventions aimed at managing or curing a disease talleviate symptoms and improve outcomes.
Significance: Effective treatment strategies can help control disease progression, reduce symptoms, and enhance quality of life.
Example: Treatment for Meniere's disease may involve medications tmanage symptoms like vertigor hearing loss, dietary modifications, vestibular rehabilitation therapy, or surgical interventions in severe cases.

Prognosis:
	Prognosis refers tthe likely course or outcome of a disease or condition, typically based on various factors such as the individual's overall health, the severity of the condition, available treatments, and other relevant variables. In the context of physical therapy, prognosis involves predicting the expected functional outcomes and recovery potential for a patient based on their specific diagnosis, medical history, response ttreatment, and other relevant factors.

	For example, in the case of Bell's Palsy, prognosis may vary depending on the severity of facial weakness, the presence of associated symptoms such as pain or sensory disturbances, the age and overall health of the individual, and how promptly treatment is initiated. In many cases, Bell's Palsy resolves spontaneously within a few weeks tmonths, with the majority of patients experiencing significant improvement in facial function. However, some individuals may experience persistent weakness or residual symptoms, impacting their long-term prognosis and functional outcomes. Physical therapists play a crucial role in assessing prognosis, developing tailored treatment plans, and optimizing rehabilitation strategies tmaximize functional recovery and improve overall quality of life for patients.

0:
	Consider the attached ICF framework as you work through the case. ('ICF framework.png' & 'ICF Model in Patient Documentation.png')
		PLANTILLA
			1.Health Condition
					↓
			2.Body Functions/Body Structures (Impairments) ←→
			3.Activity (Limitations) ←→
			4.Participation (Restrictions) ←→
					↑
			5.Contextual factors (Activities)
					↓
			5.1 or 6.Environmental factors
			5.2 or 7.Personal factors
			----------------------------------------------------------
			-What is the difference between Activity Limitations and Participation Restrictions?
				-Activity limitations and participation restrictions are both concepts used in the context of disability and rehabilitation, but they refer tdifferent aspects of a person's functioning:
					-Activity Limitations: Activity limitations refer tdifficulties an individual may have in executing tasks or actions. These limitations are specific tthe person's body functions and structures and are often related timpairments. For example, someone with arthritis may have activity limitations in walking due tjoint pain and stiffness.
			-What is the difference between Environmental factors and Personal factors in the International Classification of Functioning & Disability (ICF) Model framework?
				-In the International Classification of Functioning, Disability, and Health (ICF) model framework, environmental factors and personal factors play distinct roles in understanding and assessing an individual's functioning. Here's how they differ within the ICF framework:
					1.Environmental Factors:
					-Environmental factors, within the ICF framework, refer tthe external factors that influence an individual's functioning. These factors include the physical, social, and attitudinal aspects of the individual's surroundings.
					-Environmental factors encompass various dimensions, such as:
						-Products and technology: Assistive devices, technologies, and equipment that facilitate functioning.
						-Natural environment and human-made changes: Physical barriers, such as architectural barriers or accessibility features.
						-Support and relationships: Social support networks, relationships with family, friends, and caregivers.
						-Attitudes: Societal attitudes, stereotypes, and discrimination toward individuals with disabilities.
						-Services, systems, and policies: Access thealthcare, education, employment opportunities, and legal protections.
					-Environmental factors can either facilitate or hinder an individual's functioning and participation in daily activities and societal roles.

					2.Personal Factors:
					-In contrast, personal factors are not included as a separate component in the ICF framework. Personal factors refer tthe individual characteristics, experiences, and attributes that influence how a person interacts with their environment and experiences disability.
					-While personal factors are acknowledged as important determinants of functioning, the ICF model does not provide a standardized classification system for personal factors.
					-Instead, personal factors are considered as modifiers of health condition and functioning within the ICF framework. They can include aspects such as age, gender, race/ethnicity, socioeconomic status, education, coping strategies, resilience, beliefs, values, and personality traits.
					-Personal factors can shape an individual's perception of their health and disability, their attitudes toward their condition, and their ability tadapt tenvironmental challenges.
					------------------------------------------------------------------
					-In summary, within the ICF framework, environmental factors are external influences that impact functioning, while personal factors encompass individual characteristics and attributes. While environmental factors are explicitly classified within the ICF, personal factors are considered as modifiers that interact with health conditions and environmental factors tinfluence functioning.
			-Why dwe consider Environmental factors and Personal factors in the International Classification of Functioning & Disability (ICF) Model framework?
				-Environmental factors and personal factors are considered in the International Classification of Functioning, Disability, and Health (ICF) model framework because they both play crucial roles in shaping an individual's functioning, disability, and overall well-being. Here are several reasons why they are included:

					-1.Comprehensive Understanding of Functioning: By considering both environmental factors and personal factors, the ICF framework provides a comprehensive understanding of the factors that influence an individual's functioning. It acknowledges that disability is not solely determined by the characteristics of the individual, but alsby the interaction between the individual and their environment.

					-2.Determinants of Participation: Environmental factors and personal factors significantly influence an individual's ability tparticipate in activities and societal roles. Environmental factors such as physical barriers, social attitudes, and access tservices can either facilitate or hinder participation. Personal factors such as age, gender, and coping strategies alsimpact participation.

					-3.Identification of Barriers and Facilitators: Recognizing environmental barriers and facilitators is essential for promoting accessibility, inclusion, and participation for individuals with disabilities. By identifying and addressing barriers in the environment, such as architectural barriers or discriminatory attitudes, it becomes possible tcreate more inclusive and supportive environments.

					-4.Individual-Centered Approach: Considering personal factors acknowledges the unique attributes and experiences of individuals. It recognizes that individuals with similar health conditions may experience disability differently based on personal factors such as resilience, coping strategies, and cultural background. This individual-centered approach helps tailor interventions and support services tmeet the specific needs and preferences of individuals.

					-5.Policy Development and Advocacy: Understanding the influence of environmental factors and personal factors is critical for informing policy development, advocacy efforts, and resource allocation. It highlights the importance of creating policies and initiatives that address environmental barriers, promote social inclusion, and support individuals with diverse personal characteristics and needs.

					-In summary, considering environmental factors and personal factors in the ICF framework enables a holistic understanding of disability and functioning. It helps identify barriers tparticipation, promotes individual-centered approaches trehabilitation and support, and informs efforts tcreate more inclusive and accessible environments for all individuals.

-Participation Restrictions: Participation restrictions, on the other hand, refer tproblems an individual may experience in involvement in life situations. These restrictions are related tthe broader environmental and societal factors that can hinder participation in activities. For example, someone with a mobility impairment may face participation restrictions in accessing public transportation or employment opportunities due tarchitectural barriers or discrimination.

Plantilla Official Disease
	Not all of this apply tall diseases.
	--------------------------------------------------------
	Epidemiology (Who/Quí/els candidats per a la enfermetat)
		is the study of the incidence and prevalence of diseases within a population
			I.e.: acute ischemic stroke occurs more commonly in patients over 50 years old
	Etiology ("caused by")
		refers tthe cause of the disease
			I.e.: Acute ischemic stroke is "caused by" an abrupt occlusion of a vessel most commonly from a thrombus or blood dot
	Pathogenesis (How/Cóm)
		is what happens tgenerate the symptoms you see
			I.e.: If the left middle cerebral artery is occluded, ischemia or lack of flow tthe surroun&lng brain tisue occurs, that rapidly results in neuronal cell death and frequently gives us cfinical manifestations
	Risk Factors (Condicions negatives de la salut que posen en risk a la persona)
		who's at risk for developing this disease?
		I.e.: For acute ischemic stroke the risk factors are general cardiovascular risk factors, hypertension, hyperfipidemia, Diabetes, etc.
	Clinical Manifestations 
		are what does this disease look like ta healthcare provider?
		What are patients going tcomplain about and what are you going tsee on physical exam?
		I.e.: If the left middle cerebral artery is occluded, the cfinical manifestations we would expect tsee are right-sided facial, right upper extremity, right lower extremiy, weakness and numbness and potentially some aphasia.
	Diagnostic Criteria
		How dwe diagnose this?
		For some diseases it is going tbe a clinical diagnosis.
			We just need our history in physical exam tmake the diagnosis.
		For others we need further data.
		I.e.: For acute ischemic stroke we diagnose this either with CT angiography of the head and neck or with an MRI ofthe brain.
	First and Second Line Treatment
		Whats our first-line treatrnent and if we cannot use it for whatewer reason
		I.e.: For acute ischemic stroke of the left middle cerebral artery,
			first-line treatment
				if within an appropriate time window is thrombectomy or clot removal
			Second fine would be
				a clot busting intravenous me&cation known as TPA.
	Disposition and/or referral
		-is where does the patient need tgthave this disease managed and are there other members of the healthcare team or other specialties that need tbe involved in this patient's care?
		I.e.: For patients with acute ischemic stroke, they should all gideally ta comprehensive stroke center for acute management and after their acute management tan inpatient rehabilitation center. Patients acute ischemic stroke often require a multidisciplinary team of healtl care providers including physicians, advanced practice providers, physical and occupational therapists, speech threrapists and dietitians.
	Prevention (if appropriate)
		I.e.: What can we dtprevent a patient from either having a first episode of acute ischemic stroke, which is referred tas primary prevention or...
			Secondary Prevention
				...tprevent a patient who's already had a stroke from having a second stroke? (This is referred tas secondary prevention)
	.............................

Introduction to Immunity (Q&A_IntoImmunity) ☢
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Types of Cellular Injury
	Cell Injury
		Reversible
		Necrosis
		Subcellular Alterations
		Apoptosis

Reversible Cell Injury and Necrosis ('')

Subcellular Alterations
	Permanent change to anatomy and/or physiology of cell
		Change to shape
			Alterations of cystoskeleton proteins 
		Change to function
			Alterations of mitochondria

Apoptosis
	Programmed cell death
		Cell self-destructs
		Cell tags itself to be destroyed by other immune cells

Injury → Immune Response ('')
	Initiation: Formation of the Fatty Streak

The Immune System ('The Immune System')
	Protection → Pathogens
	Healing → Injury
	Removing → Toxins
	Destroying → Tumor cells
	--------------------------
	Innate
		Cellular
		Humoral
	Adaptive
		Cellular
		Humoral

Innate Immunity
	Nonspecific
	First line of defense
	Physical barriers
		Skin
		Cilia 
		pH stomach
		Lysozymes 

Myeloid Cells
	Eosinopnll
	Neutrophil
	Monocyte
		Macrophage
		Myeloid dendritic cell
	Basophil
	Mast cell

Humoral response ('Humoral response')

Lymphoid Cells
	Natural Killer Cell
	B Lymphocytes
		Plasma Cell
		Memory B Cell
	T Lymphocytes
		Cytotoxic T Cell
		Helper T Cell

Immune System in Action ('Initiation: Formation of the Fatty Streak')

Objectives
	Compare and contrast the types of cellular responses to injury 
	Describe the general role of the immune system
	Compare and contrast the functions of innate and adaptive immune system

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Introduction to Infectious Disease (Q&A_IntroductionInfectDisease) ☢
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Microbiome
	Human Microbiome The collection of all the microorganisms living in association with the human body
		Eukaryotes
		Archaea
		Bacteria
		Viruses
	10x more bacteria than human cells ~ 1-3% of body mass
	Affected by:
		Genetics
		Diet
		Stress
		Drugs
		Pregnancy
	Essential for maintaining health and homeostasis
		Produce vitamins that we do not have the genes to make
		Break down our food to extract nutrients we need to survive
		Challenge/teach our immune systems to recognize dangerous invaders 
		Produce helpful anti-inflammatory/“antibiotic” compounds that fight off other disease-causing microbes
		Many functions still unknown or not well understood

Colonization
	Organism living on another organism without causing immune response
	Microbiome term that encompasses all the organisms that exist in a particular area of the body
	Example: Lactobacillus in the gut

Infection
	Invasion of an organism into another organism's tissues that triggers an immune response, i.e., causes disease, becomes a pathogen
	A single type of organism can both colonize and infect a host
	Example: E. coli
		E. coli is colonized in the large intestine (colonizer)
		E. coli in the urine causes a UTI (Urinary Tract Infection) (pathogen)

Skin
	Staphylococcus epidermidis (Gram +)
	Corynebacterium (+)
	Propionibacterium (+)
	Staphylococcus aureus (+)
	Streptococcus pyogenes (+)
	Candida (Fungus)
	Pityrosporum (F)
	Acinetobacter (-)

Nasopharynx
	Staphylococcus epidermidis (+)
	Viridans Streptococci (+)
	Staphylococcus aureus (+)
	Streptococcus pneumoniae (+)
	Haemophilus (-)
	Neisseria (-)
	Corynebacterium (+)

Colon
	Bacteriodes (-)
	Clostridium (+)
	Enterobacteriaceae (-)
		Escherichia coli
		Proteus mirabilis
	Enterococcus (+)
	Lactobacillus (+)
	Corynebacterium (+)
	Mycobacterium (+/-)
	Pseudomonas (-)
	Staphylococcus (+)
	Streptococcus (+)
	Candida (F)

Precautions
	Prevent infection
		Of other patients
		Of yourself
	Mitigate colonization pressure
		Proportion of patients colonized with an organism in a given space during a given time
		Ex: 3 out of 15 patients in the Magee Adult ICU are colonized with MRSA
		As the ratio increases the risk of nosocomial infections increases

Standard Precautions
	Used for every patient encounter
	Includes hand hygiene upon entering and exiting the patient’s room
	Gloves

Contact Precautions ('Contact Precautions')

Droplet Precautions ('Droplet Precautions')

Airborne Precautions ('Airborne Precautions')

Airborne Precautions ('Airborne Precautions_')

Objectives
	Define the human microbiome
	Compare and contrast colonization versus infection
	Describe the different precaution protocols for patient care

Introduction to Respiration (Q&A_IntroResp)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Breathing
	Take in O2 → used by cells/tissues to produce ATP
	Eliminate CO2 → byproduct of Krebs cycle
	('Breathing')
		PO2= 21
		PCO2= 0.04
		----------
		PO2=13
		PC=2=5.3
		----------
		PO2=6.0
		PCO2=6.0

Breathing ('Breathing2')
	Drive to breath
	Skeletal muscle strength 
	Ability of thorax to expand
	Open airways
	Open alveoli
	Healthy alveolar epithelium 
	Blood in alveolar capillary

Drive to Breath
	Respiratory center
		Medulla and Pons
	Drugs 
		Opiates
		Sedatives
	Injury to brainstem

Skeletal muscle ('Breathing2')
	Expand the chest wall
		Determines tidal volume VT
	Generate negative intrathoracic pressure
		Pressure gradient to bring air into lungs
		Aids in venous return to heart
	Myasthenia gravis
	Spinal cord injury
		“C3, C4, C5 keep the diaphragm alive”

Expanding Thorax
	('Expanding Thorax')
		Expiration
			Thoracic cavity reduces
			External intercostal muscles relax
			Diaphragm relaxes
		Inspiration
			Thoracic cavity expands
			External intercostal muscles contract
			Diaphragm contracts
	---------------------------------
	Chest wall structural 
		Pectus excavatum
		Pectus carinatum
		Pregnancy
		Ascites
		Obesity
	Chest wall elastic
		Scleroderma
		Scar tissue

Open airways ('Open airways')
	Upper airways
		Food
		Thick secretions
	Lower airways
		Secretions
			Cystic Fibrosis
		Smooth muscle 
			Asthma
			Bronchitis

Open alveoli ('Open alveoli')
	Filled with stuff
		Blood
			Diffuse alveolar hemorrhage
		Pus
			Pneumonia
		Water
			Pulmonary Edema
	Collapse
		Atelectasis

Gas exchange ('Gas exchange') ('Gas exchange2')
	Healthy alveolar epithelium
		Interstitial lung disease
	Adequate blood flow 
		Pulmonary embolism
		Anemia
		Oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve

Spirometry ('Spirometry') Γ('spirometry-chart-healthy.png') Γ('fev1fevratio.png') Γ('Pulmó Sigles Mnemotècnia.png')
	Forced Expiratory Volume in 1 second (FEV1)
	Forced Vital Capacity (FVC)
		---------------------------------------
		Ratio FEV1/FVC differentiates restrictive from obstructive pathology

Restrictive Lung Disease ('Restrictive Lung Disease')
	Causes of pulmonary disease that result in decreased total lung volume
	Decrease in all volumes
		TV
		IRV
		ERV
		RV
	Preserved FEV1/FVC ratio

PAINT Restrictive Lung Disease
	Cause → Example
		Pleural → Pneumothorax; Pleural scarring
		Alveolar → Pulmonary Edema, Pneumonia
		Interstitial → ILD
		Neuromuscular → Myasthenia gravis, ALS
		Thoracic Pectus → Excavatum, Obesity

Obstructive Lung Disease
	Pulmonary disease impedes airflow and can result in air trapping
	Change in lung volumes
		Increase total lung volume
		Increase in functional residual volume
		Decrease in inspiratory reserve volume
	Decreased FEV1/FVC ratio
	COPD, Asthma, Cystic Fibrosis

Objectives
	Discuss the steps required for normal respiration
	Define clinically relevant lung volumes
	Apply spirometry to compare and contrast restrictive versus obstructive lung disease

Atherosclerosis (Q&A_Atherosclerosis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Atheroma ("Atheroma Composition.png")
	Plaque that forms in arteries
		Composed of cells and other material
			Calcium
			Cholesterol
			Vascular wall cells
			Leukocytes

.Atherosclerosis
	Disease of atheroma formation within arteries
	Can cause the following:
		Ischemic heart disease
		Peripheral arterial disease
		Chronic kidney disease
		Stroke
	---------------------------------------------
	-is the specific condition characterized by the buildup of
		fats,
		cholesterol, and
		other substances
			in and on the artery walls,
				leading tthe formation of plaque.
	-can lead tvarious complications such as
		heart attack,
		stroke,
		peripheral artery disease.
	-It is a common and slowly worsening condition that may begin as early as childhood and is influenced by factors like
		diet,
		lifestyle,
		genetics,
		smoking,
		high blood pressure.
	-----------------------------------
	Disease of atheroma formation within arteries

.Risk Factors
	Hyperlipidemia
	Hypertension
	Smoking
	Diabetes
	Obesity
	Obstructive Sleep Apnea
	Inactivity
	Age
	Family History
	Stress/Alcohol

.Pathogenesis I
	Process begins in childhood and advances with age
	Endothelial dysfunction
	Inflammation
	Macrophages
	Cytokines
	high sensitivity C reactive protein (hs CRP)
	Leukocyte activation

.Initiation: Formation of the Fatty Streak
	1. Endothelial Cell Dysfunction ('')
	2. Plaque Progression ('')
	3. Stable Plaque ('')
	4. Vulnerable Plaque ('')

.Pathogenesis II
	1. Endothelial cell dysfunction
	2. Modification of apoB containing lipoproteins and infiltration of immune cells
	3. Internalization of modified lipoproteins → foam cells 
	4. Activation of macrophage inflammatory pathways → positive feedback
	ΓMuscle aprt
		5. Infiltration/proliferation smooth muscle cells
		6. Smooth muscle cells produce extracellular matrix → fibrous cap
		7. Unresolved inflammation → formation of vulnerable plaques
		8. Rupture of thinning fibrous cap → thrombus formation and clinical events

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Hypertension (Q&A_Hypertension)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Hypertension
	Accounts for more CV disease related death than any other modifiable risk factor
	Second preventable cause of death for any reason
	Prevalence could increase by 15-20% by 2025
	Causes: Stroke, MI, heart failure, renal failure, retinopathy

Risk Factors
	Obesity
	Inactivity
	Age
	Family History
	Alcohol
	High-sodium diet

N/A 1
	Blood Pressure = ("Cardiac Output" x "Total Peripheral Resistance")

Definitions
	Blood pressure: 
		Measured pressure in mmHg at the level of the heart
	Cardiac output: 
		Volume of blood exiting the heart during systole
	Total Peripheral Resistance: 
		Amount of force exerted on circulating blood by the vasculature of the body

N/A 2
	Graph: Systolic pressure, Mean arterial pressure, Diastolic pressure ('Graph pressure')

N/A 3
	('Vein Arthery Cmponents') ('') ('')

N/A 4
	Blood Pressure = "(P) Amount of substance" / "(n) Volume of the space (V)""
	Ideal Gas Law:  PV = nRT

Total Peripheral Resistance
	Sympathetic Nervous System
	Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system
	Plasma volume

Sympathetic Nervous System
	α1 Receptors
		Vessels → Vasoconstriction
		Kidney → Increases reabsorption of sodium
	β1 Receptors
		Myocardium → Increases HR and contractility
		Kidney → Renin release → RAAS

Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone System (RAAS)
	Renin released → converts Angiotensinogen to Angiotensin ('Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system')
	Angiotensinogen → Angiotensin 1 → Angiotensin 2 → effects
		Kidney to increase sodium reabsorption
		Adrenal gland to stimulates release of aldosterone → increases sodium reabsorption
		Arterioles to cause vasoconstriction 
		Brain → increase oral water intake, release anti-diuretic hormone (ADH), decreases sensitivity of baroreceptor reflex

Types of Hypertension
	Essential 
		Most common
		No identifiable cause found → likely genetic component
	Secondary
		Caused by known other condition
		Ex: renal artery stenosis

Hypertension Table ('Hypertension Table')
	N/A

Clinical Presentation
	Asymptomatic
	Hypertensive emergency:
		Headache
		Vision changes
		Chest pain
		Shortness of breath
		Back pain

Diagnosis
	Screening in office
		Annually adults
		More frequently with risk factors
	Must have multiple readings from office
	Home blood pressure validated monitor 
	Ambulatory Blood Pressure Monitoring
		Exclude patients with white coat 
		syndrome

Treatment
	Evaluation to look for complications of hypertension
		Fundoscopic disease → Eye exam
		Heart disease → EKG, Bloodwork
		Kidney disease → Bloodwork, Urine test
	Non-pharmacologic:
		Weight loss
		Smoking cessation
		Limit alcohol
	When to start medication
		Individualized
		HTN stage 1 for those with cardiovascular risk factors
		Everyone with HTN stage 2 unless old enough that potential risks of medication outweigh benefit
	Medication examples:
		Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors ACE-I (Lisinopril)
		Angiotensin II Receptor Blockers ARB (Losartan)
		Calcium channel blockers (Amlodipine)
		Thiazide diuretics (Hydrochlorothiazide HCTZ)

Complications
	Coronary heart disease
	Myocardial Infarction
	Stroke (Ischemic and Hemorrhagic)
	Hypertensive encephalopathy
	Renal Failure
	Peripheral Arterial Disease
	Atrial Fibrillation (arrythmia)
	Aortic Aneurysm

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Methicillin-Resistant Staphylococcus Aureus (MRSA) (Q&A_Methicillin-ResistantStaphylococcusAureus)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

MRSA
	Gram positive cocci
	Resistant to methicillin, i.e. beta lactam antibiotics
	Hospital Acquired
		Blood stream (Bacteremia)
		Skin/soft tissue (Cellulitis)
		Lungs (Pneumonia)
		Usually more severe
	Community Acquired
		Skin
		Less severe

Risk Factors
	Direct contact with colonized surfaces or people
	Colonizer
		Nares
		Skin
		Vagina
		Oropharynx
	~ 50% healthy adults at least intermittently colonized
	Colonization
		Diabetes patients on insulin
		Human Immunodeficiency Virus-Positive (HIV+)
		Hemodialysis patients
		Intravenous drug abusers
		Patients with chronic skin lesions
	Infection: Immune deficiency or physiologic stress
		Diabetics on insulin
		Neutropenia (decreased neutrophil count)
		Rheumatoid arthritis
		Corticosteroid therapy
		Chronic skin disease or skin trauma
			IV drug use
			Lacerations/abrasions
	Hospital Acquired:
		Antibiotics
		Prolonged hospitalization/ICU
		Catheter use
		Patients with prosthetics
		Surgical and/or burn patients

Pathogenesis
	Invades via “traumatic inoculation”
	Once inside, secretes membrane-damaging enzymes and toxins that harm host tissue
	May evade host response and spread through blood to almost anywhere in body

Clinical Manifestations
	Cellulitis/Septic Arthritis
		Erythema
		Edema
		Calor
		Rubor
		Abscess formation
	Other areas:
		Osteomyelitis
		Bacteremia
		Endocarditis
		Pneumonia

Diagnosis
	Culture and sensitivity testing of blood or pus
	Confirms presence of Staphylococcus aureus
	Allows for determination of resistance or sensitivity to methicillin, a relative of penicillin

Treatment
	Antibiotic: 
		Outpatient: Trimethoprim Sulfamethoxazole
		Inpatient: Vancomycin
	Source control:
		Incision and drainage
		Removal of prosthetics

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Contact Precautions
		“Gown and glove”
	Staphyloccus aureus not easily removed from the skin by scrubbing
		Antimicrobial soaps preferred

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	All equipment that comes in direct contact with a draining lesion needs to be cleaned with an approved germicidal agent
	Clients with MRSA may attend hydrotherapy or aquatic therapy provided the area of colonization can be contained
		If wound, drainage must be contained within the dressing without evidence of breakthrough

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of MRSA
	Identify common clinical manifestations of MRSA
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding MRSA

Vancomycin-Resistant Enterococcus (VRE) (Q&A_Vancomycin-ResistantEnterococcus)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Vancomycin-Resistant Enterococcus (VRE)
	-VRE is just one example of superbugs caused by antibiotic overuse and inadequate hospital infection control. They are a serious public health threat. 
	-This bacteria is resistant to vancomycin, making it hard to treat. VRE most commonly causes an infection in the urinary tract, blood, or a wound. 
	-The following video supplies a general overview of VRE.

N/A 1
	Gram positive cocci
		Often in pairs or chains
		Lives mainly in GI tract
	Resistant to vancomycin
	Hospital Acquired
		Urinary Tract
		Bloodstream
		Heart Valves
		Wounds

Risk Factors
	Previous antibiotic use
		Vancomycin
		Broad spectrum antibiotics
	Patient factors
		Prolonged hospitalization
		Comorbidities
	Cancer
	Transplant
		Critical Illness
		Indwelling devices
	Colonization Pressure

Pathogenesis
	Colonization usually precedes infection
	Healthcare workers typical vectors
	Contact transmission
	Colonized patient
		Feces
		Urine
		Blood
	Contaminated surfaces
		Counters: 5-7 days
		Bedrails: >24 hours
		Phones: 1 hour

Urinary Tract Infection Clinical Manifestations
	Dysuria
	Frequency
	Urgency
	Hematuria
	Suprapubic pain
	Flank pain
	Can progress tourosepsis
		Blood streaminfection
		Fever
		Chills
		Delirium
		Shock

Diagnosis
	Culture andsensitivity testing ofblood, urine or pus
	Confirms presence ofenterococcus
		E. Faecium
		E. faecalis
	Allows for determination of resistance or sensitivity to vancomycin

Treatment
	Antibiotic: Linezolid
	Colonization prevention
		Hand hygiene
		Appropriate PPE
		Proper cleaning of equipment

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Contact Precautions
		“Gown and glove”
	Enterococcus not easily removedfrom the skin by scrubbing
		Antimicrobial soaps preferred
	All equipment that comes in direct contact with a draining lesion needs to be cleaned with an approved germicidal agent
	Clients with VRE may attend hydrotherapy or aquatic therapy provided the area of colonization can be contained
		If wound, drainage must be contained within the dressing without evidence of breakthrough

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of VRE
	Identify common clinical manifestations ofVRE
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment andimplications for healthcare providersregarding VRE

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Tuberculosis (TB) (Q&A_Tuberculosis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-While tuberculosis is not very prevalent in the US and most European countries, according to the CDC, 25% of the world’s population are infected with TB. Here is a list of famous people from the last 100 years that had TB:  
	Eleanor Roosevelt 
	George Orwell 
	Nelsen Mandela 
	Tina Turner 
	Ringo Star 

TB
	Bacteria belonging to the Mycobacterium tuberculosis complex
		Rod-shaped
		Non-spore-forming
		Aerobic
	1.7 billion people worldwide infected
		Sub-Saharan Africa
		India
		Southeast Asia
	US low infection rate
		<25 cases per 100,000

Risk Factors
	Poverty
	Poor access to healthcare
	Immunocompromised
		HIV
	Institutionalized

Pathogenesis
	Exposure from an infected individual
	Most commonly infectious pulmonary TB transmitted to other persons by droplet nuclei, which are aerosolized by coughing, sneezing or speaking
		As many as 3,000 nuclei can be aerosolized by one cough
		Droplets dry rapidly but may suspend in air for several hours, gaining direct access to terminal air passages
	Crowding in poorly-ventilated rooms is one of the most important factors in transmission
	Not all patients with TB show signs of infection 🡪 Latent TB
	Risk of developing disease after infection depends on endogenous factors:
		Individual’s susceptibility to disease
		Cell-mediated immunity function
	Two types of disease
		Primary
		Secondary
	Primary TB: Disease immediately after infection
		More common in young children
	Results from initial infection from tubercle bacilli
		Primary site enlarges
		Central area necrosis
		Cavitation develops
	May also spread to other parts of body via bloodstream = disseminated
	Secondary TB: Disease at some time after infection
		Due to reactivation of dormant bacilli or reinfection after treatment
		~10% of people with infection will develop
	Lung involvement varies greatly, from small pneumonia to extensive cavitary disease
		Liquefied necrotic contents of granuloma/tubercle are ultimately discharged into airways, causing satellite lesions
	One-third of untreated patients succumb to severe pulmonary TB within months after onset
	Others undergo spontaneous remission or chronic, progressively debilitating course (“consumption”)

Clinical Manifestations
	Fever
	Night sweats
	Weight loss & anorexia
	Malaise & weakness
	Cough
		Initially nonproductive
		Eventually purulent (pus-filled)
		BLOOD STREAKING
	Lymph Node TB
		Painless enlarged lymph nodes
	Pleural TB
		TB bacilli escape into the pleural cavity
		Pleural chest pain and effusions
	Miliary TB
		Hematogenous spread

Diagnosis
	Mycobacterial Culture
		DEFINITIVE DIAGNOSIS
		Isolation of M. tuberculosis from a diagnostic specimen
	Nucleic Acid Amplification
		Rapid confirmation of TB in persons with AFB-positive specimens
		High specificity and sensitivity approaching that of culture

Treatment
	Antibiotics
		Isoniazid, Pyrazinamide, Ethambutol, Rifampin
	Treat for at least 6 months
	Monitor effect of treatment by sputum cultures frequently
		If not a negative culture at 3 months, need to adjust regimen

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Airborne Precautions
		Gown, glove, N95, Face Shield
	Isolate patients with possible or diagnosed TB in a private room with negative pressure
	Continue isolation until sputum smears are negative for 3 consecutive determinations
		2-4 weeks

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of TB
	Identify common clinical manifestations of TB
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding TB


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Acinetobacter Baumannii (Q&A_AcinetobacterBaumannii)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Acinetobacter
	Gram-negative bacillus
	Typically found in water and soil
	Can colonize skin, wounds, respiratory tract, GI tract

Acinetobacter
	Hospital acquired infection in US
		Ventilator associated pneumonia
	VAP
		Central line associated bloodstream infection
	CLABSI
		Catheter-associated UTI
	CAUTI
		Surgical site infections

Risk Factors
	Critical illness
	Prolonged hospital stay or residence in long term care facility
	Tubes/Lines/Drains
	Broad Spectrum Antibiotic use

Pathogenesis
	Transmission
		Contact with common source contamination (equipment)
		Cross infection by healthcare workers
	Multiple mechanisms for adherence to surfaces (organic and inorganic), induce cell death, and evade the immune system
		Biofilm formation
		Outer membrane protein A
		K1 capsule
		Fimbriae

Clinical Manifestations
	Ventilator Associated Pneumonia:
		Fever
		Increasing O2 requirement
		Increased secretions
	Bloodstream infections
		Fever
		Shock
		Delirium
	Meningitis (rare)
		Fever
		Seizures

Diagnosis
	Positive acinetobactor culture from:
		Sputum
		Blood
		Cerebrospinal Fluid
		Urine
		Wound
	Very important to differentiate colonization from infection

Treatment
	Resistance big issue
	No great data to guide treatment
	Will be based on local resistance patterns, individual culture sensitivities, and guidance from infectious disease providers
	First line: Cefepime or Meropenem

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Contact Precautions
		“Gown and glove”
	Clean equipment with approved agent
	Good hand hygiene can help decrease the risk to your patients

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of Acinetobacter
	Identify common clinical manifestations of Acinetobacter
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding Acinetobacter


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Clostridioides Difficile (C. Diff) (Q&A_ClostridioidesDifficile)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

C. Diff
	Gram positive anaerobic bacillus
		Produces toxin
	Colonization in GI tract precedes infection
	Hospital Acquired
		Colon
	Rarely Community Acquired

Risk Factors
	Previous antibiotic use
		Broad spectrum antibiotics
		Fluoroquinolones
	Patient factors
		Prolonged hospitalization
		Comorbidities
		Advanced age
		Immunocompromised
		GI Surgery
	Gastric Acid Suppression

Pathogenesis
	Colonization occurs by the fecal-oral route
	C difficile forms heat-resistant spores that can persist in the environment for several months to years
	Spores transform into vegetative cell 🡪 cause disease
	Pathogenic strains of C difficile produce 2 distinct toxin proteins that bind to receptors on the intestinal mucosal cells and enter the cells, triggering inflammation

Clinical Manifestations
	Mild to moderate watery diarrhea that is rarely bloody
	Cramping abdominal pain
	Anorexia
	Malaise
	Fever, especially in more severe cases
	May form yellow plaques on colon lining
		pseudomembranous colitis

Diagnosis
	Positive nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT) for C. difficile toxin B gene
	Positive stool test for C. Difficile toxin
	Colonoscopy

Treatment
	Antibiotics
		Metronidazole
		PO (per oris, “by mouth”) or IV (intravenous)
		Vancomycin PO only
		Vancomycin Enema
	Fecal Microbiota Transplant
	Colectomy

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Contact Precautions
		“Gown and glove”
	Clean equipment with diluted bleach
	Alcohol-based sanitizers are INEFFECTIVE

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of C. Diff
	Identify common clinical manifestations of C. Diff
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding C. Diff

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Legionella Pneumophila (Q&A_LegionellaPneumophila)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Legionella
	Aerobic pleomorphic gram negative bacilli
		Lives in water, soil, and intracellularly
		Causes Legionnaires’ Disease 
		Both community and hospital acquired
		Associated with waterborne disease outbreaks

Risk Factors
	Advanced age
	Immunocompromised
	Chronic heart, lung, renal disease
	Smoking
	Exposure to contaminated man-made reservoirs

Pathogenesis ('')
	Transmitted as inhaled aerosols from water or soil
	Exists as both biofilm and intracellularly in amoeba
		Large number of bacteria released when biofilm disrupted or amoeba lyses
	Requires high “infectious dose”
		Only 1 case of person to person transmission 

Clinical Manifestations Pneumonia ('') ('')
	Fever
	Cough
	Sputum
	Shortness of breath
	Nausea
	Vomiting
	Diarrhea

Diagnosis
	Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR) of sputum 
	Urine antigen test
	Culture of sputum
	Suspect in following patients with CAP:
		Hospitalized
		Immunocompromised
		Known/possible exposure
		Moderate/severe disease

Treatment
	Guidelines for treating community acquired pneumonia (CAP)
	Penillicin or cephalosporin + macrolide 
	The macrolide is the effective agent against legionella and other “atypical” pathogens for CAP
	Ex: 
		Outpatient: Amoxicillin + azithromycin
		Inpatient: Ceftriaxone + azithromycin

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Standard precautions only
	Only sterile water should be used to fill and rinse respiratory therapy devices
	If the water system is implicated, decontaminate the system by superheating water to 159.8-170.6°F, and maintain until distal sites are flushed

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of Legionella pneumophila
	Identify common clinical manifestations of Legionella pneumophila
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding Legionella pneumophila

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Influenza (Q&A_Influenza)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Flu
	RNA viruses: 
		Influenza A
		Influenza B
		Influenza C
	Occurs in outbreaks and epidemics in winter months
	Also found in birds and pigs

Flu
	Antigenic drifts 
		Minor changes in envelope proteins
		Associated with outbreaks
	Antigenic shifts: 
		Major changes in envelope glycoproteins: hemagluttinin(H) and neuraminidase(N)
		Associated with epidemics
	Reassortment between animal and human viruses associated with pandemics
		Ex: H1N1 Influenza A virus pandemic 2009

Risk Factors
	Unvaccinated
	Advanced age >65
	Pregnant and postpartum women
	Extreme obesity
	Chronic medical conditions
	Residing in long term care facility
	Taking medication that affects the immune system

Pathogenesis ('')
	Transmitted via:
		Inhalation of infectious droplets
		Direct contact
		Fomite contact
	Concern for animal to human transmission of new subtypes
		Exposure to sick/dead chickens cause H5N1 influenza A

Clinical Manifestations
	Fever
	Cough
	Sore Throat
	Shortness of breath
	Myalgias
	Fatigue
	Headache

Diagnosis
	Rapid influenza diagnostic tests (RIDTs)
	Reverse-Transcriptase Polymerase Chain Reaction (RT-PCR)
		most sensitive and specific, but less accessible
	Influenza virus can be cultured
		Viral culture is the gold standard for testing

Treatment
	Most patients only need supportive care
		Rest
		Fluids
		NSAIDs
	Patients improve within 2-5 days
	Treat within 48 hours of symptom onset
	Goal is to decrease symptomatic days and prevent complications
	Antiviral: Oral Oseltamivir (Tamiflu)
	Best treatment is prevention by yearly vaccination

High Risk of Complications
	Advanced Age
	Pregnancy
	Chronic lung disease
	Cardiovascular disease
	Immune deficiency

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Droplet precautions
	Vaccinations for you & your staff 
		Up to quadrivalent targeted against 4 most likely subtypes to cause infections in the upcoming season
	Handwashing
	Covering sneezes/coughs
	Masks for patients who are coughing
	Stay home if you have influenza

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of influenza
	Identify common clinical manifestations of influenza
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding influenza


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Norovirus (Q&A_Norovirus)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Norovirus
	Retrovirus
		Genetic information is stored on single stranded RNA
		Replication requires reverse transcriptase enzyme to convert RNA to DNA
	Leading cause of gastroenteritis among all age groups in US
	Leading cause of food borne outbreaks in the US
	Higher incidence in winter months
	Very contagious
		Only need a few particles to make others sick
		When infected, shed billions
	Immunity 
		Strain specific
		Only lasts a few weeks

Risk Factors
	Outbreaks
		Restaurants
		Health care facilities
		Schools
	Age
		More common in pediatrics

Pathogenesis ('')
	Fecal/Vomitus-oral route
	Contact with contaminated:
		Food
		Water
		People
		Surfaces

Clinical Manifestations
	Last 24-72 hours
	Nausea
	Vomiting
	Diarrhea
	Abdominal Cramps
	Low grade fever
	Headache
	Myalgias
	Malaise

Diagnosis
	RT-qPCR Assays
		Detect RNA of virus
		Stool, Vomitus, Food, Water
		Preferred method

Treatment
	No specific treatment except supportive measures
	Oral fluid and electrolyte replacement
	Analgesia (Tylenol)
	If severe:
		Anti-emetics
		IV fluid resuscitation
		IV electrolyte replacement

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Contact Precautions
		“Gown and glove”
	Clean equipment with approved agent
	Good hand hygiene can help decrease the risk to your patients

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of Norovirus
	Identify common clinical manifestations of Norovirus
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding Norovirus

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) & Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (AIDS) (Q&A_HIV)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

HIV
	Retrovirus
		Genetic information is stored on single stranded RNA
		Replication requires reverse transcriptase enzyme to convert RNA to DNA
		Causes Acquired Immunodeficiency Syndrome (AIDS)
		Prevalence:
		1.2 million people in US living with HIV
		38 million worldwide living with HIV

Risk Factors
	MSM (Men who have sex with men)
	Injection drug use
	Sex workers
	Unprotected Intercourse

Pathogenesis ('')
	Transmission via blood or body fluids coming into direct contact with vascular mucus membrane or damaged skin
		Sexual: Virus is in semen and vaginal fluid 
		Blood: Direct contact between virus and one’s bloodstream
		Vertical: Mother to child during pregnancy, due to the shared blood circulation
		Can also be transmitted via breast milk
	Not transmitted
		Other bodily fluids:
	Saliva
	Tears
	Sweat
		Hugging, shaking hands
		Insect vectors
	Damage to the Immune System, especially CD4+ T cells
		Kills cells directly 
	Disrupts cell membrane during budding
	Disrupts normal metabolism needed for the survival of the cell
		Apoptosis (cell suicide)
	Regulation of cell's machinery and functions become grossly distorted because of HIV replication
	Infected cell may undergo apoptosis

Disease Course ('')
	Transmission 
		Viral prodrome
		Rapid viral replication 
		Seroconversion of antibodies
	Acute HIV Infection 

	Chronic HIV Infection 
		CD4<200
		AIDS defining conditions
	AIDS
		Asymptomatic
		Steady state viral load
		Progressive decline in CD4 counts
		More susceptible to certain infections (candidiasis, folliculitis)

Clinical Manifestations
	Early:
		Fever
		Headache
		Fatigue
		Lymphadenopathy
	Late
		Weight Loss
		Night Sweats
		Extreme fatigue
		Sores and blotches on mucus membranes and skin

Diagnosis
	Enzyme-linked Immunosorbent Assay
		Tests for antibodies against HIV
		Sensitivity and specificity >99.9%
	Western Blot
		Confirmatory test performed on positive ELISA specimens

Treatment
	Prevention/treatment of other infections
		Vaccination
	Pneumococcal
	Influenza
	Hepatitis A and B
		Screening
	STIs
	TB
	Highly-Active Antiretroviral Therapy (HAART)
		Typically 3 or more drugs that inhibit viral replication by different mechanisms
		Ensures that resistance to a single agent won’t result in propagation of virus
		Does not cure

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Standard precautions only
	All needle sticks must be reported
	99.7% of all needle stick exposures to HIV blood do not result in infection 

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of HIV
	Identify common clinical manifestations of HIV
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding HIV

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Human Immunodeficiency Virus II (HIV) Prophylaxis (Q&A_HIVProphylaxis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Pre-Exposure Prophylaxis (PrEP)
	Purpose decrease transmission >90%​
	Indications​
		High risk for exposure​
	Includes high risk sexual behavior and illicit drug use​
		Medical clearance​
	Negative HIV​
	No medical risk factors for complications from medication​
		Patient adherence​
	Strict adherence required​
	Administration​
		Daily PO​
		Patient education

Post Exposure Prophylaxis (PEP) ('')
	Nonoccupational (nPEP)​
		Exposure to known HIV positive individual​
		Unknown HIV status indications​
		Must start within 72 hours (with 24 hours best)​
		28 days of treatment​
	Hepatotoxicity​

Post Exposure Prophylaxis (PEP)
	Occupational (healthcare) low risk, NOT no risk
		High risk exposures​
		Immediate wound care​
		Pt testing if they approve​
		+/starting immediate therapy

Objectives
	Discuss indications, contraindications, risks and benefits of PrEP and PEP

Severe Acute Respiratory Syndrome Coronavirus 2 (COVID) (Q&A_COVID)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

SARS-CoV-2
	December 2019: First identified in Wuhan, China 
	January 2020: First laboratory confirmed case in US
	February 2020: WHO designates disease coronavirus 2019 aka “COVID-19”

SARS-CoV-2
	Type of coronavirus
	Positive stranded RNA virus
	Same subgenus as SARS
	Distant relation to MERS

Pathogenesis
	Transmission
	Respiratory droplets
	Inhalation of particles that remain in air 
	Viral shedding greatest around symptom onset
	Incubation period average 4-5 days

Clinical Manifestations ('')
	Asymptomatic
	Fever
	Cough
	Shortness of breath
	Fatigue
	Myalgias
	Headache
	Loss of taste/smell
	Congestion
	Nausea/vomiting
	Diarrhea

Risk Factors for Severe Disease
	Age 
	Cancer
	Chronic diseases of lung, liver, or kidney
	Heart conditions
	BMI>30
	Pregnancy
	Diabetes mellitus 
	Smoking
	Transplant
	HIV
	Mental health disorders
	Substance use disorders
	Immunosuppressive medications

Health Inequity
	Some racial and ethnic minority groups disproportionately affected by COVID-19
		Discrimination
		Health access and utilization
		Occupation
		Educational, income, and wealth gaps
		Housing/exposure

Diagnosis
	RT-qPCR Assays
		Detect RNA of virus
		Gold standard
	Antigen Test
		Detect antigens on viral surface
		Rapid at-home tests
		NPV sub-optimal
	Chest X-ray
		Diffuse ground glass opacities
	Labs
		High inflammatory markers 
			Ferritin
			ESR
			CRP
		Leukopenia
		Coagulopathy
			D Dimer

Implications for Healthcare Providers
	Airborne Precautions
		Gown and gloves
		N95 and Face Shield, or PAPR
	Continue isolation for at least 10 days and 24-hours fever free

Treatment
	Outpatient: Mild to moderate
		Supportive
		Risks for progression to severe disease
			Nirmatrelvir/Ritonavir 
			Monoclonal antibodies (mAb)
	Inpatient
		No oxygen
			Supportive care
			Consider mAB risk of progression
		Low flow oxygen 
			Remdesivir
			Dexamethasone
			Therapeutic anticoagulation
		Mechanical ventilation
			Dexamethasone
			Immunomodulatory therapy 
				Tocilizumab

Prevention

Long Covid
	Symptoms that extend beyond 4 weeks after initial infection
		Risk factors​
		Age >/= 50​
		Pre-existing conditions​
		Correlation to severity of acute COVID​
		Correlation to number of symptoms of acute COVID

Long Covid Treatment
		Symptom management
			Fatigue
				Cognitive behavioral therapy
				Graded exercise therapy
			Ongoing studies

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of SARS-CoV-2
	Identify common clinical manifestations of SARS-CoV-2
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding SARS-CoV-2

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Orthostatic Hypotension (Q&A_OrthostaticHypotension)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Orthostatic Hypotension
	Significant drop in blood pressure with position changes in space
	SBP drop ≥ 20mmHg
	DBP drop ≥ 10mmHg

N/A 1
	"Blood Pressure" = "Cardiac Output" x "Total Peripheral Resistance"
		"Cardiac Output"
			Stroke Volume
				Preload
				Contractility
				Afterload
			Heart Rate
		"Total Peripheral Resistance"
			Plasma Volume
			VesselTone

Baroreceptor Reflex
	Blood pools in legs (0.5-1L) ↓
		Decrease in preload ↓
			Decreases cardiac output ↓
				Decreases BP ↓
					Baroreceptor Reflex ↓
						Stimulates Sympathetic Response ↓
							Increase vasomotor tone ↓
								Increases total peripheral resistance ↓
									Increases BP

Etiology
	Autonomic dysfunction
		Neurodegenerative conditions
		Diabetes
		Age
		Pregnancy
	Medications
		Antidepressants
		Antihypertensives
	Dehydration

Clinical Manifestations
	Due to decreased cerebral perfusion
		Lightheadedness
		Dizziness
		Blurry vision or tunnel vision
		Syncope
	Up to 50% of patients will have systolic hypertension when seated

Diagnosis
	History
		Dehydration
	Medication List
	Physical exam
		BP seated and repeat standing
		Signs associated disease

Treatment
	Symptom relief
	Non-pharmacologic
		Discontinue medications
		Modify activity
		Compression stockings
		Increased po salt and water 
	Pharmacologic
		Fludrocortisone (mineralcorticoid)
		Midodrine (α1 agonist)

Objectives
	Discuss the baroreceptor reflex and its relationship to postural blood pressure.
	Discuss the etiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of orthostatic hypotension.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Venous Insufficiency (Q&A_VenousInsufficiency)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Lower Extremity Chronic Venous Disease ('')
	Broad term to encompass all venous dysfunction and the subsequent complications
	Valvular disease
	Venous HTN
	Venous thrombosis
	Venous occlusion

Risk Factors
	Age
	Family History
	Prolonged Standing
	BMI
	Smoking
	Pregnancy

CEAP Classification ('CEAP Classification')

Chronic Venous Insufficiency
	CEAP Clinical Signs classification 3-6

Clinical Manifestations
	Telangiectasia (spider veins)
	Dilated intradermal venules
	Most common manifestation
	More common in women
	Varicose veins
	Subcutaneous dilated tortuous veins
	≥3mm when standing
	Cosmetically undesirable
	Physically uncomfortable 
	Lower extremity discomfort → heavy feeling
	Lower extremity pain
		Worse in dependent position
		Improves with elevation and walking
	Lower extremity edema
		Can be unilateral
		Improves with elevation
		Does not respond well to diuretics
	Skin discoloration
		Brown or grey hyperpigmentation →hemosiderin deposition →breakdown of RBCs extravasated into dermis
	Stasis dermatitis
		Common signs of chronic venous insufficiency
		Rash erythematous, pruritic with possibly scaling and weeping
	Itching
		Can be severe → excoriations → skin breakdown

Venous Ulceration
	Location: 
		Low on medial ankle
		Along course great or small saphenous vein
	Appearance
		Pink or red base
		Covered by yellow plaque or exudate
	Mild pain

Diagnosis
	Symptoms
	Venous duplex ultrasound
		Evaluate of extent of reflux
		Evaluate for thrombus

Compression Therapy
	Goals
		Reduce pain
		Improve skin changes
		Heal ulcers
	Contraindications
		PAD
		Superficial or deep venous thrombosis
		Heart failure
		Acute cellulitis/necrosis
	-----------------------------------------------
	Mechanism
		Increasing venous flow velocity →increased venous return
		Need external pressure 35-40mmHg at ankle prevent capillary leaking
		Possibly changes inflammatory matrix in ulcers → promotes healing
	Complications
		Skin necrosis
		Fungal infection
		Contact dermatitis
	-----------------------------------------------
	Static
		Compression hosiery
			Traditional compression stockings
			Varying pressure gradients and styles
		Compression bandages
			Elastic →Profore
			Inelastic →Unna boot
	Dynamic
			Intermittent pneumatic compression

Other Therapy
	Exercise → calf muscle pumping
	Dry skin: emollients 
	Ulcer care
	Vasoactive agents
		Micronized purified flavonoid fraction
	Referral to venous specialist
		Nonhealing ulcers
		Persistent stasis dermatitis/cellulitis

Objectives
	Define lower extremity chronic venous disease and venous insufficiency
	Recognize the CEAP Classification system
	Discuss the risk factors, clinical presentation, diagnosis, and treatments for lower extremity chronic venous disease

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Lymphedema (Q&A_Lymphedema)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Physiology ('') ('')
	Drain excess interstitial fluid
	Large molecule uptake (fats, proteins, etc)
	Immune surveillance

Lymphedema etiology
	Primary
		Genetic
		Developmental abnormality
	Secondary
		Filariasis
		Iatrogenic
			Malignancy associated interventions
		Trauma
		Obesity

Lymphedema pathophysiology ('')
	Impaired lymphatic drainage
	Accumulation of excess interstitial fluid
	Accumulation of fats/proteins in interstitial space
	Impaired immune surveillance

Pathophysiology
	Malignancy associated
		Surgical excision
		Radiation therapy
	Infections
		Filariasis 
		Rosacea
	Morbid obesity
		Increased adipose causing lymphatic obstruction
	Trauma/surgery

Pathophysiology Filariasis ('')
	Wuchereria bancrofti (> 90% of cases)
		Mosquito born nematode (roundworm)
		Adult worm migrates to lymphatics
			Obstructing lymphatic flow

Diagnosis ('')
	History
		Risk factors consistent with lymphedema
		Lack of risk factors for other disease processes
			DVT, tumor, heart failure, kidney failure, liver failure
	Diagnostic testing
		Rule out other pathologies
	Physical exam
		Unilateral extremity swelling
		Nonpitting edema
			Acute lymphedema may be pitting
		Thick, leathery skin
			Peau d’orange

Treatment
	Management, not cure
	General care
		Self-monitoring 
		Limb elevation
		Maintain ideal body weight
		Avoid injuries/abrasions/risks for infection
		Avoid constricting cuffs/garments
			BP cuffs

Treatment
	Interventions
		Compression therapy
			Bandages, garments, intermittent pneumatic compression
		Physiotherapy
			Manual lymphatic drainage (MLD)
			Complete decongestive therapy (CDT)

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology and pathogenesis of lymphedema
	Identify common clinical manifestations of lymphedema
	Discuss diagnostic methods, treatment and implications for healthcare providers regarding lymphedema
