////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Coronary Artery Disease (CAD) (Q&A_CoronaryArteryDisease)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

N/A 1 ('Coronary Artery Disease Arteries.png')
	Atherosclerosis of the arteries that supply the heart
		Left Coronary Artery (LCA)
		Left Anterior Descending Artery (LDA)
		Circumflex Artery (Cx)
		Right Coronary Artery (RCA)
		Right Marginal Artery (RMA)
		Posterior Descending Artery (PDA)

Risk Factors
	Hyperlipidemia
	Hypertension
	Smoking
	Diabetes
	Obesity
	Sleep apnea
	Inactivity
	Age
	Family History
	Stress/Alcohol

Ischemic Heart Disease ('')
	Mismatch between amount of blood flow needed for myocardium to function and the amount of blood flow the myocardium receives
	Inability to meet demand usually due to narrowing of the vessels from atherosclerosis

Ischemic Heart Disease ('')
	Demand = How hard is heart working
		Heart rate
		Contractility 
		Afterload
	Supply = How much oxygen rich blood getting to myocytes

Rate of Oxygen Delivery ('Rate of Oxygen Delivery.png')

Ischemic Heart Disease Pathogenesis
	Increased oxygen demand
		Exercise
		Infection
		Anemia
		-------------------------------↓
		Inability to increase blood flow
			Atherosclerosis
			Hypotension
			Vasospasm
			---------------------↓
			Ischemic Heart Disease
				Stable angina
				NSTEMI
				STEMI

Types of Ischemic Heart Disease
	Acute Coronary Syndrome
		NSTEMI
		STEMI
	Chronic Coronary Syndrome
		Stable ischemic heart disease
		Angina pectoris +
			Risk factors
			Known CAD

Clinical Presentation Angina Pectoris
	Anchonē → Greek “to strangle”
	Pectoris → Latin “chest”
	Paroxysmal, usually recurrent attacks of substernal chest discomfort
	Transient (15 sec-3 min) ischemia 
	Relieved by rest
	Relieved by vasodilator medications
	Substernal chest pain or pressure
	Radiate to:
		Back
		Shoulder (Right)
		Neck and jaw (Right)
	Diaphoresis
	Shortness of breath (SOB)
	Silent

Diagnosis
	Cardiac Stress Test
		Exercise electrocardiography (pictured)
		Exercise or pharmacologic stress combined with imaging 
		Assess functional implications of patient’s coronary artery disease
		Pretest probability of CAD essential for interpretating results 
	Coronary angiography
		Gold standard for diagnosis of coronary artery disease
		NOT required for all patients with chronic coronary disease

Angina Treatment
	Prevention:
		Beta blockers (metoprolol)
		Calcium Channel blockers (amlodipine)
		Long-acting nitrates (isosorbide-mononitrate)
	Acute symptoms
		Short-acting nitrate (Nitroglycerin)

Angina Treatment
	Treat underlying issues
	Hypertension
	Arrythmias
	Anemia
	Hypoxia

	DO2 = CO x (1.39 x [Hb] x SaO2 + (0.0003 x PaO2))

Secondary Prevention 
	Antiplatelet Therapy
		Aspirin
		Clopidogrel
	Lipid lowering
		High intensity statin (atorvastatin)
	Risk factor reduction
		Weight loss
		Smoking cessation
		Control chronic HTN, DM, OSA

Objectives
	Define coronary artery disease
	Explain ischemic heart disease as it relates to myocardial demand and oxygen delivery
	Discuss the risk factors, clinical presentation, diagnosis, and treatments for chronic coronary disease


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD) (Q&A_PeripheralArteryDisease)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD)
	Blockage of an artery causing ischemia 
	Lower extremities most common sites
	Causes
		Atherosclerosis
		Thrombus

Risk Factors
	Age
	Male sex
	Smoking
	Diabetes 
	Cardiovascular disease
	Hyperlipidemia
	Hypertension
	Obesity
	Chronic kidney disease
	Inactivity
	Ethnicity
	HIV infection

Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD)
	Blockage of an artery causing ischemia 
	Lower extremities most common sites
		Aortoiliac
		Femoropopliteal
		Infrapopliteal
	Causes
		Atherosclerosis
		Thrombus

Rate of Oxygen Delivery for PAD ('Rate of Oxygen Delivery PAD.png')

Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD)
	Increased oxygen demand
		Ambulation 
		-----------------------↓
		Inability to increase blood flow
			Atherosclerosis
			-------------------↓
			Claudication
				“Angina” of the leg

Peripheral Artery Disease (PAD)
	Asymptomatic
	Intermittent Claudication
	Chronic Limb-Threatening Ischemia

PAD Asymptomatic
	71% men 48% women (40-54)
	Less than 50% of patients know they have it
	Independent risk factor for CAD
	Risk of progression to intermittent claudication ~15%

Algorithm for Screening APAD ('Algorithm for Screening APAD.png')
	Abnormal or Absent pedal pulse
	Age ≥ 70
	Age 50-69 + smoking or DM
	-----------------------------↓
		Measure ankle-brachial index
		----------------------------↓
			ABI ≤0.90 Diagnostic PAD
			------------------------
			ABI 1 1.3
			Normal 
			No further testing
			------------------------
			ABI > 1.3
			Doppler ankle waveforms
			Toe pressures

PAD Intermittent Claudication
	Reproducible pain with ambulation relieved with rest
	ACC/AHA Rates of 5-year outcomes
		Stable claudication: 70-80%
		Worse claudication: 10-20%
		Chronic limb-threatening ischemia: (3-21%)
		Non-fatal MI or stroke: 20%
		Death: 15-30% (75% of these CV)

PAD Chronic Limb-Threatening Ischemia
	Presence of PAD plus:
		Pain at rest
		Gangrene
		Lower limb ulceration
		> 2 weeks
	Risk of limb loss 
	Risk of mortality

Clinical Symptoms
	Claudication
		Reproducible muscular pain with activity
		Relieved with rest (10 minutes)
		Varies in severity
		Buttock, hip, thigh, calf, foot
	Atypical extremity pain
	Ischemic rest pain
		Forefoot and toes
		Worse with elevation

Clinical ManifestationsSkin Changes
	Ischemic ulcers
		Trauma →Doesn’t heal due to bad blood supply
		Toe joints, bony prominence, anterior shin, base of heel
		”Punched out” irregular margins, dry base
		Painful
	Gangrene
		Full thickness necrosis → foot
		Dry Black eschar

Clinical Manifestations
	Skin Changes:
		Pallor
		Dry 
		Shiny
		Hairless
	Nail changes
	Cool
	Absent/Diminished pulses 
	Long capillary refill

Ankle-Brachial Index ('Ankle-Brachial Index')
	Ankle SBP ÷ Brachial SBP
	Patient rests supine for ~10 minutes
	Measure SBP of:
		Brachial SBP,
		Dorsalis Pedis (DP)
		Posterior Tibialis (PT)

Ankle-Brachial Index ('Ankle-Brachial Index')
	Measure both right and left sides
	Use higher SBP between arms
	Use higher SBP between DP and PT
	1 1.4 →Normal
	≤ 0.90 diagnostic PAD
	≤ 0.80 →referral
	> 1.4 →Calcification

Secondary Prevention 
	Antiplatelet Therapy
		Aspirin
		Clopidogrel
	Lipid lowering
		High intensity statin (atorvastatin)
	Risk factor reduction
		Weight loss
		Smoking cessation
		Control chronic HTN, DM

Claudication Treatment
	Exercise Therapy
		Increase blood flow
		Decrease inflammation
		Improved endurance
		Improved muscle mitochondrial capacity 
	Cilostazol
		Suppresses platelet aggregation
		Arterial vasodilator
		MOA unknown

Intervention
	Angiogram
	Which patients:
		Significant disability
		Did not respond to previous treatment
		Benefit of intervention outweighs risk
	Percutaneous angioplasty +/stent
	Bypass

Acute LE Ischemia
	Causes:
		Atherosclerotic plaque rupture
		Embolism
		Injury
	Symptoms occur suddenly or subacutely
	6 Ps
		Pain
		Poikilothermic
		Pale
		Pulseless
		Paresthesia

Acute LE Ischemia ('Acute LE Ischemia.png')

Objectives
	Define peripheral artery disease
	Explain peripheral artery disease as it relates to myocyte demand and oxygen delivery
	Discuss the risk factors, clinical presentation, diagnosis, and treatments for peripheral artery disease

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Myocardial Infarction (Heart Attack) (Q&A_MyocardialInfarction)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

('Myocardial Infarction (Heart Attack).png')

Types of Ischemic Heart Disease
	Acute Coronary Syndrome
		NSTEMI
		STEMI
	Chronic Coronary Syndrome
		Stable ischemic heart disease
		Angina pectoris +
			Risk factors
			Known CAD

Acute Myocardial Infarction
	Sudden vessel occlusion causes ischemia

Risk Factors
	Smoking
	Hypercholesterolemia
	Hypertension
	Diabetes
	Obesity
	Stress
	Family History
	Daily consumption of fruit and vegetables
	Regular alcohol consumption
	Regular physical activity

Acute myocardial injury with signs and/or symptoms of ischemia ('Acute myocardial injury')

Pathogenesis Plaque Rupture ('Pathogenesis Plaque Rupture')
	Unstable atheroma with thin smooth muscle layer
	Rupture → platelet activation → platelet plug
		Adhere to vWF in subendothelial matrix via GB 1b receptor
		Changes to GP IIB/IIIa receptor → binds fibrinogen
		Secrete procoagulant substances from granules 
		Role in propogating the coagulation cascade
	Platelets + clotting factors + fibrinogen → stable clot formation and vessel occlusion
	Entire process occurs within minutes

Acute Myocardial Infarction ('Acute Myocardial Infarction')
	Ischemia
		Area at risk but reversible effectsDecrease contractility
		First 20 minutes
		---------------------↓
		Injury
			Small area of necrosis surrounded by larger area at risk
			20 minutes to 6 hours
			------------------------↓
			Infarction
				Area necrosis = area of perfusion
				Beyond 6 hours

Clinical Presentation
	Substernal chest pain or pressure
	Occurring at rest
	Radiate to:
		Back
		Upper extremities
		Neck and jaw
	Diaphoresis
	Shortness of breath (SOB)
	Nausea
	Silent

Diagnosis
	Evidence of ischemia
		Symptoms like chest pain 
		EKG changes
	Elevated biomarkers
	12 lead Electrocardiogram (EKG)
		STEMI: ST-elevation myocardial infarction
		NSTEMI: Non-ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction
	Troponin I
		Protein found in cardiac muscle and released during myocyte death
		High-sensitivity troponin assay can detect levels within the first 3 hours

Coronary Angiography ('Coronary Angiography')
	Catheter inserted in leg and guided up the aorta
	Catheter tip stops at left coronary artery
	Contrast agent injected into arteries, X-ray imaging shows stenosis in left coronary artery

Initial Treatment
	Relieve pain
		Analgesics
		Nitroglycerin
	Oxygen
	Antiplatelet therapy
	Beta-blocker therapy
	Reperfusion therapy
		Percutaneous coronary intervention (PCI)

Secondary Prevention 
	Antiplatelet Therapy
		Aspirin
		Clopidogrel
	Lipid lowering
		High intensity statin (atorvastatin)
	Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitor
		Lisinopril

Secondary Prevention ('Severely Reduced Cardiac Function')
	Risk factor reduction
		Weight loss
		Smoking cessation
		Control chronic HTN, DM, OSA
		Encourage exercise

Objectives
	Define myocardial infarction
	Explain the pathogenesis of myocardial infarction due to atheroma plaque rupture
	Discuss the risk factors, clinical presentation, diagnosis, and treatments for myocardial infarction


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Heart Failure (Q&A_HeartFailure)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Heart Failure
	Clinical syndrome
	Impaired ability to fill with blood
	Impaired ability to pump blood
	Caused by structural or functional cardiac disorder

Heart Failure
	Reduced Ejection Fraction (rEF) 
	Preserved Ejection Fraction (pEF)

Risk Factors
	Coronary Heart Disease
	Valvular Heart Disease
	Smoking
	HTN
	Obesity
	Diabetes

Heart Failure
	Systole
		Contraction of ventricles
		Opening of the pulmonic and aortic valves
		Blood flows out of the heart
	Diastole
		Ventricles relax and fill with blood
		Opening tricuspid and mitral valves
		Perfusion of coronary vessels

Stroke Volume
	Preload: Stretch of ventricular muscles at end of diastole
		Restoring force
		Ventricular compliance
		Volume of blood coming into ventricle
	Contractility: Force that myocytes can generate for a given preload
		Calcium 
	Afterload: Impedance for ejection of blood
		Ventricular volume and wall thickness
		Aortic pressure

Left Ventricular Pressure Volume Loop ('')

Heart Failure reduced EF (HFrEF)
	Ejection Fraction ≤40%
	Ischemic Heart Disease
	Hypertension 
	Valvular Disease
	Idiopathic Dilated Cardiomyopathy

Frank-Starling curves ('Frank-Starling curves')
	Decrease in stroke volume for a given preload (LVEDP)
	Not linear relationship
	Worse failure → flattening of curve
	Can’t increase cardiac output by increasing preload

Heart Failure preserved EF (HFpEF)
	Ejection Fraction ≥50%
	Ischemic Heart Disease
	Hypertension 
	Diabetes
	Hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy
	Restrictive Cardiomyopathy

Remodeling
	Changes to structure of heart in response to physiologic stress
	Adaptive
		Athletes
		Pregnancy
	Pathologic
		Pressure
		Volume

Pathologic Remodeling ('')
	Pressure overload
		Sarcomeres added in parallel
		Increased wall thickness
		HTN
		Aortic stenosis

Pathologic Remodeling ('')
	Volume overload
		Sarcomeres added in series or elongate
		Increased chamber volume and overall cardiac mass
		Valvular insufficiency

Clinical Manifestations
	Excess Fluid:
		Dyspnea, Paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea
		Orthopnea 
		Dry cough
		Edema (Lower extremities)
		Abdominal pain
	Reduced cardiac output
		Fatigue
		Weakness
		Activity intolerance

Clinical Manifestations
	Excess Fluid:
		Dyspnea, Paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea
		Orthopnea 
		Dry cough
		Edema (Lower extremities
		Abdominal pain
	Reduced cardiac output
		Fatigue
		Weakness
		Activity intolerance

New York Heart Association Classification
	Class I: Cardiac disease but no limitations in physical activity
	Class II: Cardiac disease with slight limitations in physical activity
	Class III: Cardiac disease with marked limitation of physical activity
	Class IV: Cardiac disease with no ability to do any physical activity without discomfort. Symptoms may be present at rest.

Clinical Diagnosis ('')
	Symptoms
	Chest X-Ray
	EKG
	Beta Natriuretic peptide (BNP)
	Clinical risk factors
		CAD
		Valvular Disease
	Echocardiogram
	Age

Clinical Diagnosis
	Symptoms
	Chest X-Ray
	EKG
	Beta Natriuretic peptide (BNP)
	Clinical risk factors
		CAD
		Valvular Disease
	Echocardiogram
	Age

Treatment: HFrEF
	Treat underlying cause
	Initial Therapy
		Diuretic →swelling and dyspnea
		RAAS system inhibitor (ACE-I or ARB)
		Beta blocker
	Cardiac Rehabilitation NYHA Class II-III
	Implantable device: 
		Implantable cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD)
		Cardiac resynchronization therapy (CRT)

Objectives
	Define the terms preload, contractility, and afterload as they relate to stroke volume
	Explain how pathologic remodeling affects heart function
	Discuss the risk factors, clinical presentation, diagnosis, and treatments of heart failure

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (Q&A_HypertrophicCardiomyopathy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Cardiomyopathy ('Cardiomyopathy') (REPE en (Q&A_DilatedCardiomyopathy))
	American Heart Association Definition
		Heterogenous diseases of myocardium
		Mechanical or electrical dysfunction
		Usually cause hypertrophy or dilation
		Variety of causes, often genetic
		Lead to cardiovascular death or progressive heart failure disability
		Excludes other known causes for remodeling (IHD, Valvular disease)

Cardiomyopathy (REPE en (Q&A_DilatedCardiomyopathy))
	American Heart Association Definition
		Primary
			Genetic
			Mixed (Genetic and Nongenetic)
			Acquired
		Secondary

PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES ('PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES') (REPE en (Q&A_DilatedCardiomyopathy))

Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM)
	Primary Genetic
	Most common cardiomyopathy
	Definition
		Hypertrophied, nondilated LV
		No other diseases that would cause remodeling

Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM)
		Autosomal dominant
		11 mutant genes
		>400 individual mutations
		Diverse phenotypes

Hypertrophic Obstructive Cardiomyopathy (HOCM)
	Subcategory of HCM
	Asymmetric Interventricular septum(IVS) hypertrophy 
	Obstructs blood flow through the left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT)

Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM)
	Complications
		Left ventricular outflow obstruction
		Diastolic dysfunction
		Mitral regurgitation
		Myocardial ischemia
	Most common cause of sudden cardiac death in young
	Most patients not progressive → normal life expectancy

Clinical Diagnosis ('')
	EKG
	Left Ventricular Hypertrophy
		Increased voltage S wave V1-V3
		Increased voltage R wave V4-V6
	Echocardiography
		Increased LV wall thickness
		Decreased cavity size
	Cardiac MRI
	Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy
		Thick walls
		Small chamber

Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM)
	Diagnosis
		Genetic testing
	Treatment
		Lifestyle changes
		Beta blockers
		Surgery → Septal myomectomy
	Screening
		First-degree relatives at time of diagnosis → genetic testing
		Athletes → History and physical exam

Objectives
	Define cardiomyopathy according to American Heart Association
	Discuss the pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment, and expected disease progression for hypertrophic cardiomyopathy

Dilated Cardiomyopathy (Q&A_DilatedCardiomyopathy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Cardiomyopathy (REPE en (Q&A_HypertrophicCardiomyopathy))
	American Heart Association Definition
		Heterogenous diseases of myocardium
		Mechanical or electrical dysfunction
		Usually cause hypertrophy or dilation
		Variety of causes, often genetic
		Lead to cardiovascular death or progressive heart failure disability
		Excludes other known causes for remodeling (IHD, Valvular disease)

Cardiomyopathy (REPE en (Q&A_HypertrophicCardiomyopathy))
	American Heart Association Definition
		Primary
		Genetic
		Mixed (Genetic and Nongenetic)
		Acquired
	Secondary

PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES ('PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES') (REPE en (Q&A_HypertrophicCardiomyopathy))

Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM)
	Primary Mixed:
	Definition
		Dilation of ventricles
		Normal ventricular wall thickness
		Progress to overall heart enlargement

Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM)
	20-35% familial 
	Infection
		Viral Myocarditis
		Bacterial, fungal rickettsial, parasitic
	Toxins
		Alcohol
		Chemo
	Autoimmune
	Other systemic diseases

Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM)
	Impaired systolic function
	Progressive heart failure
	Arrythmias
		Supraventricular
		Ventricular
	Thromboembolism
	Sudden cardiac death

Clinical Diagnosis
	Echocardiogram
	Dilated Cardiomyopathy
		Thin walls
		Enlarged chamber
		Poor systolic function

Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM)
	Diagnosis
		Symptoms
		Chest X-Ray
		EKG → nonspecific
	Treatment: 
		Meds: Diuretic, ACE-I/ARB, Beta blocker
		Cardiac Rehab
		Implantable device

Objectives
	-Define cardiomyopathy according to American Heart Association
	-Discuss the pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment, and expected disease progression for dilated cardiomyopathy

Restrictive and Acquired Cardiomyopathies (Q&A_Restrictive)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Cardiomyopathy REP¿?
	American Heart Association Definition
		Heterogenous diseases of myocardium
		Mechanical or electrical dysfunction
		Usually cause hypertrophy or dilation
		Variety of causes, often genetic
		Lead to cardiovascular death or progressive heart failure disability
		Excludes other known causes for remodeling (IHD, Valvular disease)

Cardiomyopathy REPE¿?
	American Heart Association Definition
		Primary
			Genetic
			Mixed (Genetic and Nongenetic)
			Acquired
		Secondary

PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES ('PRIMARY CARDIOMYOPATHIES') REPE

Primary Restrictive Cardiomyopathy
	Primary Mixed
	Significantly reduced survival
	Definition
		Normal or decreased volume of both ventricles
		Normal LV wall thickness
		Stiff walls → impaired ventricular filling
		Normal systolic function

Primary Restrictive Cardiomyopathy
	Diagnosis
		BNP
		Echocardiogram
		Cardiac MRI
		Endomyocardial biopsy
	Treatment
		No specific treatment 
		Loop diuretics (furosemide)
		Calcium channel blockers (verapamil)
		Cardiac transplant

Acquired Cardiomyopathy ('')
	Myocarditis
		Acute or chronic inflammatory process
	Stress “Tako-Tsubo”
		Broken heart syndrome
		Potentially reversible
	Peripartum 
	Prolonged tachycardia

Secondary Cardiomyopathy ('')
	Cardiomyopathy due to systemic disease process that affects the heart
		Infiltrative → amyloidosis
		Inflammatory → sarcoidosis
		Endocrine → DM, thyroid disease
		Neuromuscular → muscular dystrophy
		Autoimmune → lupus (SLE)
		Toxins → chemo

Objectives
	-Define cardiomyopathy according to American Heart Association
	-Discuss the pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment, and expected disease progression for primary restrictive cardiomyopathy
	-Recognize the heterogeneity and breadth of cardiomyopathies


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Endocarditis (Q&A_Endocarditis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Endocarditis
	Infection on inside of the heart 
	Usually involves heart valves
	Community or hospital acquired

Risk Factors
	Age > 60
	Male sex
	Injection drug use
	Structural heart disease
		HCM
		Valvular disease (rheumatic heart disease, prosthetic valves)
		Congenital heart disease
	Intravascular device
		ICD
		Chronic hemodialysis

Pathogenesis
	Most common pathogens:
		Staphylococci (42%)
		Streptococci (29%)
		Enterococci (11%)
	Bacteremia →bacteria gets to heart → infects valves

Clinical Manifestations
	Fever
	Malaise
	Night sweats
	Weight loss

Clinical Manifestations
	Heart murmur
	Vascular phenomena:
		Septic emboli: Lung & brain
		Janeway lesions
		Splinter hemorrhages
	Immunologic phenomena
		Roth spots
		Osler nodes
		Glomerulonephritis

Modified Duke Criteria ('Modified Duke Criteria')
	Definite:
		2 major clinical criteria or 1 major and 3 minor or 5 minor
	Possible:
		1 major and 1 minor or 3 minor
	Rejected:
		Resolution of clinical manifestations in ≤ 4 days of antibiotics

Diagnosis
	Blood cultures
	CBC
	Comprehensive metabolic panel (CMP) 
	Transthoracic echocardiogram (TTE)
	Transesophageal echocardiogram (TEE)

Treatment
	IV antibiotics → depends on sensitivity
		Beta-lactam
	Supportive
	Valve repair/replacement

Complications
	Cardiac → heart failure
	Septic Emboli → ischemic limbs
	Neurologic → stroke
	Renal → acute renal failure
	Musculoskeletal → osteomyelitis
	Pulmonary → pulmonary infarcts
	Death (6-month mortality 22-27%)

Objective
	Discuss the risk factors, pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment, and complications of infective endocarditis

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Acute Pericarditis (Q&A_AcutePericarditis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Acute Pericarditis
	Inflammation of pericardial sac
	Sac around heart
		Visceral layer
		Parietal layer
	Most common disorder of pericardium

Causes
	Majority of patients specific etiology not found
	Viral or idiopathic
	Malignancy
	Tuberculosis
	Autoimmune
	Purulent 

Clinical Manifestations
	Chest pain
		Sharp
		Relieved by sitting up and leaning forward
		Worse with inspiration/coughing
	Pericardial friction rub
	EKG: ST segment elevation in all leads
	Pericardial effusion

Diagnostic Workup
	EKG
	CXR
	CBC
	Troponin
	ESR/CRP
	Echocardiography

Diagnosis
	Two of the following:
		Classic chest pain 
		Pericardial friction rub
		EKG changes
		Pericardial effusion

Treatment
	Goals:
		Relieve pain
		Decrease inflammation
		Prevent recurrence/complications
	Usually treated at home
	Patients to be admitted:
		Fever
		Immunocompromised
		Anticoagulated
		Elevated troponin
		Cardiac tamponade

Treatment
	Activity restriction
	High dose NSAIDs
		Ibuprofen
		Aspirin
	Colchicine
	Glucocorticoids
	Continue until symptoms resolve → NSAID taper
	If symptoms persist > 1 week → consider hospitalization/further workup

Objective
	Discuss the risk factors, pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment, and complications of pericarditis

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Asthma (Q&A_Asthma)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Asthma ('Asthma Triggers') ('Asthma Prevalence') ('Asthma Prevalence2')
	Global Initiative for Asthma
		Heterogeneous disease
		Symptoms of wheeze, SOB, chest tightness, cough
		Expiratory airflow limitation
	Current asthma
		Bronchial hyperresponsiveness
		Wheezing
		Previous 12 months
	Majority cases diagnosed < 7 y/o

Risk Factors
	Prenatal/Childhood
		Genetics
		Prenatal exposure to maternal smoking
		Kids: AMAB
		Atopy/Allergies
		Air pollution
		Obesity
	Adult
		Obesity
		Smoking
		Active
		Secondhand
		Occupational exposures
		Bakers
		Health care workers
		Auto-workers
		Hair-dressers

Pathogenesis: Airway Inflammation
	Early Phase
		Allergen exposure ↓
			Synthesis IgE antibodies ↓
				IgE binds mast cells ↓
					Mast cells release mediators ↓
						Histamine
						Prostaglandins
						Leukotrienes
						Release TNF-alpha
						----------------------------↓
						Contract airway smooth muscle 
	Late Phase
		6 hours after exposure ↓
			Recruitment immune cells ↓
				Basophils
				Neutrophils
				T cells (CD4)
				Mast Cells
				-------------------↓
				Eosinophils
					Associated with disease severity
					Damage epithelium
					Remodeling/fibrosis
					------------------------↓
					Airway smooth muscle contraction

Pathogenesis
	Airway smooth muscle inflammation
	Bronchial hyperresponsiveness
	Airway remodeling	

Clinical Manifestations
	Dyspnea
	Cough 
	Wheezing (expiratory)
	Chest tightness
	Episodic 
	Clear triggers
	Atopy
		Pale/edematous membranes nasopharynx
		Nasal polyps
		Atopic dermatitis

Diagnosis
	Spirometry
	Bronchoprovocation testing
	Peak expiratory flow
	Allergy testing
	CXR 
		New onset adults 
		Atypical symptoms

Spirometry ('Spirometry Asthma')
	Test at baseline
	Test for reversibility
		Give 2-4 puffs albuterol
		Recheck 15 min
		Increase at least FEV1 ≥15% 
		Completely back to normal

Classification ('Classification Asthma')

Treatment
	Goals
		Decrease symptoms
		Prevent exacerbations
		Minimize side effects 
	All patients:
		Asthma action plan
		Control Triggers
		Follow up

Treatment
	Short-acting beta agonist (SABA) → albuterol
	Long-acting beta agonist (LABA) → formoterol
	Inhaled corticosteroid (ICS) → budesonide/formoterol
	Systemic glucocorticoids (PO or IV)
	Long-acting muscarinic antagonist (LAMA) → tiotropium
	Leukotriene receptor antagonist (LTRA) → montelukast
	Anti-IgE, anti-IL-5, anti-IL-4 (”mabs”)

Treatment: GINA Update 2020 ('Treatment GINA Update 2020')

('Box 3-5A')

('DIAGNOSIS OF ASTHMA')

Objectives
	Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment and of asthma
	Explain the Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) Classification and Treatment system
	Recognize the recent changes to treatment recommendations

Chronic Obstructive Lung Disease (COPD) (Q&A_COPD)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

COPD
	Fourth leading cause of death in US
	Airflow limitation from airway or alveolar abnormalities
	Smoking and environmental exposures significant risk factors
	Alpha 1 Antitrypsin Deficiency

Emphysema
	Structural distortion of alveoli
		Destruction of walls
		Enlargement of airspace
	Centrilobular (Proxima)
		Smoking
	Paraseptal (Distal)
		Spontaneous pneumothorax
	Panacinar (Diffuse)
		Alpha 1 anti-trypsin deficiency

Chronic Bronchitis
	Chronic productive cough for ≥3 months for two successive years

Asthma ('Pathology of Asthma')
	Chronic inflammatory disorder of airways
	Airway responsiveness 
	Associated airflow obstruction

COPD
	Asthma↓↑
	Emphysema↓↑
	Bronchitis↓↑

Pathogenesis
	Chronic inflammation
		CD8+ T lymphocytes
		Neutrophils
	More mucus
		Increased goblet cells
		Mucus gland hyperplasia
	Air Trapping
		Narrowing of airways
		Decreased small airways
		Acinar changes

History
	Smoking →Pack years (10-15)
	Environmental/occupational
	Symptoms/Duration 
		Exertional dyspnea
		Cough
		Sputum
		Wheezing

Clinical Manifestations ('')
	Increased resonance
	Decreased breath sounds
	Wheezes/crackles
	Increased AP diameter

Diagnosis ('')
	CBC
		Anemia
	BMP
		Bicarb (CO2)
	Imaging
		Not strictly necessary 
		CXR
		CT chest

Pulmonary Function Testing ('Pulmonary Function Testing')

Body Index 
	Assess risk of death from COPD
	BMI
	FEV1
	Dyspnea (Modified Medical Research Council Dyspnea Scale)
	Exercise Capacity

GOLD: Severity of Airflow Limitation ('GOLD Severity of Airflow Limitation')

Modified Medical Research Council Dyspnea Scale
	Grade → Description of Breathlessness
		0 → I only get breathless with strenuous exercise
		1 → I get short of breath when hurrying on ground level or walking up slight hill
		2 → On level ground, I walk slower than most people of the same age because of breathlessness, or have to stop for breath when walking at my own pace
		3 → I stop for breath after walking about 100 yards or after a few minutes on level ground
		4 → I am too breathless to leave the house or I am breathless when dressing

Treatment
	Smoking cessation
	Vaccination
		Influenza
		Pneumococcal
	Review inhaler technique → spacer
	Exercise/Pulmonary rehab
	Oxygen therapy for chronic hypoxemia

Treatment
	Short-acting beta agonist (SABA) → albuterol
	Short-acting muscarinic antagonist (SAMA) → ipratropium
	Long-acting beta agonist (LABA) → salmeterol
	Long-acting muscarinic antagonist (LAMA) → tiotropium

GOLD ABCD ('GOLD ABCD')

Objectives
	Discuss the pathophysiology of emphysema, chronic bronchitis, an asthma and explain their relationship in COPD
	Recognize the risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment and prevention of COPD

Pneumonia (Q&A_Pneumonia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Definition ('Pneumonia')
	Infection of lung parenchyma 
		Community Acquired (CAP)
		Hospital-Acquired (HAP)
	Normal alveolar microbiome 
		Anaerobes
		Microaerophillic strep
	Microaspiration
		Microbes in upper airways → lower airways

Pathogenesis
		Defect in host defense
		Exposure to virulent organism
		Overwhelming inoculum
		Local inflammation
			Alveolar macrophages → cytokines → WBC + fluid
		Systemic inflammation
			Increased production WBC
			Fever
			Increased cardiac output

Risk Factors
	Age ≥ 65
	Chronic lung disease
		COPD
		CF
		HF
		Lung CA
	Macroaspiration
		Decreased consciousness
		Dysphagia
	Immunocompromised
		DM
		HIV
		Chemo
	Lifestyle
		Smoking
		Etoh
		Toxic inhalations
	Viral respiratory tract infection

Typical vs Atypical
	Typical
		Strep pneumoniae
		H. influenzae
		Staph aureus
		Group A Strep
		Moraxella catarrhalis
		Pseudomonas aeroginosa
		Klebsiella pneumoniae
	Atypical
		Legionella
		Mycoplasma pneumoniae
		Chlamydia pneumoniae
		Chlamydia psittaci

Outpatient vs Inpatient
	Outpatient
		Strep pneumoniae
		Mycoplasma pneumoniae
		Respiratory Viruses (Flu, RSV)
		H. influenzae
		Legionella
	Inpatient
		Strep pneumoniae
		Respiratory Viruses (Flu, RSV)
		H. influenzae
		Legionella
		Staph aureus
		Pseudomonas aeroginosa
		Klebsiella pneumoniae

Clinical Manifestations
	Fever
	Cough 
	Shortness of breath
	Confusion
	Fatigue
	Headache
	Myalgias
	Nausea/Vomiting
	Tachycardia

Diagnosis
	Chest X-ray 
		PA
		Lateral
	Complete Blood Count 
	Basic Metabolic Panel 
	CT
	Airway sample
		Sputum culture
		Bronchoalveolar lavage
	Antigen tests
		Legionella
		Strep
	RVP (flu, RSV, covid-19)

Assessing Severity
	Pneumonia Severity Index
		Pneumonia Patient Outcomes Research Team (PORT) → low risk mortality CAP
		5 Classes risk of death from all causes within 30 days
		Requires calculator to determine risk
		Validated in literature
	CURB-65
		Confusion
		Urea (BUN)
		Respiratory Rate
		Blood pressure (SBP <90, DBP≤60)
		Age ≥65
		Much easier to remember

Treatment
	Outpatient
		Duration 5 days
		Healthy/No antibiotics: 
			Amoxicillin + azithromycin
		≥ 65/comorbidities/antibiotics
			Amoxicillin-clavulanate + azithromycin
		Follow-up 1-2 days
	Inpatient
		Duration 5-7 days
		No Drug resistance
			Ceftriaxone + azithromycin
		HAP/VAP
			Zosyn + vancomycin 
			Consider prior pathogen sensitivity
		Narrow to isolated pathogen

Prevention 
	Pneumonia
		Pneumococcal polysaccharide vaccination →PPSV23 (Pneumovax)
		Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine →PCV13 (Prevnar13)
			Adults 19-64 chronic conditions and all adults ≥65 →PPSV23
			Immunocompromised or risk meningitis→both
				Asplenic/Transplant/CKD/Malignancy/Medications
	Influenza vaccine annually for people ≥ 6 months old

Risk Group → Underlying Condition or Indication
	Immunocompetent Chronic Heart Disease (not HTN)
		Chronic lung disease (COPD, asthma, 
		emphysema) Diabetes mellitus
		Cochlear implant
		Alcoholism
		Chronic liver disease
		Cigarette smokers
		Cerebrospinal fluid leaks
	Functional or Anatomic Asplenia
		Sickle cell disease
		congenital or acquired asplenia
	Immunocompromised HIV infection
		Chronic renal failure
		Nephrotic syndrome
		Leukemia
		Lymphoma
		Hodgkin disease
		Generalized malignancy
		Diseases requiring treatment with immunosuppressive drugs (long term steroids, radiation)

Objectives
	Discuss the pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, treatment and prevention of pneumonia
	Differentiate community from hospital acquired pneumonia
	Compare and contrast outpatient from inpatient management

Pulmonary Embolism (Q&A_PulmonaryEmbolism)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Definition
	Obstruction in pulmonary artery or one of its branching vessels
		Acute
		Subacute
		Chronic
	Material comes from somewhere else in body
		Thrombus (Deep vein thrombosis)
		Fat
		Tumor
		Air
		Amniotic Fluid

Definition
	Hemodynamic Effects
		Stable
		Unstable
	Anatomic Location
		Saddle
		Lobar
		Segmental
		Subsegmental

Pulmonary vessels ('Pulmonary vessels')
	Right main artery
	Interlobar artery
	Lobar artery
	Lobar vein
	Segmental artery
	Segmental vein
	Subsegmental artery
	Subsegmental vein
	Intralobular artery
	Interlobular vein

Risk Factors ('Risk Factors')
	Pro-thrombotic state
		Malignancy
		Pregnancy
		Infection
		Surgery
		Inherited thrombotic disorders
		Drugs
	Immobility
		Hospitalized patients
		Neuromuscular weakness 
			Stroke/Spinal cord injury

Complications ('')
	Pulmonary infarction
	Impaired gas exchange
	Right heart strain
		Increased afterload 
	Obstructive shock
	Death

Symptoms
	Asymptomatic
	Dyspnea
	Chest pain (pleuritic)
	Cough
	Hemoptysis

Physical exam
	Tachycardia
		Sinus
		Arrythmia: Atrial fibrillation → PEA
	Tachypnea
	Hypoxia
	Hypotension
	Fever

Diagnosis
	Wells Score
	D Dimer ≥ 500ng/mL
	CT Angiography
	Point of care ultrasound
	Ventilation perfusion scan

Wells Score PE ('Wells Score PE')

Low Probability
	No further testing if all the following are true
		Age < 50
		HR < 100
		Pulse-ox ≥95%
		No hemoptysis
		No estrogen use
		No hx DVT/PE
		No signs DVT
		No surgery/trauma req hospital last 4 weeks

Moderate Probability
	D dimer ≥ 500 → CT Angiography → Diagnose or exclude PE

Moderate Probability
	D dimer ≥ 500 → CT Angiography → Diagnose or exclude PE

High Probability
	CT Angiography → Diagnose or exclude PE

Treatment
	Consider risk of bleeding
	Anticoagulation
		Heparin IV drip or enoxaparin subq
		Direct Oral Anticoagulants
	Thrombolysis
		Catheter directed tpa
	Embolectomy

Objectives
	-Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment, and complications of pulmonary 
	embolism
	-Organize the risk factors of pulmonary embolism using Virchow’s triad
	-Apply the Wells Score for PE to guide diagnostic workup

Deep Vein Thrombosis (Q&A_DVT)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Definition
	Obstruction in pulmonary artery or one of its branching vessels
		Acute
		Subacute
		Chronic
	Material comes from somewhere else in body
		Thrombus (Deep vein thrombosis)
		Fat
		Tumor
		Air
		Amniotic Fluid

Deep veins ('Deep veins')
	External iliac
	Common femoral
	Femoral 
	Popliteal

Risk Factors REPE¿?
	Pro-thrombotic state
		Malignancy
		Pregnancy
		Infection
		Surgery
		Inherited thrombotic disorders
		Drugs
	Immobility
		Hospitalized patients
		Neuromuscular weakness 
			Stroke/Spinal cord injury

Differential Diagnosis
	Muscle strain or tear → 40%
	Leg edema in paralyzed limb → 9%
	Lymphangitis/Lymph obstruction → 7%
	Venous insufficiency → 7%
	Popliteal (Baker’s cyst) → 5%
	Cellulitis → 3%
	Unknown → 26%

Clinical Manifestations
	Edema
	Pain
	Erythema
	Warmth
	Larger calf diameter
	Homan’s sign

Diagnosis ('')
	Wells Score DVT
	D Dimer ≥ 500 ng/mL
		µg/mL D-Dimer, QN n>200
	Ultrasound
		Proximal → No calf veins
		Whole leg

Wells Score DVT ('Wells Score DVT')
	
Low Probability
	D Dimer <500 → No further testing
	D dimer ≥500 → Ultrasound
Moderate Probability
	D Dimer <500 → No further testing
	D dimer ≥500 → Ultrasound
High Probability
	Whole leg ultrasound → Diagnose or exclude DVT (Except in the case where iliac thrombus is suspected → CT venography)

Negative for DVT ('')
	Vein Totally Compressible
Positive for DVT ('')
	Vein Incompressible, Echogenic focus

Treatment
	Consider risk of bleeding
	Consider location of clot: proximal vs distal (calf vein)
		Up to 90% pulmonary embolism comes from proximal DVT
		Mortality higher 8% versus 4%
	Provoked vs unprovoked
	First vs Recurrent
	Phlegmasia cerulea dolens (massive iliofemoral DVT)

Treatment: First DVT
	Proximal
		Anticoagulation at least 3 months
			Heparin IV drip or enoxaparin subq
			Direct Oral Anticoagulants
		Inferior vena cava filter
			Contraindications to anticoagulation
	Distal
		Symptomatic → anticoagulation
		Asymptomatic → anticoagulation or monitor with serial ultrasound to ensure resolution

Objectives
	Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of deep vein thrombosis
	Compare and contrast the presentation and management of proximal and distal DVT
	Apply the Wells Score for DVT to guide diagnostic workup

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Osteoarthritis (Q&A_Osteoarthritis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Osteoarthritis
	Definition
		Oste/o = bone
		Arthr/o = joint
		-itis = inflammation
	Involves entire joint
		Articular cartilage
		Bone
		Synovium
		Soft tissue

Osteoarthritis
	Affects > 32.5 million adults in US
	Second most costly health condition treated in US hospitals in 2013
		$16.5 billion
	Primary – No identifiable cause
	Secondary – Predisposing factor

Risk Factors
	Aging ≥50
	Joint injury
	Obesity
	Genetics
	Joint shape
	AFAB

Articular Cartilage ('Articular Cartilage')
	Chondrocytes
		Cells in cartilage
		Normal breakdown and synthesis of matrix
	Extracellular matrix
		Water
		Collagen 
		Proteoglycans
		Calcium
		Polyethylene “wear” particles 
			Response to microtrauma
			Ingested by macrophages

N/A 1
	Too many wear particles overwhelm synovial macrophages ↓
		Stimulate chondrocytes to release proteases to break down collagen and proteoglycan ↓
			Macrophages ingest molecules ↓
				Release proinflammatory cytokines TNFα, IL—1, IL-6 ↓
					Cytokines bind to chondrocyte receptors, inhibiting collagen production. Proteases cont destroy matrix ↓
						Weakening of collagen network and chondrocyte death → increasing cartilage breakdown ↓
							Osteoarthritis

Articular cartilage
	Fibrillation 
	Matrix degradation
	Chondrocyte death

Subchondral bone
	Progressive increase in thickness and sclerosis
	Osteophyte formation
	Progressive loss trabecular bone
	Bone cysts

Synovial Membrane
	Synovitis = inflammation of joint space
		Wear particles
		Synovial lining thickening
		Angiogenesis (VEGF)
		Inflammatory cells
		Fibrin deposition (fibrillation)

Soft Tissue
	Menisci
	Ligaments
	Muscles
	Nerves

Clinical Manifestations
	Joint pain/tenderness
		Worse with use or at night
		Improves with rest
	Stiffness
	Decreased ROM 
		Passive and active
	1 or 2 joints
		Hand
		Knee
		Hip

Clinical Manifestations ('')
	Edema without warmth
	Deformity
		Bouchard’s nodes
		Heberden’s nodes
	Muscle wasting/weakness
	Joint line tenderness
	Crepitus

Diagnosis
	Can diagnose without imaging if all three true:
		Chronic usage-related joint pain in 1 or 2 joints
		Age ≥45 y/o
		Morning stiffness ≤ 30 minutes
	Can have radiographic abnormalities without symptoms and symptoms without radiographic abnormalities

Diagnosis ('')
	Plain film (X-ray)
		Osteophytes
		Joint space narrowing
		Subchondral sclerosis
	MRI
		More sensitive, but not necessary
	Synovial fluid aspiration
		Noninflammatory or mildly inflammatory
		Mononuclear cells
		Calcium pyrophosphate crystals

WOMAC Osteoarthritis Index
	Patient-centered self-reported health questionnaire
	Scale 0-10 for each of three categories
		Pain (5 questions)
		Stiffness (2 questions)
		Physical function (17 questions)
		Total scores 0-240
		Minimal clinically significant difference ~ 12-16% improvement from baseline

Differential Diagnosis
	Rheumatoid arthritis
	Psoriatic arthritis
	Crystalline arthritis (Gout)
	Septic arthritis
	Other soft-tissue issues

Treatment
	Goals:
		Decrease pain
		Improve function 
		Slow down joint damage
	Individualized plan
		ADLs, QOL
		Mood
		Sleep
		Fall risk
		Modifiable risk factors

Non-pharmacologic
	Education/Self-management
	Exercise/Physical Therapy
		NEJM 2020: PT vs Glucocorticoid injection for OA of Knee
	Weight loss
	Walking aides/braces

Pharmacologic
	Topical nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs
	Oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs
	Duloxetine
	Intra-articular glucocorticoid injections
	Acetaminophen

Surgery
	All other therapies have failed
	Total joint replacement (arthroplasty)
		Some patients will have long term pain
	Arthroscopic surgery no benefit except maybe in hip

Objectives
	Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of osteoarthritis
	Recognize the critical role physical therapy has in the management of OA

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Rheumatoid Arthritis (Q&A_RheumatoidArthritis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Rheumatoid Arthritis
	Autoimmune symmetric inflammatory polyarthritis
	Leads to deformity and loss of physical function 
	Can have systemic effects

Joints Affected
	Hands
	Wrists
	Elbows
	Feet
	Ankles
	Knees

Risk Factors
	Genetics
		Human leukocyte antigen (HLA) major histocompatibility complex (MHC)
	Environmental
		Smoking → repeated activation of innate immunity
		Gut microbiome?

N/A 1
	Genetic predisposition + other factors ↓
		Pre-RA Anti-citrullinated protein  antibodies (ACPAs) Rheumatoid Factor (RF) ↓
			“Second hit” ↓
				Clinically apparent synovitis ↓
					Angiogenesis Lymphocyte activation (T cells)Cytokine release ↓
						Synovium transforms into invasive & destructive  tissue(Local malignancy) ↓
							Surrounding cartilage and bone chewed upFibroblast-like synoviocyts (FLS) migrate from joint to joint ↓
								Rheumatoid Arthritis

Clinical Manifestations
	Gradual onset
	Pain
	Stiffness (morning, >1 hour)
	Swelling of multiple joints (symmetric)
	Small joints of hands, wrist, forefoot
		Metacarpophalangeal (MCP)
		Proximal interphalangeal (PIP)
		Metatarsophalangeal (MTP)

	Reduced grip strength
	Swan neck deformity
	Boutonniere deformity

Clinical Manifestations
	40% patients have extraarticular manifestations of RA
		Constitutional: Fatigue
		Rheumatoid nodules
		Cardiac involvement
		Pulmonary involvement
		Vasculitis
		Depression
		Neurologic: carpal tunnel syndrome
		Sjögren’s syndrome xerostomia
		Hematologic: Anemia

2010 ACR/EULAR Criteria ('2010 ACR-EULAR Criteria')
	Presence of synovitis in at least one joint
	Absence alternative diagnosis
	At least 6/10 score

Diagnosis
	Rheumatoid factor (RF)
	Anti-citrullinated peptide antibodies (ACPA)
		Both tests negative in up to 50% patients initially
	ESR, CRP
	CBC – anemia of chronic inflammation
	CMP – liver & kidney involvement
	ANA – SLE
	Serologic studies 
		Parvovirus B19, Hep B/C, Lyme, Borrelia, Chikungunya

Diagnosis
	Plain film (X-ray)
		Joint space narrowing
		Marginal erosions
	MRI and Ultrasound
		More sensitive
	Synovial fluid aspiration
		Inflammatory
		>2000 WBC/mm

Differential Diagnosis
	Feature
		Rheumatoid
			Arthritis Osteoarthritis
	--------------------------------
	Main joints affected
		Metacarpophalangeal Proximal interphalangeal
			Distal interphalangeal Carpometacarpal
	Heberden’s nodes
		Absent
			Often present
	Joint symptoms
		Soft, warm, tender
			Hard and bony
	Labs
		+ RF, + anti-CCP Ab Elevated ESR and CRP
			Negative and normal

Differential Diagnosis
	Viral polyarthritis
	Systemic Rheumatic Disease
		Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)
		Sjögren’s syndrome
		Dermatomyositis
	Carpal tunnel syndrome
	Reactive arthritis
	Arthritis of inflammatory bowel disease
	Psoriatic arthritis
	Polymyalgia rheumatica
	Gout
	Lyme arthritis
	Fibromyalgia
	Sarcoid arthropathy

Treatment
	Early recognition and diagnosis
	Care by rheumatologist
	Early use of disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs for all patients
	Tight control → goal of remission
	Antiinflammatory agents only as adjuncts
	EDUCATION

Non-pharmacologic
	Physical Therapy 
		Decreases disease activity and pain
		ROM exercises
		Muscle strength
		Aerobic exercise
		Splinting
	Occupational therapy
		Joint protection education
		Provision and instruction of assistive devices
	Nutritional counseling
		Mediterranean diet

Pharmacologic
	DMARDs
		Nonbiologic
			Methotrexate
			Hydroxychloroquine
			Sulfasalazine
		Biologic
			Tocilizumab – IL-6
			Infliximab TNFα
			Etanercept TNFα
	Anti-inflammatory therapy
		NSAID 
			Ibuprofen
		Glucocorticoid
			Medrol dose pack

Functional Capacity ('Functional Capacity')

Prognosis
	Progressive disease
		Slow vs rapid
		15-20% intermittent disease with periods ofexacerbation
	Long term outlook variable
	Comorbidities
		Infection
		Renal disease
		Lymphoproliferative disorders
		Cardiovascular disease
	Mortality

Objectives
	Discuss the etiology, pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of rheumatoid arthritis
	Compare and contrast RA from OA
	Recognize the broad differential diagnosis of RA
	Explain the long-term prognosis of RA


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Gout (Q&A_Gout)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Gout
	Accumulation of monosodium urate crystals
	Acute arthritis
	Chronic arthritis
	Tophi

Pathogenesis
	Hyperuricemia (>6.8mg/dL)
	Poor excretion
		Decreased efficiency of renal and gut excretion
		Genetics
	Overproduction
		Excess dietary intake purines/precursors
			Meat
			Seafood
			Alcohol
		Genetics

Pathogenesis
		Hyperuricemia + Monosodium urate (MSU) crystals ↓
			MSU Crystals deposit in joints ↓
				Activation of innate inflammatory response in joint ↓
					Acute gout flair ↓

Risk Factors
	Non-modifiable
		AMAB
		Advanced age
		Pacific Islander ethnicity
		Genetics 
			>200 genetic loci
	Modifiable
		Obesity/Metabolic Syndrome
		Alcohol/Pop 
		HTN
		CKD
		Diuretics

Typical Disease Course
	Asymptomatic hyperuricemia and  MSU crystals in synovium ↓
		Acute gout flare ↓
			Interictal gout +/recurrent gout flares ↓
				Tophaceous gout

Clinical Manifestations Acute Flare
	Rapid onset of the following in a joint:
		Pain
		Edema
		Erythema
		Calor
	Monoarthritis involving a joint of the lower extremity ~80% cases
		Podagra – first metatarsophalangeal 
		Other tarsal joints
		Knee
	Resolves within 7-10 days

Clinical Manifestations Tophaceous Gout
	Subcutaneous nodules without acute inflammation
	Granuloma forms around a collection of MSU crystals
	Presence of tophi associated with nearby joint damage
	Skin and ear

Diagnosis
	Synovial Fluid
		Inflammatory: WBC 10,000-100,000/mm, neutrophil predominance
		Monosodium urate crystals
			Negatively birefringent 
			Needle-shaped crystals
	Hyperuricemia
		May not always be elevated in patient treated with gout having an acute flare
		Best time to measure this is ≥2 weeks after a flare

Diagnosis
	Plain radiography
		Subcortical cysts
		“Overhanging edge” erosive appearance
	MRI
		Tophi or bone erosions
	Ultrasound
		Double contour sign 
		Tophaceous-appearing deposits
	Dual-energy computed tomography
		Specifically identifies urate deposits
		Potential artifacts

Clinical Diagnostic Rule ('Clinical Diagnostic Rule')

Differential Diagnosis
	Septic arthritis
	Trauma
	Calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition disease (pseudogout)
	Cellulitis
	Basic calcium phosphate crystal disease
	Rheumatoid arthritis

Treatment All Patients
	Education
		Symptoms, provoking factors
	Weight reduction
	Exercise
	Dietary recommendations
		Avoid liver, limit other purine rich meats and seafood
		Avoid high fructose corn syrup
		Avoid alcohol overuse
		Encourage low-fat dairy and veg

Treatment Acute Gout Flare
	Start treatment as soon as possible
	Stop treatment 2-3 days after resolution of symptoms
	Oral glucocorticoids (Prednisone)
	NSAIDS (naproxen or indomethacin)
		Avoid in CKD, PUD, CVD
	Colchicine
	Joint injection

Treatment: Urate Lowering Therapy
	Patients to treat:
		Tophus/tophi
		Frequent flares ≥2/year
		CKD stage 2 or worse
		Past urolithiasis
	Goal decrease serum urate levels ≤6mg/dL, sometimes ≤5mg/dL
	Add acute flare prophylaxis
		Colchicine or NSAID (naproxen)
		At least 6 months
	Xanthine oxidase inhibitor 
		Inhibits purine metabolism
		Allopurinol
	Uricosuric Therapy
		Promotes renal excretion of uric acid
		Probenecid
		Losartan
		Fenofibrate

Objectives
	-Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of gout.
	-Compare and contrast the clinical presentations and treatment for an acute gout flare, chronic gouty arthritis, and tophaceous gout.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Calcium Pyrophosphate Crystal Deposition Disease​ (Pseudogout) (Q&A_Pseudogout)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Definition
	EULAR Terminology
	Calcium pyrophosphate crystal deposition disease
		Accumulation of calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate (CPP) crystals
	Acute CPP crystal arthritis
		Pseudogout

Pathogenesis
	Chondrocytes generate pyrophosphate
	Pyrophosphate prevents mineralization of cartilage matrix with basic calcium phosphate 

Pathogenesis
	Chondrocytes produce too much pyrophosphate ↓
		Calcium pyrophosphate supersaturation and crystal formation ↓
			Activation of innate inflammatory response in joint ↓
				CPP crystal-induced synovitis

Risk Factors
	Most cases idiopathic
	Advanced age
	Associated diseases
		Hemochromatosis
		Hyperparathyroidism
		Gout (%5)
		Prior trauma
		Familial chondrocalcinosis

Types of Clinical Manifestations
	Asymptomatic CPPD Disease
	Acute CPP Crystal Arthritis
	Chronic CPP Crystal Arthritis
	Osteoarthritis with CPPD

Clinical ManifestationsAcute CPP Crystal Arthritis
	Rapid onset of the following in a joint:
		Pain
		Edema
		Erythema
		Calor
	Involves 1-2 joints
		Knee >50% cases
		Wrist, elbows, shoulders
		Feet, ankles
	Self limiting lasting days to weeks

Diagnosis
	Synovial Fluid
		Inflammatory: WBC 15,000-30,000/mm, neutrophil predominance
		Calcium pyrophosphate crystals
			Weakly positively birefringent 
			Polymorphic shape: rods, cuboid, rhomboid
			Very small

Diagnosis
	Plain radiography
		Chondrocalcinosis
		Joint degenerative changes
		AP view both knees
		AO view pubic symphysis
		PA view both wrists
	Ultrasound
		Potential but not validated for widespread clinical use

Diagnostic Criteria
	Probability → Criteria
	-----------------------
		Definite 
			Presence both positively birefringent crystals AND cartilage calcification of any typically affected joint
			Demonstration of CPP crystals in synovial fluid by definitive means (spectroscopy)
		Probable
			Positively birefringent crystals OR 
			Cartilage calcification of any typically affected joint
		Possible
			Acute arthritis of large joint, especially knee OR
			Chronic arthritis resembling OA but
				Involved joints are not typical for OA
				Accompanied by acute, self limited attacks

Differential Diagnosis
	Septic arthritis
	Trauma
	Gout
	Osteoarthritis
	Rheumatoid arthritis

Treatment 
	One-two joints
		Joint fluid aspiration
		Intraarticular glucocorticoid injection
	Multiple joints:
		NSAIDs
		Colchicine
		Oral glucocorticoids
	Colchicine prophylaxis NSAIDs

Objectives
	-Discuss the pathophysiology, risk factors, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of acute calcium pyrophosphate crystal arthritis
	-Recognize the symptoms of degenerative chronic arthropathy caused by CPPD
	-Compare and contrast gout and acute ccp crystal arthritis

Course Outcomes 
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Upon completion of the course the student will be able to:
	-Understand and appropriately use basic medical terminology to allow effective interdisciplinary communication.
	-Identify the basic anatomical components of the related organ system(s), and be able to describe normal physiology and pathophysiologic mechanisms.
	-For each of the musculoskeletal, neurologic, endocrine, renal, gastrointestinal, and hematologic conditions discussed in class:
		-Recognize “red flags” that require immediate medical referral.
		-Recognize race, gender, and age differences in rates of disease or pathology.
		-List known modifiable and non-modifiable risk factors.
		-Identify signs and symptoms that aid in diagnosis of the pathology, or disorder of each system.
		-Review patients' medical histories and medications lists with insight into the potential impact of the condition and related medical interventions on the physical therapy examination and plan of care.
	-Apply current best evidence to describe how a specific condition or disease may cause impairment and functional limitation
	-Develop an understanding of the multidisciplinary approach to caring for the condition
	-Practice communicating efficiently to patients by following the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach.

Osteoporosis (¦Iaia Tere) (Q&A_Osteoporosis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Medical Terminology
	Osteoclast
	Definition: Cells that resorb bone. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; -clast – breaking down. 
	Osteoblast
	Definition: Cells that generate new bone. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; -blast – embryonic. 
	Osteocyte
	Definition: Main cells of bone that form from osteoblasts. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; cyte – cell. 
	Osteonecrosis
	Definition: Death of bone due to lack of blood supply. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; necrosis – death. 
	Osteoporosis
	Definition: Bone disease characterized by compromised bone strength. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; por from “poros” (ancient Greek) pore or passageway; -sis – abnormal condition. 
	Osteopenia
	Definition: Decreased bone density. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; -penia – deficiency. 
	Osteomyelitis
	Definition: Infection of bone. 
	Etymology: Oste/o – bone; myel/o – bone marrow; -itis – inflammation. 
	Arthroplasty
	Definition: surgical repair of joint. 
	Etymology “arthr/o – joint; -plasty – surgical repair.

.Got Milk?
	Why should kids drink milk (or a calcium-rich milk alternative)? Why aren’t their campaigns for adults? Because bone mass is generated in childhood. Essentially, it’s already too late for us. We build up bone mass in childhood to try and prevent osteoporosis in late adulthood.   

	Watch the video to view an introduction to osteoporosis. 

.bjectives
	Discuss the cause, pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of osteoporosis

N/A
	Most common bone disease
	Affects older patients
		Women 30%
		Men 12%
	Compromised bone strength
		Decreased bone density
		Decreased bone quality
	Fracture (low trauma)

Pathogenesis
	Women
		Estrogen falls after menopause
		Loss of estrogen leads to increased osteoclast activity
		Progressive loss of bone mass → breaking connections of trabecula
	Men
		Age leads to decreased bone formation
		Thinning of trabeculae without breakage
	Both
		Decrease calcium 
		Vit D deficiency
		Inactivity

Clinical Manifestations
	Asymptomatic
	Fracture
		Vertebral most common
			Height loss
			Kyphosis
		Hip fractures
		Colles fracture

Diagnosis
	Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry (DXA) 
	T score ≤ -2.5 SD
	Screening
		Women ≥ 65
		Men ≥ 70
		Younger with risk factors
	Fragility fracture
		Spine
		Hip
		Wrist
		Humerus
		Rib 
		Pelvis
	Plain film (X-ray), CT, MRI

Treatment
	Non-pharmacologic
		Calcium/Vitamin D
			Calcium rich diet (1200mg)
			Calcium supplements (500-1000mg)
			Vitamin D supplements (800IUs)
		Weight bearing exercise
		Smoking cessation
		Fall risk reduction
	Pharmacologic
		Fragility fracture
		T score ≤ -2.5
		Osteopenia with Fracture Risk Assessment (FRAX) score ≥ 3
			Corresponds t10-year risk of major osteoporotic fracture of ≥ 20%
		Oral bisphosphonate 
			Alendronate 5 years
		Follow-up DXA 2 years

.Knowledge Checker
	Weight-bearing exercise is a non-pharmacologic treatment for Osteoporosis.
	True 
	False 

	True

Paget Disease of Bone (PBD) AKA Osteitis Deformans (Q&A_Paget) (¦Piaget)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Deaf Talent
	Ludwig van Beethoven (1770-1827) had been deaf for a decade when he wrote his Symphony No. 9. An autopsy demonstrated that Beethoven’s deafness had been caused by compression of the eighth cranial nerve due to Paget’s disease of the bone. 

	Watch the video to view an introduction to Paget disease of bone.

Objectives
	Discuss the cause, pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, diagnosis, and treatment of paget disease of bone

N/A
	Second most common bone disease
	Affects elderly
	Focal disorder bone metabolism
	Combination genetic and environmental etiologies
		Role of measles virus
	Often asymptomatic

Pathogenesis
	(See image in pdf)
	Γ(See folder 'Extra Videos from Yt\Paget Disease')

Clinical Manifestations
	Asymptomatic
	Bone pain (40-45%)
		Deep
		Aching
		Consistent throughout day
	Arthritis (40-50%)
	Bone Deformity (12-36%)
	Fracture (4-16%)
	Impaired functional status
	Osteosarcoma

Where? Areas affected by Paget Disease of Bone (See image in pdf)
	Skull 
		Headache
		Cochlea → Hearing loss
	Spine 
		Radiculopathy
		Back pain
	Pelvis
		Arthritis
	Long bones 
		Bowing
		Arthritis
		Fracture

Diagnosis (See image in pdf)
	Plain films
		Mixed lytic/sclerotic lesions
		Cortical thickening
		Prominent trabeculations Γ('prominent trabeculations Paget Disease.png') 
		Protrusiacetabuli Γ('Protrusiacetabuli')
	Elevated alkaline phosphatase (alk phos)
		Decreased osteoblast activity
	Bone scintigraphy
		Increased uptake pagetic bone lesions
		Increased bone remodeling and blood flow
		More senstiive in early disease
		Establishes extent and distribution of lesions

Treatment
	Who to treat
		Symptomatic patients
		Asymptomatic patients
			Active disease + Risk future complications due to location of lesion 
			Planned surgery
	Goal of treatment
		Improve pain
		Decrease rate of remodeling
	Anti-resorptive agent
		Bisphosphonates
			Zoledronic acid (IV)
			Alendronate (PO)
		Calcitonin (subq)
	Monitor alk phos levels tassess disease response
		Expect decrease

.Knowledge Checker

		Improve Pain
		Promote a healthier lifestyle
		Decrease rate of remodeling
		Foster neurologic growth

		Decrease rate of remodeling

Osteo-necrosis = BONE-DEATH

N/A 1
	Avascular necrosis
		Death of bone due to decreased perfusion
	Risk Factors
		Direct damage to bone
			Femoral neck fracture
			Radiation
		Glucocorticoids
		Bisphosphonates
		Alcohol + Cigarette smoking
		Renal Transplant
		Systemic lupus erythematosus

Pediatric Population
	Legg-Calvé-Perthes
	Slipped capital femoral epiphysis

		Groin
		Buttock
		Thigh

		Gold standard
		Sensitivity 100%
		Hypointense in T1

		Increased density
		Femoral head collapse

Treatment

	Joint-preserving procedures
		Core decompression
		Early disease (pre-collapse)
	Total hip arthroplasty
		Removes necrotic bone and replaces with prosthesis

Osteomyelitis (Q&A_Osteomyelitis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Roadblocks
	Infection of the bone, or osteomyelitis, requires weeks of antibiotics to treat. Why? Because the blood supply to bones is not robust. The body’s ability to mount a meaningful immune response is stifled by bad roadways. 

	Watch the video to view an introduction to osteomyelitis.

Osteo-myel-itis = BONE BONE MARROW INFLAMMATION

N/A 1
	Infection of bone
	Type of spread
		Hematogenous
			Bacteremia
		Nonhematogenous
			Contiguous spread from soft tissues
			Direct inoculation (trauma or surgery)
			Staphylococci and aerobic gram-negative bacilli
	Duration
		Acute days to weeks
		Chronic months to years

Clinical Manifestations
	Acute
		Dull pain 
		Bone tenderness
		Overlying erythema and/or edema
		Fever
	Chronic
		Pain
		Draining sinus tract
		Non-healing ulcers
		Diabetic patients

Diagnosis
	Labs
		CBC with diff and platelets
		ESR, CRP
		Blood cultures
	Imaging
		CT or MRI < 2 weeks symptoms
		Plain films > 2 weeks symptoms
	Bone biopsy

Treatment
	Surgical
		Debridement Γ(excisional)
			+ or hardware removal
		Soft tissue envelope
			Closure
			Flap
	Pharmacologic
		Antibiotics (IV) for ~ 6 weeks (residual bone)
		Additional Γoral antibiotics (retained hardware)

.Knowledge Checker
	Acute
		Dull pain
		Bone tenderness
		Overlying erythema and/or edema
		Fever
	Chronic
		Non-healing ulcers
		Draining sinus tract
		Diabetic patients
		Pain

1.3. Connect: Patient-Centered Overview Group A
	(See also: 
		'Ass 01 1.3. Connect::1.3. Connect Patient-Centered Overview.txt'
		'7.5. Do: Patient-Centered Simulation Final Reminder')
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

A study conducted by The Journal of Family Practice (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11032203/) ('The impact of patient-centered care on outcomes PubMed.pdf') found that practices focused around a patient-centered approach resulted in
	better patient recovery from discomfort or concern,
	increased emotional health for the future, and
	fewer diagnostic tests and referrals.  

Aside from these benefits, the New England Journal of Medicine found that improved patient satisfaction scores, morale, productivity, resource allocations, and long-term reduced expenses were also results of having a patient-centered approach. 

This assignment will give you the opportunity to learn more about patient-centered care and the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach.  

You will also learn more of the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Simulation Final you will need to complete at the end of this course as part of your final grade

	(Γ)Take aways
		('Shared Decision Making.pdf')
			Eight characteristics of care as the most important indicators of quality and safety, from the perspective of patients:
				1.Respect for the patient’s values, preferences, and expressed needs;
				2.coordinated and integrated care;
				3.clear, high-quality information and education for the patient and family;
				4.physical comfort, including pain management;
				5.emotional support and alleviation of fear and anxiety;
				6.involvement of family members and friends, as appropriate;
				7.continuity, including through care-site transitions; and
				8.access to care.
				
				Successfully addressing these dimensions requires enlisting patients and families as allies in designing, implementing, and evaluating care systems.

			The most important attribute of patient-centered care is
				the active engagement of patients when fateful health care decisions must be made
					-when an individual patient arrives at a crossroads of medical options, where the diverging paths have different and important consequences with lasting implications.
						Examples include
							decisions about major surgery
							medications that must be taken for the rest of one’s life
							screening and diagnostic tests that can trigger cascades of serious and stressful interventions.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
1.3.1. Connect: PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

In this course, you will examine and practice the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach which comprises four interpersonal skills that will help you become a more patient-centered PT. The four skills are: 
	Emotional Intelligence
	Empathy
	Active Listening
	Effective Communication

Since most of these interpersonal skills, or human skills as they will be referred to in this course, overlap as you practice and strengthen one skill others will be strengthened too. 

For each of these skills, you will also be given a challenge that you will have the option of doing during the week you complete the module. The challenge requires you to put into practice what you have learned to your day-to-day job/life. Although it is not graded, I highly recommend you doing it to prepare for the final simulation and to develop the skills. 

View the short process below for a brief description of the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach. As you scroll down, there is also an optional knowledge checker.

	Introduction [HSD2_4Skills]
		Health care professionals have discovered that when patients play an active role in their own care, they’re empowered, and results improve. The PT2045 Patient Approach will help reduce unnecessary procedures, honor patient preferences, improve patient health, and build trust between you and the patient.

			Emotional Intelligence
				The core of emotional intelligence is self-awareness if you don’t understand your own motivations and behaviors, it is nearly impossible to develop an understanding for others.
			Empathy
				The ability to sense other people’s emotions, coupled with the ability to imagine what someone else might be thinking or feeling.
			Active Listening
				-The ability to be deeply engaged and attentive to what the other person is saying. This requires more listening and less talking, with the intention to increase understanding.
			Effective Communication
				Leaves all parties feeling involved and satisfied with no room for misunderstanding.
			Summary
				-Your patients will have health concerns that may cause anxiety and stress. However, as you strive to practice the skills learned in the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach, it will create strong partnerships between both of you which can relieve some of that anxiety and improve overall care.

		Knowledge Checker
			PT2045 Patient-Centered Skills
				empathy
				emotional intelligence
				communication
				active listening
			Other Skills
				sympathy
				collaboration
				teamwork
				problem solving

1.3.2. Do: PT 2045 Patient-Centered Simulation Final (See Folder: 'Ass 02 1.3.2. Do')
	Final Simulation Group A7 Final Simulation Groups
		Me, Justice Colon & Nolan Curran
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

The main purpose of the PT 2045 patient-centered simulation final is for you to learn that talking to patients is as important as what information you are providing to them. Although, as PT you will be most likely have overloaded schedules and your time with patients will become limited, practicing these skills will result in better clinical results and job satisfaction. The students who fully invest themselves in these simulations will get the most benefit out of it.

Part of your simulation final for this course will consist of:

	Simulating both a patient and a PT with two different classmates (date and time are to be coordinated outside of class). 
	Completing a one-page reflection of the experience including grading yourself. 

The simulations will be centered around practicing all four skills that comprise the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach (See [HSD2_4Skills]). 

You will work in your peer debriefing group discussion board. Your discussion board will have further instructions including, a PDF attached with details such as the reason for the PT appointment, medical manifestations, diagnosis, the emotional perspective of the patient, and desired outcomes for both the patient and PT. Each simulation should last between 10-15 minutes. It is not mandatory for the whole team to meet for each simulation. You can also complete both simulations through Zoom or in person.

In brief, to successfully complete the simulation, you must: 

	Complete the simulation as a PT with a classmate in your small group
	Complete the simulation as a patient with a different classmate in your small group

Once the two simulations are completed, each of the members of your group will need to complete a one-page reflection and grade yourselves on the overall experience by submitting the scaled grading as a PT and patient. All of you will have until the end of Module 8 to complete and submit the reflection and self-graded on an individual basis. 

Read the grading criteria below to fully understand what each of you will be grading yourselves on and the questions asked in the reflection. Keep in mind, that it is not expected of you to perfect all these skills by the end of the course, but to be aware and practice these when communicating with any patient.

	Role: PT Self Grade Scale
		Emotional Intelligence (Likert Scale)
			I found it difficult to read my patient’s emotions. 
			I was self-aware of my emotions as I spoke with my patient. 
			At certain points of the conversation, I felt frustrated or annoyed with my patient. 
		Empathy (Likert Scale)
			While talking, I tried to imagine how I would feel if I were in my patient’s place.  
			I found it difficult to see things from my patient’s point of view. 
			I tried to look at my patient’s side before I made a decision. 
		Active Listening (Likert Scale)
			While my patient was talking, I was planning how I was going to respond. 
			While my patient was talking, I found myself thinking about other things. 
			While my patient was talking, I asked more questions to get more information and encourage them to speak. 
		Effective Communication (Likert Scale)
			-I asked open-ended questions. 
			-If there was something that I did not know, I told my patient I did not know and redirected them to the correct place to find their answers. 
			-I listened more than I spoke. 
	Role: Patient Grade Scale
		Emotional Intelligence (Likert Scale)
			At certain points of the conversation, my PT was frustrated or annoyed with me.  
		Empathy (Likert Scale)
			My PT tried to understand what I was feeling. 
			My PT tried to see things from my point of view. 
			My PT took my opinion in mind as they made a treatment decision. 
		Active Listening (Likert Scale)
			My PT was actively listening to me (not interrupting me) and validating what I was saying. 
			My PT asked more questions than he/she/they talked.
		Effective Communication (Likert Scale)
			-My PT asked open-ended questions. 
			-If there was something that my PT did not know, they told me they did not know and redirected me to the correct place to find my answers. 
		
	Reflection Questions
		-Were you satisfied with the results of the PT appointments? If not, what could have been done differently?
		-How did you prepare for both simulations? Examples could be practicing with a family member, asking classmates for help with brainstorming ideas on how to best help the patient, or taking notes before simulation of how you will interact with PT as a patient or vice versa.
		-If you could have done something differently, what would you do?
		-What are two things that went well?

2. Discuss the following: (!!)(Llegir per a saber com presentar un Research Paper)
	An Overview of the study, including:
		.Type of study
		.Criteria

			.Exclusion criteria
