	----------------------------------------------------- Detailed explanation of the previous
	An Overview of the study, including:
		.Type of study
			-What are the important data on "Type of study" in a research paper? What are the questions I should ask myself when reading a research paper to identify "Type of study"? 
				The "Type of study" section in a research paper provides crucial information about the design or methodology used to conduct the study. When reading a research paper to identify the type of study, consider asking yourself the following questions:

					1. What is the main objective of the study?: Understanding the primary goal of the research can help determine the type of study. For example, is the study aiming to assess the effectiveness of a treatment, explore associations between variables, or describe a phenomenon?

					2. How were participants selected or assigned?: Consider how participants were recruited or allocated to different groups. Was randomization used to assign participants to groups in an experimental study, or were participants selected based on specific criteria in an observational study?

					3. What data collection methods were employed?: Look for information on the methods used to collect data, such as surveys, interviews, laboratory tests, or observations. This can provide clues about the nature of the study and how information was gathered.

					4. Is there an intervention or exposure being studied?: Determine whether the study involves an intervention, treatment, or exposure that is being tested or evaluated. Experimental studies typically involve an intervention, while observational studies observe naturally occurring exposures.

					5. How are outcomes assessed?: Examine how the study measures outcomes or endpoints. Are they objectively measured, such as through clinical tests or observations, or subjectively reported by participants?

				By asking yourself these questions and carefully reviewing the information provided in the research paper, you can identify the type of study and better understand its design and methodology. This helps you interpret the findings accurately and assess the strength of evidence presented in the study.
		.Criteria
			.Inclusion criteria
				When examining a research paper to identify inclusion criteria, consider the following questions:

				1. Who were the participants?: Look for information about the characteristics of the individuals included in the study. This may include factors such as age, gender, health status, medical history, or other relevant demographics.

				2. What were the specific criteria for inclusion?: Determine the specific requirements or conditions that participants had to meet in order to be eligible for inclusion in the study. This could involve factors such as having a particular medical condition, meeting certain health criteria, or fitting within a specified age range.

				3. How were participants identified or recruited?: Understand the methods used to identify and recruit participants who met the inclusion criteria. This could involve strategies such as recruiting from clinical settings, community outreach, advertisements, or referral networks.

				4. Were there any restrictions on inclusion?: Determine if there were any limitations or exclusions applied to certain groups of individuals, such as those with certain medical conditions, medications, or other factors that may have influenced eligibility for participation.

				By considering these questions and examining the information provided in the research paper, you can gain insights into the inclusion criteria used in the study. This helps you understand the characteristics of the participants included in the research and the extent to which the findings may be applicable to specific populations.
			.Exclusion criteria
				When reviewing a research paper to identify exclusion criteria, consider the following questions:

				1. Who were excluded from the study?: Determine which individuals or groups were not eligible to participate in the research. This may include individuals with specific medical conditions, certain demographic characteristics, or other factors that could affect the study outcomes.

				2. What were the specific exclusion criteria?: Look for details about the criteria used to exclude individuals from participation. This could involve factors such as pre-existing medical conditions, use of certain medications, history of specific treatments, or other relevant characteristics.

				3. How were exclusion criteria applied?: Understand how the exclusion criteria were implemented during participant selection or recruitment. This could involve screening procedures, medical assessments, or other methods used to determine eligibility for inclusion in the study.

				4. Were there any limitations or exceptions to the exclusion criteria?: Determine if there were any circumstances in which individuals who met the exclusion criteria were still included in the study. This could involve specific considerations or exceptions made by the researchers.

				By considering these questions and examining the information provided in the research paper, you can gain insights into the exclusion criteria used in the study. This helps you understand the characteristics of the participants who were excluded from the research and the potential impact of these exclusions on the study findings.
		.Outcomes
			.Primary outcomes
				When analyzing a research paper to identify primary outcomes, consider the following questions:

				1. What are the main objectives of the study?: Understand the primary goals or research questions that the study aims to address. This can provide context for identifying the primary outcomes.

				2. Which outcomes are highlighted as primary?: Look for specific outcomes or variables that are explicitly identified as primary measures of interest in the study. These outcomes are typically the main focus of the research and are often specified in the study protocol or research plan.

				3. How are the primary outcomes defined and measured?: Determine how the primary outcomes are defined and assessed in the study. This may involve specific criteria, measurements, or assessment tools used to quantify or evaluate the outcomes.

				4. Why are these outcomes important?: Consider the significance of the primary outcomes in relation to the study objectives and research questions. Understand why these outcomes were selected as the main focus of the research and how they contribute to the overall understanding of the topic.

				By addressing these questions and carefully reviewing the information provided in the research paper, you can identify the primary outcomes of the study. This helps you understand the key findings and results that are central to the research findings and conclusions.
			.Secondary outcomes
				When examining a research paper to identify secondary outcomes, consider the following:

				1. Additional Measures: Look for additional outcomes or variables that are not considered primary but are still assessed in the study. These secondary outcomes may provide supplemental information or insights related to the research objectives.

				2. Relationship to Primary Outcomes: Determine how the secondary outcomes relate to the primary outcomes. They may serve to further explore aspects of the primary outcomes, provide additional context, or offer alternative perspectives on the study findings.

				3. Measurement and Assessment: Understand how the secondary outcomes are defined, measured, and assessed in the study. This may involve specific criteria, measurement tools, or assessment methods used to quantify or evaluate these outcomes.

				4. Significance: Consider the significance of the secondary outcomes in relation to the primary objectives of the study. While not the main focus, secondary outcomes may still provide valuable information that contributes to a comprehensive understanding of the research topic.

				By addressing these points and reviewing the information provided in the research paper, you can identify the secondary outcomes and understand their role in the study. This helps you grasp the broader scope of the research findings and their implications beyond the primary outcomes.
		.Study Methods:
			.Report the study methods, including the number of subjects in each arm, study protocol, and results
				When examining the "Study Methods" section of a research paper, consider the following key aspects:

				1. Participant Selection: Understand how participants were recruited or selected for the study. This may include details about inclusion and exclusion criteria, recruitment methods, and sample size considerations.

				2. Study Design: Identify the specific design or methodology used in the study, such as experimental, observational, qualitative, or mixed methods. Understand how the study was structured to address the research question or hypothesis.

				3. Interventions or Exposures: If applicable, examine the interventions or exposures studied in the research. This may involve descriptions of treatments, interventions, experimental conditions, or exposures administered to participants.

				4. Data Collection: Explore how data were collected during the study, including the use of surveys, interviews, observations, medical tests, or other assessment methods. Understand how data were recorded, measured, and documented.

				5. Study Protocol: Review any protocols or procedures followed during the study, such as ethical considerations, informed consent processes, and adherence to relevant guidelines or regulations.

				6. Statistical Analysis: Understand how data were analyzed to address the research objectives. This may involve specific statistical methods, tests, or procedures used to analyze the data and draw conclusions.

				7. Results: Look for a summary of the study results, including key findings, outcomes, or observations. Understand how the results are presented and interpreted in relation to the study objectives.

				By examining these aspects of the study methods, you can gain insights into how the research was conducted, the methods used to collect and analyze data, and the overall rigor and reliability of the study findings. This helps you evaluate the quality of the research and understand how the study contributes to the broader body of knowledge in the field.
		.Study Analysis and Critique:
			.Provide an appropriate critique of the study including discussion of the study’s
				.Strengths
					When evaluating the strengths of the study analysis and critique in a research paper, consider the following aspects:

					1. Thorough Examination: Determine if the analysis provides a comprehensive evaluation of the study, including its methodology, results, and interpretation. Look for evidence that the researchers have thoroughly examined all aspects of the study design and implementation.

					2. Objective Assessment: Assess whether the analysis is conducted in an objective and unbiased manner. Look for indications that the researchers have approached the critique with a neutral perspective, considering both strengths and limitations of the study.

					3. Identification of Strengths: Identify specific strengths highlighted in the analysis, such as robust study design, appropriate data collection methods, rigorous statistical analysis, or meaningful results. Look for evidence that the researchers have identified and acknowledged the positive aspects of the study.

					4. Evidence-Based Evaluation: Determine if the analysis is supported by evidence from the study itself, relevant literature, or established research methodologies. Look for references to specific data, findings, or citations that support the strengths identified in the critique.

					5. Contextual Understanding: Assess whether the analysis demonstrates a contextual understanding of the study within the broader research landscape. Look for discussions of how the study contributes to existing knowledge, fills gaps in the literature, or addresses important research questions.

					6. Impact Assessment: Consider whether the analysis includes an assessment of the overall impact or significance of the study findings. Look for discussions of how the study results contribute to advancing knowledge in the field, informing practice or policy, or addressing important clinical or research questions.

					By considering these aspects, you can evaluate the strengths of the study analysis and critique in a research paper. This helps you understand the quality and validity of the study findings and their implications for future research and practice.
				.Weaknesses
					When assessing the weaknesses of the study analysis and critique in a research paper, consider the following aspects:

					1. Biases: Determine if there are any biases present in the analysis, such as confirmation bias (favoring information that supports preconceived notions) or publication bias (favoring studies with positive results). Look for indications that the critique may be influenced by the researchers' personal beliefs or agendas.

					2. Limited Scope: Assess whether the analysis provides a narrow or limited assessment of the study, focusing only on certain aspects while neglecting others. Look for evidence that the critique may overlook important methodological flaws, limitations, or alternative interpretations of the results.

					3. Lack of Depth: Determine if the analysis lacks depth or detail, providing only surface-level criticisms without delving into the underlying reasons or implications. Look for indications that the critique may be superficial or cursory, failing to fully explore the strengths and weaknesses of the study.

					4. Inconsistent Evaluation: Assess whether the analysis provides inconsistent or contradictory evaluations of the study. Look for discrepancies or inconsistencies in how different aspects of the study are critiqued, which may indicate a lack of coherence or rigor in the analysis.

					5. Unsupported Assertions: Determine if the analysis makes unsupported assertions or claims without sufficient evidence or rationale. Look for instances where the critique relies on conjecture, speculation, or personal opinion rather than empirical evidence or logical reasoning.

					6. Lack of Context: Assess whether the analysis lacks context or fails to situate the study within the broader research landscape. Look for indications that the critique may overlook relevant literature, theoretical frameworks, or methodological considerations that could inform a more nuanced assessment.

					7. Failure to Address Limitations: Determine if the analysis fails to adequately address the limitations of the study. Look for omissions or oversights in the critique, such as neglecting to discuss methodological weaknesses, sources of bias, or alternative interpretations of the findings.

					By considering these aspects, you can identify potential weaknesses in the study analysis and critique in a research paper. This helps you critically evaluate the validity and reliability of the critique and its implications for the interpretation of the study findings.
				.Impact
					When assessing the impact of the study analysis and critique in a research paper, consider the following aspects:

					1. Insightful Interpretation: Determine if the analysis provides insightful interpretations of the study findings, methodology, and implications. Look for evidence that the critique offers valuable insights into the strengths, weaknesses, and significance of the research.

					2. Contribution to Knowledge: Assess whether the analysis contributes to advancing knowledge in the field. Look for discussions of how the critique adds to existing literature, fills gaps in knowledge, or offers new perspectives on the research topic.

					3. Implications for Practice: Determine if the analysis has practical implications for clinical practice, policy-making, or decision-making. Look for discussions of how the study findings can be applied in real-world settings, inform interventions or treatments, or guide future research directions.

					4. Critical Reflection: Assess whether the analysis engages in critical reflection on the study's methodology, assumptions, and interpretations. Look for evidence that the critique goes beyond a surface-level evaluation and examines the underlying assumptions, biases, and limitations of the research.

					5. Discussion of Controversies: Determine if the analysis addresses controversies or debates surrounding the study topic. Look for discussions of conflicting evidence, alternative interpretations of the findings, or areas of uncertainty that may impact the study's impact and relevance.

					6. Consideration of Stakeholders: Assess whether the analysis considers the perspectives and interests of various stakeholders, such as researchers, clinicians, policymakers, or patients. Look for discussions of how the study findings may affect different stakeholders and what implications this has for the broader field.

					7. Future Directions: Determine if the analysis identifies future research directions or areas for further investigation. Look for discussions of unanswered questions, unresolved issues, or potential avenues for expanding upon the study findings.

					By considering these aspects, you can evaluate the impact of the study analysis and critique in a research paper. This helps you understand the significance of the research findings, their implications for the field, and the potential for future research and practice.
		.Study conclusion:
			Provide
				.Key points from the study
				.Knowledge gaps that still exist
				.Future research still needed
		-----------------------------------------------------------
		Explain what are these and why are they important in a presentation:
			1. Type of Study: This indicates the design or methodology employed in the research, such as observational (e.g., cohort, case-control) or experimental (e.g., randomized controlled trial). The type of study influences the strength of evidence and the conclusions that can be drawn.
				What are the most common types of study and their strengths and weaknesses?
					The most common types of studies in the field of healthcare and medicine include observational studies (such as cohort studies and case-control studies) and experimental studies (such as randomized controlled trials). Each type has its own strengths and weaknesses, which are important to consider when designing, interpreting, and applying research findings. Here's an overview:

					1. Observational Studies: (See layman explanations down below)
						1.1.Cohort Studies:
							Strengths:
								Allow for the assessment of exposure preceding the outcome, making them useful for studying rare exposures.
								Provide information on the natural history of a disease.
								Can evaluate multiple outcomes related to a single exposure.
							Weaknesses:
								Susceptible to selection bias, as participants may differ systematically based on exposure status.
								Long duration and large sample sizes may be required to observe rare outcomes.
								Control of confounding variables may be challenging, despite statistical adjustments.
						1.2.Case-Control Studies:
							Strengths:
								Efficient for studying rare diseases or outcomes.
								Relatively quick and inexpensive compared to cohort studies.
								Useful for investigating multiple exposures for a single outcome.
							Weaknesses:
								Prone to recall bias, as participants may have difficulty accurately recalling past exposures.
								Not suitable for establishing temporal relationships between exposure and outcome.
								Selection bias can occur if cases and controls are not adequately matched or representative of the population.

					2. Experimental Studies:
						2.1.Randomized Controlled Trials (RCTs):
							Strengths:
								Gold standard for evaluating cause-and-effect relationships between interventions and outcomes.
								Minimize confounding variables through randomization and blinding.
								Provide high internal validity, as they control for bias and chance.
							Weaknesses:
								May be expensive, time-consuming, and logistically challenging to conduct.
								Ethical considerations may limit randomization or blinding in certain situations.
								Findings may not be generalizable to broader populations or real-world settings due to strict eligibility criteria or controlled conditions.

					Each study type has its place in the hierarchy of evidence, with RCTs generally considered the strongest design for assessing treatment efficacy. However, the choice of study type depends on various factors, including research objectives, feasibility, ethical considerations, and available resources. Researchers must carefully weigh the strengths and weaknesses of each study type to ensure the validity and relevance of their findings.

					-------------------------------------------------------------------------
					The levels of understanding and explanations can vary depending on the complexity of the subject matter and the target audience. However, we can broadly categorize them into several levels, such as:

					0.N/A
						Explain this information from a "kid understanding Level" and give examples:
						Explain this information whith analogies and give examples:

					1. Layman or Everyday Level: Explanations at this level are simple, devoid of technical jargon, and suitable for a general audience with no specific expertise in the subject.
						Explain this information from a "Layman or Everyday Level" and give examples:

					2. High School Level: These explanations are more detailed than layman explanations but still cater to a broad audience with a basic understanding of the subject matter. They may include some technical terms but are generally easy to comprehend for high school students.
						Explain this information from a "High School Level" and give examples:

					3. College Level: Explanations at this level assume a higher level of prior knowledge and understanding of the subject. They delve deeper into concepts, theories, and principles, often requiring a foundation in the field to fully grasp.

					4. Academic or Expert Level: This level of explanation is highly specialized and intended for individuals with advanced knowledge or expertise in the subject. It may involve complex theories, advanced methodologies, and in-depth analysis suitable for researchers, scholars, and professionals in the field.

					These are just general categories, and the specific levels may vary depending on the subject area and the context in which the explanation is provided.

					-------------------------------------------------------------------------
					Explain this information from a "Layman or Everyday Level" and give examples:
						1.1.Observational Studies - Cohort Studies - Strengths:
							Imagine you're trying to figure out if something you're exposed to, like eating a certain food or living in a particular area, affects your health in the long term. Observational studies, specifically cohort studies, are like taking a long-term snapshot of a group of people to see how their habits or environments relate to their health.

							Now, here are some strengths of cohort studies, explained in everyday terms:

							1. Allow for the assessment of exposure preceding the outcome: This means that researchers can look back in time to see if something people were exposed to in the past might be linked to a health issue they're experiencing now. For example, if researchers follow a group of people who drank a specific type of water years ago and now want to see if it affected their health, they can look at the water they drank in the past and compare it to their health outcomes today.

							2. Provide information on the natural history of a disease: This is like watching a movie of how a disease develops over time. Cohort studies can show researchers how a disease progresses in a group of people, from its early stages to its later effects. For instance, if researchers follow a group of smokers for years, they can see how smoking affects their health over time, from minor issues like coughing to more serious conditions like lung cancer.

							3. Can evaluate multiple outcomes related to a single exposure: This is like looking at all the different ways one thing might affect your health. In a cohort study, researchers can examine how a particular exposure, like eating a certain type of food, might impact various aspects of health, such as weight gain, heart health, or even mental well-being. For example, if researchers study a group of people who regularly eat fast food, they can see how it affects not just their weight, but also their cholesterol levels, blood pressure, and overall mood.

							So, in simpler terms, cohort studies are like watching a group of people over time to see how their habits or environments affect their health. They're useful for studying rare exposures because they allow researchers to look back in time, provide insights into how diseases progress naturally, and can explore how one thing might impact many different aspects of health.
						1.1.Observational Studies - Cohort Studies - Weaknesses:
							Let's break down the weaknesses of cohort studies in simpler terms:

							1. Susceptible to selection bias: This means that the people who are chosen to be in the study might not represent everyone accurately. For example, if researchers only include healthy volunteers in their study about the effects of exercise, they might miss out on understanding how exercise affects people who are already sick. So, the results might not apply to everyone equally.

							2. Long duration and large sample sizes may be required to observe rare outcomes: This is like trying to find a needle in a haystack. If something doesn't happen very often, like a rare disease or a rare side effect of a medicine, researchers might need to study a lot of people over a long period to see it happening. For instance, if researchers want to understand a rare side effect of a new medication, they might need to follow thousands of people for many years to detect it.

							3. Control of confounding variables may be challenging, despite statistical adjustments: Confounding variables are like sneaky influencers that can mess up the results of a study. Even if researchers try to account for these variables, like age or gender, they might miss some or not fully understand how they affect the outcomes. So, even if the numbers say one thing, there could be other factors at play that aren't accounted for. For example, in a study about the effects of coffee on heart health, researchers might adjust for age and smoking status but forget to consider other factors like stress levels or diet, which could also influence the results.

							To put it simply, cohort studies have their drawbacks. They can be influenced by who gets chosen for the study, it can take a long time and a lot of people to see rare events, and sometimes it's hard to make sure all the factors that could affect the results are accounted for. So, while cohort studies are valuable, researchers need to be aware of these limitations when interpreting their findings.

						1.2.Observational Studies - Case-Control Studies - Strengths:
							Let's simplify the strengths of case-control studies:

							1. Efficient for studying rare diseases or outcomes: Imagine trying to find out why only a few people get a rare disease, like a specific type of cancer. Case-control studies are like detective work. Instead of waiting for many people to get sick, researchers can start by looking at those who already have the disease (cases) and compare them to people who don't (controls). This way, they can quickly gather information and clues about what might have caused the disease without having to wait for it to happen in a large group of people.

							Example: Suppose researchers want to understand why some people develop a rare autoimmune disease. They can compare a group of individuals who already have the disease with a similar group of people who don't. By asking about their past behaviors, exposures, or genetics, researchers can pinpoint factors that might be linked to the disease.

							2. Relatively quick and inexpensive compared to cohort studies: Picture a race between two cars. Cohort studies are like a long journey, while case-control studies are more like a quick sprint. Since case-control studies start with people who already have the disease, researchers don't need to wait years to see if they develop it. This makes the study faster and cheaper because they only need to gather information from the past instead of following people over time.

							Example: Let's say researchers want to investigate the link between a certain type of food poisoning and a particular restaurant. Instead of tracking customers' health over months, they can quickly find people who got sick (cases) and compare them to those who didn't (controls). This saves time and money because they don't have to wait for more cases to occur.

							3. Useful for investigating multiple exposures for a single outcome: Think of this like solving a mystery with multiple suspects. In a case-control study, researchers can look at several potential causes (exposures) and see which one might be responsible for the outcome (disease). This allows them to explore different possibilities and narrow down the list of suspects.

							Example: Consider a study looking into the causes of heart disease. Researchers can compare individuals with heart disease (cases) to those without (controls) and investigate various factors such as smoking, diet, exercise, and family history. By examining multiple exposures, they can identify which ones are more strongly associated with the development of heart disease.

							So, in simple terms, case-control studies are like fast and efficient detective work. They're great for investigating rare diseases, quick to conduct, and allow researchers to explore multiple potential causes for a single health outcome.
						1.2.Observational Studies - Case-Control Studies - Weaknesses:
							Let's simplify the weaknesses of case-control studies:

							1. Prone to recall bias: Imagine trying to remember what you had for breakfast last Monday. Sometimes our memories aren't perfect, especially when it comes to things that happened a long time ago. In case-control studies, people who already have the disease (cases) might have trouble remembering past events or exposures accurately. This can skew the results because their memories might not be reliable, leading to incorrect conclusions about what caused the disease.

							Example: Suppose researchers are conducting a case-control study to investigate the link between a specific type of cancer and exposure to a certain chemical. They ask participants to recall their exposure to the chemical in the past. However, some individuals with the disease might have trouble remembering their exposure accurately, leading to biased results.

							2. Not suitable for establishing temporal relationships between exposure and outcome: Temporal relationships are like figuring out which event came first in a story. In case-control studies, researchers can't always tell if the exposure happened before the disease because they're looking back in time. This makes it difficult to determine whether the exposure caused the disease or if the disease influenced the exposure.

							Example: Let's say researchers are investigating the relationship between smoking and lung cancer using a case-control study. Since they're asking people about their smoking habits after they've been diagnosed with lung cancer, they can't be sure if smoking caused the cancer or if the cancer diagnosis influenced the person's smoking behavior.

							3. Selection bias can occur if cases and controls are not adequately matched or representative of the population: Picture trying to compare apples and oranges. In case-control studies, researchers need to make sure that the people with the disease (cases) and those without (controls) are similar in all aspects except for the exposure being studied. If they're not, it's like comparing apples to oranges, and the results won't accurately reflect the true relationship between exposure and disease.

							Example: Consider a case-control study investigating the link between a certain type of exercise and heart disease. If the researchers only include cases from a gym and controls from a retirement home, they might not accurately represent the general population. This could lead to biased results because the groups are not comparable.

							So, in simple terms, case-control studies have their drawbacks. People's memories may not be reliable, making it hard to get accurate information. Also, because researchers are looking back in time, it's tough to figure out which came first, the exposure or the disease. Plus, if the groups being compared aren't similar, the results might not be trustworthy.

						2.1.Experimental Studies - Randomized Controlled Trials - Strengths:
							Let's simplify the strengths of randomized controlled trials (RCTs):

							1. Gold standard for evaluating cause-and-effect relationships between interventions and outcomes: Imagine you're testing a new medicine. In an RCT, researchers randomly assign people to either take the medicine or not. By doing this, they can compare how the two groups fare and see if the medicine actually caused any changes in their health. This makes RCTs the top choice for figuring out if something causes something else.

							Example: Suppose scientists want to test a new vaccine for a disease. They randomly divide a group of volunteers into two: one group gets the vaccine, and the other doesn't. By comparing how many people in each group get sick, they can determine if the vaccine actually protects against the disease.

							2. Minimize confounding variables through randomization and blinding: Confounding variables are like hidden factors that could mess up the results of a study. In an RCT, researchers use randomization to make sure that both groups are similar at the start, which helps cancel out the effects of any hidden factors. Also, by keeping the participants and sometimes even the researchers "blind" (meaning they don't know who's getting the treatment), it prevents biases from affecting the results.

							Example: Let's say researchers are testing a new drug for pain relief. They randomly assign participants to either take the drug or a placebo (a harmless pill). Since the assignment is random, both groups are likely to be similar in terms of factors like age, gender, and severity of pain. Plus, if neither the participants nor the researchers know who's taking the real drug, it prevents any biases from influencing the results.

							3. Provide high internal validity, as they control for bias and chance: Internal validity is like the accuracy of the results. In an RCT, researchers go the extra mile to make sure that the results are as accurate as possible by controlling for biases and chance. By doing this, they can be more confident that any differences observed between the groups are actually due to the treatment and not just random chance.

							Example: Imagine scientists are testing a new therapy for depression. By using randomization and blinding, they can ensure that any improvements seen in the group receiving the therapy are genuinely because of the treatment and not just because of other factors like placebo effects or differences between the groups.

							So, in simple terms, randomized controlled trials are like the superheroes of research studies. They're the best at figuring out if something causes something else, they minimize the chances of getting tricked by hidden factors, and they give us the most accurate results possible.
						2.1.Experimental Studies - Randomized Controlled Trials - Weaknesses:
							Let's simplify the weaknesses of randomized controlled trials (RCTs):

							1. May be expensive, time-consuming, and logistically challenging to conduct: Picture planning a big event, like a concert. RCTs are like organizing a concert where everything has to be just right. It can take a lot of money, time, and effort to set up and run an RCT because researchers need to recruit participants, provide treatments, and collect data in a controlled way. This can make the whole process slow and tricky.

							Example: Think about a clinical trial testing a new cancer treatment. It requires lots of resources to recruit patients, provide treatment, monitor their health, and collect data over a long period. All of this can add up to a hefty bill and take several years to complete.

							2. Ethical considerations may limit randomization or blinding in certain situations: Ethics are like the rules that guide how we treat others. Sometimes, it might not be ethical or fair to randomly assign people to different groups or keep them in the dark about their treatment. This can happen if the new treatment being tested is known to be very effective or if there's a risk of harm from not receiving the treatment.

							Example: Consider a study testing the effectiveness of CPR (cardiopulmonary resuscitation) for heart attacks. It would be unethical to withhold CPR from some participants because it's a life-saving intervention. In such cases, researchers might not be able to use randomization or blinding, which could affect the study's design and results.

							3. Findings may not be generalizable to broader populations or real-world settings due to strict eligibility criteria or controlled conditions: Think of this like a movie that's great in the theater but doesn't quite work when you watch it at home. In RCTs, researchers often have strict rules about who can participate, which can make the study group different from the general population. Also, the controlled conditions in RCTs might not reflect how things work in the real world, so the results might not apply to everyone outside the study.

							Example: Let's say researchers conduct an RCT to test a new exercise program for older adults with arthritis. They might only include people who meet specific criteria, like age and severity of arthritis. However, this group might not represent all older adults with arthritis, so the findings might not apply to everyone in the real world.

							So, in simple terms, while randomized controlled trials are powerful tools for studying treatments, they can be costly and time-consuming, face ethical challenges in certain situations, and their findings might not always apply to everyone in real life.		

			2. Criteria:
			Inclusion Criteria: These are the characteristics or conditions that subjects must possess to be eligible for participation in the study. Inclusion criteria help ensure that the sample is representative of the population being studied.
			Exclusion Criteria: Conversely, exclusion criteria are characteristics or conditions that disqualify individuals from participating in the study. Exclusion criteria help control for confounding variables and ensure the safety and integrity of the research.

			3. Outcomes:
			Primary Outcomes: These are the main variables or endpoints that the study aims to measure or evaluate. Primary outcomes are typically the focus of the research hypothesis.
			Secondary Outcomes: These are additional variables or endpoints that are also measured in the study but are of lesser importance compared to primary outcomes. Secondary outcomes provide supplementary information and insights.

			4. Study Methods: This section details how the study was conducted, including the study protocol, procedures, interventions (if applicable), and the number of subjects or participants in each group or arm of the study. Reporting study methods ensures transparency and reproducibility.

			5. Study Analysis and Critique: Here, the presenter provides an evaluation of the study's strengths, weaknesses, and overall impact. This involves assessing aspects such as the study design, sample size, data analysis methods, and potential biases. Critiquing the study helps the audience interpret the findings accurately and assess the reliability of the conclusions.

			6. Study Conclusion: This section summarizes the key findings of the study and highlights any significant implications. It also identifies any knowledge gaps or limitations of the research, indicating areas where further investigation is warranted. The study conclusion provides closure to the presentation and guides future research directions.

			Incorporating these components in a presentation ensures that the audience comprehensively understands the study's objectives, methods, results, and implications, facilitating informed discussion and decision-making.

Create a basic presentation (see the journal club rubric for a guide can be a word document, power point)
	('Journal Club Rubric.pdf')

	-Osteoporosis and Paget’s disease of bone are common disorders of bone metabolism that affect the elderly, reduce quality of life, and are often asymptomatic.

	-Osteomyelitis is a rare but serious complication of surgery, trauma, and bloodstream infections that requires long-term antibiotic therapy.

	-Distinguish between osteonecrosis and osteomyelitis in their presentation and treatment. 
	-Discuss the current literature related to physical therapy management of osteoporosis, Paget’s disease of bone, osteonecrosis, and osteomyelitis.  
	-Appraise the management of these diseases from the perspective of the patient-centered philosophy.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 2 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

About 4% of the world’s population is affected by one of more than 80 different autoimmune diseases, the most common being: type 1 diabetes, multiple sclerosis, rheumatoid arthritis, lupus, Crohn’s disease, psoriasis, and scleroderma (NSCF, 2021). 

In the United States, autoimmune diseases represent the third most common cause of chronic illness. The National Institutes for Health (NIH) estimate that they affect 5 to 8% of the population. For reasons that are still unknown, the numbers are increasing (NSCF, 2021). 
	(Γ)What are the first two most common cause of chronic illness in the USA?
		In the United States, the first two most common causes of chronic illness are cardiovascular diseases and cancer.

This module shifts the focus to rheumatic disease which deals mainly with autoimmune disorders of the musculoskeletal system. Specifically, it will provide an overview of the development of autoimmune diseases. As a reminder, an autoimmune  disease is a disease in which the body's immune system attacks healthy cells. 

Although there are similarities in the clinical manifestations between many of the diseases studied in this module, pay close attention to what differentiates these diseases and how physical therapy plays a key role in improving function and maintaining quality of life for patients diagnosed with one of these diseases.  

In this module, you will also practice developing the PT 2045 Patient-Centered skill of emotional intelligence. 

.Learning Objectives
	By the end of this week, you will be able to: 
		-Explain the general steps in the development of an autoimmune disease. 
		-Compare and contrast the clinical manifestations, treatment, and prognosis of systemic lupus erythematosus, psoriatic arthritis, polymyalgia rheumatica, myositis, and scleroderma.
		-Develop a differential diagnosis, including primary diagnosis and/or a general physical therapy plan when given a patient scenario.
		-Practice emotional intelligence in a patient PT setting.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
2.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Hematoma (Folder: 'Skin Lesions')
	Definition: Collection of extravasated blood within soft tissue.
	Etymology: Hemat/o – blood; -oma –mass.
Macule/Patch 
	Definition: Flat discrete discoloration of skin < 1 cm in size (macule) or > 1 cm (patch). 
	Etymology: Macul/o – spot. 
Nodule  
	Definition: Palpable solid lesion < 2 cm. 
	Etymology: Nod/o – knot; -ule – small. 
Papule/Plaque 
	Definition: Raised solid lesion < 1 cm (papule) or > 1 cm (plaque). 
	Etymology: Papul/o – pimple. 
Petechia/Purpura 
	Definition: Spots of hemorrhage under the skin that can be tiny (petechia) or larger collections (purpura). 
Vesicle/Bulla 
	Definition: Circumscribed raised lesion filled with fluid that is < ½ cm (vesicle) or > ½ cm (bulla). Common name is blister. 
	Etymology: Vesicul/o – small sac.
Arthroplasty
	Definition: surgical repair of joint.
	Etymology: arthr/o – joint; -plasty – surgical repair.

2.2. Explore: Clinical Manifestations of Rheumatology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

There are many rheumatologic diseases that will require additional clinical care by either a physician or healthcare professional. As you study these diseases, pay close attention to the similarities and differences between clinical manifestations and when you should refer them to another healthcare professional.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
2.5. Module Summary
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Review these three key learnings from this module: 
	-Immune-mediated systemic diseases are diverse in their pathogenesis, clinical manifestations, and prognosis. 
	-A careful history and physical exam can help to differentiate between these diseases. 
	-Physical therapy plays a key role in improving function and maintaining quality of life for patients diagnosed with one of these diseases.

Many students in this course also find it very useful to study and review the Module 2 Study Guide after completing the module.
	('Module_2_Study_Guide_1.docx')
		1. PSORIATIC ARTHRITIS
		Autoimmune inflammatory arthritis that is associated with 30% of patients who have cutaneous psoriasis.  
		Check out this video courtesy of Johns Hopkins of Dr. Ana-Maria Orbai speaking with psoriatic arthritis patient Suzanne.   
		For an overview of the typical PT treatment for psoriatic arthritis, review the article "New Insights in Physical Therapy and Rehabilitation in Psoriatic Arthritis: A Review" in the "Extra resources" section of this module.

		A. Physical therapy should be initiated early in the disease process
		B. Consider:
			i. Use of rest, both local and systemic
			ii. Exercise: passive and active stretching, ROM, strengthening and endurance
				1. Avoid high-impact activities, move in pain-free range
			iii. Aquatherapy (if no open skin lesions)
			iv. Pain management modalities: heat, cold
			v. Orthotics
			vi. Assistive devices for gait and adaptive devices for self-care tasks
		D. Fatigue and morning stiffness that lasts >30 minutes
			i. Avoid first thing appointments, or move around before the appointment
		E. Discuss weight management
		F. Dermatology referral
		G. OT: distal joints (hands) effected to help get their fine motor movement back
		H. Evidence for other modalities like stim and laser therapy.

		2. POLYMYALGIA RHEUMATICA
		-Rheumatologic disease characterized by aching and morning stiffness of the shoulders, hips, and neck. It is associated with giant cell arteritis, which is a vasculitis typically of the superficial temporal artery.  
		-Check out this video of a patient with polymyalgia rheumatica describing how it started and how he is doing.  

		A. Refer new or established patients who complain of bilateral pain and stiffness of shoulders, hips, and neck for medical evaluation  
		B. Patients with polymyalgia rheumatica should be monitored for headaches, tenderness on the sides of their head above their ears, scalp sensitivity, or visual complaints. These are all signs of giant cell arteritis refer for medical evaluation
		C. If a patient with known polymyalgia rheumatica complains to you about increased pain and stiffness, ask them if their steroid regimen has changed or if they are still using it as prescribed. Patients may try to self-wean off steroids which can worsen symptoms
		D. PT Implications
			i. Schedule in afternoon so they can tolerate PT more
			ii. Moving frequently to avoid joint stiffness
			iii. Pair aquatic PT with land therapy
			iv. Continue to encourage low impact activities, walking, biking, swimming
			v. Glucocorticoid education.

		3. MYOSITIS
		Umbrella term for inflammation of muscle due to an immune mediated muscle injury. The most common types are polymyositis and dermatomyositis.
		Check out this video about three patients living with different types of myositis.  

		A. Refer anyone with muscle pain and weakness (especially when worsened by exercise) for medical evaluation to identify cause.
		B. If treating a patient diagnosed with myositis
			i. Maintain strength to prevent contractures
				1. Submaximal exercise
					a. Avoid eccentric or intense exercise
				2. Splints
				3. Speech therapy evaluation for swallowing function if evidence of dysphagia
				4. Importance of warm up and cool downs
				5. Postural balance exercises and gait draining
				6. Seated marches, clam shells, isometric quad sets, sit to stand, hand and foot proprioceptive exercises
				7. Adapting ADLS  
				8. Trending CPK levels
		C. DM/Polymyositis:
			i. Decreased exercise tolerance,  
			ii. Deltoid, hip, neck weakness
			iii. Skin changes
			iv. Heightened risk for MI, myocarditis and interstitial lung disease
		D. IBM:
			i. Slow progressive proximal leg weakness
			ii. Nutritional deficiency: dysphasia --> refer out, choking, pneumonia, malnutrition
		E. Drug induced myositis
			i. SBERT screening
		F. Acute viral myositis
			i. Monitor trends in clinical  
			ii. Benefit from back and LE strength training
			iii. Rhabdomyolysis.

		4. SYSTEMIC SCLEROSIS
		Systemic disease due to progressive fibrosis of skin, vasculature, and internal organs.
		Check out this video about four patients living with systemic sclerosis.  

		A. PT treatment
			i. Treat with strengthening and ROM exercises.
			ii. Stretching (active and passive) is painful for these patients, but necessary to prevent worsening stiffness from fibrosis and prevent contractures.
			iii. Be aware if patient is complaining of discomfort with less than expected pressure that their skin can be very sensitive to pressure.
			iv. Protect joints with modified ADLs, lightweight splints, or bracing.
			v. Consider finger web – used to strengthen hand intrinsic muscles.
				1. Demo video here
		B. Regular exercise keeps skin and joints flexible, helps to promote adequate blood flow, and prevents contractures.
			i. Careful cardiopulmonary exercise to help maintain function, but listen to patient in terms of limits.
		C. Other considerations:
			i. Ensuring patients getting appropriate cardiopulmonary screening.
			ii. Ensuring patients are up to date on immunizations.
			iii. Assess digit ulcers (if present) for signs of infection.

		5. SYSTEMIC LUPUS ERYTHEMATOSUS
		-Chronic autoimmune disease that commonly affects young women and can cause damage to any organ in the body. Physical therapy is a mainstay of treatment for SLE.
		-Check out this video of Lenaki Alexander talking about her journey with SLE.

		A. Physical therapy plan should be individualized since the clinical manifestations of this disease are highly variable. The plan should address:
			i. Improving fatigue
			ii. Improving associated symptoms of depression
			iii. Pain reduction --> arthritis/myalgia
			iv. Improving/preventing stiffness. → including joint ROM
			v. Improve functional mobility
			vi. Control inflammation  
		B. Patients often have lower exercise capacity than controls even though they are young, do not think they will have the same endurance as an age-matched peer
		C. If pain lasts for more than 1-1.5 hours after an activity, the exercise regimen should be reduced in intensity or duration.
		D. Types of exercises that can be helpful:
			i. Aerobic exercise improves aerobic capacity, endurance, and fatigue
				1. Arm bike
			ii. Isometric exercises: especially of hip and knee
			iii. Isotonic exercises: when there is little or no joint inflammation
			iv. Transfers and ambulation activities
			v. Aquatic therapy
		E. Other considerations:
			i. Remember these patients are at higher risk of DVT and stroke
			ii. Photosensitivity --> sun protection
			iii. Immunizations
			iv. Remember the cardiac and lung involvement.

		6. COMPLICATIONS OF STEROID USE
		A. Osteoporosis  
		B. Stress fractures
		C. Proximal muscle weakness
		D. Avascular necrosis
		E. Hyperglycemia can get steroid induced diabetes
		F. Adrenal insufficiency
		G. Weight gain
		H. Cataracts and glaucoma
		I. Skin thinning purpura, red striae (stretch marks)
		J. Gastric/esophageal ulcer formation
		K. Myopathy (muscle weakness)
		L. Psychiatric/cognitive disturbances
			i. Memory impairment
			ii. Mood lability
			iii. Psychosis
			iv. Insomnia and nightmares
		M. Immunosuppression.

After completing this module, you can now:
	-Explain the general steps in the development of autoimmune diseases. 
	-Compare and contrast the clinical manifestations, treatment, and prognosis of systemic lupus erythematosus, psoriatic arthritis polymyalgia rheumatica, myositis, and scleroderma. 
	-Develop a differential diagnosis, including a primary diagnosis, when given a patient scenario. 
	-Construct a general physical therapy plan when given a patient scenario.

Module 2 - Ungraded Quiz

.Question 1
Match the following diseases with their respective treatments. Note: Some treatments may match with more than one disease, but each disease only has one correct treatment.
	Scleroderma
    Psoriatic arthritis
    Polymyositis
    Polymyalgia rheumatica
    Systemic lupus erythematosus

	Mycophenolate mofetil
	Low dose glucocorticoid (prednisone)
	IVIG
	Hydroxychloroquine + prednisone
	Biologic DMARD (infliximab)

	---------------------------------------------------------------

    1. Scleroderma: Mycophenolate mofetil
    - Mycophenolate mofetil is commonly used to treat scleroderma, particularly for managing lung involvement and skin manifestations.

    2. Psoriatic arthritis: Biologic DMARD (infliximab)
    - Infliximab, a biologic DMARD, is frequently used in the treatment of psoriatic arthritis to reduce inflammation and slow disease progression.

    3. Polymyositis: IVIG
    - Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) is often used in the treatment of polymyositis, especially in patients who do not respond to first-line therapies like glucocorticoids.

    4. Polymyalgia rheumatica: Low dose glucocorticoid (prednisone)
    - Low dose glucocorticoid (prednisone) is the primary treatment for polymyalgia rheumatica, effectively reducing inflammation and symptoms.

    5. Systemic lupus erythematosus: Hydroxychloroquine + prednisone
    - The combination of hydroxychloroquine and prednisone is a common treatment regimen for systemic lupus erythematosus, helping to manage both chronic symptoms and acute flare-ups.

    Therefore, the correct matches are:

    - Scleroderma: Mycophenolate mofetil
    - Psoriatic arthritis: Biologic DMARD (infliximab)
    - Polymyositis: IVIG
    - Polymyalgia rheumatica: Low dose glucocorticoid (prednisone)
    - Systemic lupus erythematosus: Hydroxychloroquine + prednisone

    Genetic predisposition - 1
    Environmental factor - 2
    Inappropriate activation of immune system - 3
    Inflammation – either local or systemic - 4
    End organ damage - 5

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Example of BMI Calculation
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

## Calculating BMI
	Given:
	- Weight = 47 kg
	- Height = 5 feet (5')

	To calculate BMI, we first need to convert the height to meters.

	Converting 5 feet to meters:
	5 feet = 1.524 meters [1]

	Now, we can use the formula:
	BMI = Weight (kg) / [Height (m)]^2

	Substituting the values:
	BMI = 47 kg / (1.524 m)^2
	BMI = 47 kg / 2.3225 m^2
	BMI = 20.25 kg/m^2

	## BMI Interpretation

	According to the BMI categories provided by the CDC [2]:

	- Underweight = BMI < 18.5
	- Normal weight = BMI 18.5 - 24.9
	- Overweight = BMI 25 - 29.9 
	- Obesity = BMI ≥ 30

	With a BMI of 20.25 kg/m^2, the person with a weight of 47 kg and height of 5 feet falls within the normal weight category.

	Citations:
	[1] https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/educational/lose_wt/BMI/bmicalc.htm
	[2] https://www.cdc.gov/healthyweight/assessing/bmi/adult_bmi/english_bmi_calculator/bmi_calculator.html
	[3] https://www.cdc.gov/nccdphp/dnpao/growthcharts/training/bmiage/page5_2.html
	[4] https://www.calculator.net/bmi-calculator.html
	[5] https://www.webmd.com/diet/body-bmi-calculator

	Organ inflammation/damage

Psoriatic Arthritis (Q&A_PsoriaticArthritis)

.N/A 1
	Is It a Rash?
		When most people think about psoriasis, they think about the classic rash. But psoriasis, just like other autoimmune diseases, is a systemic disease.  

N/A 1
	Psoriatic Arthritis
		Autoimmune inflammatory arthritis
		Associated with skin psoriasis
			~30% patients

Pathogenesis ('Psoriatic Arthritis Pathogenesis.png')
	Genetic predisposition
		MHC 
	Environmental Factors
		Infection
			Streptococcus
			Lyme
		Altered microbiomes
			Gut
			Skin

		Fracture

Clinical Manifestations

	Pain and stiffness
	Morning stiffness > 30 minutes
	Improved with activity, worse with rest
	History of psoriasis
	Peripheral arthritis
		Asymmetric vs. symmetric
		Polyarthritis
		Distal interphalangeal joints (40%)
		Arthritis mutilans
	Axial arthritis (50%)
	Enthesitis (~35%)
		Inflammation at site of tendon/ligament insertion into bone
	Dactylitis (~50%)
		Swelling of a finger or toe
		“Sausage digit”
	Skin and nail disease (80-90%)
	Ocular involvement

CASPAR Criteria
	Inflammatory musculoskeletal disease (peripheral arthritis, spondylitis, enthesitis)
	+ 3 points from below
		Clinical Features                                   Points
			Skin psoriasis
				Present                                        2
				History of                                     1
				Family history of                              1

		Pencil in cup deformity

		Occupational Therapy

.Knowledge Checker
	A patient has severe psoriatic arthritis. What are some potential treatment options they have? (Select all that apply)
		NSAIDs
		Physical Therapy
		Weight reduction
		Biologic DMARD
			(Γ)--------------------------------------------------
			Patients with severe psoriatic arthritis have several treatment options available to manage their condition. These options may include:
				1. NSAIDs (Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs): NSAIDs can help reduce inflammation and relieve pain associated with psoriatic arthritis. They are often used as first-line therapy for mild to moderate symptoms.

				2. Physical Therapy: Physical therapy plays a crucial role in the management of psoriatic arthritis by improving joint function, reducing pain, and increasing mobility. It includes exercises, stretches, and modalities tailored to the patient's needs.

				3. Weight Reduction: Excess weight can exacerbate symptoms of psoriatic arthritis by putting additional stress on the joints. Weight reduction through a combination of dietary changes and exercise can help alleviate symptoms and improve overall joint health.

				4. Biologic DMARDs (Disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs): Biologic DMARDs are a class of medications that target specific components of the immune system involved in the inflammatory process of psoriatic arthritis. They are often prescribed for patients with severe or refractory symptoms who have not responded adequately to other treatments.

				It's important for patients with severe psoriatic arthritis to work closely with their healthcare provider to determine the most appropriate treatment plan based on their individual needs and disease severity. This may involve a combination of medications, physical therapy, lifestyle modifications, and other interventions to effectively manage the condition and improve quality of life.

Polymyalgia Rheumatica (Q&A_PolymyalgiaRheumatica)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.N/A
	Giant Cell Arteritis
	Polymyalgia Rheumatica is associated with Giant Cell Arteritis, which is a type of vasculitis. 

	Poly-mya-lgia-Rheumatica = Many-Muscles-Pain-Joints & Connective Tissue

N/A 1
	Rheumatologic disease characterized by aching and morning stiffness of 
		Shoulders
		Hips
		Neck
	Second most common rheumatic disease in US
		Affects adults ≥ 50 y/o
		Scandinavia/Norther European
	Associated with Giant Cell Arteritis 
		Vasculitis of arteries
		Most commonly of temporal scalp

Pathogenesis
	Genetic predisposition + Environmental exposure ↓
	Activation of adaptive cellular and humoral immunity ↓
	<Helper T cells> Regulatory T cells ↓
	Increase circulation cytokines like IL-6 ↓
	Inflammation of shoulder & hip joints ↓
	Referred Pain

Clinical Manifestations
	Bilateral pain
		Shoulders
		Pelvic girdle
		Neck
	Bilateral stiffness 
		Gel phenomenon
		Worse in morning and at night
		Improves with movement
	Limited ROM
		Shoulder abduction
		Difficulties with ADLs
	Systemic symptoms
		Low grade fever
		Fatigue
		Poor appetite
		Weight Loss

Diagnosis
	Combination of clinical findings and laboratory testing
	Age ≥ 50
	Bilateral shoulder/pelvic girdle pain
	Morning stiffness
	Duration > 2 weeks
	Elevated ESR or CRP
	Rapid resolution of symptoms with low dose glucocorticoids
	Ruled out alternative diagnoses
		Rheumatoid arthritis
	---------------------------------
	Physical Exam
		Joint exam 
			ROM → inability to abduct shoulders > 90 degrees
		Normal muscle strength
	Laboratory Tests
		CBC
		CRP
		ESR
		CMP
		TSH, CPK, RF, ACAPs
	Imaging
		MRI
		Ultrasound

Treatment
	Goal: Alleviate symptoms
	Low-dose glucocorticoids
		Prednisone
		Improvement is fast and complete
		Duration varies: 1-2 years
	Assessment for Giant Cell Arteritis
	Referral to rheumatologist 
		Complicated or atypical cases
	Overall good long-term prognosis

.N/A

	The mechanism involved in tissue injury is incompletely understood

		Muscle Weakness
			Back 
			Legs
		Associated Symptoms
			Fever
			Hx of URI
			Myalgias
			Muscle edema
			Rhabdomyolysis
		Diagnosis
			Clinical Rule out rhabdo
				CPK
				UA
Treatment
	Dermatomyositis & Polymyositis (DM)
		Steroids (prednisone) + Azathioprine or methotrexate IVIG
	Inclusion Body Myositis (IBM)
		Physical therapy; Occupational therapy, Speech therapy
		No data to support immunosuppressive therapy
	Acute Viral Myositis
		Supportive; Treat rhabdomyolysis if develops
	Drugs
		Remove offending agent

.(Γ)MINDMAP ('MINDMAP Myositis')
Myositis

	1. Introduction
	    Definition
	    Broad Term: "My" = muscle, "-itis" = inflammation

	2. Types and Causes
	    Infection
		    Virus
		    Bacteria
		    Parasites
		    Fungus
	    Inflammatory
		    Polymyositis
		    Dermatomyositis
		    Inclusion body myositis (IBM)
		    Immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy
	    Drugs
		    Direct myotoxicity
		    Alcohol
		    Cocaine
		    Steroids
		    Immune-mediated
		    Statins

	3. Pathogenesis
	    Immune-mediated muscle injury
	    Each type has its own specific pathway
	    Mechanism involved in tissue injury is incompletely understood

	4. Types of Myositis (Details)
	    Dermatomyositis (DM) & Polymyositis (PM)
		    Onset of Symptoms: Subacute, Common before 50
		    Muscle Weakness: Proximal (Deltoid, Hip flexor, Neck flexor), Symmetric, Rapid progression
		    Associated Symptoms: Myocarditis/MI, Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD), Dysphagia, Skin changes (Gottron papules, Poikiloderma, Heliotrope rash)
		    Diagnosis: >10X normal, Myositis-specific & associated autoantibodies
	    Inclusion Body Myositis (IBM)
		    Onset of Symptoms: Insidious, Uncommon before 50
		    Muscle Weakness: Asymmetric (Finger flexor, Proximal leg), Slowly progressive
		    Associated Symptoms: Dysphagia (Cricopharyngeal muscle)
		    Diagnosis: Normal or <10X, No autoantibodies, Muscle biopsy
	    Acute Viral Myositis
		    Onset of Symptoms: Acute
		    Muscle Weakness: Back, Legs
		    Associated Symptoms: Fever, History of URI, Myalgias, Muscle edema, Rhabdomyolysis
		    Diagnosis: Clinical, Rule out rhabdo (CPK, UA)

	5. Treatment
	    Dermatomyositis & Polymyositis (DM & PM)
		    Steroids (prednisone) + Azathioprine or methotrexate, IVIG
	    Inclusion Body Myositis (IBM)
		    Physical therapy, Occupational therapy, Speech therapy
		    No data to support immunosuppressive therapy
	    Acute Viral Myositis
		    Supportive treatment; Treat rhabdomyolysis if develops
	    Drug-induced Myositis
		    Remove offending agent

	6. Most Common Myopathy
	    Inclusion body myositis
		    Most common in the 50+ age group
		    Prevalence: 180 per 1 million individuals
		    Example: Peter Frampton, English rock musician

	To create this visually:
		1. Central Node: Myositis
		2. Branches: From the central node, create branches for each main topic (Introduction, Types and Causes, etc.).
		3. Sub-branches: For each main topic, create sub-branches detailing the specific points (e.g., under "Types and Causes", sub-branches would include Infection, Inflammatory, Drugs, etc.).

.Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following are treatments for patients with myositis? (Select all that apply)
		Steroids (prednisone)
		Immunosuppressive therapy
		Speech therapy
		Occupational therapy

		✓Steroids (prednisone)
		✗Immunosuppressive therapy
		✓Speech therapy
		✓Occupational therapy

	(Γ) No Correcte
	Patients with myositis may require various treatments to manage their condition effectively. Among the options listed:
		1. Steroids (prednisone): Corticosteroids such as prednisone are commonly used as first-line therapy for myositis to reduce inflammation and suppress the immune system's abnormal response. They can help alleviate muscle weakness and other symptoms associated with the condition.
		2. Immunosuppressive therapy: In addition to corticosteroids, immunosuppressive agents may be prescribed to further suppress the immune system and control inflammation in patients with myositis. These medications help prevent the immune system from attacking healthy muscle tissue.

		Speech therapy and occupational therapy, while valuable interventions for certain conditions, are not typically primary treatments for myositis. Speech therapy primarily focuses on improving communication and swallowing function in individuals with speech and swallowing disorders, while occupational therapy aims to help patients regain independence in performing daily activities and improve quality of life.

		Therefore, the correct treatments for patients with myositis among the options provided are:
		Steroids (prednisone)
		Immunosuppressive therapy

.Knowledge Checker
	A patient that has scleroderma would have which of the following clinical manifestations?
		a) Fatigue, thick skin with loss of wrinkles, and skin thickening fingers extending proximal to MCP joint
		b) Fatigue, hip fracture, and height loss
		c) Fatigue, bone pain, bone deformity, fracture, and impaired functional status

	(Γ)
	a) A patient with scleroderma typically presents with clinical manifestations such as:
	Fatigue: Scleroderma often causes fatigue, which can significantly impact the patient's quality of life.
	Thick skin with loss of wrinkles: One of the hallmark features of scleroderma is skin thickening, which can lead to a loss of skin elasticity and the disappearance of wrinkles.
	Skin thickening fingers extending proximal to MCP joint: Scleroderma can cause thickening of the skin on the fingers, extending beyond the metacarpophalangeal (MCP) joints, known as sclerodactyly.

	Based on these clinical manifestations, the correct option is:
	Fatigue, thick skin with loss of wrinkles, and skin thickening fingers extending proximal to MCP joint

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 3 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-Welcome to Module 3! This module shifts the focus from the autoimmune system to the nervous system. You will be learning about neurologic disorders throughout the next five modules. 
-This module will provide an overview of the development of neurologic disorders. The stroke is one of several neurologic disorders you will learn about in this module. In particular, you will learn about the distinct acute phase and long-term management principles for stroke patients. 
-Part of learning about these neurologic disorders in this module will entail differentiating between the clinical management of cerebral territory and brainstem strokes. You will also practice developing the PT 2045 patient-centered skill of empathy in this module.
-Every year, more than 795,000 people in the United States have a stroke. About 610,000 of these incidents are first or new strokes. As a result, stroke is a leading cause of serious long-term disability. It reduces mobility in more than half of stroke survivors aged 65 and over (Montana Government, 2021). 
-Even though it is a leading cause of death among Americans, the risk of having a stroke varies according to race, ethnicity, and age.

('https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-NJm4TJ2it0')

Learning Objectives
	By the end of this module, you will be able to: 
		Compare and contrast the clinical management of cerebral territory and brainstem strokes. 
		Explain general acute phase and long-term management principles for stroke patients.  
		Develop a differential diagnosis and management plan for a patient with encephalopathy. 
		Explore empathy in a patient and PT setting. 


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
3.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-The list below contains key neurology-related terms that you will need to understand. These terms contain key roots, such as “taxis” and “delirare”, that will be repeated in other contexts throughout your career.

-Review the list below for important medical terminology and its etymology. 

Encephalopathy
	Definition: An umbrella term for any patient with an altered mental status. 
	Etymology: Latin ecncephalon “brain”, from Greek, in —  and kephalē "head" – pathy - suffering.
Delirium
	Definition: Clinical condition characterized by waxing and waning mental status changes that result in confusion, disorientation, or inattention. 
	Etymology: Latin delirare — “deviate” or “be deranged”. 
Infratentorial
	Definition: Of an area of the brain below the tentorium cerebelli, i.e. the cerebellum. 
	Etymology: Infra — “below”; tentorium — the extension of the dura mater separating the cerebellum from the occipital lobes. 
Ptosis 
	Definition: Drooping of upper eyelid. 
	Etymology: Greek piptein — "to fall". 
Ataxia
	Definition: Discoordination. 
	Etymology: Greek a- — “without”; taxis  — “order”.  
Ischemia 
	Definition: Lack of perfusion resulting in cell injury.
	Etymology: Greek iskhein — “keep back”; haima — “blood”.

As you study these diseases, pay close attention to the similarities and differences between clinical manifestations and when you should refer them to another healthcare professional.

3.3.2. Activity: Podcast Episode 1 & 2
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.If asked to describe what characteristics make an excellent PT, many would most likely not include humility at the top of the list. On the contrary, most would consider confidence and knowledge as more important attributes. There is however, a clear distinction between confidence and overconfident and knowledge and arrogance. 
-"Knowing what you don’t know" is the key to humility as a PT. The humble PT is not ashamed to admit ignorance and is not too proud to ask for help when a patient’s condition is outside of their knowledge. A study done by the Quebec Health Care System showed that medical providers who scored high on licensing examinations were more likely to request help from providers outside their expertise than those that scored poorly.

-For this assignment, you will listen to the first episode of the podcast series, “What does it mean to know what I don't know?.” 
-After listening to Episode 1, you might probably be wondering, “Why do I need to know this?” or “How is this relevant to me as a PT?” or “How will this help me achieve my long-term goal in the medical field?”  For this activity, you will listen to the second episode of the podcast series, “Know what you don’t know” which focuses on why you should care about being aware of knowing what you don’t know.

Watershed Infarcts (Q&A_WatershedInfarcts)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Unique Stroke
	-Watershed infarcts are a unique type of stroke in that they are not the patient’s primary problem. Patients do not come to the hospital with watershed strokes. They come to the hospital with severe septic shock or post-cardiac arrest complicated by watershed infarcts. 

Pathophysiology ('Watershed Infarcts')
	-Caused when oxygenated and nutrient Rich blood cannot adequately perfuse the more distal portions of the brain due to low blood pressure. 
	-The territories most at risk for this Ischemia are at the edges of the Arteries territories. 

Clinical exam 
	Stroke-like symptoms
	Unilateral in appearance
	vs.
	man in a barrel
		bilateral symptoms 
		proximal weakness
	visual impairment vs. Cortical blindness

Imaging findings ('Watershed Infarcts Watershed Infarcts.png')
	bilateral areas of ischemia   
	Symmetric edges of vascular territories

	Unilateral ischemic injury possible depending on collateral flow or intra-/extra-cranial stenosis

Treatment 
	reverse the cause of hypoperfusion supportive care

Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following best describes the cause of watershed infarcts?
		Infection
		Thrombus
		Low blood pressure
		Hemorrhage

		Low blood pressure

.Study Guide WATERSHED INFARCTS ('Watershed Infarcts Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Ischemic strokes caused by a deficit in blood, oxygen or glucose feeding the brain that does not involve a blockage of an artery, but instead, a global decrease in perfusion, usually caused by hypotension, or low blood pressure.   
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		Hypotension from any cause can lead to global cerebral ischemia and watershed infarcts.  The smallest and most distal arteries are the first ones to lose blood flow during a hypotensive episode.  Therefore, the areas of the brain that are most susceptible to watershed infarcts are those that are supplied by distal arterial branches.  
		i.	Most often infarcts are bilateral because the hypotension affects both sides the same
		ii.	However, if a patient has underlying stenosis, or narrowing, of a big artery, like the carotid artery, on only one side, that side will be more greatly affected by the hypotension than the side without stenosis.  In this case, the infarcts may be more pronounced on the side with the stenosis.
	3.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Bilateral involvement
		i.	Cortical blindness: Total or partial loss of vision in a normal eye due to damage to the cortex of the brain
		ii.	Proximal muscle weakness (Man in a barrel) – shoulders and hips
		B.	Unilateral involvement due to stenosis: 
		i.	Typical hemispheric specific symptoms – see stroke study guide for details
	4.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	MRI is the best test to diagnose watershed strokes.  It has a classic bilateral and scattered distribution.  Although any bilateral more distal pattern in the right clinical setting should be suspicious for watershed infarct.
		i.	Click here to scroll through an MRI with a watershed infarct. 
	5.	TREATMENT
		A.	Fix Hypotension
		B.	Typical stroke treatment – see stroke study guide for details 
	6.	PROGNOSIS
		The prognosis varies greatly depending on the cause of the hypotension that led to the watershed infarcts in the first place.  If a patient had transient reversible hypotension the prognosis may be pretty good.  However, if they had hypotension due to severe sepsis or cardiac arrest, they watershed strokes may be the least of their problems.
