Stroke (Q&A_Stroke)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Every 40 seconds...
	-According to the CDC, in the United States, someone has a stroke every 40 seconds and someone dies of a stroke every four minutes. This means that in the time it takes you to watch this video on stroke, 35 people will have had a stroke, and five people will have died from one. We all need to know the signs of a stroke.  

N/A 1
	2nd highest cause of mortality and disability Worldwide 
	Incidence of ischemic stroke is higher than hemorrhagic stroke 87% vs. 13%
	Men more common at a younger age but sex predominance evens out over time

Transient Ischemic vs. Stroke
	transient ischemic attack (TIA)
		Acute onset of neurological deficit from temporary ischemia of the brain, spinal cord or retina without evidence of infarct 
			Warning sign
	Stroke Γ(Ischemic Stroke)
		Infarction of the central nervous system

		AVM
		AV-fistula

Ischemic Stroke ('Ischemic Stroke')
	Blockage in an artery of the brain
	Hypoperfusion

	Inability for the blood carrying oxygen and nutrients to reach the brain tissue → cell death

	Pathophysiology Of Ischemic Stroke REPE¿?
		Blockage of artery deprives the distal brain tissue of glucose and oxygen →
			Cell death (Apoptosis & Necrosis) & Activation of inflammatory pathways →
				Loss of structural integrity of the blood vessels and tissue (Blood-Brain Barrier)
					Consequence → Hemorrhage into brain tissue
				Cerebral edema (Cytotoxic & Vasogenic edema)
					Consequence → Increased intracranial pressure (ICP), decreased cerebral blood flow (CBF), secondary injury

Hemorrhagic Stroke ('Hemorrhagic Stroke')
	Direct injury to the brain tissue from bleeding → increased intracranial pressure

	Hypertensive small penetrating vessels
		Deeper structures, pons, cerebellum
	Amyloid Angiopathy Congophilic deposits in vessel walls
		Lobar hemorrhages
	Aneurysmal Subarachnoid space (worst headache of life)

PRESENTATION
	Acute onset of focal neurological deficit
	Various levels of arousal 
	Hemorrhages → Headache, Nausea, Vomiting more common
	----------------------------------------------------

.Study Guide STROKE ('Stroke Study Guide.docx') ('Cervical Arterial Dissections and Association With Cervical  Manipulative Therapy.pdf')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Stroke is the second leading cause of morbidity and mortality worldwide
		Check out this video about Joe working with physical therapists after his left sided stroke. 
		A.	Transient Ischemic Attack (TIA): Acute neurological deficits caused by temporary ischemic of the brain, spinal cord, or retina without evidence of infarction. It’s a warning sign – like angina for the brain
		B.	Stroke: Infarction of the central nervous system
		i.	Ischemic: 87%
		1.	Embolic – Clot comes from somewhere else. Ex: Atrial fibrillation
		2.	Thrombotic – Clot develops in the involved vessel. Ex: carotid stenosis  plaque rupture and full occlusion 
		3.	Hypoperfusion – Low blood pressure causes reduced blood flow.  Ex: Watershed infarct
		ii.	Hemorrhagic: 13%
		1.	Direct hemorrhage of blood into the brain tissue itself from an artery rupture
		2.	Hypertension
		3.	Amyloid angiopathy 
		4.	Vascular malformations: Arteriovenous malformations (AVMs), arteriovenous fistulas (AV fistulas), aneurysms
	2.	RISK FACTORS
		The same for atherosclerosis:
		i.	Hypertension
		ii.	Diabetes 
		iii.	Hyperlipidemia
		iv.	Smoking
		v.	Heart disease: specifically atrial fibrillation or valvular disease
		vi.	Hypercoagulable state
		vii.	Alcohol and recreational drug use
	3.	PATHOGENESIS
		i.	Ischemic: Blockage of artery deprives distal brain tissue of glucose and oxygen which leads to cell death  dead cells release stress mediators that lead to secondary injury and inflammation 
		ii.	Hemorrhagic: Blood escapes from a vessel to surrounding brain tissue that leads to cell injury and death.  Blood is essentially toxic to cells  dead cells release stress mediators that lead to secondary injury and inflammation
		1.	Hypertension: Chronic htn causes some tears within the vessels wall forming small hemorrhages into the vessel lining.  Eventually, these vessels will give out and the wall will rupture causing a large intracerebral hemorrhage
		2.	Amyloid angiopathy: Deposition of congophilic agents into the vessel wall  blood vessel wall weakening  rupture
	4.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Depends on the area of brain that is involved.
		i.	Right Hemisphere: 
		1.	Left sided weakness
		2.	Dysarthria
		3.	Right gaze deviation
		4.	Visual field cuts 
		5.	Hemi-neglect
		ii.	Left Hemisphere:
		1.	Right sided weakness
		2.	Left gaze deviation 
		3.	Impaired speech processes 
		a.	Expressive aphasia (Broca’s aphasia): Loss of ability to produce language
		b.	 Receptive aphasia (Wernicke’s aphasia): Someone can speak well but their content doesn’t make any sense.  They may get frustrated that others cannot understand them. They may not understand the meaning of words
		iii.	Anterior Cerebral Artery (ACA): Loss of lower extremity motor and sensation
		iv.	Middle Cerebral Artery (MCA) Loss of potentially both upper and lower extremity motor and sensation.  Aphasia
		v.	Posterior Cerebral Artery (PCA) Loss of vision
		vi.	Cerebellum: Superior cerebellar artery (SCA) & Posterior-inferior cerebellar artery (PICA)
		1.	Dizzines
		2.	Nausea and vomiting
		3.	Ataxia: Lack of coordination of voluntary movements
		4.	Gait disturbances
	5.	NEUROANATOMY ('')
	6.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	NIH Stroke Scale 
		B.	CT Head: Differentiates between ischemic and hemorrhagic
		C.	CT Angiogram: Uses intravenous contrast to evaluate the major vessels of the brain for occlusion
		D.	MRI: Used to diagnose infarcts (brain death)
	7.	TREATMENT
		A.	Ischemic: 
		i.	TPA: Tenecteplase/Alteplase- clot busting medication given IV
		ii.	Thrombectomy in interventional radiology – physically removing clot
		iii.	Permissive hypertension: If patient not a candidate for tpa or thrombectomy, this involves letting the BP go as high as 220/110 to optimize cerebral perfusion through collateral blood vessels.  The idea is that the brain is smarter in determining the BP it needs to perfuse itself than we as providers are.  
		iv.	Secondary prevention
		1.	Antiplatelets: Aspirin +/- Plavix
		2.	Statin therapy 
		3.	Determining cause of stroke
		B.	Hemorrhagic
		i.	Blood pressure control: < systolic BP 140 (although there is ongoing debate in the literature about the “right” BP and it likely should be individualized for each patient
		ii.	If there is a vascular structural problem that can be fixed, like an AVM or aneurysm, can fix it either through interventional radiology or craniotomy
		C.	All patients benefit from early and aggressive physical therapy and rehabilitation.  The sooner patients can get therapy, the better their functional outcome tends to be. 
	8.	PROGNOSIS
		The prognosis with stroke varies based on the location of the stroke, the amount of involved brain tissue, and the resulting deficits.  Younger patients, even those with larger strokes, tend to have better functional recovery than older patients.

Lateral Medullary Syndrome (Q&A_LateralMedullarySyndrome)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Nystagmus
	-What do facial anesthesia, arm ataxia, and nystagmus have in common? They are all clinical manifestations of lateral medullary syndrome, a syndrome caused by an ischemic stroke of the posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA).  

N/A 1 
	Also called Wallenberg’s Syndrome or Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery (PICA) syndrome

Causes
	Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery (PICA) occlusion
		Γ('Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery (PICA)')
		Γ('Posterior-inferior-cerebellar-artery-PICA.png')
		Γ('Posterior-inferior-cerebellar-artery-PICA 2.png')          
	vs. 
	Vertebral Artery occlusion or dissection

	Take hx for Trauma

Symptoms 
	Horner’s syndrome ipsilateral (Descending sympathetic fibers)
	Ipsilateral loss of facial pain and temperature (CN5)
	Ipsilateral arm ataxia (Cerebellar connections)
	Vertigo/Nystagmus (vestibular nuclei & connections)
	Hoarseness/dysphagia (CN IX & X)
	Contralateral loss of pain and temperature on body (Spinothalamic tract)

Diagnosis Neurimaging
	MRI BRAIN ('MRI BRAIN')
		DWI restriction on MRI Brain within the lateral medulla
	CTA Head/Neck ('CTA Head Neck.png')
		May reveal vessel occlusion of the PICA or vertebral artery or vertebral artery dissection

Treatment
	Stroke Management
	Vertebral artery dissection Aspirin vs. Anticoagulation

Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following are clinical manifestations of lateral medullary syndrome? (Click all that apply).
		Horner's syndrome
		Drooping eyelid
		Loss of pain and body temperature
		Bone tenderness

		Horner's syndrome
		Drooping eyelid
		Loss of pain and body temperature

.Study Guide LATERAL MEDULLARY SYNDROME ('Lateral Medullary Syndrome Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Also called Wallenberg’s Syndrome or Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery (PICA) Syndrome.  
		It is a constellation of symptoms that result from a stroke to the lateral medulla, which is part of the brainstem.
		Check out this video, courtesy of the Cleveland Clinic, that interviews David Ford talking about living with lateral medullary syndrome. 
	2.	RISK FACTORS
		Same as for other strokes and atherosclerosis in general:
		i.	Hypertension
		ii.	Diabetes 
		iii.	Hyperlipidemia
		iv.	Smoking
		v.	Heart disease: specifically atrial fibrillation or valvular disease
		vi.	Hypercoagulable state
		vii.	Alcohol and recreational drug use
	3.	PATHOGENESIS
		This syndrome is caused by an arterial occlusion. 
		A.	Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery (PICA) Occlusion
		B.	Vertebral Artery 
		i.	Occlusion: From a thrombus
		ii.	Dissection:  an abrupt tear in the arterial wall.  If large enough, the tear can form a false lumen and fill up with blood.  Dissections cause occlusion either from filling up the false lumen so much that it blocks distal blood flow, or the tear itself induces the clotting cascade that leads to thrombus formation
		1.	Cervical manipulation can rarely result in vertebral artery dissection.  
		a.	Here is a brief article from UCSF 2003 that discusses the basics 
		b.	Please refer to the 2014 article “Cervical arterial dissections and association with cervical manipulative therapy: a statement for healthcare professionals from the American heart association/American stroke association in the “Additional Resources” for a more up to date and detailed review of the literature 
	4.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Horner’s Syndrome (ipsilateral): Injury of descending sympathetic fibers
		i.	Myosis (pupil constriction)
		ii.	Ptosis (eyelid drooping)
		iii.	Anhidrosis (loss of sweating on forehead)
		B.	Ipsilateral loss of facial sensation: Injury of descending spinal tract and nucleus of trigeminal nerve (CN V)
		i.	General loss of sensation
		ii.	Loss of temperature sensation
		iii.	Paradoxical increased sensitivity to tactile stimuli  
		C.	Ipsilateral arm ataxia (loss of coordination): Injury of cerebellar peduncle
		i.	Difficulty completing tasks with the involved arm  can’t aim to pick up objects, will overshoot target, movements jerky
		D.	Nausea/ Vomiting/Vertigo/Nystagmus: Injury to vestibular nuclei & connections in medulla
		i.	Nystagmus: involuntary rapid back and forth movement of the eye  can be either horizontal or vertical, but usually horizontal in patients with lateral medullary syndrome
		ii.	Vertigo: Feeling dizzy or off-balance; Will fall if they try to walk; Causes nausea and volume
		E.	Hoarseness/Dysphagia: Injury to nucleus ambiguous – CN IX and X
		F.	Contralateral loss of pain and temperature on body: Injury to spinothalamic tract, which is located laterally within brainstem and spinal cord.  It eventually crosses over to the other side of the body, which is why this finding occurs on the side contralateral to the stroke
	5.	NEUROANATOMY ('')
	6.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	The workup for LMS is the same as any suspected stroke
		i.	Rule out mimics like hypoglycemia
		ii.	CT head without contrast to assess for intracerebral hemorrhage
		iii.	CT angiogram to evaluate vessels  can see PICA or vertebral artery occlusion or vertebral artery dissection
		iv.	MRI is the best test to definitively diagnose lateral medullary syndrome.  
	7.	TREATMENT
		A.	Occlusion – Treat for stroke
		i.	If within window can give tpa
		B.	Vertebral artery dissection: treat with either aspirin or full anticoagulation with heparin
	8.	PROGNOSIS
		The prognosis in functional outcome for these patients is generally very good.  They can learn to adapt to their deficits and report a good quality of life.

Locked-In Syndrome (Q&A_Locked-InSyndrome)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

The Diving Bell and the Butterfly
	-Jean-Dominique Bauby, formerly the editor-in-chief of French Elle, suffered a devastating brainstem stroke that left him with locked-in syndrome at the age of 44. Left with his cognition and his ability to blink his left eye, he wrote a book titled The Diving Bell and the Butterfly ('the-diving-bell-and-the-butterfly.pdf'), one letter at a time. Thomas Mallon, in a book review for the New York Times, explains the title best, “The diving bell of Bauby's title is his corporeal trap, the butterfly his imagination.” As you watch the following video on locked-in syndrome, think of Jean-Dominique Bauby and the incredible gift he gave to us as both healthcare providers and humans.  

N/A 1
	Injury to the Pons. Patients are unable to move or speak but have preserved cortical function

Anatomy
	Pons ('Anatomy of the PONs.png')
	Midbrain

Causes
	Ischemic Stroke
	Hemorrhagic Stroke
	Trauma

EXAMINATION

Imaging
	CT/CTA Head & Neck 
	MRI Brain (¦ Ulleres de tubo per a MIRar)
	------------------
	('Imaging Locked-In Syndrome.png')
		Basilar Artery Occlusion ¦Basenilla ocluida
		Hemorrhagic Pons Stroke

Treatment 
	Ischemic stroke TPA (¦Tapa), Thrombectomy, Modifiable stroke risk factors 
	Hemorrhagic stroke Blood pressure control
	Trauma Supportive care

	Prevention of secondary injury

Prognostication 
	Greater than 10-year survival rate with some potential recovery 
	Decannulation, gastrostomy removal 
	Improvements in communication tools

Knowledge Checker
	A clinical manifestation of locked-in syndrome is:
		Acceptable responses: ischemic stroke, hemorrhagic stroke, trauma
			The medical manifestations of locked-in syndrome are ischemic stroke, hemorrhagic stroke, and trauma.

.Study Guide LOCKED-IN SYNDROME ('Locked -in Syndrome Study Guide.docx') ('Locked-In Syndrome_ Practical Rehabilitation Management.pdf')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Locked in syndrome is a debilitating neurological condition caused by damage to the lower brain – the midbrain and/or the brainstem that spares the upper brain (cortex).
		Check out this video about Nick Chisholm, who is living with locked-in syndrome, and his wife Nicola.  It is 29 minutes long.  Please do not feel obligated to watch the whole thing!
		A.	Types
		i.	Classic: Quadriplegia and anarthria with preserved vertical eye movement and consciousness
		ii.	Incomplete: A patient with locked-in syndrome that has more function than the classic definition  patients with improved function over time will become “incomplete”
		iii.	Total: Patients with retained consciousness but no movement ability, even vertical eye movements; Very difficult to differentiate from coma
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		Neuronal death in the ventral pons (most common – typically bilateral) or midbrain
		i.	Corticobulbar tract
		ii.	Corticospinal tract
	3.	CAUSES
		i.	Stroke – most common
		1.	Occlusion of basilar artery  ischemic stroke 
		2.	Hemorrhagic stroke
		ii.	Trauma
		iii.	Tumor
		iv.	Infection
	4.	NEUROANATOMY
		A.	Ventral pons
		i.	Corticospinal tracts  voluntary movement for the contralateral side of the body
		ii.	Corticobulbar tracts  facial and tongue movement and the act of chewing food
		iii.	Cranial Nerves IX, X, XI are also related to the corticobulbar pathways and injury causes inability for talking and swallowing
	5.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Loss of Function
		i.	Loss of voluntary muscles of limbs and torso
		ii.	Greatly decreased/lost sensation
		iii.	Bulbar muscle weakness: 
		1.	Anarthria – inability to speak
		2.	Dysphagia – difficulty swallowing
		iv.	Difficulty with voluntary breath
		1.	 Apnea -not taking breaths
		2.	Hyperpnea - taking deep breaths
		3.	Ataxic breathing – irregular breathing with pauses 
		v.	Development of dizziness or vertigo
		B.	Preserved Function
		i.	Vertical eye movements and blinking  tire quickly
		ii.	Preserved consciousness, language comprehension, orientation
		iii.	Hearing
	6.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Physical exam 
		B.	CT head or MRI brain
	7.	TREATMENT
		A.	Support of breathing with tracheostomy: 
		i.	Majority of patients will not be ventilator dependent forever
		ii.	Cough assist to prevent atelectasis and pneumonia
		B.	Nutritional support with gastrostomy tube:
		i.	Speech therapy to assist with oral motor control
		C.	Musculoskeletal support
		i.	Most patients will need a wheelchair
		ii.	Splinting for atonia
		iii.	Proper positioning to avoid pain and skin breakdown
		D.	Communication Support
		i.	Eyes up for yes, eyes closed for now
		ii.	Picture boards & AEIOU boards
		iii.	Computer based augmentative and alternative communication systems
	8.	PROGNOSIS
		Most patients (~70-80%) will regain some minimal motor function in a distal to proximal pattern.  The first areas to show recovery are usually the fingers and toes.  
		Around half of patients will be decannulated from their tracheostomy and ~20% of those will regain the ability to speak.  Most other patients use another type of communication aid.  Only 25% of patients will use eye movements as their sole method of communication.
		Most patients will require life-long feeding tube.
		Despite the challenges of living with locked-in syndrome, most patients report they are happy and satisfied with their life.

Bell's Palsy (Q&A_BellsPalsy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Charming Grin
	-Angelina Jolie and George Clooney have both had Bell’s palsy. In fact, Clooney considers his charming side grin as a residual deficit from Bell’s palsy.    

Objectives
	Recognize bell’s palsy disease and its pathophysiology
	Distinguish bell’s palsy from other differentials 
	Describe treatment options

N/A 1
	Cranial Nerve VII Paralysis 
	Acute onset 
	Most common cause Herpes Simplex 
	Risk factors Pregnancy

Presentation
	Inability to close eye
	Facial droop on affected side
	Flattening of nasiolabial fold
	Eyebrow sagging
	Forehead included
	Taste can be affected 
	Hyperacusis

Localizing the lesion
	Internal auditory meatus → Motor weakness, Loss of taste, tinnitus, loss of tearing
	Facial Canal → Motor weakness & Taste anterior 2/3 tongue
	Stylomastoid foramen → Isolated motor weakness
	---------------------------------------------------------
	('Bells Palsy Localizing the lesion.png')
		Internal Auditory Meatus → Lacrimal gland
			Facial Canal → Taste buds Anterior 2/3 tongue
				Stylomastoid Foramen
		Somatic Motor, Vestibulocochlear Nerve, Greater Petrosal Nerve, Chorda Tympani

Diagnosis
	diagnosis is clinical
	look for red flags!!

Differentiating between Bell’s Palsy & Stroke
	The forehead

Treatment 
	Steroids
	+/Antivirals (acyclovir/valacyclovir)
	Eye care

Knowledge Checker
	Match the treatment on the left to the correct description to the right.
		(+/-)antivirals
		eye care
		steroids

		typically for a duration of one week
		prescribed in case of severe facial Bell's palsy
		taping eye closed and maintaining lubricant on eye

		-------------------

		(+/-) antivirals - prescribed in case of severe facial Bell's palsy
		eye care - taping eye closed and maintaining lubricant on eye
		steroids - typically for a duration of one week

.Study Guide BELLS PALSY ('Bells Palsy Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		It is a syndrome resulting from the paralysis of cranial nerve VII
		Check out this video, courtesy of the Loyola Medicine, that interviews Audrey REx talking about her experience with Bell’s Palsy. 
	2.	CAUSES
		Believed to be caused usually by a viral infection  most commonly herpes simplex (HSV)
		i.	Tumor compression
		ii.	Lyme disease
		iii.	Pregnancy significant risk factor for developing Bell’s Palsy
	3.	PATHOGENESIS
		This syndrome is caused by inflammation and swelling of CNVII  causes compression of the nerve as it travels through narrow pathway from the internal auditory meatus facial canal  stylomastoid canal
		i.	Compression of nerve leads to loss of function 
		ii.	The specific location of compression along this pathway will determine individual patient symptoms
	4.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Acute onset – typically hours – of facial weakness.  Lower motor neuron problem
		i.	Can differentiate between lower and upper motor neuron etiology of facial weakness.
		1.	Lower motor neuron (Bell’s Palsy): involvement of forehead ('Bells Palsy VS. Stroke.png')
		2.	Upper motor neuron (Stroke): spares the forehead
		ii.	Visible findings of facial weakness
		1.	Inability to close eye
		2.	Flattening of nasolabial fold
		3.	Eyebrow sagging
		B.	May have ear pain that develops prior to development of weakness
		C.	Loss of tearing 
		D.	Loss of taste
		E.	Hyperacusis – sensitivity to everyday sounds
	5.	NEUROANATOMY
		Facial Nerve Branches.  Responsible for:
		i.	Facial movement, including eyelid closure
		ii.	Taste in anterior 2/3 of tongue
		iii.	Tear and saliva production
	6.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Clinical diagnosis – history and physical exam 
		i.	Should be a complete CNVII palsy
		ii.	If there are unusual findings, should make you suspicious of something else going on
		1.	Duration of onset: did this occur over weeks rather than hours
		2.	Lack of resolution of symptoms in first 3 months
		3.	History makes you suspicious for lyme disease
	7.	TREATMENT
		A.	Steroids: reduce inflammation and therefore nerve compression  typically for 1 week
		B.	Antiviral therapy (Acyclovir, Valcyclovir): For severe cases or those where HSV is the suspected cause
		C.	Eye care: unable to close eye  at risk for dry eye and corneal injury
		i.	Artificial tears
		ii.	Taping eye closed, especially at night
		D.	Physical therapy if symptoms persist
		i.	Massage
		ii.	Facial Stim
		iii.	Exercises
		E.	Botox injections – patients with synkinesia (involuntary movement of paralyzed muscle accompanying voluntary movement elsewhere)
		F.	Decompressive therapy for severe and persistent cases (like Audrey)
	8.	PROGNOSIS
		70-80% of patients will have complete recovery – typically within a month

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Encephalopathy (Q&A_Encephalopathy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Encephalopathy
	-Encephalopathy is a catch-all term used to describe a patient not acting like themselves, suffering from what is most often referred to as “altered mental status" (AMS). A clinical manifestation of this disease is confusion. If you plan to work in the hospital, you will be caring for patients with encephalopathy.

-"Encephalopathy" means damage or disease that affects the brain. It happens when there's been a change in the way your brain works or a change in your body that affects your brain. Those changes lead to an altered mental state, leaving you confused and not acting like you usually do.
-The term 'encephalopathy' is used broadly to refer to a wide range of brain illnesses. These disorders can be caused by a wide range of factors, and can have a wide range of symptoms, treatments, and costs.

Encephalopathy
	Delirium
	Confusion

Causes
	Not due to primary brain disease
		Sepsis 
		Hepatic Encephalopathy
		Uremia 
		Electrolyte Disturbances 
		Hypoand Hyper-glycemia 
		Wernicke’s encephalopathy 
		Hypoxic Ischemic insult
		Transplant related 
		Hypertensive
	Primary Brain involvement
		Intracranial mass
		Meningitis/Encephalitis 
		Post-Traumatic Encephalopathy 
		Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy syndrome

Clinical Presentation
	Inattention
	Hyperarousal vs. coma 
	Tremor, Asterixis, primitive reflexes, posturing 
	Seizure 
	Cardiac irregularity

Encephalopathy Syndromes
	Wernicke’s Encephalopathy 
		Severe Thiamine deficiency leading to neuronal injury
		Alcoholics, malabsorption syndromes
		Confusion, oculomotor palsies, ataxic gait
		High dose Thiamine
	PRES
		Severe HTN, Immunosuppresive agents leading to cerebral edema
		Confusion, Visual disturbances, Seizures 
		MRI Increased cerebral edema on T2 Flair specifically of parietal occipital lobes
		Control of Blood pressure, removal of immunosuppressive agents
	Chronic Traumatic Encephalopathy
		Repeated Brain injury 
		Confusion, personality changes, gait
			Disturbances
		Abnormal hyperphosphorylated TAU protein
	HSV Encephalitis
		HSV-1 infection of the brain
		Confusion, Fever, Seizures 
		MRI Increased T2 hyperintensities of the mesiotemporal lobes
		PCR HSV-1 in CSF gold standard
		IV Acyclovir

Work-up
	Mental status and Neurological exam
	Comprehensive laboratory work-up 
	HCT/MRI Brain 
	EEG 
	Lumbar Puncture

Treatment 
	Fix reversible causes
	Avoid interventions or medications that can worsen delirium 
	Thiamine for pertinent patients

Knowledge Checker
	If a patient has encephalopathy, which clinical manifestation will they most likely show? 
		Altered mental status
		Seizure
		Hemiparesis
		Cardiac irregularity

		Altered mental status
			Even if the symptom is confused but has hemiparesis, it is a stroke symptom not encephalopathy.

.Study Guide ENCEPHALOPATHY ('Encephalopathy Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		General clinical condition of confusion and disturbed cognition with a variety of causes.  It is an umbrella term for any patient with disturbed mental status.
	2.	CAUSES
		A.	Global
		i.	Sepsis - #1 cause
		ii.	Metabolic
		1.	Electrolyte abnormalities – sodium, calcium magnesium
		2.	Glucose abnormalities – hyperglycemia ( high) or hypoglycemia (low)
		3.	Uremia – increased BUN from kidney disease
		4.	Elevated ammonia (NGH3) level – liver disease – also called hepatic encephalopathy
		5.	Thiamine deficiency (Wernicke’s Encephalopathy)  if untreated can cause primary brain lesions 
		6.	Severe range hypertension (Hypertensive emergency)
		iii.	Toxic
		1.	Alcohol
		B.	Primary Brain Involvement
		i.	Intracranial mass
		ii.	Infection
		1.	Meningitis: infection of meninges
		2.	Encephalitis: infection of brain parenchyma
		iii.	Traumatic brain injury
		iv.	Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome (PRES)
	3.	PATHOGENESIS
		A.	Metabolic: disruption of homeostasis that impairs the brain’s pathways that contribute to awareness and cognition
	4.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		The clinical picture is broad.  The patient’s behavior, cognition, and personality is very different from his/her/their baseline. 
		A.	Inattention
		B.	Hyperarousal or Decreased arousal
		C.	Tremor/Asterixis in some 
		D.	Seizure (not always clinically visible)
	5.	EXAMPLES
		A sampling of different types of encephalopathy.  Remember that sepsis, which is an infection with systemic symptoms, is the most common cause of encephalopathy, followed by other metabolic abnormalities.  The following examples provide other potential etiologies for encephalopathy, but none of these are especially common.  
		A.	Wernicke’s Encephalopathy: 
		i.	Definition: Syndrome caused by severe thiamine deficiency  neuronal injury
		ii.	Risk Factors: Alcohol use disorder, Hyperemesis of pregnancy, Malabsorption syndrome (gastric bypass), Poor nutritional intake (anorexia)
		iii.	Clinical manifestations: confusion, nystagmus, bilateral CN VI palsy, ataxic gait
		1.	Can progress to coma  death if untreated
		iv.	Diagnosis: Clinical, can have classic lesions on MRI, but not necessary to make the diagnosis
		v.	Treatment: High dose thiamine intravenously
		vi.	Prognosis: Fully reversible if treated early

		B.	Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome (PRES)
		i.	Definition: Syndrome with very specific pattern of lesions on MRI (occipital lobe)
		ii.	Causes: Severe hypertension, including pre-eclampsia of pregnancy, immunosuppressant agents (transplant and cancer patients)
		iii.	Clinical manifestations: Confused, agitated, Visual disturbances (diminished or blind), New onset seizures
		iv.	Diagnosis: MRI - increased hyperintensity of white matter in parietooccipital lobs 
		v.	Treatment: 
		1.	Blood pressure control
		2.	Removing offending agent or decreasing dose
		vi.	Prognosis: Usually fully reversible if treated early

		C.	Chronic Traumatic Encephalopathy
		i.	Definition: Syndrome of cognitive disfunction due to repeated brain injury (repeat concussions)
		ii.	Clinical manifestations: Changes to personality  increased aggression, gait disturbances, depression
		iii.	Diagnosis: Clinical.  Abnormal hyperphosphorylated TAU proteins seen on autopsy
		iv.	Treatment: Supportive

		D.	HSV Encephalitis
		i.	Definition: Viral encephalitis caused by HSV 1 that causes immune mediated responses in brain  tissue injury
		ii.	Clinical manifestations: Abnormal behavior, altered mental status  headache, fever, seizures
		iii.	Diagnosis: 
		1.	Lumbar puncture: pleocytosis (increased white blood cell count), + PCR HSV-1 of spinal fluid (can get false negative if tested too early)
		2.	MRI: Hyperintensity of mesiotemporal lobes
		iv.	Treatment: Antivirals (acyclovir/valcyclovir)
	6.	GENERAL WORKUP
		A.	Thorough history
		B.	Laboratory studies
		i.	Comprehensive metabolic panel: Electrolytes, glucose, BUN, liver function
		ii.	CBC: evidence of sepsis
		iii.	Tox screen
		C.	Neuroimaging: CT, MRI
		D.	Electroencephalogram (EEG)
		E.	Lumbar puncture
	7.	TREATMENT
		A.	Treat underlying cause
		B.	Delirium prevention in hospital
		i.	Encourage sleep/wake cycles
		ii.	Avoid delirium inducing medications  benzodiazepines (lorazepam)
		iii.	Frequent reorientation  “Hello Mr. L.  It is Friday, May 14th.  It is 9am in the morning.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 4 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////		

Disabilities have often appeared in medieval religious art. One of these examples is a painting of figures from El Burgo de Osma Cathedral in Spain ('https://burgodeosmacatedral.com') to an external site., where it depicts humans with abnormal neck positions which have been assumed to be dystonic postures.  

Although it wouldn’t be known as dystonia until many centuries later, it would be described as a disease where “the neck was not able to bend in any other direction without also bending the shoulder” or the “necks of those suffering from melancholy would suddenly thrust or twist backward causing marvel like experiences.”

We are in a period where there is still a lot to be learnt and understood about dystonia. However, compared to what was previously known about dystonia, much has been learnt on how to improve the quality of life for patients suffering from it. 

Welcome to Module 4! This module will provide an overview of additional neurologic diseases. This week will be different in that most diseases studied will have similar clinical manifestations. It can be easy to confuse one disease with another if not aware of the differences between them.  

Part of learning about the differences between the neurologic diseases studied in this module will consist of you creating a basic physical therapy plan for a patient with a neurodegenerative disease, and later reflecting on the experience of patient’s living with a neurogenerative disease. 

You will also be listening to the first episode of the “know what I don’t know” podcast series.  

Learning Objectives
	By the end of this week, you will be able to: 

	Compare and contrast the clinical manifestations and prognosis of the neurodegenerative diseases for this week.
	Develop a basic physical therapy plan for a patient with each of the neurodegenerative diseases for this week.
	Reflect on the patient's experience of having one of the neurodegenerative diseases for this week.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
4.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Bradykinesia 
	Definition: Slow movement.
	Etymology: brady - slow kinēsis "movement, motion".
Autonomic 
	Definition: Involuntary functions. 
	Etymology: Pertaining to self-governance. 
	Greek “Autos” - self, “nomos” law “-ic” pertaining to.
Aphasia 
	Definition: Impairment of the ability to form or understand speech. 
	Etymology: “a” - without; “phas/o” speech; “-ia” - condition. 
Paresthesia 
	Definition: Feeling of prickling, burning, or numbness. 
	Etymology: “para” - abnormal; “esthesi/o” - feeling; “-ia” condition. 
Hemiparesis 
	Definition: Muscular weakness on the left or right side of the body.
	Etymology: “hemi” - half; “-paresis” slight paralysis.
Hemiplegia  
	Definition: Paralysis on the left or right side of the body. 
	Etymology: “hemi” - half; “-plegia” - paralysis.
Paraplegia 
	Definition: Paralysis on the lower limbs and trunk.
	Etymology: “para” - abnormal; “-plegia” - paralysis.
Quadriplegia 
	Definition: Paralysis of arms, legs, and trunk.
	Etymology: “quadri” - four; “-plegia” - paralysis.

As you study these diseases, pay close attention to the similarities and differences between clinical manifestations and, when you should refer them to another healthcare professional.

4.4. Reflection: Perception of Disease (In Class) (See Q&A)

-Often one creates a perception of a disease based solely on the science behind why the disease occurs. These assumptions can cause you to become less empathetic towards what your patients are experiencing physically, mentally, and emotionally. Which, in turn, can result in less overall improvement of the healing process for patients. 
-For this assignment, you will work in your breakout rooms. Your group will be assigned one or two diseases and must watch the associated videos on the study guides highlighting patient experiences with the neurodegenerative diseases we are learning about in this module. 
	Group 1 & 2: Parkinson’s Parkinson’s
	Group 3 & 4: Essential Tremor

	Group 7 & 8 : Dystonia
	Group 9 & 10: Huntingon’s and Friedreich’s Ataxia
-After watching the associated video(s), you will complete the reflections with your group to discuss your findings.

	-Your response should be 200 to 300 words. 
	-Your response should pose critical questions, highlight information and ideas from the content, and critically reflect on your relevant personal experience.

Match the term with the correct definition.
	1.Friedreich's Ataxia
	2.Dystonia
	3.Huntington's Disease
	4.Parkinson's Disease
	5.Multiple Systems Atrophy
	6.Oculomotor abnormalities
	7.Essential Tremor
	8.Progressive Supranuclear Palsy

	-a.Partial or complete paralysis of CN III, IV, and/or VI with various causes including ischemia masses, infection and trauma.
	-b.Progressive neurodegenerative disease of the basal ganglia pons medulla and cerebellum characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms and early onset of autonomic dysfunction.
	-c.The most common type of action tremor. It's a moderate to high frequency tremor of varying amplitude (amplitude gets bigger with activity) that is both postural and kinetic.
	-d.Sustained or periodic contraction of a muscle group causing abnormal movements or postures.
	-e.Progressive neurodegenerative disease caused by destruction of the dopamine secreting neurons of the substantia nigra in the basal ganglia resulting in, most notably, tremor bradykinesia
	-f.Neurodegenerative disease characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms ocular symptoms and frontal lobe involvement like disinhibition and abulia.
	-g.Genetically inherited autosomal recessive disease that causes progressive muscle discoordination and weakness, cardiomyopathy and diabetes.
	-h.Genetically inherited autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disease characterized by choreiform movements, dementia and psychiatric illness.

1. Friedreich's Ataxia
   -g.Genetically inherited autosomal recessive disease that causes progressive muscle discoordination and weakness, cardiomyopathy and diabetes.

2. Dystonia
   d.Sustained or periodic contraction of a muscle group causing abnormal movements or postures.

3. Huntington's Disease
   -h.Genetically inherited autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disease characterized by choreiform movements, dementia and psychiatric illness.

4. Parkinson's Disease
   -e.Progressive neurodegenerative disease caused by destruction of the dopamine secreting neurons of the substantia nigra in the basal ganglia resulting in, most notably, tremor bradykinesia

5. Multiple Systems Atrophy
   -b.Progressive neurodegenerative disease of the basal ganglia pons medulla and cerebellum characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms and early onset of autonomic dysfunction.

6. Oculomotor abnormalities
   a.Partial or complete paralysis of CN III IV and/or VI with various causes including ischemia masses, infection and trauma.

7. Essential Tremor
   -c.The most common type of action tremor. It's a moderate to high frequency tremor of varying amplitude (amplitude gets bigger with activity) that is both postural and kinetic.

8. Progressive Supranuclear Palsy
   -f.Neurodegenerative disease characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms ocular symptoms and frontal lobe involvement like disinhibition and abulia.

Parkinson's Disease (Q&A_ParkinsonsDisease)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Parkinson's effects
	-Today, Parkinson’s disease affects more than 200,000 Americans per year.  

	-It usually begins with a tremor in one hand then increases to other symptoms such as slow movement, stiffness, and loss of balance. -Although, the condition can be treated it can’t be cured. However, there have been efforts to decrease the effects of the disease such as treating disabling tremors.   

N/A 1 ('') Γ('striatum Substantia nigra.png')
	The Pathways

.Knowledge Checker

		A 75- year-old female has been having difficulty finding balance while walking. She tends to fall backward.

.('Parkinsons Study Guide.docx')
Parkinson's Disease
	1.	DEFINITION
		-Progressive neurodegenerative disease caused by destruction of the dopamine secreting neurons of the substantia nigra located in the basal ganglia
		-Dopamine: Neurotransmitter responsible for regulating voluntary movement and various behavioral components of personality → stimulating response
		-Basal ganglia: Collection of structures in the brain that help facilitate the act of intentional movement
		-Alpha-synuclein: Intracellular protein responsible for neurotransmitter release in brain
		Check out this video, where people with Parkinson’s talk about the symptoms of their disease that make them the most embarrassed. ('https://healthtalk.org/experiences/parkinsons-disease/embarrassing-symptoms-of-parkinsons-disease/')
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		The pathogenesis of Parkinson’s Disease is not well understood but is thought to include two key mechanisms. The first is a loss of dopamine releasing cells in the substantia nigra. The second is Lewy pathology, which is the finding of Lewy bodies, or clumps of the protein, in the brains of patients with Parkinson’s disease
		A.	Programed apoptosis (cell death) leads to a decrease in dopamine.
		i.	Unclear cause, likely multifactorial: genetic, environmental exposure, immune factors
		ii.	Decreased dopamine causes inhibition of the direct and indirect pathways for the initiation of voluntary movement → makes it harder for patients to contract their muscles → bradykinesia
		B.	Lewy Pathology: 
		i.	Alpha-synuclein protein normally found in the cytoplasm of cells becomes deformed and forms clumps called lewy bodies or alpha-synuclein inclusions
		ii.	These inclusions lead to cell death and surrounding inflammation
	3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		A.	Tremor
			i.	Usually presents in one upper extremity first, but eventually becomes bilateral
			ii.	More pronounced at rest and improves with voluntary movement
			iii.	Described as “pill rolling tremor”
			iv.	Here is a video demonstrating pill rolling tremor. 
		B.	Bradykinesia (“Slowed movement”)
			i.	Difficulty initiating movement
			ii.	Difficulty performing ADLs and walking
			iii.	Shuffling gait
				1.	Here is a video demonstrating shuffling gait and postural abnormalities ('Moderate and Severe Parkinsonian Gait.mp4')
			iv.	Evaluate the patient’s ability to do rapid movements like finger tapping
		C.	Rigidity
			i.	Resistance with passive range of motion, typically in a staccato fashion, also known as “cogwheel rigidity.”
			ii.	Here is a video demonstrating rigidity.  
		D.	Postural instability
			i.	Off balance → can’t initiate muscle contractions to reestablish balance
			ii.	Here is an example of the pull test. 
			iii.	Here is a video of a patient doing toe raises to improve his balance (you’ll also notice the improvement of his tremor with movement) ('Parkinson's, Balance and the Alexander Technique.mp4')
		E.	Masked facies
			i.	Loss of facial expression
			ii.	Here is a video demonstrating facial masking
		F.	Speech difficulties
			i.	Reduced volume
			ii.	Monotone pitch
			iii.	Hoarseness
		G.	Stooped posture
		H.	Autonomic dysfunction
		I.	Dementia
	4.	NEUROANATOMY ('')
	5.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Clinical Diagnosis → no need for imaging 
		i.	Bradykinesia + rigidity or tremor + 2 supportive characteristics
		ii.	No red flags at time of diagnosis → would make you think about MSA instead
		1.	Rapidly progressive gait issues
		2.	Respiratory dysfunction
		3.	Autonomic dysfunction
		4.	Bulbar dysfunction: difficulty swallowing, drooling, diminished gag reflex
	6.	TREATMENT
		A.	Purpose of treatment is symptom relief → they DO NOT prevent disease progression
		1.	Pharmacologic
		a.	Early in disease: Monoamine Oxidase Inhibitors (MAOI) (Selegiline)
		b.	Later in disease
		i.	< 65y/o: Dopamine agonists (pramipexole, ropinirole)
		ii.	≥ 65 y/o: Carbidopa-Levodopa
		2.	Non-pharmacologic
		a.	Deep brain stimulation → severe tremor or bradykinesia
		i.	Here is a UPMC webpage with more information
		b.	Physical therapy
		i.	CPG:Physical Therapist Management of Parkinson’s Disease. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9046970/
		ii.	Parkinson’s Disease Treatment Toolkit – APTA: https://neuropt.org/practice-resources/anpt-clinical-practice-guidelines/pt-management-of-parkinson-disease
		iii.	Outcome Measure Recommendations: https://neuropt.org/docs/default-source/parkinson-edge/recommendations-for-patients.pdf?sfvrsn=890d5543_0
		iv.	Communication with the medical team regarding changes in mobility are important. 
		v.	Consider the timing of medications as you observe mobility. 
		Patient’s function may vary with peaks and troughs of the medication. 
		c.	Occupational therapy
		i.	Here is a webpage reviewing the types of things OTs will work on with Parkinson’s patients
		d.	Speech therapy
		i.	Here is a webpage by the Cleveland Clinic that reviews speech therapy techniques and communication devices 
	7.	PROGNOSIS
		Parkinson’s disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disease with no significant treatments to slow down the progression. There are 5 stages of Parkinson’s symptoms. To learn more about these 5 stages, visit this webpage. With disease progression, symptoms become worse and more resistant to treatment. Eventually patients will no longer be able to perform their ADLs, have significant dysphagia which makes eating challenging, suffer from recurrent falls, and eventually develop dementia. Many patients with advanced Parkinson’s are placed in facilities because properly caring for these patients and ensuring their safety becomes too challenging for families. Patients with Parkinson’s disease also have a higher mortality. From the time of diagnosis, the average length of time patients have is between 7 to 14 years. Keeping these patients feeling positive, motivated, and in control of their disease (as best we can) is critical for maintaining the quality of life of these patients.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Multiple System Atrophy (Q&A_MultipleSystemAtrophy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Loss of Motor Functions
	Multiple system atrophy (MSA) is a rare disease that affects about 15,000 to 50,000 Americans, including men and women from various racial groups. 

	It tends to affect people in their 50’s and advances rapidly over the course of 5-10 years with increased loss of motor function and eventually confinement to bed. A difference between MSA and Parkinson's is that patients with MSA typically show symptoms at a younger age in the early 50's.

Pathophysiology ('')
	Inclusion cells within the cytoplasm of glial cells 
		Aggregated alpha-synuclein 
		Oligodendrocytes
	Inclusion cells cause:  
		Myelin destruction
		Cell death
		Inflammatory cascade
	Location:
		Basal Ganglia 
		Pons
		Medulla 
		Cerebellum

Onset 
	Age 50-60 years old 
	Equivalent male: female predominance 
	Rapid progression 6-10 years prognosis

	Multiple Systems Atrophy
		Olivopontocerebellar Atrophy
		Striatonigral Degeneration
		Shy-Drager Syndrome

Symptoms
	Parkinsonian symptoms Bradykinesia , rigidity, postural instability 
	Autonomic dysfunction postural hypotension, urinary incontinence, erectile dysfunction 
	Cerebellar symptoms wide-based gait, ataxia, coordination difficulty
	Psychological symptomsDepression, anxiety, emotional lability
	Respiratory symptomsOSA, Respiratory stridor 

MSA Subtypes
	MSA-P
		Parkinsonian features predominate 
		Little, no, or transient response to levodopa
		Dystonic facial movements in response to levodopa 
		“Pisa syndrome” posture 
		Anterocollis
		Raynaud’s
	MSA-C
		Gait instability 
		Wide based gait 
		Difficulty with coordination 
		Slower progression of disease process

Diagnosis
	Pathology is the Gold standard for diagnosis
	---------------------------------------------
	Levodopa challenge
	High dose trial to avoid false negatives 
	Minimal, transient or no response rules out Parkinson’s Disease

Clinical Diagnosis
	Sporadic, progressive onset in adulthood
	Autonomic Failure 
	Resistance to levodopa OR classic cerebellar symptoms

Diagnostic ('')
	MRI Brain Not diagnostic “Hot Crossed Buns” sign 
		OR
			Putamen, middle cerebellar peduncle and pons atrophy 
	EMG Rectal EMGs painful can suggest MSA if chronic reinnervation with prolonged motor units

Treatment
	Treatment focuses on symptom, medication shown slow progression
		Autonomic Dysfunction:
			1. Fludrocortisone
			2. Midodrine vs. Droxidopa
		Urinary Symptoms:
			1. Overactive Bladder Oxybutin
			2. Retention Straight catherization
		Respiratory Symptoms:
			1. CPAP
			2. Botulism injections
			3. Tracheostomy Physical Therapy

MSA vs. Parkinson's Disease 
	MSA
		Pathologically Glial inclusion cells with alpha-synuclein 
		Symptoms more rapid progression, 
		Autonomic symptoms early 
		Tremor less common 
		Emotional lability, less cognitive decline 
	Parkinson’s Disease 
		Pathologically Lewey Bodies 
		Symptoms Unilateral tremor onset, autonomic changes late finding
		Cognitive decline

.Knowledge Checker
	Multiple Systems Atrophy
		Respiratory symptoms
		sleeping abnormalities such as snoring, apnea, stridor, and acting out dreams
		Anxiety, depression, emotional lability
	Parkinson's Disease
		tremor
		Typically develops dementia
		often described as "masked facies"
		decreased blink reflex
		shows symptoms at a younger age, with the average of 50's
	Both
		difficulty initiating movement
		poor coordination
		body stiffness

.('Multiple System Atrophy Syndrome Study Guide.docx')
MULTIPLE SYSTEM ATROPHY SYNDROME
	1.DEFINITION
		Progressive neurodegenerative disease that results in inflammation and cell death in the basal ganglia, pons, medulla, and cerebellum characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms and early onset of autonomic dysfunction.
		Basal ganglia: Collection of structures in the brain that help facilitate the act of intentional movement
		Oligodendrocytes: A type of glial cell that produces myelin
		Alpha-synuclein: Intracellular protein responsible for neurotransmitter release in brain
		Check out this video which is an overview of MSA with brief patient interviews.
	2.PATHOGENESIS
		The pathogenesis of MSA is not well understood, but likely involves a genetic predisposition, which leads to abnormal structure of alpha-synuclein proteins that causes them to clump together and form inclusions.
			A.	Accumulation of aggregated alpha-synuclein inclusion in the cytoplasm of glial cells, particularly oligodendrocytes
			B.	The alpha-synuclein inclusions → myeline destruction → cell death → surrounding inflammatory response → additional cell injury to surrounding cells
			C.	This process occurs in the basal ganglia, pons, medulla, and cerebellum
	3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		This disease typically manifests in patients 50-60 years old. It often will present with Parkinsonian symptoms, but a hallmark difference between Parkinson’s and MSA is the rapid development of autonomic dysfunction early in the disease. There are two subtypes of MSA categorized by their predominant constellation of symptoms: MSA-P (Parkinsonian) and MSA-C (Cerebellar)
			A.	Parkinsonian symptoms
				i.	Bradykinesia
				ii.	Rigidity
				iii. Postural instability
			B.	Autonomic dysfunction ¦Casi tot te que vore amb la pelila
				i.	Postural hypotension → syncope
				ii.	Urinary symptoms: incontinence or nocturia (increased voiding at night)
				iii. Erectile dysfunction
			C.	Cerebellar symptoms
				i.	Wide based gait
				ii.	Ataxia
				iii. Coordination difficulty
			D.	Psychological symptoms: Depression, anxiety, emotional lability
			E.	Respiratory symptoms:
				i.	Obstructive sleep apnea
				ii.	Stridor → due to laryngeal dystonia
			F.	Speech changes → can sound like they are under water, garlging
	4.	SUBTYPES
		A.	MSA-P (Parkinsonian)
			i.	Bradykinesia, rigidity, postural instability → levodopa does not help or even worsens symptoms
			ii.	Raynaud’s
			iii. “Pisa syndrome” posture → patients lean to one side
			iv.	Anterocollis (Forward flexed neck)
		B.	MSA-C
			i. Gait instability → wide based gait
			ii.	Difficulty coordination
			iii. Tend to have slower progression of disease
	5.DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Clinical Diagnosis 
			i.	Poor response or no response to levodopa
			ii.	Autonomic failure
			iii. Cerebellar symptoms
		B.	Gold standard → finding Alpha-synuclein inclusions on pathology
		C.	MRI brain not required 
			i.	Hot Crossed Buns sign Γ('Hot Crossed Buns sign')
	6.TREATMENT
		Purpose of treatment is symptom relief → they DO NOT prevent disease progression
		A.	Autonomic dysfunction: 
			i.	Fludrocortisone or midodrine
			ii.	Compression stockings
		B.	Urinary Symptoms → mediation to help overactive bladder 
		C.	Respiratory symptoms
			i.	Obstructive sleep apnea → CPAP machine
			ii.	Botox injections for laryngeal spasm
			iii. Tracheostomy → in patients with significant stridor as this keeps the airway open
		D.	Physical Therapy
			i.	Fall prevention
			ii.	Stretching and positioning (bracing, wheelchair prescription)
			iii. Gait training (assistive device as needed – consider rollator)
			iv.	Refer to Parkinson’s Disease CPG for strategies
		E.	Occupational Therapy
		F.	Speech Therapy
	7.PROGNOSIS
		MSA is a progressive neurodegenerative disease with no significant treatments to slow down the progression. From the time of diagnosis, the average length of time patients have is between 6 to 10 years. Keeping these patients feeling positive, motivated, and in control of their disease (as best we can) is critical for maintaining the quality of life of these patients.  


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Progressive Supranuclear Palsy (Q&A_ProgressiveSupranuclearPalsy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Speech problems
	Progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP) is a rare brain disorder that causes problems walking, balance, and eye movement. 

	Although progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP) shares some symptoms with Parkinson’s disease, it is different in that it progresses quicker than Parkinson’s disease, problems with speech and swallowing are much more common, and tremor is rare in PSP.

Progressive Supranuclear Palsy
	Progressive Neurodegenerative disease with Parkinsonian Features 
	AKA Steel Richardson Olszewski syndrome
	---------------------------------------
	Age onset > 40 years old
	Males = Females 
	No identified risk factors 
	Life expectancy approximately 7 years from diagnosis

Pathophysiology 
Tau Inclusion Bodies or Neurofibrillary Tangles “Tufted Astrocytes”
Insertion of abnormal Tau inclusion Bodies → Neurodegeneration 
Targeted areas
	Basal Ganglia
	Cerebellum
	Oculomotor Complex
	Prefrontal, Cortex

Progressive Supranuclear Palsy 
	Falls → Postural Instability
	Vertical supranuclear palsy
	Akinesia 
	Cognitive decline

Comparison
	PSP
		Early Postural Instability
		Posture Extended arms/trunk
		Vertical Saccades 
		Facial Expression Surprised look 
		Rigidity Proximal Neck, Trunk
		Gait “Drunken Sailor”
	Parkinson’s Disease
		Late Postural Instability
		Posture Stooped posture, bent back/knees
		Facial ExpressionMasked Facies
		Rigidity Limb 
		Gait Shuffling

Diagnosis
	Autopsy Neurofibrillary Tangles Gold standard
	Referral to Neuropathologist 
	Lack of response to levodopa

Diagnostic Clinical Criteria
	Inclusion Criteria
		Age of onset > 40 y/o 
		Sporadic onset
	Clinical exclusion criteria
		Predominancy of any of the following: 
			Episodic memory impairment suggestive of Alzheimer’s
			LMN disease symptoms
			Visual hallucinations 
			Autonomic Dysfunction 
			Hx of encephalitis 
			Sudden onset and rapid deterioration 
	Radiographic exclusion criteria 
		Leukoencephalopathy 
		Structural lesion 
		Acute disease process 
		Suggestion of prion dx
	--------------------------------------------
	Core
		1) Occulomotor Dysfunction 
		2) Postural Instability 
		3) Akinesia 
		4) Cognitive Dysfunction
	↓
	Each category has a score of 1-3 
		1 → likely 
		3 → less likely 
		Lower numbers = more likely PSP
	----------------------------------
	Supportive features
		Lack of response to Dopamine 
		Dysarthria Hypokinetic, spastic
		Dysphagia
		Photophobia

Neuroradiology ('')
	Hummingbird Sign"

Treatment 
	Supportive therapy focusing on symptom care 
		Dieticians, Speech, Physical Therapy, Occupational Therapy 
		Support groups 
	No pharmaceutical relief with Dopamine containing agents 
		Meds to help with sleep, gait hesitancy options

.Knowledge Checker
	Which are treatment options for patients with progressive supranuclear palsy ? (Select all that apply).
		Dopamine containing agents
		Medicine to help with sleep
		Support groups
		Levodopa

		Medicine to help with sleep
		Support groups
			There is no pharmaceutical relief with dopamine containing agents or levodopa.

.('Progressive Supranuclear Palsy Study Guide.docx')	
	1.	DEFINITION
		Also known as Steel Richardson Olszewski Syndrome, progressive supranuclear palsy is a neurodegenerative disease characterized by Parkinsonian symptoms, ocular symptoms, and frontal lobe involvement, like disinhibition and abulia (lack of will or initiative.)   
		Tau protein: Protein that provides maintenance care of the cell and promote cell growth
		Check out this video which is an overview of PSP with video footage of patient symptoms.
		Check out this video interviewing Keith, a patient diagnosed with PSP, and his family.
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		The pathogenesis of PSP is not well understood, but likely has some genetic predisposition, which leads to abnormal structure of Tau proteins that causes them to clump together and form inclusions.
		A.	Accumulation of Tau inclusion bodies in the cytoplasm of oligodendrocytes, astrocytes, and neurons.
		B.	Tau inclusion bodies → disrupted cell maintenance → cell death → degeneration in specific areas of the brain
		C.	This process occurs in the basal ganglia, cerebellum, prefrontal cerebral cortex, oculomotor complex
	3.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		Patients with PSP most commonly present after 40 years old. The most common presenting symptom is frequent falls due to early postural instability. The most notable clinical manifestations are listed below.
		A.	Parkinsonian symptoms
		i.	Akinesia/Bradykinesia
		ii.	Postural instability → frequent falls EARLY in disease
		B.	Gait & Posture
		i.	Drunken sailor gait
		ii.	Extension of arms and trunk
		iii.	Proximal rigidity → neck and trunk
		C.	Ocular symptoms
		i.	Vertical supranuclear palsy → unable to look vertically
		ii.	Disruption of saccades – Here is a video demonstrating this finding
		iii. Blepharospasm – involuntary twitching or blinking of eyelids
		D.	Facial dystonia → “surprised look”
		E.	Dysphagia
		F.	Cognitive decline
	4.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Clinical Diagnosis 
		i.	Core features: Oculomotor dysfunction, postural instability, akinesia, cognitive dysfunction 
		ii.	Poor response or no response to levodopa
		iii.	Rapid deterioration of disease
		iv.	Slowing of vertical saccades
		B.	MRI → rule out other causes
		i.	Atrophy of midbrain → “Hummingbird sign”
		C.	Gold standard → finding Tau body inclusions/Neurofibrillary tangles on pathology
	5.	TREATMENT
		Purpose of treatment is symptom relief → they DO NOT prevent disease progression. Medical providers may consider medications to help with symptom management, but they typically do not work well or only work for a short time. 
		A.	Physical Therapy
		Focus on muscle strength, gait, coordination, and balance. These patients are at high risk of falls
		i.	Gait training
		1.	Large steps, adequate foot clearance, heel strike
		2.	Teach safety with turns
		
		3.	Consider compensatory strategies.

		a.	Wide stance
		b.	Visually scanning the environment before walking
		c.	Avoid bending low and standing up quickly to decrease risk of retropulsion.
		d.	Assistive device
		ii.	Balance training
		iii. Fall prevention
		B.	Occupational Therapy
		i.	Reduce fall risk
		ii.	Recommend adaptive equipment.
		C.	Speech therapy
		i.	Help manage dysphagia
	6.	PROGNOSIS
		PSP is a progressive neurodegenerative disease with no significant treatments to slow down the progression. From the time of diagnosis, the average length of time patients have is around 2-7 years. Keeping these patients feeling positive, motivated, and in control of their disease (as best we can) is critical for maintaining the quality of life of these patients.

Essential Tremor (Q&A_EssentialTremor)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Essential tremor or Parkinson's? 
	Essential tremor is often confused with Parkinson’s disease mostly due to the tremors associated with both diseases.

	However, both differ in that essential tremor of hands typically occur when using the hand whereas, in contrast, Parkinson’s tremors occur when hands are in resting position. Both diseases also differ in that essential tumor involves hands, head, and voice whereas, in contrast, Parkinson’s affects the legs, chin, and other parts of the body.

Tremor
	The back-and-forth movement of a body part.

Γ Tremor vs. Essential Tremor ('Tremor VS Essential Tremor.png')

CLINICAL CHARACTERISTICS

		Also Head, Face, Voice

	Caffeine does not worsen symptoms

	Diagnostic criteria of Parkinson’s and Movement Disorder Task Force
		Isolated tremor of bilateral limbs postural or kinetic in quality without additional motor irregularities
		Symptoms occurring for at least 3 years 
		+/tremor location in other areas head, face, voice, lower limbs
		Lack of other neurological symptoms ataxia, dystonia, other symptoms of parkinsons

.Essential Tremor ('Essential Tremor Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		A tremor is the fast back and forth movement of a body part. Classified by frequency (how fast is the tremor), amplitude (how large of a movement is the tremor), quality (when do they occur).
		Postural: Tremor gets worse with activity against gravity
		Kinetic: Tremor gets worse with voluntary activity
		Rest: Tremor at rest 
		Essential Tremor: The most common type of action tremor. It’s a moderate to high frequency tremor, varying amplitude (amplitude gets bigger with activity) that is both postural and kinetic.
		Check out this video that chronicle’s Beverly McGowan’s experience with essential tremor. 
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		The pathogenesis of essential tremor is not well understood but can be inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern.
	3.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		This disease typically manifests in patients between 20-60 years old and has a slow progressive course. As tremor worsens it interferes with their ADLs.
		A.	Bilateral symptoms of hands, wrists, and arms → most common
		B.	Occasional involvement of head, trunk, and voice
		C.	Tremor most noted with intentional movement and resolves within minutes when the patient is at rest
		D.	Stress, anxiety, and medications can worsen tremor
		E.	Alcohol and rest will make the tremor better
	4.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Clinical Diagnosis: Diagnostic criteria of Parkinson’s and Movement Disorder Task Force 
		i.	Isolated tremor of bilateral limbs, with or without tremor in other locations
		ii.	Symptoms occur ≥3 years
		iii.	Lack of other symptoms – ataxia, dystonia, dementia, parkinsonian symptoms
	5.	TREATMENT
		Treatment is warranted when it affects the patient’s quality of life, either embarrassment or inability to do their typical tasks.  
		A.	Medications:
		i.	As needed: propranolol or benzodiazepine (Ativan)
		ii.	Maintenance therapy → decrease amplitude of tremor, will stop working well over time
		1.	First line: propranolol and primidone → consider combo therapy
		2.	Second line: Topamax, Neurontin, Botox
		B.	Surgical management → for limb tremors only, there are exclusion criteria
		Targets the Ventral Intermediate Nucleus of the Thalamus
		i.	Deep brain stimulator → lower risk, can be done bilaterally
		1.	Check out this video that shows a patient before and after they turn her deep brain stimulator on. (Notice how her tremor gets worse with postural and kinetic activity.)
		ii.	Thalamotomy → higher risk, can only be done on one side
		C.	Physical Therapy
		i.	Focus on resistant training: bicep curls, wrist flexion/extension, isometric loads on index finger
		ii.	Relaxation techniques → help to lower stress and improve tremor
	6.	PROGNOSIS
		Essential tremor is a very slowly progressive disease that can be managed with both medications and physical therapy.

Huntington's Disease (Q&A_HuntingtonsDisease) 
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

."This land is your land"
	Woody Guthrie was an American singer-songwriter and folk musician whose legacy encompasses hundreds of political, traditional, and children songs he wrote and performed including his most popular song, “this land is your land.” 

	Unfortunately, his career was cut short in 1954, when he was diagnosed with Huntington's disease. He spent the rest of his years in the hospital declining in physical, mental, and behavioral shape.

CAUSE  
	HTT gene CAG sequence repeat > 40 sequences 
	Disrupts HTT gene function 
	NeuroimagingPutamen & Caudate Nucleus atrophy

HUNTINGTON’S DISEASE
	Chorea like movements
	Slowed eye saccades 
	Dementia 
	Psychiatric symptoms

DIAGNOSIS
	Known family history 
	Symptoms
	Genetic Testing (CAG trinucleotide expansion of HTT Gene)

PROGNOSIS 
	Early stage 
	Middle stage 
	Late 
	10-to-20-year life expectancy after diagnosis

TREATMENT 
	Vesicular monoamine transporter Type 2
	Anti-psychotics 
	Benzodiazepines
	--------------------------------------
	Physical Therapy 
	Occupational Therapy
	Speech Therapy

.Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following would be treatment options for patients with Huntington's disease? (Select all that apply).
		Anti-psychotics
		Benzodiazepines
		prn Propranolol
		Vesicular monoamine transporter Type 2

		Anti-psychotics
		Benzodiazepines
		Vesicular monoamine transporter Type 2

.('Huntington Disease Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Genetically inherited autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disease characterized by choreiform movements, dementia, and psychiatric illness. 
		Check out this video about Tammy Stewart and her family’s experience with Huntington’s Disease.
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		Caused by a mutation of the Huntington gene with an increase in CAG sequence.  This sequence typically repeats up to 28 times on the Huntington gene. 
		Repeats of 29-39 put a person at risk for mild symptoms and puts their offspring at risk for greater mutations.  Repeats >40 cause Huntington disease.  The number of mutations correlates with disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset, with higher numbers of mutations/repeats causing worsening disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset. 
		This mutation impairs the Huntington gene function of facilitating DNA healing  neuronal death.
	3.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		This disease typically manifests in patients between 40 and 50 years old.
		A.	Movement changes
		i.	Chorea like movements 
		1.	Uncontrolled involuntary movements → “dance-like”
		2.	Worsen over time
		3.	Check out this video of classic choreiform movement
		ii.	Progressive loss of voluntary movement control 
		1.	Bradykinesia
		2.	Dystonia
		3.	Rigidity
		B.	Speech/Swallowing Difficulties
		C.	Ocular involvement
		i.	Slowing of eye saccades
		D.	Psychiatric symptoms
		i.	Anxiety/depression
		ii.	Psychosis
		E.	Dementia and Cognitive decline
		i.	Struggle with decision-making
		ii.	Issues with memory
	4.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	Genetic Testing: CAG trinucleotide expansion of HTT gene
		i.	Need to have good counseling → can affect family members of the person being tested
		B.	Known family history
	5.	TREATMENT
		Treatment focuses on symptomatic relief or specific symptoms  
		A.	Chorea:
		i.	Vesicular monoamine transporter type 2 inhibitors (deutetrabenazine)
		B.	Psychiatric symptoms:
		i.	Anti-psychotics
		ii.	Benzodiazepine
		C.	Physical Therapy
		i.	 American Physical Therapy Association Fact Sheet on Huntington Disease.
		ii.	CPG: Clinical Recommendations to Guide Physical Therapy Practice for Huntington Disease. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7080285/
		iii.	Highlight safety with mobility, positioning, pressure relief, and family education
	6.	PROGNOSIS
		The life expectancy after diagnosis is 10-20 years with a predictable disease course that goes through three stages.
		i.	Early stage – mild symptoms that don’t affect quality of life
		ii.	Middle stage – chorea impairs ability to perform ADLs
		iii.	Late stage – completely incapacitated, unable to communicate, require 24-hour care.

Dystonia (Q&A_Dystonia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Muscle Contraction
	Whereas this comic depicts what occurs when a person works out, dystonia is the involuntary muscle contraction that causes repetitive or twisting movements (click comic to zoom in). It can affect one or more parts of a patient’s body. With proper treatment, it can be controlled but there is not a cure for this condition yet. 

Dystonia 
	Sustained, prolonged muscle contraction
	Associated with abnormal movements or postures.
	Twisting, repetitive, tremor
	Eyes and larynx can also be affected

Dystonia Causes
	Genetic predisposition
	Acquired
	Idiopathic

Dystonia subtypes
	Focal
	Segmental
	Generalized
	Multifactorial
	Hemidystonia

Clinical Manifestations
	Patterned 
	Repetitive
	Sustained
	Twisting Movements
	Abnormal postures
	Intention based
	Rapid blinking
	Voice fluctuations

	Secondary effects:
		Pain 
		Depression

Multiple types of Dystonia
	Early onset (pediatric)
	Adult onset (Focal vs. segmental isolated)
		Cervical Dystonia
		Blepharospasm
		Spasmodic dystonia
		Arm dystonia
		Oromandibular dystonia and facial dystonia
	Combined dystonia

Diagnosis
	Clinical
		Location of involvement
		Age of onset
	Diagnostic Tests
		Genetic testing
		Head CT
		Electromyography
		Bloodwork

Treatment
	Pediatric
		Dopa responsive Dystonia 
		Anticholingeric drugs 
		First line Trihexiphenidyl
		Second line Baclofen, Benzodiazepines 
		Botulinum Toxin 
		Deep Brain stimulator 
	Adult
		Dopa responsive Dystonia 
		Focal DystoniaBotulism
		Non-focal Dystonia-
		Benzodiazepinesfirst line 
		Trihexiphenidyl/Baclofen second tier 
		Deep brain stimulator

.Knowledge Checker
	Match
		Primary effects
		Secondary effects

		twisting movements, abnormal postures, rapid blinking
		pain and depression

		----------------------------
		Primary effects → twisting movements, abnormal postures, rapid blinking
		Secondary effects → pain and depression

.('Dystonia_Study_Guide_1.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION 
		Sustained or periodic contraction of a muscle group causing abnormal movements or postures. Movements tend to be repetitive and twisting.  Worsened with voluntary movement and associated with “overflow activation,” which is when the originally affected muscle group elicits sustained contractions of another muscle group. 
		Check out this video interviewing children with pediatric dystonia and their parents.  Please ignore the poor production value of the video.   
		Check out this video of Billy McLaughlin, a famous guitarist, discussing his experience with focal dystonia. 
	2.	CAUSES 
		A.	Genetic predisposition or idiopathic  onset in toddler to adolescent years 
		B.	Acquired 
	3.	CLASSIFICATOIN 
		A.	Focal: One body part affected 
		B.	Segmental: 2 or more contiguous regions affected (voice  neck face) 
		C.	Generalized: Trunk involvement plus 2 other involved areas of the body 
		D.	Multifactorial: Areas of dystonia in two different regions of the body that aren’t connected (leg plus arm) 
		E.	Hemidystonia: Affects a unilateral part of the body (left or right side0 
	4.	PATHOGENESIS 
		Caused by a mutation of the Huntington gene with an increase in CAG sequence. This sequence typically repeats up to 28 times on the Huntington gene.  
		Repeats of 29-39 put a person at risk for mild symptoms and puts their offspring at risk for greater mutations.  Repeats >40 cause Huntington disease.  The number of mutations correlates with disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset, with higher numbers of mutations/repeats causing worsening disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset.  
		This mutation impairs the Huntington gene function of facilitating DNA healing  neuronal death. 
	5.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS 
		Patients typically have patterned behavior that is repetitive, and they will often sustain in an abnormal posture.  These postures often cause pain and embarrassment for the patients, which can lead to depression. 
		A.	Early onset (pediatric) 
		i.	Typically starts as focal dystonia, usually of the leg, and progresses to contiguous areas until it diffusely affects their body over 1-2 years 
		B.	Adult onset 
		i.	More likely to develop focal or segmental dystonia  most commonly involves upper extremities 
		1.	Often will be action induced (writing, playing instruments, running) 
		ii.	Cervical dystonia  most common type 
		iii.	Blepharospasm  bilateral symmetric involuntary blinking 
		iv.	Spasmodic dystonia  laryngospasm 
		1.	Develop a breathy voice when they are speaking 
	6.	DIAGNOSIS 
		A.	Clinical Diagnosis 
		There is no one test that can diagnose a dystonia.   
		i.	Pattern of onset and locations affected can help to differentiate different types 
		ii.	Labs, head CT, electromyography can help rule out other diseases like Wilson’s disease 
		iii.	Genetic testing available for some known inherited dystonias 
	7.	TREATMENT 
		Treatment focuses on symptomatic relief or specific symptoms   
		A.	Pediatric: 
		i.	Levodopa trial 
		ii.	First line: Anticholinergic medications (Trihexyphenidyl) 
		iii.	Second line: Benzodiazepines (Lorazepam) 
		iv.	Consider Deep Brain Stimulator 
		B.	Adults: 
		i.	Focal dystonia: Botox therapy (especially cervical dystonia) 
		ii.	Benzodiazepines 
		iii.	Deep brain stimulator 
		iv.	Can consider levodopa trial in these patients too 
		C.	Physical Therapy 
		i.	 Address: stress, pain management, mobility, bracing, sensory training
		i.	EMG biofeedback
		ii.	Task specific interventions. 
		ii.	Check out this video of Dr. Alison McKenzie discussing physical therapy treatments specifically for focal hand dystonia. Minute 8 is when she begins to show examples of treatment modalities. 
		D. Occupational Therapy: 
		i. Address: energy conservation, adaptive equipment, splinting, sensory training
	8.	PROGNOSIS 
		The prognosis of dystonia varies greatly from patient to patient. Generally dystonia greatly affects a person’s quality of life because it either becomes diffuse, as is the case with pediatric dystonia, or it affects a repetitive action that has a large impact on a person’s life and identity, like playing an instrument.

		.Cranial Nerve III Palsy ('')
			Can occur anywhere along its pathway from midbrain to muscles responsible for movement 
				1. Midbrain
				2. Subarachnoid Space
				3. Cavernous Sinus
				4. Orbital lesion
		.Cranial Nerve III Palsy Causes
			1. Midbrain Ischemia
				1. Brainstem ischemia → Additional Neurological defects ataxia, hemiparesis
			2. Subarachnoid Space Pathology
				1. Aneurysmal → Pain
				2. Infection → Meningitis → Headache, neck stiffness, fever, altered mental status, additional CN palsies
				3. Intracranial Pressure/Compressive lesion → additional neurological deficits
				4. Nerve Ischemia → (DM) Pupil spared 
			3. Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis → Additional CN Nerve palsy (IV, V, VI)
			4. Orbital Region → Proptosis, conjunctival injection

.Cranial Nerve IV Function
	Superior Oblique Musle of the Eye (SO) ('04 superior oblique.png')
		Lowers, abducts (lateral movement) and internal rotation¿?

	.Cranial Nerve IV 
		Injury to the nerve impairs lateral movement, depression and internal rotation¿? of the eye

	.Cranial Nerve IV Assessment
		Parks Bielschowsky 3 Step Method:
			1Which eye is higher? 
			2Which eye is higher when assessing lateral gaze?
			3Which eye is higher with tilting of the head?
	.Cranial Nerve IV Palsy Causes Γ(Paregut a lo que es diu en el N IV)
		1. Congenital → lifetime compensatory behaviors
		2. Midbrain

			2. Intracranial Mass/lesion
		3. Subarachnoid Space
			1. Superior Cerebellar Aneurysm 
			2. Infectious process → Headache, Fever, AMS, Neck stiffness
			3. Trauma (Mild TBI)
		4. Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis → additional CN deficits
		5. Orbital → Additional CN finding

.Cranial Nerve VI
	Lateral Rectus Musle of the Eye (LR)
	Eye abduction (Lateral movement)
	-----------------------------------
	.Cranial Nerve VI 
		Impairment of lateral rectus muscle leaves the eye unable to abduct affected eye
		Clinical symptoms: Horizontal Diplopia 
			Patients turn head instead of isolated eye movements
			Close one eye at a time
		Clinical exam:  Pupil is adducted and cannot abduct, midline
	.Cranial Nerve VI Palsy Causes Γ(Paregut a lo que es diu en el N IV)
		1. Pons
			1. Ischemia/Hemorrhage → additional neurological deficits
			2. Intracranial Mass/lesion
		2. Compressive lesion → tumor
		3. Increased Intracranial Pressure
		4. Skull Base Fractures 
		5. Congenital lesions
