.Prism Therapy and Strabismus Surgery are both treatment options.
	True
	False

	True

.('Oculomotor abnormalities Study Guide.docx')
	1.DEFINITION
		Dysfunction of the muscles innervated by cranial nerves III, IV, and VI, the Oculomotor, Trochlear, and Abducens nerves respectively.
	2.CRANIAL NERVE III PALSY
		Here is a video example of complete CN III palsy
		A.	Function
		i.	Lifts eyelid → Levator Palpebrae suprioris
		ii.	Eye adduction (medial) → Medial rectus
		iii.	External rotation, lifts eye, eye abduction, (lateral) → Inferior rectus, Inferior oblique
		iv.	Internal rotation, lowers eye, eye abduction (lateral) → superior rectus
		v.	Pupil constriction → constrictor pupillae
		B.	Causes
		i.	Midbrain Infarct (ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke)
		ii.	Compression along nerve tract
		1.	Tumor
		2.	Abscess
		3.	Aneurysms: Posterior communicating artery (PCOM), Internal carotid artery (ICA)
		iii. Elevated intracranial pressure (traumatic brain injury, edema)
		iv.	Cavernous Sinus Thrombosis
		v.	Orbital lesions
		C.	Symptoms
		i.	Diplopia (double vision)
		ii.	Eye pain
		iii. Drooping eyelid (ptosis)
		iv.	Complete: “Down and Out” and unreactive pupil, ptosis
		v.	Incomplete: some decreased function, but not all
	3.CRANIAL NERVE IV PALSY
		Here is a video demonstrating CN IV palsy. The positive finding is easiest to see as the patient is looking to the left focusing on a distant object and he has one eye covered at a time – you’ll notice his eyes move up and down, indicating his eye is slightly higher on his affected side
		A.	Function
		i.	Internal rotation, lowers eye, eye abduction → superior oblique (SO4)
		B.	Causes
		i.	Congenital → compensate by slightly tilting their head
		ii.	Midbrain ischemia/hemorrhage or space occupying lesion (like tumor)
		iii.	Subarachnoid space: Superior cerebellar aneurysm, infectious, traumatic brain injury
		iv.	Cavernous sinus thrombosis
		Symptoms
		v.	Vertical diplopia
		1.	Worse when looking down → like looking down the stairs
		2.	Improves with head tilting to side contralateral to the palsy. 
		vi.	Pupil slightly inward and upward
		C.	Diagnosis: Parks Bielschowsky 3 Step Method → demonstrated in the example video above.
		i.	Which eye is higher when patient looks straight ahead?
		ii.	Which eye is higher when the patient looks to either side?
		iii.	Which eye is higher with tilting the head to either side?
	4.CRANIAL NERVE VI PALSY
		Here is a video demonstrating a patient with CN VI palsy.
		A.	Function
		i.	Eye abduction → lateral rectus (LR6)
		B.	Causes
		i.	Congenital
		ii.	Pons ischemia/hemorrhage or space occupying lesion (like tumor)
		iii. Increased intracranial pressure
		iv.	Skull base fractures
		C.	Symptoms
		i.	Horizontal diplopia → get double vision when they turn their head side to side
		1.	Tend to turn their head toward the affected eye to decrease the double vision or close one eye
		ii.	On exam iris is midline, adducted, cannot abduct
	5.DIAGNOSIS
		A.	CT head and CT angiogram of head → if you’re worried about acute vascular process like subarachnoid aneurysm rupture
		B.	MRI brain
		C.	Lumbar puncture if concern for infection
	6.TREATMENT
		Treatment of cranial nerve palsies really depends on the cause. Sometimes they can resolve spontaneously. If there is a treatable underlying cause, like increased compression from a tumor or aneurysm, surgical intervention may be warranted.
		A.	Vision Therapy
		i.	Typically performed by optometrists and ophthalmologists
		ii.	Eye patch → short term
		iii. Prism therapy → specialty lenses placed in glasses to help correct double vision
		iv.	Check out this video of Dr. Julie describing the treatment of CN IV palsy with a patient case example
		B.	Physical Therapy and Occupational therapy
		i.	Can help train patients to accommodate for their nerve palsy
		C.	Strabismus surgery
	7.PROGNOSIS
		The prognosis of cranial nerve palsies really depends on the cause. Many patients with ischemic related cranial nerve palsies will show improvement with time alone. Correction of the palsy with vision therapy can help patients with their ADLs.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Friedreich's Ataxia (Q&A_FriedreichsAtaxia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Trouble walking
	Friedreich’s ataxia is a rare disorder that is passed down from parents. Symptoms are shown late in childhood years and tend to worsen over time. These symptoms include trouble walking, fatigue, and slow speech. There is currently no cure but treatment which minimizes the symptoms. 

Friedreich's Ataxia
	Inherited as an autosomal recessive trait 
		Mutation affiliated with unstable GAA repeat in the Frataxin gene
	Most common inherited ataxia
	Age of onset 2 → 70 but most common in adolescence

Clinical Picture
	Ataxia with progressive spastic paraplegia 
	Cardiomyopathy 
	Diabetes Mellitus 
	Dysarthria
	Decreased Deep tendon reflexes

Neuroanatomy 
	Degeneration of 3 spinal tracts 
		Spinocerebellum → ascending tract → sends position sense, touch, pressure to the cerebellum 
		Corticospinal → descending tract → voluntary movement → tells the body to move
		Dorsal Columns → ascending tracts → position sense, vibratory sense, discrimination

Cardiac 
	Left Ventricular Hypertrophy → dilated cardiomyopathy 
	Cardiac arrhythmias

.Knowledge Checker
	Fill in the blank.
		When it comes to diagnosis, _______ gets tested to rule out differentials in Friedreich's ataxia.
		Acceptable responses: Vitamin E

.('Friedreich's Ataxia Study Guide.docx')
	1.	DEFINITION
		Genetically inherited autosomal recessive disease
		Frataxin: protein found in the mitochondria of cells and helps with ATP production and iron metabolism
		Ataxia: loss of control of body movements
		Check out this video made by a boy named Jake in which he talks about living with Friedreich’s Ataxia. Content warning. It may make you cry. 
	2.	PATHOGENESIS
		Caused by a mutation of the trinucleotide repeat GAA sequence. This sequence typically repeats 7-34 times.
		Patient with Friedreich’s ataxia can have up to 1,700 GAA sequences. The number of repeats correlates with disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset, with higher numbers of mutations/repeats causing worsening disease severity and earlier age of symptom onset. 
		Increase in repeats results in decreased frataxin production → decreased ATP and increased buildup of iron → oxidative stress → cell death
		Affects mitochondrial rich tissue
		i.	Neurons, particularly of the dorsal root ganglion → unknown cause
		ii.	Pancreas
		iii. Heart.
	3.	CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
		Onset of symptoms typically occurs in adolescence but can occur anywhere from 2-70 years. The most common presenting symptoms are balance difficulty and difficulty ambulating, which will eventually lead to progressive paraplegia and increased spasticity.
		A.	Ataxia
		i.	Spinocerebellar ascending tract → proprioception, touch, pressure → balance issues, knowing where their body is in space
		ii.	Corticospinal descending tract → voluntary movement → progressive weakness and paraplegia
		iii. Dorsal columns ascending tract → proprioception, vibratory sense, two-point discrimination → balance issues, knowing where their body is in space
		iv.	Progressive
		1.	Will eventually have decreased lower extremity strength that requires assistance with ambulation
		2.	Many eventually require mobility devices like wheelchairs
		B.	Cardiomyopathy → most likely cause of death in these patients
		i.	Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy more common
		ii.	Dilated cardiomyopathy can occur, but it is rare
		iii. Can also develop arrythmias
		C.	Diabetes
	4.	DIAGNOSIS
		A.	MRI brain not required for diagnosis
		i.	Spinal cord and medullary atrophy → typically do NOT have cerebellar atrophy
		B.	Genetic testing for GAA repeat
		C.	Frataxin levels
		D.	Vitamin E levels → deficiency can cause similar symptoms
		E.	Electromyogram will demonstrate sensory axonal neuropathy
		i.	Here is a video demonstrating an EMG/Nerve conduction study
	5.	TREATMENT
		The goal of treatment is to improve symptoms and functionality. 
		A.	Physical Therapy 
		Please refer to the article, “ A Multi-Modal Treatment Approach for a Young Adult with Friedreich’s Ataxia: A Case Report” in the Additional Resources section of this module for an example of a physical therapy treatment plan for these patients.
		i.	Strengthening exercises
		ii.	Stretching
		iii. Gait training
		iv.	Balance exercises
		v.	Cardiovascular conditioning
		B.	Occupational therapy
		i.	Adaptive equipment
		ii.	Assistive technologies
		iii. Environmental adaptiation
		C.	Speech therapy → to help manage the dysarthria that can occur
		D.	Cardiac, Diabetes, ophthalmology surveillance
	6.	PROGNOSIS
		The prognosis for patients is poor. Most patients are diagnosed in adolescence and live until their 30s or 40s. The most common cause of death in these patients is heart failure.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 6 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-The average survival rate for all primary brain tumor patients is 75.2%. The survival rates vary by age and tumor type and generally decrease with age. For patients with a non-malignant brain tumor, the average five-year survival rate is 91.7%. Whereas in contrast, patients with malignant brain tumors’ five-year survival rate following diagnosis is 36%. Brain tumors cause symptoms either by compressing on surrounding structures or stimulating neuronal excitement and seizure. 
-One of the potential complications of either benign or malignant brain tumor is blocking the drainage of cerebral spinal fluid causing hydrocephalus.

('Signs and Symptoms of a Brain Tumor _ Dana-Farber Cancer Institute.mp4')

-This module will focus on neurologic disorders. In particular, you will be learning more about disorders that occur in the brain. The brain tumor is one of several other disorders that you will learn about in this module.  
-As in the last module, there will be some similarities between the diseases covered this week. Focus on what makes them different, specifically, what differentiated all three Chiari malformations and their typical presentations. 


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
6.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Intracranial Pressure 
	Definition: Pressure in the craniospinal compartment
	Etymology: Intra – within or inside “cranial” - skull

Hydrocephalus 
	Definition: Condition of excess cerebrospinal fluid in the ventricles of the brain
	Etymology: Hydro – water, “cephal” - head

Glial Cells 
	Definition: Cells in the central nervous system that are not neurons but rather support the neurons
	Etymology: Glia – Greek “glue”

Ventriculoperitoneal Shunt 
	-Definition: A device that connects the lateral ventricles of the brain to the abdominal cavity to allow the drainage of excess cerebrospinal fluid.
	-Etymology: “Ventriculo” – relating to ventricle “peritoneal” - relating to peritoneum or membrane lining the abdominal cavity

Metastasis 
	Definition: The development of secondary malignant tumors at a site distant from the primary tumor.
	Etymology: "Meta" beyond "stasis" control or displaced

Meningioma  
	Definition: Benign brain tumor arising from the meninge cells
	Etymology: “Meningi” – relating to the meninges (3 membranes around central nervous system) “oma” - tumor or mass

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
6.2. Explore: Clinical Manifestation of Neurologic Diseases III
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-There are many neurologic diseases that will require additional clinical care by either a physician or healthcare professional. As you study these diseases, pay close attention to the similarities and differences between clinical manifestations and, when you should refer them to another healthcare professional. 

-In this section, you will watch a series of video lectures exploring neurologic diseases, etiology, pathogenesis, and clinical manifestations. For the diseases covered, this series of lectures will help you:
	Describe the etiology and pathogenesis. 
	Identify common clinical manifestations. 
	Discuss disease-related diagnostic methods, treatments, and implications for healthcare providers related to the disease.

('Hybrid Brain tumor etc.pptx')
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Brain Tumor
	.Brain Tumor Characteristics
		3.Primary
			Tumors that arise from the glial cells in the brain
			Ex. Astrocytoma, Ependymoma, Oligodendroglioma, Meningioma
		4.Metastatic
			Cancer cells that spread to the brain from another cancer in the body
				Most commonly: 
					Lung
					breast
					Melanoma
					Colon
					Kidney
		1.Benign
			Non-Cancerous mass
			Tend to grow more slowly and do not metastasize (Meningioma)
		2.Malignant
			Cancerous Tumor
			Tend to grow quickly and metastasize (Ex. Glioblastoma)

	.Brain Tumor Clinical Signs
		Vary by type but can include
			Headache 
				Bifrontal
				Increased at night 
				Increase with cough or sneeze
			Nausea
			Seizure – often first sign that leads to testing
			Neurological deficits – vary by location/size of tumor
		Diagnosis
			MRI 

	.Brain Tumor Treatment
		Steroids
			Decrease cerebral edema
		Anti-seizure medications
		Surgery
			Assist with diagnosis
			May be used to remove or debulk tumor
		Radiation
			Destroy malignant cells
			Ex. Gamma knife: focal radiation that destroys tumor cells
		Chemotherapy
			Prevent tumor growth
			IV or oral

	.Brain Tumor – PT considerations
		Monitor for changes in cognition and function. Alert medical team of changes
		Consider prognosis for grade IV glioblastoma and other high-grade gliomas.
			Goal: get them home or to next setting safely and as soon as possible.
		Bone Metastasis (Bone mets)
			Check location on imaging and confirm weight bearing status
			Safe mobility: If mets are in weight bearing joints, consider assistive device for ambulation. 
		Apply Neuro Treatment Principles: Monitor vitals closely

.Hydrocephalus
	.Build up of cerebral spinal fluid (CSF) → dilation of ventricles → elevated intracranial pressure (ICP)
		.Intracranial Pressure (ICP): Pressure within the craniospinal compartment. 
			Normal < 15 mmHg
			≥ 20 mmHg is intracranial hypertension or high ICP
		.Clinical Presentation
			Headache
			Nausea/Vomiting
			Decreased arousal → Coma
			Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus:
				Dementia
				Gait instability: “Glue-footed gait”, slow, wide base of support
				Incontinence

	.Types of Hydrocephalus: Elevated ICP
		.Obstructive
			Tumor
			Bleed
			Ex. Aqueductal Stenosis – Narrowing of cerebral aqueduct that leads to occlusion of the 4th ventricle. 
			Ex. Chiari Malformation Type II
		.Communicating Hydrocephalus
			Increased CSF production	
				Ventricular papilloma
			Impaired ability to resorb
				Blood in subarachnoid space
				Infection (ex. Meningitis)
				Venous sinus thrombosis

	.Hydrocephalus
		.Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus: Normal ICP
			Multifactorial 
			Decreased CSF resorption
			Increased ventricular compliance
			Vascular changes
		.Pediatric Hydrocephalus
			Congenital or Acquired (trauma or ICH)
			Increased head circumference or bulging fontanel
			Lethargic or irritable
			“sunset eyes” - downward gaze

	.Hydrocephalus Treatment *New material
		External Ventricular Drain (EVD) 
			Temporary
			Used to normalize ICP
			Do not move bed or mobilize patient unless drain is clamped
			Stop PT if ICP >20 mmHg
			Avoid activity that increases ICP: cough, isometrics, bending forward, lying flat supine
		Ventriculoperitoneal Shunt (VPS)
			Long term
			PT often consulted to do pre-shunt and post-shunt gait assessments
				Function gait assessment (FGA) and 10 MWT 
		Surgery
			Remove obstruction
			Craniotomy to decompress
			Dilate 3rd ventricle
			
.Chiari Malformation Type 1
	Cerebellum descends through the foramen magnum into the spinal canal (≥ 5 mm)
	Congenital: can present in childhood, adolescent or adulthood, or never
	Impairs the flow of CSF (obstructive hydrocephalus)
	Clinical Signs Type I
		Headache
		Neck pain
		Symptoms increase with Valsalva (cough or sneeze)
		40-75% develop a syrinx
			-(Γ)Syringomyelia cyst (syrinx) in the spinal cord Cerebrospinal fluid usually flows around the outside of the brain and spinal cord. In people with syringomyelia, cerebrospinal fluid collects inside the spinal cord and forms a fluid-filled cyst. The cyst is sometimes called a syrinx.
		May see cranial nerve compression (dysphagia, dysarthria, sleep apnea)
	Treatment: Surgery if neuro deficits; VPS if hydrocephalus

.Acoustic Neuroma (Vestibular Schwannoma)
	Benign Tumor arising from Schwann cells of CN VIII
		Occur ∼ 50 yo
		Usually unilateral
		Bilateral with neurofibromatosis Type 2
	Symptoms 
		CN V (Trigeminal): Facial pain
		CN VII (Facial): Facial twitching or weakness
		CN VIII (Vestibulocochlear): hearing loss, tinnitus, general imbalance

6.5. Module Summary
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Before moving on to the next module, remember to review these three key learnings: 
	Headache can be a sign of increased intracranial pressure and often requires MRI as part of the workup 
	Brain tumors cause symptoms either by compressing on surrounding structures or stimulating neuronal excitement and seizure 
	Not all patients with Chiari malformation need to have surgical intervention. 

Outcomes
	After completing this module, you can now: 
		Explain the physiology of headache as it relates to elevated intracranial pressure. 
		Compare and contrast the presentation, management, and prognosis of common central nervous system tumors. 
		Differentiate the types of Chiari malformations and their typical presentations.

Brain Tumor (Q&A_BrainTumor)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Brain Tumor?
	-Typically, brain tumors do not have obvious symptoms. For instance, headaches can be symptoms have many different ailments. The same applies to symptoms such as nausea. As you watch the following video lecture pay attention to how brain tumors are manifested and what are the treatments that are given to patients. 

Brain Tumors
	Cause: Uncontrolled cell growth and reproduction, loss of bodies defense system  
	Primary vs. Metastatic 
	Benign vs. Malignant

Symptoms
	Headache
	Nausea
	Seizure 
	Neurological Deficit (Depends upon location of intracranial mass)

Diagnosis 
	HCT initial typically¿? ΓAKA "Bone Marrow Transplantation"
	MRI Brain with contrast 
	Tissue sample Histopathologic and genetic analysis

Treatment
	+/Steroids 
	+/Anti-seizure medications
	Surgery
	Radiation
	Chemotherapy

Types of Brain Tumors
	Glioma
		Low grade
		High grade
	CNS lymphoma
	Metastatic 
	Meningioma
		-------------------------------------------
		Low grade glioma 
			35-45 years old 
			Slow growing, mutate into aggressive tumors
			Present with seizures
			Dx: Non-enhancing mass on MRI Brain, tissue pathology 
			Tx: Surgery + Radiation + Chemotherapy
		High Grade Glioma 
			Age onset 55 65-years-old
			M > F
			Malignant
			Dx: Rim enhancing mass with necrotic center, typically solitary on MRI Brain with gadolinium, tissue pathology 
			Tx: Maximum resection without causing neurologic injury + Chemotherapy + Radiation
			14 month mean survival
		-----------------------------------------------------
		CNS lymphoma 
			Age 45-65 y/o in non-immunocompromised patients 
			Age 20-64 in immunocompromised patients 
			Meninge, parenchyma, dura, ocular, nerve roots, spinal canal involvement without systemic involvement
			Caused by spread of malignant lymph cells
			Dx: Stereotactic bx, CSF analysis
			Tx: Methotrexate induction Chemotherapy
				High-Dose Chemotherapy with autologous HCT
				Nonmyeloablative Chemotherapy
				Whole Brain Radiation 
			Steroids
		Metastatic
			Stage IV Cancer
			Survival < 1 year 
			Headache, focal deficits, altered mental status
			Lung CA, Breast CA, Renal Cell CA, Melanoma, Colorectal CA 
			Dx: MRI Brain with gadolinium, typically more than one mass, some have hemorrhagic component, Tissue bx vs. known met
			Tx: Surgery vs. Chemotherapy vs. Radiation 
		Meningioma ('')
			40’s
			Female > Males
			Most common benign brain tumor 
			Meningothelial cells within the arachnoid space
			DX: MRI Brain with gadolinium homogenously enhancing, dural tail, extra-axial collection
			TX: Surgical Resection + Radiation 

.Knowledge Checker
	A patient who has brain tumor will most likely have which clinical manifestations? (Select all that apply)
		Headaches that get worst at night
		Trouble recognizing "b" from "d"
		Neurological deficits such as numbness 
		Dizziness

		Headaches that get worst at night
		Neurological deficits such as numbness 
	
.Strudy Guide ('Brain Tumor Study Guide.docx')
	BRAIN TUMOR
		1.DEFINITION
			Cancer: Uncontrolled cell growth and reproduction → result in disruption of body’s defense system
			Primary Brain Tumor: Tumors that arise from the brain parenchyma. Arise from glial cells
				i. Astrocytes = Astrocytoma
				ii.	Ependymal cell = Ependymoma
				iii. Oligodendroglia cells = Oligodendroglioma
				iv. Meninges = Meningioma 
			Metastatic Brain Tumor: Cancer cells that spread to the brain from another cancer in the body.  
			Benign Tumor: Non-cancerous mass. They tend to grow slowly and do not metastasize.
				Ex: Meningioma
			Malignant Tumor: Cancerous tumors. They tend to grow quickly, are more destructive to surrounding tissue, and metastasize.
				Ex: Glioblastoma
			----------------------------------------------------------
			-Check out this video of Tamara explaining her experience with having a brain tumor. 
			-Check out this video, which is a compilation of VLOGs from Charlotte Eades, a teenager with brain cancer. Content warning. This is a very sad video and the final minutes are difficult to watch.
			-Please refer to the article Intensive Rehabilitation Therapy Following Brain Tumor Surgery: A Pilot Study of Effectiveness and Long-Term Satisfaction for information on the physical therapy regiments that can be most helpful for these patients. 
		2.LOW GRADE GLIOMA: 
			Extremely slow growing, typically diagnosed between 35-45 y/o
				A.Clinical manifestations: New-onset seizures
				B.Diagnosis: MRI → do not enhance with contrast 
				C.Treatment: surgery + radiation + chemotherapy
					-i.Must treat because these can mutate into aggressive brain tumors
					-ii.Intraoperative electrical stimulation with awake craniotomy helps to improve the precision of tumor removal and avoidance of harming important functions 
				D.Ex: Grade I or II Astrocytoma or oligodendroglioma
		3.HIGH GRADE GLIOMA: 
			Very aggressive, typically diagnosed 55-65 y/o, more common in men. Come from mutation of a low-grade glioma that went undetected
				A.Clinical manifestations: Headache, cognitive decline, personality changes, balance issues
				B.Diagnosis: MRI brain with contrast → rim enhancing mass with necrotic center; lots of surrounding edema
				C.Treatment: Maximum resection + radiation + chemo
					i.Unfortunately, even with what appears to be a complete resection, there are unseen tumor cells in other parts of the brain → tends to come back
				D.Prognosis: 14 month mean survival
				E.Ex: Grade III Astrocytoma or oligodendroglioma, Grade IV Astrocytoma is called Glioblastoma (GBM) → these are the most malignant primary brain tumors
		4.CNS LYMPHOMA: 
			-Central nervous system variant of non-Hodgkin’s Lymphoma. Affects both the brain parenchyma and the meninges. Can be primary or secondary (from spread of systemic lymphoma)
				A.Occurs earlier in immunocompromised patients and later in immunocompetent patients
				B.Clinical manifestations: Headaches, seizures, focal deficits, cranial nerve palsies, ocular involvement
				C.Diagnosis: MRI brain with contrast
				D.Treatment: Typical lymphoma treatments not effective against CNS lymphoma
					-i.Methotrexate induced chemotherapy
					-ii.Avoid using steroids as first line agents until you have a diagnosis because steroids destroy the lymph cells and will make diagnosis more difficult
				E.Prognosis: Varies greatly. Untreated, mean survival of primary CNS lymphoma is 1.5 months but it does respond better to chemotherapy and radiation than other primary brain tumors.  
		5.METASTATIC BRAIN TUMORS: 
			Presence of brain mets means the patient has stage IV cancer.
				A.Most common primary cancers that metastasize to the brain:
					i.Lung
					ii.Breast
					iii.Melanoma
					iv.Colorectal
					v.Renal cell carcinoma 
				B.Clinical manifestations: confusion, seizures, focal deficits
				C.Diagnosis: MRI brain with contrast
					i.Multiple enhancing masses 
					ii.Multiple areas of cerebral hemorrhage
				D.Treatment: may or may not surgical candidate
				E.Prognosis: poor, typical survival < 1 Year
		6.MENINGIOMA
			-Most common benign brain tumor. Very slow growing. Arise from the dura mater, which is the outer most layer of the meninges (brain membranes).
				A.Clinical manifestations: Commonly present in 40s and symptoms related to mass effect or seizure
				B.Diagnosis: MRI brain with homogeneous uptake of contrast and occur where meninges are located
				C.Treatment: surgical resection + or – radiation
		7.PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			A.Monitor for changes in cognition and function. Alert medical team of changes.
			B.Consider prognosis for grade IV glioblastoma and other high-grade gliomas. 
				i.Goal: Get them home or to next setting safely and as soon as possible. 
				ii.Set patient and family up for success should the disease progress (i.e. Wheelchair, first floor set up with hospital bed)
			C.Bone Metastasis:
				i.Check location and confirm weight bearing status with medical team.
				ii.Safe mobility it key – If metastasis are in weight bearing joints, consider and assistive device for ambulation.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Acoustic Neuroma (Q&A_AcousticNeuroma)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Rare Benign Tumor
	-Acoustic neruoma is a very rare noncancerous tumor on the main nerve starting from the inner ear to the brain. It is very rare with fewer than 20,000 US cases a year. It typically begins at benign but can still be fatal if untreated. 

Epidemiology 
	Age 50 years 
	Male = Female
	Risk Factors 
	Neurofibromatosis Type-2 (bilateral)
	Childhood radiation exposure

N/A 1
	Vestibular portion of the 8th cranial nerve

Symptoms
	CN 8 Vestibulocochlear Nerve 
		Cochlear Involvement → Hearing loss, Tinnitus 
		Vestibular Involvement → Imbalance 
	CN 5 Trigeminal Nerve 
		Facial pain
	CN 7 Facial Nerve 
		Facial twitching

Diagnosis 
	Audiometry → Sensorineural Hearing loss 
	MRI Brain with and without gadolinium → tumor enhancement 

Treatment
	Surgery
	Radiation

.Knowledge Checker
	Match the cranial nerve on the left to the correct symptom(s) associated with it to the right.

	CN7 Facial Nerve
	CN8 Vestibulo cochlear Nerve
	CN5 Trigeminal Nerve

	Hearing Loss, Tinnitus, and Imbalance
	Facial pain
	Facial twitching

	-------------------------------------

	CN7 Facial Nerve - Facial twitching
	CN8 Vestibulo cochlear Nerve - Hearing Loss, Tinnitus, and Imbalance
	CN5 Trigeminal Nerve - Facial pain 

.Strudy Guide ('Acoustic Neuroma Study Guide.docx')
	ACOUSTIC NEUROMA
		1.DEFINITION
			-Acoustic neuroma, also known as vestibular schwannoma, is a benign tumor arising from the Schwann cells of CN VIII. It can grow into the internal auditory canal or the cerebellopontine angle and as it grows it causes symptoms due to nerve compression. It is the most common posterior fossa tumor.
			-Check out this video of Colleen talking about her experience having an acoustic neuroma. Pay special attention to her left side of her lips – it’s a great example of partial facial paralysis. Also, consider skipping ahead to minute 2:28 when she talks specifically about the importance your role played in her recovery!
		2.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-Acoustic neuromas typically occur around 50 years old. They most commonly occur unilaterally except in patients who have neurofibromatosis type 2. Symptoms result from nerve compression by tumor. Trigeminal and Facial nerve involvement are due to the proximity of these nuclei to the vestibulocochlear nuclei at the cerebellopontine angle.
				A.CN VIII: Vestibulocochlear Nerve
					i.Cochlear: Hearing loss, Tinnitus → most common complaints
					ii.Vestibular: Imbalanced
				B.CN V: Trigeminal Nerve
					i.Facial pain 
				C.CN VII: Facial Nerve
					i.Facial twitching or weakness
		3.DIAGNOSIS
			A.Audiometry → sensorineural hearing loss
			B.MRI brain with contrast → homogeneously enhancing tumor 
		4.TREATMENT
			A.Radiation: typically first line
			B.Surgical resection if tumor continues to grow with radiation
				i.The surgery often results in some degree of facial nerve injury which results in partial facial paralysis.
			C.Physical therapy is most critical in the acute post-operative period, when expected injury to the vestibulocochlear nerve and facial nerve result in significant vertigo symptoms and sometimes facial paralysis.  
				i.CPG: Vestibular Rehabilitation for Peripheral Vestibular Hypofunction: An Updated Clinical Practice Guideline From the Academy of Neurologic Physical Therapy of the APTA. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8920012/
		5.PROGNOSIS
			-The prognosis for patients with acoustic neuroma is generally pretty good as these tumors respond well to treatment and complications are uncommon.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Hydrocephalus (Q&A_Hydrocephalus)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Water on the brain
	How does the comic below relate to hydrocephalus? 
		-Hydrocephalus is a buildup of fluid in the cavities within the brain, also referred to as water on the brain. This is caused due to a disproportion of how much cerebrospinal fluid is produced and how much is absorbed into the bloodstream. It is common in both infants and adults.  

Cerebrospinal Fluid ('Cerebrospinal Fluid.png')
	Γ('The-Arachnoid-Granulations-and-CSF-Fluid-Reabsorption.png')
	Γ('Overview-of-the-Meninges-of-the-Brain.jpg')
	Located in between the arachnoid and pia meninges 
	500 mL secreted daily by the choroid plexus within the ventricles 
	150 mL in constant circulation 
	Reabsorbed by the arachnoid villi at the dural venous sinus

Monroe-Kellie Doctrine
	The intracranial contents are a fixed sum, increase of one component will necessitate a decrease in another. 
	Intracranial Contents 
		1. Brain tissue
		2. Blood
		3. Cerebrospinal fluid

Hydrocephalus 
	The increased build-up of cerebrospinal fluid on the brain leading to dilation of the ventricles.
	Symptoms
		1. Headache
		2. Nausea/emesis
		3. Decreased arousal, coma

Obstructive Hydrocephalus ('Obstructive Hydrocephalus')

Communicating Hydrocephalus

Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus

Diagnosis
	Head CT 
	MRI Brain 
	Lumbar Puncture (Non-obstructive hydrocephalus only!)
	Lumbar Drain Trial

Treatment 
	1. External Ventricular Device
	2. Ventriculoperitoneal Shunt
	3. Surgery for removal of obstructive lesion
	4. 3rd Ventriculostomy

Pediatric Hydrocephalus
	Causes
		1. Congenital
		2. Acquired
	Diagnosis
		1. Clinical exam findings
		2. Ultrasound through anterior fontanelles 
		3. Head CT
	----------------------------------------------
	Treatment
		Same as adult treatment options

.Knowledge Checker
	Treatment
		3rd Ventriculostomy
		surgery for removal of obstructive lesion
		External Ventricular Device
		Ventriculoperitoneal Shunt
	Not a treatment
		levodopa
		physical therapy
		botox
		topamax

.Strudy Guide ('Hydrocephalus Study Guide.docx')
	HYDROCEPHALUS
		1.DEFINITIONS
			Cerebrospinal fluid: clear fluid surrounding brain and spinal cord that provides protection and removes waste.  
				i.Found between the pia and arachnoid meninges
				ii.Produced by the choroid plexus in the ventricles
					1.Makes 500ml per day
					2.At any given time, there is 150ml of circulating CSF 
				iii.Resorbed by arachnoid villa at the dural venous sinus
					-Intracranial pressure (ICP): Pressure within the craniospinal compartment.
						Normal < 15mmHg
						≥ 20mmHg is intracranial hypertension or abnormally high ICP
					-Monroe-Kellie Doctrine: Intracranial contents are within a fixed volume (the skull), therefore increase of one component will necessitate a decrease in another or it will cause an increased ICP
				iv.Brain – 80%
				v.Blood – 10%
				vi.CSF – 10%

			Hydrocephalus: Buildup of CSF
				-Check out this video of Patricia discussing her experience with normal pressure hydrocephalus.
				-Check out this video of Rhys’s story – a baby boy with hydrocephalus. (This one will not make you cry. He is very cute and active baby). For learning, notice the differences in his skull shape compared to his twin brother.
		2.PATHOGENESIS
			-Something causes the buildup of cerebrospinal fluid which leads to dilation of the ventricles. Given the Monroe-Kellie Doctrine, this dilation of the ventricles after a certain point will cause an increase in intracranial pressure that results in symptoms to the patient. 
				A.Obstructive: Something is impeding the drainage of CSF
					i.Tumor
					ii.Bleed
					iii.Occlusion of 4th ventricle or narrowing of cerebral aqueduct 
				B.Communicating Hydrocephalus: Increased CSF production or impaired ability to resorb the CSF
					i.Increased CSF production
						1.Ventricular papilloma
					ii.Impaired ability to resorb
						1.Blood in subarachnoid space → clogs up the arachnoid villa
						2.Infectious → meningitis
						3.Venous congestion (from a venous sinus thrombosis) leads to decreased flow to the arachnoid villa
				C.Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus: Buildup of CSF without an increase in ICP
					i.Increased spinal buildup likely multifactorial involving decreased resorption, increased ventricular compliance, vascular changes 
				D.Pediatric Hydrocephalus
					i.Congenital: Dandy-walker
					ii.Acquired: Intracranial hemorrhage, trauma
					iii.Clinical manifestations: 
						1.Increased head circumference or bulging fontanel
						2.Increased lethargy or irritability
						3.“Sunset eyes” → downward gaze, impaired from looking up
					iv.Diagnosis: Ultrasound through anterior fontanelles or CT head
					v.Treatment: see below
		3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			The symptoms of hydrocephalus are related to intracranial hypertension (increased ICP)
				A.Headache
				B.Nausea and emesis
				C.Decreased arousal → coma
				D.Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus: “wacky, wobbly, and wet”
					i.Dementia
						1.Apathy
						2.Difficulty making decisions
					ii.	Gait instability – wide based, slow step, difficulty navigating their turns
						1.“Glue-footed gait”
					iii.Incontinence 
						1.Urinary urgency
		4.DIAGNOSIS
			A.MRI → dilated ventricles bigger than would be expected
			B.Lumbar puncture → contraindicated in patients with obstructive hydrocephalus
				i.Evaluate ICP → is it elevated or normal?
				ii.Evaluate for presence of blood or evidence of infection
				iii.Lumbar drain trial: Place drain into the lumbar spine that removes CSF
					1.Pre-drain and post-drain placement cognitive and gait assessments to determine if removal of fluid is improving symptoms
		5.TREATMENT
			A.External Ventricular Device (EVD) → drain that is placed percutaneously into the lateral ventricle
				i.Used in acute phase to normalize ICP (commonly used in acute subarachnoid hemorrhage patients)
				ii.Try to remove as quickly as possible. If patient cannot tolerate EVD being removed, can get a shunt placed
			B.Ventriculoperitoneal shunt (VPS) → drain that goes into the lateral ventricle, tubing is tunneled under the skin (usually behind the ear, down the neck and over chest) and then enters the peritoneal space (abdominal cavity) where it drains the CSF.
			C.Surgery
				i.Remove the obstruction
				ii.Craniotomy to decompress
				iii.3rd ventriculostomy → dilate the 3rd ventricle
			D.Physical Therapy Considerations
				i.Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus (NPH):  
					1.PT consulted for gait assessment prior to placement of VPS.
					2.Functional Gait Assessment (FGA) can be utilized pre and post VPS.
					3.If VPS successful, you will notice a dramatic difference in the quality of gait. 
				ii.Lumbar Drain:	
					1.Patients may be on flat bedrest.
					2.If cleared for OOB: Drain must be clamped by RN.
				iii.External Ventricular Drain (EVD):
					1.Head of bed is usually 30 degrees; zero line is roughly level of the ear in supine. 
					2.Do NOT adjust the bed position unless drain is clamped.
					3.Drain. Must be clamped by RN for mobilization and OOB activity.
					4.Stop treatment if ICP >20 mmHg.
					5.Avoid activities that increase ICP: cough, isometric exercises. 
		6.PROGNOSIS
			-The prognosis for patients with hydrocephalus varies greatly with the etiology of the hydrocephalus. With isolated hydrocephalus and appropriate management, patients can do quite well. However, if the hydrocephalus is secondary to a big bleed or a tumor or a serious congenital condition, the prognosis is more dependent on the underlying problem rather than the hydrocephalus itself.

Chiari Malformation (Q&A_ChiariMalformation)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.More than 200,000 cases a year
	-Chiari malformation is common in the US with more than 200,000 cases a year. Patients diagnosed with Chiari malformation are typically diagnosed during adulthood and have a normal life expectancy. However, it can last for years or be lifelong.  

('Chiari Malformation')

Chiari Malformation 
	Congenital Anomaly mechanism of development unclear
	Under-developed Posterior Fossa
	Neural Tube defects
	Intra-utero Hydrocephalus development

Syrinx 
	Develops from the obstruction of CSF flow
	Development of neurological deficits based on location 

Chiari Malformation I
	Headache & Neck pain that worsens with Valsalva maneuver 
	40-75% of patients develop syrinx 

Chiari Malformation II ΓAKA Arnold-Chiari malformation
	Presence of myelomeningocele
	Early Infancy have respiratory symptoms 
	Develop progressive Hydrocephalus

Myelomeningocele ('')

Chiari Malformation III
	Encephalocele 
	High mortality
	Severe neurological deficits

Encephalocele ('')

Treatment ('')
	Hydrocephalus Shunt
	Surgical intervention. When to operate?
	Posterior fossa decompression to decompress Foramen Magnum

.Knowledge Checker
	The decision for surgical intervention is a subject of debate of when to decompress the post europa.
		True
		False

		True

.Strudy Guide ('Chiari Malformation Study Guide.docx')	
	CHIARI MALFORMATION
		1.DEFINITIONS
			-Congenital anomaly that causes the cerebellum to descend through the foreman magnum and into the spinal canal. Defined as ≥5mm descent of cerebellar tonsils into the spinal canal.
			-Causes crowding around the brainstem, compresses the medulla, and impairs the flow of CSF
			-Syrinx of cervical spine → swelling within spinal cord due to impairment of CSF flow
				i.Symptoms based the exact level that the syrinx develops and how large it is

			-Check out this video of Muriel discussing she and her daughter’s experience in dual diagnoses – Chiari Malformation (called Arnold Chiari in the video) and rheumatoid arthritis when she was a college student. 
			-Check out this video of Nichole’s story with Chiari Malformation Type I and syrinx when she was in high school.
			-Check out this video of Aden’s story with Chiari Malformation Type I diagnosed when he was a baby.
		2.PATHOGENESIS
			Mechanism of development unclear. Possibly due to under-developed posterior fossa of the skull so the cerebellum doesn’t fit.
			Also possibly due to neural tube defects or fetal hydrocephalus that pushes the cerebellum inferiorly into the spinal canal.
		3.CHIARI MALFORMATION I
			A.Presents in childhood, adolescence, or adulthood
			B.Low-lying cerebellar tonsils that dip into spinal canal
			C.Clinical manifestations
				i.Headache
				ii.Neck pain
				iii.Symptoms worsen with Valsalva (coughing, sneezing)
				iv.40-75% develop syrinx
				v.Can end up compressing the lower cranial nerves → patients can get dysphagia, dysarthria, sleep apnea.
		4.CHIARI MALFORMATION II
			A.Presents antenatally or early infancy
			B.More pronounced displacement of the contents of the posterior fossa
				i.Not just cerebellar tonsils but also vermis and part of the medulla
				ii.Myelomeningocele → failure of spinal cord to close during development
			C.Respiratory difficulty, trouble swallowing, upper extremity weakness
			D.Progressive hydrocephalus
			E.Cognitively intact
		5.CHIARI MALFORMATION III
			A.Most severe 
			B.Encephalocele → displacement of cerebellum, lateral ventricle, and occipital lobe into a sac-like structure that is outside of their skull
			C.Severe neurologic deficits
			D.High mortality
		6.DIAGNOSIS
			A.MRI → brain and spine
		7.TREATMENT
			-Treatment is surgical, but not all patients with a Chiari malformation will need treatment. Surgical intervention involves removing a portion of the skull in the posterior fossa to allow the cerebellum to expand out, rather than down through the foramen magnum and into the spinal canal.
			-Patients who have neurologic deficits are offered surgery. However, there is debate in the neurosurgical community about what to do with asymptomatic patients who have a Chiari malformation and a syrinx on MRI.  
			-Patients with Chiari malformations in childhood will often need a ventriculoperitoneal shunt for management of their concomitant hydrocephalus.
		8.PROGNOSIS
			The prognosis for patients varies with the type of Chiari malformation.
				Type I are the least severe and type III are very severe.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 7 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-Every September, organizations such as the United Spinal Association and Project 34 raise awareness of spinal cord injuries which affect approximately 291,000 people each year. Patients with spinal cord injuries face unique challenges in that they have higher rates of unemployment and are high risk for both acute and chronic health issues such as depression and chronic pain. Frequently you will have several patients who suffer from spinal cord injury. Unfortunately, it can’t be cured and affects a patient for the rest of their lives.

-This module will focus on neurologic disorders. Compared to last week, you won’t be covering as many diseases in this module. This module focuses on the results of spinal cord injury as compared to brain injury, and how identifying and treating spinal cord injury, sooner than later, can have a profound influence on the well-being of a patient.

-You will also be learning more about the third human skill of the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach, active listening.

References
	https://disabilityhealth.medicine.umich.edu/news/spinal-cord-injury-awareness-month-research-roundup


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
7.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Tabes dorsalis  
Definition: Syndrome of ataxia caused by progressive degeneration of the dorsal columns from syphillis.
Etymology: “tabes dorsalis” - wasting of the back.

Syrinx 
Definition: Fluid filled sac in the center of the spinal cord that causes injury to the surrounding spinal cord.
Etymology: Greek “surinx” - tube or pipe. This is also the root for syringe.

Optic Neuritis 
Definition: Inflammation of optic nerve.
Etymology: “neur/o” - nerve, “-itis" - inflammation.

Multiple Sclerosis 
Definition: Autoimmune neurodegenerative disease which results in destruction of the myelin sheaths and replacement with hardened plaques.
Etymology: “sclerosis” - condition of hardening. Remember Systemic sclerosis.

Plasmapheresis  
-Definition: Treatment for autoimmune diseases whereby the plasma (where antibodies exist) is separated from the blood cells and removed, thereby removing the auto-antibodies that are causing disease.
-Etymology: “plasma” - liquid part of blood “apheresis” - taking away.

Video 2: Transverse myelitis ('V2 Living with Transverse Myelitis.mp4')
	Presented By: SiouxCity Journal

	David Meisinger, 23, a Morningside College senior, talks about how he has lived with Transverse Myelitis and grown from the experience.

	The comments posted below this video on youtube are also helpful to give you an idea of the heterogeneity of the disease course.

Video 3: Tabes dorsalis ('https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U1sKZ0CwvUE')
	Presented By: Baniaz 'jiz' Hospital and Health Care society

	A 47-year-old man with a history of human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) infection presented with an 8-month history of severe paroxysmal shooting pains in his legs, progressive difficulty in walking, tinnitus, and urinary incontinence. 

	He had a fully suppressed HIV viral load and a CD4+ count of 400 cells per cubic millimeter while receiving antiretroviral therapy. The physical examination showed Argyll Robertson pupils, which are nonreactive to bright light but briskly constrict when focusing on a near object (see video).

	Magnetic resonance imaging showed high signal changes in the dorsal columns of the thoracic spine, and samples of blood and cerebrospinal fluid were positive for syphilis on Venereal Disease Research Laboratory (VDRL) testing and Treponema pallidum particle agglutination assay. 

	The patient was treated with intravenous penicillin for 14 days, and gabapentin was started for the neuropathic leg pains. 

	Tabes dorsalis is a form of neurosyphilis that is characterized by degeneration of the nerves in the dorsal columns of the spinal cord. Along with Argyll Robertson pupils, the condition is associated with ataxia and loss of proprioception. After treatment, the patient’s symptoms and mobility slowly improved, although the shooting pains in his legs have continued despite pharmacotherapy. The serum VDRL level fell appropriately, and the result on VDRL testing of the cerebrospinal fluid was negative.

Video 4: Central cord syndrome ('V4 Central Cord Syndrome  Real Patient Case 2 2.mp4')
	Presented By: Dr. Adeleke Adesina, DO

	(Please only watch through minute 2:55.  After that it is an advertisement.)

	This patient suffered C4-C5 spinal stenosis from a hyperextension injury.

	Allodynia: Pain that is caused by a stimulus that typically does not cause pain, like the light touch you see that elicits pain in this video.

Video 5: Anterior spinal artery infarct ('V5 Dropped head and man-in-barrel syndrome in ALS.mp4')
	While this video shows a man with ALS, it is a good example of man-in-barrel syndrome which is also a typical presentation for anterior spinal artery infarct.

.Simulation as a PT, Me (1) - With Student/Patient Justice (2)
	.Initial comments:
		-As a reminder, the purpose of this final simulation is for you to learn how talking to patients is as important as the information you are providing to them. You can always look up facts but your ability to interact meaningfully with patients is a skill that must be practiced. 
		This final simulation will be a way to practice what you have learned in this course and those who fully invest themselves in this final simulation will get the most benefit out of it. 

		.Instructions
			-You are a rookie PT seeing a new patient in the clinic. The patient is 55 y/o and coming to you with  progressive left knee stiffness and pain over the last five months. Below is the information you get from the chart and your physical exam. You will need to ask the patient questions to "get the history of their current problem". Print a rationale for each question asked (why is the reason you ask that and/or what do you get from this question). Also, print three probable diagnosis and indicate which one is the most probable and why.

		.Case
			PMHx: HTN
			SurgHx: None
			FamHx: M (L) HTN, OA, hypothyroidism, F (D) HTN, CAD, DM
			SocHx: Denies smoking or previously smoking, occasional EtOH at the holidays. Lives alone in a single-story
			house. History professor at the local college.

			Medications: lisinopril 10mg
			Allergies: No known drug allergies (NKDA)

			.Tests and Measures
				Vitals
					BP 138/84 mm Hg, Pulse 72 bpm, RR 16, Temp 37.0°C; Wt 92 kg, Ht 5’10″ (29.0, "overweight" [25.0 to 29.9])
				Arousal, Attention, Communication and Cognition
					Awake, alert and cooperative.  No obvious cognitive deficits
				Pain
					Dull pain with PROM and AROM of R knee 4/10
				Integument
					Warm and well perfused. No lesions or rashes
				Area of interest
					-L knee with small effusion, no overlying erythema or warmth; Circulation 2+ dorsalis pedis pulses bilaterally Strength L: 5/5 hip flexion, 4/5 knee extension, 5/5 ankle (dorsiflexion and plantar flexion), 5/5 shoulder flexion and abduction, 5/5 elbow flexion, 5/5 elbow extension
					-R: 5/5 hip flexion, 5/5 knee extension, 5/5 ankle (dorsiflexion and plantar flexion) DF, 5/5 shoulder flexion and abduction, 5/5 elbow flexion, 5/5 elbow extension
				ROM
					Intact PROM and AROM throughout - crepitus noted with L knee extension/flexion, not noted elsewhere
				Functional Mobility, Balance, Gait
					No ataxia, normal gait, no foot drop
	
	.Research -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		1."Can you describe when and how your knee pain started?"
			Rationale:
				-This question helps establish the onset and progression of symptoms, which is crucial for determining if the condition is acute, chronic, or related to a specific event.
		2."What activities make your knee pain worse or better?"
			Rationale:
				-Understanding aggravating and alleviating factors can provide insights into the nature of the condition and guide treatment recommendations.
		3."Have you experienced any locking, catching, or giving way of your knee?"
			Rationale:
				-These symptoms could indicate meniscal or ligamentous involvement, helping to differentiate between various knee pathologies.
		4."Do you have any pain or stiffness in other joints?"
			Rationale:
				-This question helps determine if the condition is localized or part of a systemic issue, which is important for differential diagnosis.
		5."How does your knee feel first thing in the morning compared to later in the day?"
			Rationale:
				-Morning stiffness that improves with activity is characteristic of certain conditions like osteoarthritis.
		6."Have you tried any treatments or medications for your knee pain?"
			Rationale:
				-This information helps assess what has been effective or ineffective so far and guides future treatment planning.
		7."Has your knee pain affected your work as a history professor or your daily activities?"
			Rationale:
				-Understanding the functional impact of the condition is crucial for setting appropriate treatment goals and assessing the severity of the problem.
		8."Have you experienced any recent weight changes or changes in your overall health?"
			Rationale:
				-Weight changes can impact knee pain, and overall health changes might indicate systemic conditions affecting the knee.

		Three probable diagnoses based on the information provided:
			1. Osteoarthritis (OA) of the left knee (Most probable)
			2. Meniscal tear
			3. Patellofemoral pain syndrome

		Osteoarthritis is the most probable diagnosis for the following reasons:
			The patient's age (55 years old) is consistent with the typical onset of OA.
			The symptoms are progressive over five months, indicating a chronic condition.
			There is crepitus noted with left knee extension/flexion, which is characteristic of OA.
			The patient has a small effusion in the left knee without erythema or warmth, common in OA.
			The patient is overweight (BMI 29.0), which is a risk factor for OA.
			There is a family history of OA (mother), suggesting a potential genetic predisposition.

		While further information from the patient history would help confirm this diagnosis, the combination of age, progressive symptoms, physical findings, and risk factors strongly points towards osteoarthritis as the most likely cause of this patient's knee pain and stiffness.

.Simulation as a Patient (Me), with Student (3) Noland as a PT
	-You are a parent of a seven-year-old. Your child saw their pediatrician seven days ago for left ankle pain which developed two days prior to the appointment (nine days ago) after they had been hit a few times playing soccer.
		[Day 0]-----[Day 2]---------------------[Day 9]
		Soccer      Pediatrician              Today
		incident    visit
		& pain 
		starts
	You were told that they had sprained their left ankle and should try to move it as little as possible. However, after a week, the pain isn’t any better and seems like it might be getting worse. You called the pediatrician who recommends physical therapy as a next step rather than further medical evaluation. 
	-You and your child are incredibly frustrated. You feel like the pediatrician is not taking your child’s pain seriously. You are upset that your child has not been able to hang out with their friends or play soccer because the pain is too bad. Your feel that the pain has affected your child’s performance at school. 
	-You agree to see a physical therapist as an attempt to figure out what can be causing this pain. You feel frustrated and angry that the pediatrician has not been able to help. You want answers and are fed up with the “sprained ankle” excuse. 
	-Negative Review of Systems (ROS): Patient denies pain in any other joints. Patient denies family history of autoimmune diseases such as rheumatoid arthritis and systemic lupus erythematosus, and denies any symptoms associated with rheumatologic autoimmune diseases.

		7 seven-year-old
			left ankle pain
				hit a few times playing soccer
		saw their pediatrician 7 days ago
			You were told that
				they had sprained their left ankle and should try to move it as little as possible
		after a week from the incedence
			the pain isn’t any better and seems like it might be getting worse
				called the pediatrician
					who recommends physical therapy as a next step rather than further medical evaluation. (9 days now)

			You feel like the pediatrician is not taking your child’s pain seriously

				pain in any other joints.

					rheumatoid arthritis
					systemic lupus erythematosus

	.What you want to accomplish by the end of this appointment: 
		-Get answers as to what is happening to your ankle 
		-Vent to the PT about your horrible experience and express your expectation that the PT will not 
		be able to help you either.

	.What are typical symptoms of:
		.N/A
			Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is a chronic inflammatory disorder that primarily affects the joints. Typical symptoms include:
				Joint Pain and Swelling: Symmetrical joint involvement, particularly in the small joints of the hands and feet.
				Morning Stiffness: Lasting more than 30 minutes.
				Fatigue: Generalized tiredness and lack of energy.
				Fever: Low-grade fever may be present.
				Rheumatoid Nodules: Firm lumps under the skin, often around pressure points.
				Decreased Range of Motion: Due to joint damage and inflammation.
				Systemic Symptoms: Such as weight loss and anemia.
		.Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)
			Systemic lupus erythematosus is an autoimmune disease that can affect multiple organ systems. Typical symptoms include:
				Butterfly Rash: A characteristic rash across the cheeks and nose.
				Photosensitivity: Skin rash as a result of exposure to sunlight.
				Arthritis: Non-erosive arthritis involving two or more peripheral joints.
				Oral Ulcers: Painless sores in the mouth or nose.
				Serositis: Inflammation of the lining around the heart (pericarditis) or lungs (pleuritis).
				Renal Disorders: Such as proteinuria or cellular casts in the urine.
				Neurological Disorders: Seizures or psychosis.
				Hematologic Disorders: Hemolytic anemia, leukopenia, lymphopenia, or thrombocytopenia.
		.Rheumatologic Autoimmune Diseases
			-Rheumatologic autoimmune diseases encompass a variety of conditions characterized by inflammation and autoimmunity. Typical symptoms include:
				Joint Pain and Swelling: Common in conditions like RA and psoriatic arthritis.
				Fatigue and Malaise: Generalized tiredness and feeling unwell.
				Skin Rashes: Such as the malar rash in SLE or the psoriatic plaques in psoriatic arthritis.
				Systemic Involvement: Including organs such as the kidneys, lungs, and heart.
				Muscle Weakness: Seen in conditions like polymyositis and dermatomyositis.
				Raynaud's Phenomenon: Episodes of reduced blood flow to the fingers and toes, causing them to turn white, blue, and then red.
				Fever and Weight Loss: Common systemic symptoms.

.Reflection Questions
  -Question 1: Were you satisfied with the results of the PT appointments? If not, what could have been done differently?
  -Question 2: How did you prepare for both? Examples could be practicing with a family member, asking classmates for help with brainstorming ideas on how to best help the patient, or taking notes before simulation of how you will interact with PT as a patient or vice versa.
  -Question 3: What are two things that went well?
  -Question 4: If you could have done something differently, what would you do?

	.Rubric for the Reflection
		Depth of reflection
			Response demonstrates an in-depth reflection on, and personalization of the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach presented in the course. Viewpoints and interpretations are insightful and well supported.
		Required components
			Response thoroughly answers all questions. It is apparent that the questions were reflected on before responding
		Structure
			Writing is clear, concise, and well organized with excellent sentence/paragraph construction. Thoughts are expressed in a coherent and logical manner. There are no more than three spelling, grammar, or syntax errors per page of writing.
		Evidence and practice
			Response shows strong evidence of synthesis of ideas presented and insights gained throughout the entire course. The implications of these insights for the respondent's overall teaching practice are thoroughly detailed, as applicable.

	.Answers -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
		Reflection on Physical Therapy Appointments
			The outcome of the interview was satisfying, primarily due to the therapist's composed and competent demeanor. His ability to actively listen to my concerns and address my questions demonstrated a good level of professional empathy and expertise. What stood out most was the therapist's capacity to maintain a calm presence even when faced with my expressions of frustration, showcasing the importance of emotional intelligence in patient care.

			In preparation for this appointments, I took a multifaceted approach. I engaged in role-playing exercises with a family member, which helped me anticipate the scenario and practice articulating my concerns angry but effectively. Additionally, I revisited notes from my bachelor's degree in Health & Behavioral Sciences, refreshing my understanding of patient-provider interactions and the psychological aspects of healthcare delivery. This preparation proved me with the ability to engage in this peculiar interview.

			Reflecting on the positive aspects of the appointment, two elements went well. First, the physical therapist's ability to maintain composure in the face of my anger was remarkable. This emotional regulation not only prevented escalation but also fostered a safe environment for open communication. Second, the therapist's explanations had a calming effect on me. The clear, concise, and empathetic manner in which information was conveyed helped alleviate my anxieties and built trust in the therapeutic relationship.

			However, upon reflection, I realize that including the pediatrician in the conversation could have enhanced the overall care experience. This multidisciplinary approach would have facilitated a more comprehensive understanding of my child's condition. It would have also promoted better coordination of care.

			This experience has impacted my perspective on patient-centered care. It has reinforced the importance of emotional intelligence, clear communication, and interdisciplinary collaboration in healthcare. Moving forward, I am committed to incorporating these insights into my practice, striving to create an environment where patients feel heard, understood, and actively involved in their care journey.

7.6. Module Summary
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

-You have completed this module. Before moving on to the next module, remember to review these three key learnings from this module: 
	-Spinal cord injury of any cause will most often result in bilateral symptoms below the level of injury, compared to brain injury that results in unilateral symptoms. 
	-Spinal cord injury from compression needs to be identified ASAP as surgical decompression can greatly reduce the patient’s long-term symptoms. 
	-Multiple sclerosis and transverse myelitis are both autoimmune diseases with a significant heterogeneity of clinical manifestations and disease course.

Outcomes
	After completing this module, you can now: 
		Recognize the clinical manifestations of various types of spinal cord injury. 
		Explain the general management guidelines for patients with acute spinal cord injury. 
		Compare and contrast the presentations and courses for multiple sclerosis and transverse myelitis. 
		Describe the process of syrinx formation.

Spinal Cord Injury and Infarct (AKA spinal cord infarction ¿?) (Q&A_SpinalCordInjury&Infarct)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

What causes Spinal Cord Injuries?
	-Frequent causes of spinal cord injuries are car accidents, gunshots, and falls. Other causes include diseases such as polio, Friedreich's ataxia, etc. Unfortunately, spinal cord injuries are usually permanent and require treatment to help reduce symptoms.  
	-Watch the video for an introduction to spinal cord injury and infarct. 

Epidemiology
	Young, males
	Risk Factors:
		Spinal abnormalities 
		Congenital stenosis 
		Degenerative disease of the spine

Causes
	Trauma 
	Spinal Cord stroke 
	Demyelinating Disease 
	Tumor/Epidural Hematoma 
	Infection

Spinal Cord Injury
	Primary Injury
		Mechanical force/pressure/laceration applied to the spinal cord
	Secondary Injury
		Pathophysiology not completely understood
		Hypotension
		Hypoxia
		Inflammatory reactions
		Programmed cell death
	A primary injury includes car accidents that can cause spinal cord injury

Neurogenic Shock vs. Spinal Shock  
	Neurogenic Shock 
		Injury to autonomic pathways during brain or spinal cord Injury causing hypotension and bradycardia 
		Injury above T6 
	Spinal Shock 
		Flaccid Paralysis 
		Bowel & Bladder dysfunction 
		Loss of reflexes 
		Symptoms can be transient with later development of hyperreflexia

ASIA Classification System
	A. Complete Spinal Cord Injury Loss of motor/sensory below level of injury
	B. Incomplete Spinal Cord Injury Loss of motor below level of injury but preserved sensory below level or injury
	C. Incomplete Spinal Cord injury Preserved at most caudal level or preservation of motor 3 levels below injury
	D. Incomplete Spinal Cord injury 50% or more motor function preserved below the level of injury
	E. No new neurological deficits but noted neurological deficits from prior injury. 

Spinal Cord Tracts ('Spinal Cord Tracts')
	White Matter/UMN
	Lateral Corticospinal Tracts → Motor 
	Lateral Spinothalamic Tracts → Pain & Temperature 
	Dorsal Column → Proprioception & Vibration

Central Cord ΓSyndrome (CCS) ('Spinal Cord Injury Central Cord.png')
	Tract: Injury to anterior horn, bilateral medial corticospinal tract 
	Cause: Fall (Hyperextension, Syrinx)
	Physical Exam: Upper extremity weakness, Lower extremities can be spared. Pain 
	Tx: Surgical decompression

Anterior Cord Injury
	Anterior cord Syndrome
		Neuroanatomy
			Corticospinal Tract
			Spinothalamic Tract 
			Descending Autonomic Tracts 
		Cause
			Mechanical causes
			Spinal Cord Stroke
			Anterior Spinal Artery
		Tx
			Avoidance of hypotension
			Hypoxia

Dorsal Column Injury ('Dorsal Column Injury')
	Tract: Injury to the Dorsal Column +/additional tracts
	Cause: Tabes Dorsalis, tumor compression, MS 
	Physical Exam: Impaired position & vibration sense, Ataxic gait, urinary incontinence
	Tx: Depending on cause

Brown-Séquard Syndrome ('Brown Sequard Syndrome')
	Tract: Unilateral injury to corticospinal tract, dorsal column injury, injury to bilateral spinothalamic tracts 
	Cause: Stabbing 
	Physical Exam: Ipsilateral spastic paralysis & loss of vibration, position sense and contralateral loss of pain & temperature
	Tx: Supportive, dependent upon cause

Diagnosis ('')
	MRI
	CT

Treatment
	ABC’s
	Avoiding secondary injury Hypoxia, Hypotension
	Mean Arterial Blood Pressure (MAP) parameters > 85 mmHg post-injury
	Surgery Stabilization vs. Decompression
	Additional Treatment based on cause
		ie. Multiple Sclerosis, Tabes Dorsalis

Mini Index
	SPINAL CORD INJURY 
		1.DEFINITION
		2.RISK FACTORS
		3.CAUSES
		4.PATHOGENESIS 
		6.DIAGNOSIS AND CLASSIFICATION
		7.TREATMENT

SPINAL CORD INJURY 
	1.DEFINITION 
		-Damage to the spinal cord resulting in neurological deficits 
		-Check out the courtesy video of the Memorial Healthcare System, which interviews Ralph talking about his experience with spinal cord injury from a motor vehicle accident. ('Go Beyond a Spinal Cord Injury.mp4')
	
		A.Complete:
			-Spinal cord is completely compressed or severed and no signals can get through the injury → patients will have complete loss of function from that level distally 
		B.Incomplete:
			-Types of spinal cord injuries that only involve a portion of the spinal cord and whose symptoms depend on the anatomic areas involved. Review of the most common below. 
	2.RISK FACTORS
		A.Congenital stenosis
		B.Degenerative spinal disease 
			i.Osteophytes 
			ii.Disc degeneration 
		C.Spinal deformities → ankylosing spondylitis 
		D.Younger males
	3.CAUSES 
		A.Trauma - Trauma causes spinal cord injury by causing increased pressure on the spinal cord from shifted anatomy within a small and immobile space. The common cause is motor vehicle accidents, but gunshot wounds and stabbings can also cause them. Additionally, falls even from a modest height in patients with underlying spinal disease can lead to significant spinal cord injury. 
			i.Fracture 
			ii.Dislocation of vertebral body 
			iii.Traumatic epidermal hematoma 
			iv.Disc herniations 
			v.Osteophytes/stenosis 
		B.Stroke (infarction) - Just like in the brain, strokes can occur in the spinal cord. These most commonly involve the anterior spinal artery. 
		C.Demyelinating disease - Injury to the myelin sheath of the neuron, which leads to the disruption of the generation of neuronal impulses.  
		D.Other space-occupying lesions 
			i.Tumor 
			ii.Spontaneous epidural hematoma
			iii.Epidural abscess 
	4.PATHOGENESIS 
		A.Primary injury 
			i.The initial insult causing mechanical force, pressure, or laceration of cord 
		B.Secondary injury 
			i.The resulting inflammation and apoptosis that occurs or other complications that will worsen an injury 
				1.Hypotension 
				2.Hypoxia 
	5.NEUROANATOMY ('SpinalCordInjury&Infarct - NEUROANATOMY.png')
	6.DIAGNOSIS AND CLASSIFICATION 
		A.Diagnosis is typically made with a combination of clinical exam, CT, and MRI. MRI is the best test to visualize the spinal cord. 
		B.The American Spinal Injury Association Impairment (ASIA) Scale or International Standards for Neurological Classification of Spinal Cord Injury (ISNCSCI) is a standardized way to classify motor and sensory impairment for patients with spinal cord injury. 
			i.View a copy of the worksheet ('ASIA-ISCOS-Worksheet_10.2019_PRINT-Page-1-2.pdf')
			ii.View a more detailed explanation of the scale and review of common errors.
				('Common Errors Made During the ISNCSCI Examination (ASIA Exam).mp4')
	7.TREATMENT 
		A.Airway, breathing, circulation 
			i.Patients with high cord injury will have respiratory insufficiency or failure and need additional support 
			ii.Neurogenic or spinal shock may require augmentation of blood pressure with fluids and vasopressor medication 
		B.Avoiding secondary injury  
			i.Hypotension → generally have BP goals in the acute phase of injury 
				1.Mean arterial pressure (MAP) >85 mmHg 
			ii.Hypoxia 
			iii.Spinal precautions:  
				1.Avoid moving a patient with potentially unstable injury 
				2.Cervical collar and/or backboard with flatbed → if you see this do not move the patient until authorized by medical provider 
				3.Surgical stabilization or decompression  
		D.Treat underlying cause   

INCOMPLETE SPINAL CORD INJURIES 
	8.CENTRAL CORD SYNDROME 
		A.Injury to anterior horn, bilateral lateral corticospinal tracts 
			i.Cervical and Thoracic portions of lateral corticospinal tract 
			ii.Cervical and Thoracic portions of spinothalamic tract 
		B.Cause 
			i.Hyperextension injury from fall 
			ii.Syrinx (Syrinomyelogia)
		C.Clinical Manifestations 
			i.Upper extremity weakness and numbness > lower extremity weakness 
				1.Distal extremities typically more affected than proximal due to cervical and thoracic sections of the spinothalamic and corticospinal tracts located more medially in the spinal cord 	
			ii.Dysesthesia of distal extremities → cutaneous symptom like burning, itching, tingling without a primary skin issue due to nerve injury 
		D.Treatment: Supporting with or without surgical decompression 
	9.ANTERIOR CORD INJURY 
		Injury to the anterior portion of the cord 
			i.Corticospinal tracts affected because of the involvement of the anterior horn 
			ii.Spinothalamic tracts affected due to their anatomic location of the synapse in the anterior portion of the cord before going to the nerve root 
			iii.Descending autonomic tracts 
		A.Cause 
			i.Most commonly from anterior spinal artery infarct → stroke 
			ii.Trauma 
		B.Clinical Manifestations 
			i.Weakness below the level of injury 
				1.Increased muscle tone 
				2.Spasticity 
				3.Hyperreflexia  
			ii.Loss of pain and temperature sensation below the level of injury 
			iii.Position and vibration sense spared 
		C.Treatment: Supportive
	10.DORSAL COLUMN INJURY 
		Injury to dorsal columns of spinal cord 
		A.Cause 
			i.Tabes dorsalis 
			ii.Posterior compression from: 
				1.Tumor 
				2.Hematoma 
				3.Abscess 
			iii.Degenerative processes favoring the dorsal tract 
				1.Multiple sclerosis 
				2.Friederichs ataxia 
		B.Clinical Manifestations 
			i.Loss of position and vibration sense 
			ii.Ataxic gait 
			iii.Frequent falls 
		C.Treatment: Supportive and treat underlying cause is possible  
	11.BROWN SEQUARD SYNDROME 
		Unilateral injury to both the posterior and anterior sections of spinal cord 
		A.Cause 
			i.Most commonly from stab wound 
		B.	Clinical Manifestations 
			i.Ipsilateral loss of motor and position  
				1.Corticospinal tracts and dorsal columns cross above the spinal canal 
			ii.Contralateral loss of pain and temperature 
				1.Spinothalamic tracts cross within the spinal canal  
		C.Diagnosis: CT and MRI 
		D.Treatment: Supportive

PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
	1.Autonomic Dysreflexia (T6 and above): 
		a.48-70% of patients will develop https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK482434/ → ('Autonomic Dysreflexia.pdf')
		b.Dangerous and sudden increase in systolic and diastolic BP > 25 mmHg, headache, flushing of face, torso, goose bumps at level of lesion, blurred vision, visual field changes, nasal congestion, increased anxiety, arrhythmia
		c.Triggers (not limited to)
			i.Full bladder or bowel
			ii.Scrotal compression
			iii.Changes in temperature
			iv.Irritant below level of lesion
			v.Tight constrictive clothing
			vi.DVT/PE ΓDeep Vein Thrombosis/Pulmonary Embolism
			vii.First line treatment is to remove the trigger and sit patient upright. 
	2.Complete SCI
		a.Respiratory management, pressure relief/positioning, ROM, wheelchair prescription
			i.Highlight hamstring length.
			ii.Maintain tenodesis for C6/C7 injury.
		b.Monitor closely for secondary complications: hypotension (abdominal binder), pressure ulcers, pneumonia, PE/DVT.
	3.Incomplete SCI: 
		a.CPG: Clinical Guideline to Improve Locomotor Function Following Chronic Stroke, Incomplete Spinal Cord Injury, and Brain Injury. https://journals.lww.com/jnpt/Fulltext/2020/01000/Clinical_Practice_Guideline_to_Improve_Locomotor.8.aspx → ('clinical_practice_guideline_to_improve_locomotor.8.pdf')
		b.Orthotics
		c.Monitor closely for secondary complications: hypotension (abdominal binder), pressure ulcers, pneumonia, PE/DVT.
	4.Benefits of Exercise: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41393-017-0017-3 → ('Evidence-based scientific exercise guidelines.pdf')
		-Martin Ginis, K.A., van der Scheer, J.W., Latimer-Cheung, A.E. et al. Evidence-based scientific exercise guidelines for adults with spinal cord injury: an update and a new guideline. Spinal Cord 56, 308–321 (2018).
	5.Further injury prevention
		a.Hold PT until imaging complete and medical intervention determined.
		b.Report a progression or increase in symptoms (numbness, tingling, weakness) to medical team and hold PT until imaging and medical plan determined. 
		c.Bracing: (abdominal binder, LSO, TLSO, halo, Cervical collar)
			i.Check for order.
			ii.Typically donned while sitting EOB unless otherwise specified in order.
		d.Spine Precautions (“BLT Sandwich”)
			i.NO Bending
			ii.NO Lifting > 5 lbs
			iii.NO Twisting
			iv.NO Sitting > 30 min without changing position
		e.Log Roll 
		f.Use precautions with risk factors: osteophytes, disc degeneration, ankylosing spondylitis, congenital stenosis
			i.Ex Compression Fracture: Trauma (fall, jump, cough) or weakening of vertebra due to osteoporosis, osteogenesis imperfecta, lytic lesions, or infection.
				1.Utilize spine precautions and log roll technique.
				2.Brace may be ordered for stability or for comfort.

Tabes Dorsalis (Q&A_TabesDorsalis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Les Fluers du Mal
	-Charles Baudelaire, was a French poet who produced poetry on controversial themes of sex, lesbianism, metamorphosis, depression, corruption, and lost innocence. His themes quickly gained him, loyal followers. His best-known volume of poems is titled “Les Fleurs du mal,” which expresses the changing nature of beauty in Paris.
		('https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Fleurs_du_mal')
	-His career came to a halt when he contracted syphilis in 1839 and became a prisoner to opium to help alleviate the pain of tabes doralis. Later he died in 1883, due to complications of syphilis and tabes doralis, at the age of 51. 
	-Watch the video for an introduction to tabes doralis. 

Syphilis 
	Treponema pallidum Gram negative spirochete
		Limited information regarding pathogenesis
		Nationally reportable disease
	Increase in incidence, particularly in females
	Risk factors: HIV, IV drug abuse

Syphilis
	Early
	Secondary
	Late

Neurosyphilis 
	Early Neurosyphilis 
	Late Neurosyphilis / Tertiary Syphilis
		General paresis 
		Tabes Dorsalis

Tabes Dorsalis AKA “Locomotor ataxia”
	Dorsal columns involvement
		Proprioception & vibration deficits 
		Sensory ataxia 
		Charcot deformities 
	Dorsal Root involvement 
		Absent lower extremity reflexes
		Lancinating pains
	Midbrain
		Lesion dorsal to Edinger-Westphal → Argyll-Robertson Pupil

Argyll-Robertson Pupil ('Argyll-Robertson Pupil')
	Pupils DO NOT constrict when exposed to bright light. ("light reflex")
	Pupils DO constrict on a near object. ("accommodation reflex")

Diagnosis ('')
	CSF Analysis
	MRI with and without Gadolinium 
		Candle guttering 
	Serum Treponemal 
		VDRL 
		RPR 
	Serum Non-Treponemal

Treatment 
	PCN G 3-4 million units q 4 H X 1014 days 
	PCN G IM daily + probenecide 500 units 4 x daily
