		PCN IM bi-weekly + probenecide 1000 units 2 x daily
		PCN F 3-4 million units IV q4 H x 10-14 days
		PCN G 9-10 million units q 4 H X 5-7 days
		PCN G IM daily + probenecide 500 units 4 x daily

		✗PCN IM bi-weekly + probenecide 1000 units 2 x daily

		✗PCN G 9-10 million units q 4 H X 5-7 days
		PCN G IM daily + probenecide 500 units 4 x daily

('Tabes Dorsalis Study Guide (1).docx')
	TABES DORSALIS
		1.DEFINITION
			-Type of late neurosyphilis that involves progressive degeneration of the dorsal columns of the spinal canal and dorsal nerve roots. 
			-Syphilis: Nationally reportable disease caused by the spirochete Treponema pallidum. The rates of syphilis have been increasing in recent years, particularly in females. Increased rates of syphilis in females have led to a higher incidence of congenital syphilis, which is syphilis that has spread to a fetus from a mother. 
				A.Primary: Painless ulcer (chancre) 
				B.Secondary: Occurs without treatment. Patients develop generalized symptoms like fatigue, malaise, fever and a maculopapular rash on the trunk and palms.  
				C.Late/Tertiary: Cardiac problems, gummatous lesions (on skin and internal organs), and neurosyphilis.  
				D.Neurosyphilis: Occurs on average 10-25 years from infection 
					i.Early: Involves the CSF, meninges, and blood vessels. Ex: syphilitic meningitis 
					ii.Late: Involves actual brain/spine tissue, not just the surrounding structures 
						1.General paresis Γ(More psychologcal effects)
						2.Tabes dorsalis Γ(More physical effects)
		2.NEUROANATOMY
		3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS [CM_Tabes Dorsalis]
			A.Dorsal column involvement 
				i.Proprioception and vibration deficits
				ii.Ataxic wide based gate
					1.Can’t tell where their feet are in space, so they need to watch their feet while walking 
				iii.Sensory ataxia -> can’t feel their feet -> increased risk for trauma and charcot deformities 
			B.Dorsal Root Involvement 
				i.Absent lower extremity reflexes 
				ii.Lancinating pains -> pain shoots up their legs and into their back 
			C.Midbrain 
				i.Loss of visual acuity 
				ii.Argyll-Robertson Pupil -> small pupils that constrict to accommodation but not to light 
		4.DIAGNOSIS  
			-Diagnosis of tabes dorsalis can be difficult. There is no one definitive test, but rather clinical diagnosis based on known or newly diagnosed syphilis in the setting of the above symptoms, with or without some of the below tests if, they are positive.
				A.Lumbar puncture 
					i.Often normal  
					ii.CSF VDRL test of choice, but is not 100% sensitive, i.e., it can be negative in a patient with neurosyphilis. 
				B.MRI – can show findings associated with tabes dorsalis but cannot be used for diagnosis 
				C.Bloodwork to demonstrate t. pallidum infection in a patient without a known history 
					i.Serum Non-treponemal test -> will always be positive in a patient who has ever had syphilis. 
					ii.VDRL and RPR are often used to diagnose syphilis in the early stages, but they can be negative in neurosyphilis. 
		5.TREATMENT 
			A.Penicillin – either intravenously or intramuscularly for 10-14 days. 

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.Intervention
				a.Balance
				b.Transfer and gait training – consider assistive device
				c.Strength
				d.Endurance
				e.Coordination

Syringomyelia (Q&A_Syringomyelia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Weakness of Limbs
	-Most causes of syringomyelia are associated with chiari type 1 malformation. However, trauma, tumors, and hemorrhage can also cause syringomyelia. Some patients with syringomyelia may struggle with walking or have permanent weakness with limbs. After treatment, is hoped that these conditions will decrease and improve over time.  

What is Syringomyelia?

Syrinx
	Fluid filled sac in the central canal or the spinal cord
	Most common in the cervical spine​
	Asymptomatic vs. complicated neurologic deficits

Causes
	Chiari malformation 
	Meningitis, arachnoiditis 
	Trauma 
	Spinal cord tumors 

Symptoms
	Pain
	Loss of pain/temperature sense in upper extremities
	“Cape-like distribution”​
	Muscle atrophybilateral hands​
	Weaknessspastic vs. flaccid​
	Bowel & bladder disturbances

			Cervical
         Anterior corticospinal tract

         Cuneate fasciculus

         Anterior spino cerebellar tract
      Anterolateral System

         Anterior spinothalamic tract
      Anterior horn of grey matter

('Syrinomyelogia Study Guide (1).docx')
	SYRINGOMYELOGIA 
		1.DEFINITION 
			-Also called syrinx. It is a fluid-filled sac in the center of the spinal cord that causes injury to the surrounding spinal cord as they enlarge. They most commonly occur in the cervical portion of the cord, but can also occur in the thoracic region.  
		2.CAUSES 
			-Syrinx is caused by a backup of CSF due to impairment of CSF flow either from structural impedance, as is the case with Chiari malformation, trauma, and tumors, or inflammatory mediators clogging the drain, as is the case with meningitis. 
				A.Chiari malformation  
				B.Meningitis  
				C.Trauma -> can occur years after the traumatic event 
				D.Spinal cord tumors 
		3.NEUROANATOMY 
		4.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS [CM_Syrinomyelogia]
			-The symptoms of syrinx do not always correlate to the size of the syrinx. Some patients can have a large syrinx and be asymptomatic while others can have significant deficits from a relatively small syrinx.   
			-The symptoms are related to the area of the spinal cord that is being smooshed. You’ll notice many of these symptoms are the same as central cord because if the syrinx develops uniformly, those central structures will be affected. However, syrinx can grow more towards one side or more towards the anterior or posterior aspects of the spine, and so the symptoms will be different.
				A.Pain at the level of the syrinx
				B.Loss of pain and temperature of the upper extremities -> cape like distribution
				C.Upper extremity muscle atrophy -> especially in the hands
				D.Ipsilateral weakness: Can be either flaccid or spastic depending on area of the cord involved
					ΓFlaccid Paralysis: characterized by weakness or paralysis and reduced muscle tone without other obvious cause
					ΓSpastic Paralysis: characterized by increased muscle tone (hypertonia) and exaggerated reflexes (hyperreflexia)
					------------------------------------------------------------------------
					i.Lateral corticospinal tract -> upper motor neuron -> spastic and stiff
					ii.Anterior horn (gray matter) -> lower motor neuron -> flaccid and floppy
				E.Bowel and bladder dysfunction (in later disease)
		5.DIAGNOSIS
			A.MRI
		6.TREATMENT
			A.Asymptomatic -> either monitor with physical exam and serial MRIs or treat prior to developing symptoms
			B.Surgery
				i.Syrinx stent or shunt
				ii.Decompression of obstructive lesion (Chiari, tumor)

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATONS
			1.Focus on functional mobility (transfers, gait, w/c mobility)
			2.Maintain ROM

Multiple Sclerosis (Q&A_MultipleSclerosis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Most widespread neurological condition among young adults
	What does this comic have in common with multiple sclerosis (click image to zoom in)? If you don’t get it, that’s okay, return back to the comic once you are done reviewing the video lecture. 

	A person can develop multiple sclerosis (MS) at any age but most people receive a diagnosis between the ages of 20-50 years old. In multiple studies conducted by the Multiple Sclerosis International Federation, it has shown that MS is the most widespread neurological condition that affects young adults around the world.  

	Fortunately, many people with MS don’t develop severe symptoms and can live a normal life span.  

	Watch the video for an introduction to multiple sclerosis. 

Multiple Sclerosis 
	Autoimmune inflammatory neurodegenerative disease
	Attacks the myelin cells
	CNS plaques with demyelination, gliosis and inflammation
	Females > Male predominance
	Multiple Phenotypes

Multiple Sclerosis Phenotypes
	Clinically Isolated Syndrome 
	Relapsing Remitting Disease
	Secondary Progressive Disease 
	Primary Progressive Disease

Multiple Sclerosis Phenotype
	Clinically Isolated Syndrome
	Solitary focal neurological event suggestive of a Multiple Sclerosis episode without known Multiple Sclerosis diagnosis 
	Symptoms > 24 hours 
	No evidence of infection
	------------------------------------------------------------
	Relapsing-Remitting Disease 
		Most common type 85-90% of cases 
		”Attacks” followed by period of variable recovery
	↓
	Secondary Progressive 
		Transition from Relapse-Remitting with more progressive disease
		Occurs 10-20 years from initial diagnosis

Primary Progressive 
	Later onset approximately 40 years old 
	M=F 
	Progressive Disease Process No intervals of recovery

Types of Multiple Sclerosis ('Types of Multiple Sclerosis')
	Relapsing-Remitting MS (RRMS)
	Secondary-Progressive MS (SPMS)
	Primary-Progressive MS (PPMS)
	Progressive-Relapsing (PRMS)

Symptoms 
	Lhermitte’s Syndrome 
	Optic Neuritis 
	Marcus Gunn Pupil 
	Intranuclear Ophthalmoplegia
	----------------------------
	sensory symptoms
	motor symptoms
	bowel/bladder dysfunction
	additional symptoms

Multiple Sclerosis 
	Dissemination in space 
	Dissemination in time

MacDonald 2017 Criteria ('MacDonald 2017 Criteria')

Primary Progressive 
	Dx symptoms over 1 year 
	+ MRI Brain with at least 2 lesions in stereotypical locations 
	+ MRI Spine with at least 2 lesions 
	Oligoclonal bands in spinal fluids

Diagnostic Therapies 
	MRI Brain/Spinal cord with contract
	CSF
	Evoked Potentials
	Optical Coherence Topograph
	Serum Antibodies

Treatment 
	Acute Flair
		High Dose steroids (IV vs. PO)
		Corticotropin gel
		PLEX, ACTH
	Chronic disease
		Disease modifying therapies
		Symptom management

Knowledge Checker
	Which of these patients is showing clinical manifestations of multiple sclerosis?
		A 25-year-old female develops progressive weakness in her feet and legs two weeks after having a viral upper respiratory infection.
		A 21-year-old male has noticed that he has declined in his memory and concentration.
		A 28-year-old female has complained of monocular painful vision loss for two days.

		A 28-year-old female has complained of monocular painful vision loss for two days.
			25-year-old patient is showing clinical manifestations of syrinomyelogia
			21-year-old patient is showing clinical manifestations of hydrocephalus

('Multiple Sclerosis Study Guide.docx')
	MULTIPLE SCLEROSIS 
		1.DEFINITION
			-Autoimmune inflammatory neurodegenerative disease. It is the most common permanently disabling disorder of the central nervous system in young adults. Typical age of onset is between 28-31 years. MS is more prevalent in females than males. 
			-Check out ('Living with multiple sclerosis _ Felix's story.mp4') ('Living with Progressive MS_ Jeanne's Story.mp4')
		2.PHENOTYPES ('MS PHENOTYPES.png')
			A.Clinically Isolated Syndrome -> first symptom that suggests a patient may go on to develop MS, i.e., the “first attack” 
			B.Relapsing-Remitting -> most common type (85-90% of patients). Attacks followed by periods of variable recovery 
			C.Secondary Progressive - transition from relapsing-remitting to a more progressive disease. Typically occurs 10-20 years from initial diagnosis 
			D.Primary Progressive -> Later onset ~ 40 years old, prevalence between men and women equal. Rapid progressive decline. 
		3.PATHOGENESIS 
			-Combination of a genetic predisposition and an environmental trigger causes an autoimmune inflammatory attack on the myelin and axons of the central nervous system that lead to plaques. The location of these plaques determines the clinical manifestations of MS. 
				A.CNS plaques -> areas of demyelination, hypertrophy of glial cells (gliosis) and inflammation in both white and gray matter that are visible on MRI. 
				B.Common areas involved: 
					i.Periventricular (around the ventricles) white matter 
					ii.Optic nerve 
					iii.Spinal cord 
					iv.Brainstem 
					v.Cerebellum 
					vi.Juxtacortical (next to the cortex) white matter 
		4.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS [CM_Multiple Sclerosis]
			-The symptoms of multiple sclerosis are highly variable as they depend on where the CNS plaques occur. However, there are some symptoms that are more common. For many patients, symptoms will present during an attack and then improve to some degree.  Physiologic stress, particularly fever and infection, can worsen current symptoms or cause new symptoms to occur. 
				A.Classic Symptoms 
					i.Lhermitte’s sign 
						1.Electrical impulse that shoots down the back when a patient’s head is flexed (chin to chest)  
					ii.Optic neuritis 
						1.Painful monocular vision loss that develops over hours to days with pain that worsens with lateral gaze 
						2.Common presenting symptom 
					iii.Marcus Gunn pupil 
						1.Relative afferent pupillary defect ('MS Relative Afferent Pupillary Defect.mp4') 
					iv.Intranuclear ophthalmoplegia 
						1.Slowed or absent adduction of one eye ('Internuclear Ophthalmoplegia.mp4')
				B.Sensory symptoms 
					i.Often due to injury to the spinothalamic tract or dorsal column in the spinal cord 
					ii.Pins and needles most common 
				C.Motor symptoms 
					i.Often due to corticospinal tract injury in the spinal cord 
					ii.Upper motor neuron symptoms: increased tone, spasticity, hemiplegia 
				D.Bowel and bladder dysfunction 
					i.Either difficulty with retention/constipation or incontinence 
				E.Other general symptoms 
					i.Generalized pain 
					ii.Fatigue 
					iii.Depression 
					iv.Ataxia/balance difficult -> if cerebellum involved. 
		5.DIAGNOSIS  
			-Clinical diagnosis involving dissemination over space and time. Typically need history, physical exam, and MRI with contrast of brain and spinal cord.  
					A.Space: Two distinct areas of plaques on MRI in the typical MS locations (see above) 
					B.Time:  
						i.History of more than one attack that signify two areas of CNS involvement 
							1.Ex: Previous episode of hand pins and needles and now patient complaining of painful vision loss in left eye 
						ii.Comparing old MRI with a new MRI that shows new plaques in a different area 
						iii.A single MRI with contrast where one lesion enhances and another does not signify a difference in age of the lesions 
					C.McDonald 2017 Criteria 
					D.Cerebrospinal fluid analysis: 95% will have oligoclonal IgG bands 
					E.Evoked potentials: Detect demyelinating lesions when there are not clear lesions on MRI 
					F.Optical coherence tomography (OCT) -> used for optic neuritis 
					G.Serum antibodies 
		6.TREATMENT 
			A.Rule out infectious etiology  
			B.Acute flair 
				i.High dose steroids for 3-7 days or corticotropin gel 
				ii.Plasmapheresis -> washes out the plasma of the blood to get rid of the autoantibodies that are attacking the myelin 
			C.Chronic disease 
				i.Disease modifying agents (DMARDs) -> takes ~ six months to see effects, used mainly for patients in the relapsing-remitting phenotype 
					1.Monocloncal antibodies (mabs) 
					2.Methotrexate 
				ii.Symptom management -> for all patients but focused especially for patients in a progressive phenotype, either primary or secondary 
					1.National Multiple Sclerosis Society: Physical Therapy in Multiple Sclerosis 

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.Fatigue and extreme temperatures (high or low) can exacerbate symptoms!
				a.May be immediate or delayed.
				b.Build in increased rest time.
				c.Encourage fluid intake
			2.Modify program or hold PT during acute flairs.
			3.High fall risk 
			4.Early stages 
				a.Benefits of exercise: decrease cardiovascular risk, improve quality of life, decrease depression, improve walking speed, balance and strength
				b.FITTE: Aerobic 2-5 days/wk, 40-70% with RPE 12-15, progress gradually; Resistance 2 days/wk, 60-8-% 1 RM, gradual progression; Flexibility 5-7 days/wk, 1-2x/day, hold 30-6- sec. 
			5.Later stages – shift to accommodative/adaptive
				a.Wheelchair prescription, positioning/pressure relief, safety with mobility
				b.Communicate with medical team if spasticity impairs function/positioning.

Transverse Myelitis (Q&A_TransverseMyelitis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Transverse myelitis or M.S?
	Transverse myelitis is a condition where a section of the spinal cord gets inflamed. As a result of this, communication between nerve cells in the spinal cord and the whole body can be interrupted.

	Although M.S and transverse myelitis share some common symptoms, typically transverse myelitis is a one-time illness that can lead to M.S. Also, patients with transverse myelitis commonly experience back pain as the first symptom. 

	Watch the video for an introduction to transverse myelitis. 

Transverse Myelitis 
	Inflammatory process that injures myelin and axons of the central nervous system (CNS)
	Disruption of motor, sensor and autonomic pathways
	Symptoms reflect anatomical location of inflammatory lesion
	Bimodal incidence 10-19 years old & 30-39 years old 
	Equal sex predominance

Causes
	Demyelinating
	Systemic Inflammatory Disease 
	Infectious
	Paraneoplastic 
	Idiopathic (30%)

Symptoms
	Pain 
	Motor
		paresis → paralysis
		Flaccid vs. Spastic 
	Sensory → Sensory Level 
		“Anaconda squeeze”
	Autonomic symptoms → Bowel & Bladder dysfunction
	Onset Hours to days 
	Reflect complete or incomplete lesion
		Bilateral Symptoms vs. Unilateral 
	Brain vs. Spine 
		Face involvement, hemiparalysis, hemisensory deficit (vs.) Motor, sensory symptoms below the level of injury
	Demographics 
	History 
		Recent infection 
		Immunization
	Trauma

Diagnostic work-up 
	MRI Spine w/ and w/o gadolinium 
		T2 hyperintensity Size, location of lesions can help focus differentials 
		Enhancement 
	MRI Brain with and w/o gadolinium
		Lesions and found suggest demyelinating process such as Multiple Sclerosis 
	Lumbar puncture 
		CSF analysis → cell count, oligoclonal bands

MRI Spine

Treatment 
	Corticosteroids
		IV, High-dose = 1gm methylprednisolone X 3-5 days
	PLEX
	Cytoxan

Knowledge Checker
	Transverse Myelitis
		Sharp pain in the lower back
		Anaconda squeeze
	M.S
		Marcus gunn pupil
		Painful vision loss
	Both
		Muscle weakness
		Bowel and bladder problems

('Transverse Myelitis Study Guide (1).docx')
	TRANSVERSE MYELITIS 
		1.DEFINITION 
			-Inflammatory process that injures myelin and axons of the central nervous system creating a space occupying lesion within the white and gray matter of the spinal cord. This lesion causes disruption of motor, sensory, and autonomic pathways.  
		2.CAUSES 
			-It is important to identify the cause of transverse myelitis to ensure patients receive appropriate treatment. They share a similar pathogenesis in that they are all believed to be the instigating or precipitating factor for the abnormal immune response that leads to myelin and axonal injury 
				A.Demyelinating disorder – patients develop as part of their demyelinating disorder or could be the first symptom that they have a demyelinating disorder 
					i.Ex: Multiple sclerosis, Neuromyelitis optima 
						1.Often will present with incomplete transverse myelitis 
				B.Systemic inflammatory disease – patients develop as part of their autoimmune disease 
					i.Ex: Systemic lupus erythematosus, rheumatoid arthritis, etc. 
				C.Post-infectious – patients develop after a viral infection 
					i.Ex: West Nile, HSV, HIV 
				D.Paraneoplastic – patients develop as a complication of their cancer 
					i.Ex: small cell lung cancer 
				E.Idiopathic (30%) – no identifiable cause is found 
		3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS [CM_Transverse Myelitis]
			-The symptoms of transverse myelitis are highly variable as they depend on where the lesions occur. It typically occurs in a bimodal distribution affecting patients 10-19 years and 30-39 years of age.  
			-Symptoms typically have an acute onset developing over hours to days. Patients with complete transverse myelitis involving an entire cross section of the cord will have bilateral symptoms below the level involved. Patients with incomplete transverse myelitis involving only one side of a cross section of the cord will have unilateral symptoms that may resemble Brown Sequard syndrome. 
				A.Pain 
					i. Typically first complain about back pain 
				B.Sensory symptoms 
					i.“Anaconda squeeze” 
					ii.Distinct sensory level involvement 
				C.Motor symptoms 
					i.Initially since it is acute, weakness will be flaccid but if the lesion involves the corticospinal tract the weakness will progress to spastic  
				D.Bowel and bladder dysfunction 
		4.DIAGNOSIS  
			-Transverse myelitis is a clinical diagnosis. It requires putting together the patient’s history and physical exam with MRI imaging and lumbar puncture. No one single test is definitive for transverse myelitis. It needs to be a diagnosis of exclusion.  
				A.First step: Need to get MRI to look for compressive lesion that would require emergent neurosurgical intervention 
					i.MRI spine with and without contrast -> look for lesion 
					ii.MRI brain with and without contrast -> look for evidence of demyelinating process like multiple sclerosis 
				B.Lumbar puncture:  
					i.Cell count – see if suggestive of infection 
					ii.Oligoclonal bands – suggestive of multiple sclerosis 
					iii.Paraneoplastic panel – will help to see if cancer is precipitating cause 
				C.Bloodwork 
					i.Rheumatologic panel – look for underlying autoimmune case 
					ii.Vitamin levels – Nutritional deficiencies can mimic transverse myelitis 
						1.Ex: B12, copper 
		5.TREATMENT 
			A.Acute 
				i.High dose intravenous steroids -> first line! 
				ii.Plasmapheresis 
				iii.Cytoxan 
			B.Chronic 
				i.Supportive care of potential complications 
				ii.Check out the informational page on long term transverse myelitis care from Johns Hopkins Medicine ¿? 
				iii.Please see the case report “Physical Therapy for Transverse Myelitis” in the “additional resources” section for a more detailed example of a physical therapy program. 
		6.PROGNOSIS 
			-The prognosis depends on the underlying cause. Most patients with transverse myelitis will have some improvement in symptoms and some may have near or complete resolution. Improvement in symptoms can take months or even years. 

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.Fatigue and High or low temperatures can exacerbate symptoms.

Module 8 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

This is the final module that will focus on neurologic disorders. In particular, you will be learning more about musculoskeletal disorders. 

Similar to the last two modules, there will be some similarities between the diseases covered this week. Focus on what makes them different, specifically, what differentiates myasthenia gravis and Guillain-Barre. You will also be learning more of the final human skill of the PT 2045 Patient-Centered Approach, effective communication. 

While the final PT 2045 Patient-Centered simulation final won’t be due until the end of Module 8, it will also be opening in this module to give you enough time to complete the assignment. 

Learning Objectives
	By the end of this week, you will be able to: 
		Compare and contrast the clinical manifestations and prognosis for common demyelinating and neurodegenerative diseases. 
		Differentiate between two common autoimmune neurologic diseases: myasthenia gravis and Guillain-Barre. 
		Discuss the common causes of peripheral neuropathy.
		Recognize the role physical therapists have in improving patients’ quality of life with these diseases.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
8.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Fibromyalgia 
-Definition: Syndrome characterized by widespread pain affecting soft tissues such as muscles, ligaments, and tendons, without evidence of tissue inflammation.
-Etymology: fibr/o - fiber, my/o - muscle, -algia – pain

Ophthalmoplegia 
Definition: Complete paralysis of the extraocular muscles. 
Etymology: Ophthalm/o -  eye (Greek) -plegia - paralysis

Dysautonomia 
-Definition: Dysfunction of the autonomic nervous system.
-Etymology: Dys – bad/malfunctioning, Auntono – from autónomos, “living under one's own laws, independent” (Greek) - referring here to autonomic nervous system, -omia - 

Electromyography (EMG)  
Definition: Procedure that records electrical activity of muscles. 
Etymology: Electr/o - electricity, my/o - muscle, -graphy – process of recording

Gastrostomy tube 
Definition: Feeding tube that goes directly from the skin and into the stomach.
Etymology: Gastr/o - stomach, -omy (oma) Greek stóma, “mouth”

Radiculopathy  
Definition: Symptoms related to compression or injury of a nerve root, rather than a peripheral nerve.
Etymology: Radic – root (Latin) -pathy “suffering or disease” (Greek)

.In-Class Active Listening Discussion and Reflection Instructions 
	How often do you find yourself staring at a patient pretending you are listening while they are talking but, at the same time, you are drifting off to some other thought? Or how often do you find yourself thinking of how you will respond to a patient before they are done talking? 

	If you have done any of those two, you are not alone, we are all guilty of having done that. Active listening is about being deeply engaged and attentive to what the other person is saying without judging or criticizing them. This requires more listening and less talking, with the intention to increase understanding. A PT cannot become an effective communicator without first being an active listener. Active listening enables a PT to more effectively absorb and pass on information gleaned during a conversation with a patient. When a PT actively listens to a patient it conveys respect for their self-knowledge and builds trust. Likewise, it decreases the chances of misunderstanding what the patient or PT really said.  

	In this assignment, you will be learning the third skill of the PT2045 Patient-Centered Approach, active listening. You will analyze how to put it into practice and the impact it has on developing trust, avoiding misunderstandings, and avoiding conflicts.

	1.In-class, we will watch the the TED talk on "The Power of Deliberate Listening"
		('The Power of Deliberate LIstening.mp4') (See Folder: '8.5. Class')
	2.Reflect on the TedTalk we just watched and answer the following questions:  
		Did you learn anything new from this lecture?  
		Where do you think you currently are in terms of your active listening?  
		What are some key areas of strengths and weaknesses?  
		What will you change to strengthen your active listening skills?  

	Submission Requirements
		Your response should be between 200-300 words 
		Your response should pose critical questions, highlight information and ideas from the content, and show critical reflection on your relevant personal experience.

References ↓ ('https://www.concorde.edu/blog/active-listening-matters-health-care')
	ACTIVE LISTENING MATTERS IN HEALTH CARE
		More than 10 years ago, a Conflict Research Consortium at the University of Colorado defined active listening as "a way of listening and responding to another person that improves mutual understanding."

		Let's face it, how often have we found ourselves not so much staring at a person and pretending to be listening to what they're saying to you as much as staring through that person and drifting off to some other thought?

		No need to feel shame or embarrassment. We've all done it. Especially in this modern digital age, where distractions are abundant and easily engaged, taking our minds to places often off-topic and, at best, superfluous.

		Active listening is a structured form of listening and responding that focuses the attention on the speaker. When it comes to working in health care, it's even more important that we be active listeners.

		That's because the speaker, in many instances, is the patient, who demands and deserves the full attention of the health care professional providing care. The speaker also could be the physician or practitioner whom you're working under, so it's important that you give full attention to what they're telling you, as well.

		Absorbing information through active listening
		Nick Ewell, JD, Campus President at Concordes Garden Grove, Calif. campus, said active listeners are able to more effectively absorb and pass on information gleaned during a conversation or lecture.

		While this might seem like common sense, Ewell said, the practical application requires discipline and a specific plan. A few "tips and tricks" you can employ right away:

		Eliminate any potential distractions, such as cell phones, laptops and side conversations
		Take time to consider your responses before speaking
		Repeat back the information presented
		Knowing that you will be giving this summation at the end will help you retain focus. Additionally, the summation and follow-up questions will often reveal deeper insights from the material presented. This might sound difficult, Ewell said, but all you need to do is summarize the conversation and then lead with an open-ended question like, "Does that make sense?" or "Did I get that right?"

		Strengthening relationships through active listening
		According to the Institute for Healthcare Communication (IHC), research evidence indicates there are strong positive relationships between a health care team member's communication skills and a patient's capacity to follow through with medical recommendations, self-manage a chronic medical condition and adopt preventive health behaviors.

		A clinician's ability to explain, listen and empathize can have a profound effect on biological and functional health outcomes as well as patient satisfaction.

		It is estimated that one-third of adults with chronic illnesses underused their prescription medication because of cost concerns, yet they failed to communicate this information to their physician.

		Another IHC study found that less than half of hospitalized patients could identify their diagnoses or the names of their medications, an indication of ineffectual communication with their health care provider.

		That's where active listening comes in.

		Other benefits include:

		It avoids misunderstandings, as people have to confirm that they do really understand what another person has said
		It tends to open people up, to get them to say more
		It helps people avoid conflicts, because people become more attuned to concerns and don't feel as though they're being dismissed
		Improving health care outcomes through active listening
		The Institute of Medicine (IOM) Report on Health Professions and Training underscores the importance of communication training for clinicians and members of the health care team. Similar to other health care procedures, communication skills need to be practiced and improved upon.

		"As we are increasingly bombarded with stimuli, both auditory and visual, we can find it difficult to focus attention on just one source of information," Ewell said. "That is precisely what we are called to do, however, when we learn a new skill or gather important information."

		"It is for this reason that we must work to become more conscious, engaged and ACTIVE listeners."

.Question 1
Match the following terms with their appropriate definition.
    Myasthenia Gravis
    Guillain-Barre
    Spinal Muscle Atrophy
    Fibromyalgia
    ALS
    Peripheral neuropathy

	autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disease that causes destruction of the anterior horn cells.
	autoimmune condition in which patients develop autoantibodies to peripheral nerves after a trigger — like an infection.
	Autoimmune disease that affects skeletal muscle by attacking the neuromuscular junction.
	general term for injury to a nerve once it leaves the spinal cord or brainstem.
	syndrome characterized by widespread pain affecting soft tissues and associated with migraine IBS and depression.
	Progressive neurodegenerative disease affecting the motor neurons of the brain and spinal cord (upper and lower motor neurons)

	1. Myasthenia Gravis: Autoimmune disease that affects skeletal muscle by attacking the neuromuscular junction.
	2. Guillain-Barre: Autoimmune condition in which patients develop autoantibodies to peripheral nerves after a trigger — like an infection.
	3. Spinal Muscle Atrophy: Autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disease that causes destruction of the anterior horn cells.
	4. Fibromyalgia: Syndrome characterized by widespread pain affecting soft tissues and associated with migraine, IBS, and depression.
	5. ALS (Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis): Progressive neurodegenerative disease affecting the motor neurons of the brain and spinal cord (upper and lower motor neurons).
	6. Peripheral neuropathy: General term for injury to a nerve once it leaves the spinal cord or brainstem.

Fibromyalgia (Q&A_Fibromyalgia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Can't sleep?
	-Fibromyalgia is a very common disorder that typically manifests itself through musculoskeletal pain. Patients with fibromyalgia have altered sleep, memory, and mood which is accompanied by fatigue. According to Mayo Clinic, “researchers believe that fibromyalgia amplifies painful sensations by affecting the ways the brain and spinal cord process painful and nonpainful signs." (Mayo Clinic, 2020)

N/A 1
	Central sensitization 
		Temporal summation of pain 
		Decreased endogenous pain inhibition 
		Pain receptors and pain-related neuropeptides
		Neuroimaging findings

N/A 2
	Pain, fatigue, sleep disturbances  
	Constant fatigue regardless of adequate sleep at night 
	Anxiety/Depression 
	Difficulty with attention/concentration 
	Symptoms worsen with increased activity or decreased 
	IBS, GERD, Migraines

EXAM
	Tenderness to touch at soft tissue sites, joints excluded 
	Should not have swelling, erythema, bruising

DIAGNOSIS 
	Clinical Diagnosis 
	Basic Laboratory evaluation including inflammatory marker (ESR vs. CRP)
	Clinical Criteria
		1990 ACR Classification
		2010 ARC Criteria 
		AAPT Diagnostic Criteria

TREATMENT
	Comprehensive treatment plan including primary physician, physical medicine, rehabilitation, and mental health 
	Non pharmacologic interventions
	Single medication
	Combination medication

Knowledge Checker
	Treatment
		Anti-depressant medication
		Exercise therapy plan
		Sleep hygiene plan
	Not a suitable treatment
		Steroids
		Thymoma
		Plasmapheresis Therapy (PLEX)

('Fibromyalgia Study Guide (2).docx')
	FIBROMYALGIA
		1.DEFINITION
			-Syndrome characterized by widespread pain affecting soft tissues such as muscles, ligaments, and tendons, without evidence of tissue inflammation. It is hypothesized that it is caused by a central sensitization process. This desensitization causes a dysfunction in the way the central nervous system perceives and responds to pain
			-Check out this video of Lucy describing her experience with fibromyalgia and the way in which her multidisciplinary treatment plan helped to give her hope. 
		2.RISK FACTORS
			A.First degree relative with fibromyalgia
		3.PATHOGENESIS
			-There is evidence that the protective pathways the shield us from pain are dysfunctional in patients with fibromyalgia. A summary of some of the evidence for this pathogenesis is below:
				A.Temporal summation of pain
					i.Pain stimulus applied repeatedly in a short duration, patients with FM will report a significantly higher intensity of pain compared to patients without FM
				B.Decreased endogenous pain inhibition
					i.Unable to filter out less noxious stimuli so they feel everything; very overwhelming
				C.Increased number of pain receptor and pain related neuropeptides
				D.Functional MRI: Increased activity in the pain centers of the brain and they were less likely to respond quickly to the expectation that relief was coming from a painful stimulus
		4.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			A.Pain: chronic widespread pain
			B.Fatigue → feeling like they are in a haze Γ(Experiencing a fog/neblina/bruma/boirina/calitja/brume/Lat:Nebulam,caligine), difficulty concentrating
			C.Sleep disturbances
			D.Anxiety/Depression
			E.Associated Symptoms:
				i.Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS)
				ii.Gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD) i.e. heartburn
				iii.Migraine
			F.Physical Exam:
				i.Tenderness to touch of soft tissues but NOT joints
				ii.No evidence of an underlying cause for the pain (edema, erythema, ecchymosis, trauma, etc)
		5.DIAGNOSIS 
			-Diagnosing fibromyalgia is a clinical diagnosis. Fibromyalgia should be suspected in any patient with widespread soft tissue pain that has been going on for more than three months in the absence of an identifiable cause.
				A.2010 American College of Rheumatology Criteria
					i.Widespread Pain Index (WPI) → total of 19 areas (>7
					ii.Symptom Severity Scale: 0-12
						1.0 = no issues and 3 = severe and affects patient’s quality of life and ADLs
						2.Fatigue 0-3
						3.Waking unrefreshed 0-3
						4.Cognitive symptoms 0-3
						5.Somatic symptoms 0-3
					iii.Diagnosis: Patients fulfill the criteria below within a 3-month time frame
						1.> 7 WPI and > 5 Symptom severity scale
						2.3-6 WPI and >9 symptom severity scale
				B.AAPT (ACTTION-APS Pain Taxonomy) – used for chronic pain disorders
					i.Asked if having pain at 9 different body locations – must have 6 out of 9
					ii.Plus moderate to severe fatigue
					iii.Present for at least 3 months
		6.TREATMENT
			-Treatment for fibromyalgia needs to be multidisciplinary care with primary medical provider, physical medicine, rehabilitation, and mental health.
				A.Non-pharmacologic treatments
					i.Patient education on their pain
					ii.Sleep hygiene
					iii.Exercise and/or physical therapy
					iv.Cognitive behavioral therapy
				B.Medications
					i.Depression → antidepressants
					1.Tricyclic antidepressants 
					2.Serotonin and Norepinephrine Reuptake Inhibitors
					ii.Gabapentin for pain-causing sleep disturbances

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.There is strong evidence for the benefits of regular exercise. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC3673928/
			2.Consider pain is CNS driven.
			3.FITTE: Aerobic 1-2 days/wk and progress to 2-3 days/wk, 30-39% HRR w/ progression to 40-59% HRR; Low impact: aquatic, walking, dance, cycling; Resistance 2-3 days/wk w/ min of 48 hours between sessions, 40-80% 1RM - gradual progression starting at 4-5 or 8-12 reps, increase reps/sets and decreased time between reps as progression; Flexibility 2-3 day/wk (not supported as stand-alone intervention)
			4.RPE may be useful – especially during flairs – teach self-regulation.

Guillian-Barre (Q&A_GuillianBarre)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Potential leg paralysis
	-Guillain Barre is a syndrome that is triggered by an acute bacterial or viral infection. As a result of this, the immune system attacks the nerve cells and damages them. Symptoms range from weakness of the legs to paralysis.   

N/A 1
	Autoimmune inflammatory condition
		Autoantibodies attack the peripheral nerve
		Myelin
		Axon “molecular mimicry”
	Disruption of motor and sensory nerve impulses

Guillain-Barre Variants
	Acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (AIDP)
	Acute motor axonal neuropathy (AMAN)
	Acute motor-sensory axonal neuropathy (AMSAN)
	Miller Fisher Variant

Epidemiology & Risk Factors
	Men > Women 
	Incidence increases with age 
	Axonal variants → China, Japan and Mexico
	Risk Factors  
		Infections → Campylobacter Jejuni (30%), Haemophilus influenza, HIV, 
		Influenza, Cytomegalovirus, Mycoplasma Pneumonia 
		Trauma 
		Surgery
		Immunizations

Symptoms
	Monophasic ↓
		Preceding respiratory or gastrointestinal illness ↓
			1-4 weeks interval between virus and onset of GBS symptoms ↓
				Symptom's peak 2-4 weeks
	--------------------------------------------------------------------
	AIDP
		Symmetric, ascending weakness, absent deep tendon reflexes
	AMAN
		C. Jejuni
		Motor axonal neuropathy
		No sensory impairment
	AMSAN
		Motor & Sensory axonal neuropathy
	Miller Fisher Variant
		Ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, absent reflexes
	Dysautonomia

Diagnosis
	History and Physical exam
	Lumbar Puncture elevated protein, normal WBC count
		"albuminocytologic dissociation"
	Nerve Conduction Tests
		Axonal vs. Demyelinating
		AMAN/AMSAN delay of amplitudes ( Motor vs. Sensory)
		Demyelinating variantsDecreased motor velocity, prolonged motor latency, "F" wave latency
	Conduction Blocks
	Antibody Tests
	MRI Spine with and without gadolinium

Treatment
	Supportive Care
		Monitor for signs/symptoms of respiratory decompensation as reinnervation occurs
	Immunoglobulin Therapy (IVIG)
	Plasmapheresis Therapy (PLEX)
	Physical and Occupation therapy, Rehabilitation

Knowledge Checker
	Clinical Manifestation
		Absent reflexes
		Symmetric, ascending weakness
		Dysautonomia
		Ataxia
	Not a Clinical Manifestation
		Difficulty with attention/concentration
		Receptive aphasia: Inability to understand language
		Migraines
		Anxiety/Depression

('Guillain-Barre Study Guide (1).docx')
	GUILLAIN-BARRE
		1.DEFINITION
			-Autoimmune inflammatory condition that develops after a stimulus (most commonly infection) in which a patient develops auto-antibodies against peripheral nerves
			-The following two videos demonstrate the significant difference in patient prognosis with Guillain-Barre.
			-Check out this video of Luther Glenn discussing his experience with Guillain-Barre
			-Check out this video of Fran Cosper discussing his experience with Guillain-Barre
		2.VARIANTS
			-There are multiple variants of Guillain-Barre that explain the different clinical manifestations between patients. They are classified based on the structures (myelin, axons, or cranial nerves) that are most affected.
				A.Acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (AIDP) → most common; affects myelin
				B.Acute motor axonal neuropathy (AMAN) – affects motor axons only
				C.Acute motor-sensory axonal neuropathy (AMSAN) – affects both motor and sensory axons
				D.Miller Fisher Variant → affects cranial nerves
		3.EPIDEMIOLOGY
			-Guillain-Barre is more common in men than women and the risk of developing it increases with age. Axonal variants tend to be more common in certain parts of the world, particularly China, Japan, and Mexico. 
		4.RISK FACTORS
			A.Infections
				i.Campylobacter Jejuni (30%)
				ii.H. flu, HIV, influenza, cytomegalovirus (CMV), mycoplasma pneumoniae
					¦El megalodó i el mico van al camp 
			B.Trauma
			C.Surgery
			D.Immunizations → very are (1-2 excess cases of Guillain-Barre per million people vaccinated)
		5.PATHOGENESIS
			A.Myelin subtype (AIDP): Pathophysiology unclear 
			B.Axon (AMAN, AMSAN): Molecular mimicry → the body confuses its own peripheral nerve axons with the foreign virus it just fought off so it starts to attack itself 
		6.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-Patients will have a combination of motor and/or sensory deficits depending on their subtype. Because this disease affects only peripheral nerves, the motor symptoms will be lower motor neuron symptoms → flaccid paralysis and absent deep tendon reflexes.  Rarely patients will have dysautonomia (dysregulation of the autonomic nervous system)
			-The severity of symptoms varies greatly between patients from minimal motor and sensory deficits to profound paraplegia and respiratory failure. Symptoms typically occur ~4 weeks after the infection or other precipitating cause.  
				A.AIDP: Symmetric ascending paralysis and absent deep tendon reflexes
					i.Max weakness ~ 2-4 weeks and then they start to recover
					ii.20-30% will develop respiratory failure
				B.AMAN: Only motor symptoms, no dorsal column or sensory involvement
				C.ASMAN: Have both motor and sensory involvement
				D.Miller Fisher Variant: Cranial nerve symptom → Ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, absent reflexes
		7.DIAGNOSIS 
			-Guillain-Barre is a clinical diagnosis that can be made given an appropriate history and physical exam that is consistent with Guillain-Barre. 
				A.Lumbar Puncture: Elevated protein with normal WBC count (albuminocytologic dissociation)
				B.Nerve Conduction Tests: Help to differentiate an axonal process from a demyelinating process
				C.MRI Spine with contrast: Done to rule out other compressive etiologies, but may show enhancement of the nerve roots.
		8.TREATMENT
			-Treatment is largely supportive. Patients will need monitoring for rapidly progressive disease or signs of respiratory muscle involvement.  
				A.Immunoglobulin Therapy (IVIG) and/or Plasmapheresis
					i.Done for all patients with significant weakness that prevents ambulation
					ii.Considered in any other patients who do not have symptom improvement within 4 weeks of onset
				B.Non-pharmacologic
					-Please review pages 10-15 of the GBS-CIDP Foundation International Physical and Occupational Therapy Guidelines for specific physical therapy treatments.
						i.Physical Therapy
						ii.Occupational Therapy
		9.PROGNOSIS
			-The overall prognosis of functional recovery varies greatly between patients. Older patients or patients with more severe disease requiring hospitalization and intensive care unit admission tend to have more lasting deficits than patients with mild or moderate disease.  
			-In general, 60% of patients will have full motor strength recovery and 14% will have persistent severe motor problems. 84% of patients will be able to walk independently at one year. 

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.APTA Clinical summary: https://www.apta.org/patient-care/evidence-based-practice-resources/clinical-summaries/guillain-barre-syndrome

Myasthenia Gravis (Q&A_MyastheiaGravis)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Myasthenic gravis
	Not inherited
		Unlike other neurologic disorders such as Huntington’s disease and Parkinson’s disease, myasthenic gravis is not inherited. It develops later in life. Symptoms may include things such as difficulty swallowing, double vision, and unsteady walking. Visually it is manifested through droopy eyelids and mouth. 

N/A 1
	Autoimmune disease attacking the neuromuscular junction
	Neuromuscular Junction: 
		Motor Neuron 
		Synapse 
		Neurotransmitter → Acetylcholine 
		Acetylcholine receptors on muscle surface 

Autoantibodies of Myasthenia Gravis 
	Acetylcholine receptor antibodies (AChR)
	Muscle-specific Kinase (MUSK)
	Low-density lipoprotein receptor-related protein (LRP4)

Epidemiology 
	Bimodal 
		Females 20-30’s
		Males 60-80’s
	Increased incidence over time
	Risk Factors Post-partum

N/A 2
	Fatigable disease
	Types of Myasthenia Gravis

('Myasthenia Gravis Study Guide (1).docx')
	MYASTHENIA GRAVIS
		1.DEFINITION
			Autoimmune disease that attacks the neuromuscular junction affecting skeletal muscles.
		2.EPIDEMIOLOGY
			-MG has a bimodal distribution for unclear reasons. It affects persons between 20-30 years old and then 60-80 years old. Women tend to be more represented in the 20-30 y/o age group and men are more represented in the 60-80 y/o age group.
		3.RISK FACTORS
			A.Post-partum
		4.PATHOGENESIS
			-Body produces auto-antibodies against acetylcholine receptors or receptor proteins at the synapse between the motor neuron and the muscle.  
				A.Acetylcholine receptor antibodies (AChR): most common autoantibody
					i.Destruction of nicotinic acetylcholine receptors → less total number to work with
						1.Activate the compliment pathway which results in inflammation and destruction of muscle cells and acetylcholine receptors
					ii.Blocking of acetylcholine from attaching to the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor → blocking the ones that are left from working
						1.Attach to the nicotinic acetylcholine receptors and prevent the binding of acetylcholine, which in turn reduces the muscle’s ability to contract → weakness
				B.Muscle-specific Kinase antibodies (MUSK)
					i.Causes decreased acetylcholine receptor availability
				C.Low-density lipoprotein receptor related protein antibodies (LRP4)
					i.This protein activates the MUSK protein and so decreasing it’s function leads to decreased MUSK function and thus decreased acetylcholine receptor availability
			-So, patients have less total receptors to work with, less receptors being recruited to work, and of those activated receptors, some are getting blocked from doing their job. Overall, the muscle has less signal to contract coming from the neuron, less recruitment of myofibrils, and subsequent muscle weakness.
		5.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-Patients will complain of muscle weakness. The key distinguishing feature of MG weakness is that patients will have fatigable muscle weakness, which means patients may not have much weakness first thing in the morning but then experience more and more weakness and they day goes on or with increasing activity.  
			-They also are more likely to have symptoms related to muscles that are used a lot, like the extraocular muscles or the muscles of the eyelid. 
			-Check out this video of Laura describing her symptoms of myasthenia gravis. She displays many of the symptoms described below. 
				A.Ocular
					i.Ptosis
					ii.Diplopia
					iii.No pupillary involvement
				B.Bulbar
					i.Affects muscles of speech and swallowing
					ii.Changes to voice – nasal or hypotonic voice
					iii.Dysphagia (difficulty swallowing)
				C.Generalized
					i.Facial muscles → expressionless
					ii.Neck muscles → dropped head syndrome
					iii.Limbs → proximal muscle involvement that affects upper extremities > lower extremities
					iv.Respiratory muscles → respiratory failure
				D.Myasthenic Crisis
					i.Occurs when patients have a rapid deterioration of symptoms
					ii.Certain things can trigger a crisis
						1.Medications (Beta blockers, antibiotics)
						2.Infections
					iii.Need to be hospitalized, possibly in the intensive care unit
						1.Follow vital capacity and negative inspiratory force to determine respiratory muscle involvement
		6.DIAGNOSIS 
			A.Physical Exam: Stress the muscles (like making them hold their arms in front of them) and measure muscle strength before and after the stress.
				i.Icepack test: improvement of ptosis after 2 minutes
			B.Bloodwork: Can test for the presence of the above antibodies
				i.Acetylcholine receptor antibodies, MUSK antibodies, LRP4 antibodies
			C.Nerve Conduction Tests: Assess the fatigability of the muscles with stimulation
				i.Repetitive Nerve Stimulation: 75-80% sensitivity to diagnose MG
					1.Record muscle action potential at rest and after repeated stimulation of the muscle with electrical impulses. If action potential declines by at least 10%, can make diagnosis of MG
					2.Can also do this test using exercise as the stimulation and repeat the test in timed intervals 1, 3, and 5 minutes after completion of exercise
				ii.Single Fiber Electromyography: 90% sensitivity
					1.Measures the action potentials of two muscles innervated by the same motor neuron. These action potentials should be the same, but in patients with MG, the action potentials will become more different over time. This finding is referred to as “jittering.”
		7.TREATMENT
			A.Acetylcholinesterase Inhibitors – Pyridostigmine
				i.Prevents the breakdown of acetylcholine in the synapse so there is more neurotransmitter availability to compete with the antibodies for attaching to the receptors
			B.Steroids or Immunosuppresive therapy – to decrease the immune response
			C.Evaluate for thymoma – often the place where the autoantibodies are produced
				i.Can consider thymectomy in young patients even without evidence of a thymoma
			D.Crisis: 
				i.Hold acetylcholinesterase inhibitors
				ii.Pulse steroids, IVIG and/or plasmapheresis → significantly dampen down the autoantibody response

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERSATIONS
			a.Weakness can be exacerbated by stress and activity.
				i.Build in frequent rest breaks.
				ii.Avoid excessive exposure to sun or cold weather.
			b.Be alert for signs of Myasthenic Crisis
			c.Long term corticosteroid use can cause osteoporosis.
			d.Interventions should include deep breathing and cough technique

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) (Q&A_ALS)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Average life expectancy two-three years
	Lou Gehrig’s disease (ALS) is fatal. After being diagnosed with the disease, which weakens muscles and impacts physical function, the average life expectancy is two to five years. However, some patients outlive those years and can even live for decades such as famous physicist and cosmologist Stephen Hawking who lived more than 50 years after being diagnosed with ALS.  

AMYOTROPHIC LATERAL SCLEROSIS AKA Lou Gehrig's disease
	Progressive neurodegenerative disease
	Motor Neurons 
		Upper motor neurons → Motor Cortex of the Brain, Corticospinal Tracts 
		Lower motor neurons → Anterior Horn of the Spinal Cord

Pathophysiology 
	Familial ALS 
	Sporadic 

	Degradation & Demyelination 
		Glutamate
		Inflammatory Processes 
		Free radical 

Epidemiology & Risk Factors
	Slight Male predominance 
	Incidence increases with age, decreases in the mid-70’s 
	Risk FactorsSmoking, Veterans

Clinical Presentation
	Asymmetric muscle weakness
	Lower Motor Neuron
		Weakness, muscle atrophy, fasciculations 
	Upper Motor Neuron
		Spasticity, clumsiness, weakness, slowed motor function, hyperreflexia
	Bulbar ALS (25%)
	UMN & LMN involvement of the lower cranial nerves 
		Swallowing difficulties 
		Speaking difficulties 
		Emotional lability

Diagnosis 
	Evidence of progressive UMN and LMN symptoms
	Gold Coast Criteria 
		Progressive LMN and UMN in one limb or body segment 
		OR
		Progressive LMN signs in two or more body segments
		Absence of EMG, MRI, laboratory data suggestive of a separate diagnosis

EMG
	Evidence of acute and chronic denervation and reinnervation in multiple, separate muscle groups 

Treatment
	Supportive care
	Riluzole

Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following patient might most likely need to be further diagnosed for ALS? 
		-A 55-year-old male has had been feeling weakness in his legs. He has also been having issues swallowing and speech.
		-A 60-year-old female has been feeling very tired since it has been hard for her to sleep at night. She has also had difficulty with attention/concentration.
		-A 50-year-old male has been having difficulty with swallowing and speech. He has also noticed that his upper eyelid has been drooping. These symptoms get worse at the end of the day. 

		-A 55-year-old male has had been feeling weakness in his legs. He has also been having issues swallowing and speech.

('Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (1).docx')
	AMYOTROPHIC LATERAL SCLEROSIS ¦Amy viu en el tròpin (Green Hill) al costat en barranc de pedra (sclero)
		1.DEFINITION
			-Progressive neurodegenerative disease that affects the motor neurons of the brain and spinal cord.
				¦Amb el martell mata neurones en l'aserp en forma de cervell i espina dorsal
			-Check out this video in which Corey Reich and his mom discuss their experiences as Corey lives with ALS.
		2.RISK FACTORS
			A.5-10% of cases are familial ALS → autosomal dominant inheritance. All other cases are sporadic.
				¦La familia Sònic
			B.Incidence increases with age until mid 70s
			C.Smoking
				¦Amy fuma i es veterana en la realitat alternativa de Sonic Boom serie.
			D.Veterans?
		3.PATHOGENESIS
			-The pathogenesis of why the body begins to demyelinate the motor neurons is not well understood. However, the following processes are thought to contribute to ALS in the that they lead to the breakdown of motor neurons and cell death. The injured areas get replaced with scar tissue (gliosis and sclerosis).
				A.Elevated glutamate: High levels cause destruction of motor neurons
					¦"-mate" = Mata les nerones
				B.Inflammation: Patients with ALS demonstrate increased inflammation of the motor pathways
				C.Build-up of free radicals: Patients with ALS found to have dysfunction of specific genes responsible for management of cell waste and elimination of free radicals. 
					¦Lluita per la llibertat contra Robòtnix
		4.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-Patients will present with asymmetric muscle weakness with evidence of upper motor neuron and lower motor neuron involvement on exam. May start with subtle change in the hand, like difficulty opening a jar but as more motor neurons in adjacent areas are affected, the weakness will become more pronounced. 
					A.Classic ALS
						Upper motor neuron
							Lower motor neuron
						-----------------------
						Destruction of corticospinal tracts
							Destruction of anterior horn (gray matter)
						Spasticity
							Flaccid
						Hyperreflexia – all patients
							DO NOT have absent reflexes
						Slowed muscle movement
							Muscle atrophy
						Clumsiness
							Fasciculations
					B.Bulbar ALS (25%)
						Upper motor neuron
							Lower motor neuron
						----------------------
						Emotional lability - Laughing or crying inappropriately
							Swallowing difficulties
						N/A
							Speaking difficulties and voice changes
		5.DIAGNOSIS 
			-Diagnosis can be made in a patient who has identification of progressive upper motor neuron and lower motor neuron symptoms involving multiple areas over time. 
				A.Gold Coast Criteria
					i.Either progressive lower motor neuron and upper motor neuron in one limb/body segment
					ii.Progressive lower motor neuron signs in two or more body segments
					iii.Absence of any data suggesting an alternative diagnosis
					iv.Diagnostic tests that can support the diagnosis of ALS
						1.MRI → rule out other causes
						2.EMG (Electromyography) → evidence of acute and chronic denervation and reinnervation in multiple separate muscle groups
						3.Bloodwork → rule out other causes
		6.TREATMENT
			-Treatment is largely supportive and focused on improving their quality of life.  
			-Riluzole – only drug approved for ALS → decreases levels of glutamate around the motor neurons → helps to slow down disease progression → modest effect (increases life expectancy by ~ 3 months)
		7.PROGNOSIS
			-Median survival for patients with ALS is 3-5 years form the time of diagnosis, however 30% of patients live more than 5 years and 10-20% live more than 10 years. 

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.APTA Clinical Summary: https://www.apta.org/patient-care/evidence-based-practice-resources/clinical-summaries/amyotrophic-lateral-sclerosis-als
			2.Intervention depends on stage:
				a.Early stages (I/II): Strengthening is warranted.
				b.Late stages: 
					i.Monitor fatigue levels closely with strengthening activities.
				c.Consider: respiratory strengthening, posture, mobility, w/c prescription, family education

Spinal Muscle Atrophy (SMA) (Q&A_SpinalMuscleAtrophy)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

ALS or SMA?
	ALS and Spinal Muscle Atrophy (SMA) have similarities such as the lack of ability to control muscles. Likewise, in both diseases the most common cause of death occurs due to the loss of muscle function in the chest, resulting in respiratory failure. However, SMA differs in that patients that have it tend to be younger, such as children and infants. Whereas, ALS tends to affect adults. 

Spinal Muscle Atrophy (SMA) ('Spinal Muscle Atrophy')
	Autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disease 
		Degeneration of anterior horn cells due to absent or deficient survivor motor neuron protein 
	Chromosomal abnormalities on 5-q13.2
		SMN1 gene→produces SMN protein 
		SMN1 gene mutation/deletion of exon 7 → no SMN protein production
		SMN2 gene →essentially identical but can only produce functional SMN1 protein 10-15% of the time

Clinical Picture
	Anterior Horn Cells = Lower motor neuron pathology 
		Progressive weakness to paralysis 
		Atrophy
		Fasciculations 
		Absent or decreased deep tendon reflexes
	Affects alpha motor neurons of anterior horn cells
		Lower extremities > Upper extremities 
	Intercostal muscle involvement 
	Motor nucleus of lower cranial nerves 

SMA Subtypes
	Most severe
		SMA 0
		SMA 1
	SMA 2
	SMA 3
	SMA 4

SMA-0
	Prenatally, post-partum
	Decreased Uterine movement
	Hypotonia
	Contractures 
	Respiratory distress early 
	Life expectancy 6 months of age 

	SMN-2 Gene = 1 copy

SMA-1
	Normal perinatal period until 3 months 
	Motor milestones not met
	Progressive weakness to quadriplegia
	Life expectancy 2 years of age 

	SMN-2 Gene = 2-3 copies

SMA-2 
	Symptom recognition 3-15 months 
	Able to sit unassisted → lost as disease progresses 
		Unable to walk, stand
	Lower extremity involvement > Upper extremities 
	Tongue fasciculations 

	Orthopedic sequalae
		Hip dislocations
		Contractures  
		Scoliosis 
	Respiratory insufficiency 
		Restrictive Lung disease 
	Life expectancy = 20’s 

	SMN-2 Gene = 2-3 copies

SMA-3
	Symptom onset 18 months → adolescence 
	Motor Milestones reached 
		Progressive weakness → wheelchair 
		Gait disturbances, ascending stairs 
	Hip dislocations, scoliosis not prevalent 
	Normal Life span

	SMN2 Gene = 3-4 copies

SMA-4
	Onset symptoms 30’s 
	Mild symptoms, stay ambulatory

	SMN-2 Gene = 4-8 copies

Diagnosis 
	Genetic Testing 
		Mutations of exon 7 on SMN-1
	Not commonly completed anymore : 
	EMG 
	Muscle Biopsy 

	Pathology Anterior horns of spinal cord degenerated and absence of motor neurons, replacement of normal cells with gliosis

Treatment 
	Disease Modifying Treatment 
		Nusinersen intrathecal
		Zolgensma injection
		Risdiplam oral 
	Supportive therapy and symptom relief 
	Pulmonary Support Noninvasive vs. Mechanical ventilation
	Gastrostomy tube 
	Physical Medicine support Passive Range of Motion, Bracing

Knowledge Checker
	Match
		SMA-0
		SMA-1
		SMA-2
		SMA-3
		SMA-4

		Decreased uterine movement, hypotonia, and contractures.
		Life expectancy two years of age and motor milestones not met.
		Symptom recognition 3-15 months. Able to sit unassisted but unable to stand or walk.
		Normal Life span and symptom onset 18 months, adolescence.
		Onset symptoms30's, Mild symptoms, stay ambulatory, and SMN-2 Gene-4-8.

		SMA-0 Decreased uterine movement, hypotonia, and contractures.
		SMA-1 Life expectancy two years of age and motor milestones not met.
		SMA-2 Symptom recognition 3-15 months. Able to sit unassisted but unable to stand or walk.
		SMA-3 Normal Life span and symptom onset 18 months, adolescence.
		SMA-4 Onset symptoms30's, Mild symptoms, stay ambulatory, and SMN-2 Gene-4-8.

('Spinal Muscular Atrophy Study Guide (1).docx')
	SPINAL MUSCLE ATROPHY
		1.DEFINITION
			-Autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disease that involves the destruction of the anterior horn cells in the spinal cord and the motor nucleuses of the cranial nerves in the brainstem. 
			-Check out this video highlighting a few families who have children with SMA-1 talking about the hope they feel with Nusinersen.
			-Check out this video of an interview with Nick Sinagra who has SMA-2
		2.PATHOGENESIS
			-Spinal muscle atrophy is caused by a mutation in the survival motor neuron (SMN) gene on chromosome 5 that leads to an absence or severe decrease in the production of SMN protein. The SMN protein controls homeostasis of motor neuron cell function by synthesizing messenger RNA and preventing programed cell death. SMN is typically produced through activation of the SMN-1 gene.
			-Some patients can produce a fraction of SMN protein through activation of the SMN-2 gene, which only produces functional SMN protein 10-15% of the time. The more SMN protein patients can produce with their SMN-2 gene, the less severe their SMA symptoms will be. 
		3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-Patients have a progressive atrophy paralysis with fasciculations, absent or decreased deep tendon reflexes that tends to affect the lower extremities more than the upper extremities. Patients also often have intercostal muscle involvement that leads to respiratory insufficiency.
			-Typically CN V-XII affected with sparing of CN I-IV 
			-Normal cortical development and often have exceptionally high IQs
		4.SMA SUBTYPES
			A.SMA 0 
				i.Present prenatally or immediately postpartum 
					1.Decreased fetal movement
					2.Diffuse hypotonia with contractures
					3.Respiratory distress
				ii.Life expectancy of ~ 6 months
				iii.Only ~ 1 SMN-2 genes
			B.SMA 1
				i.Normal development until ~ 3 months of age
					1.Missing motor milestones 
					2.Impaired feeding (because they can’t suck easily)
					3.Belly breathing
					4.Progressive weakness and quadriplegia
				ii.Life expectancy ~ 2 years old
				iii.2-3 SMN-2 genes
			C.SMA-2
				i.Present ~ 3-15 months of age
					1.Able to sit unsupported initially but are not able stand or walk
					2.Tongue fasciculations
					3.Orthopedic sequalae
						a.Hip dislocations
						b.Contracture
						c.Scoliosis
					4.Respiratory insufficiency
						a.Restrictive lung disease → due to spinal deformities limiting the ability of the lungs to expand and the inability of the lungs to expand due to thoracic muscle weakness
				ii.Life expectancy ~ 20-30y/o
				iii.2-3 SMN 2 genes
			D.SMN-3
				i.Symptom onset between 18 months – adolescence
				ii.Motor miles reached but then they develop progressive motor weakness
					1.Gait disturbances → difficulty with stairs → require wheelchair
				iii.Normal lifespan
				iv.~3-4 SMN-2 genes
			E.SMN-4
				i.Onset of symptoms in 30s
				ii.Mild symptoms → stay ambulatory
				iii.~ 4-8 SMN-2 genes
		5.DIAGNOSIS 
			Diagnosis is made with genetic testing that finds mutations of SMN-1
			EMG and muscle biopsy used to be done but are not commonly done anymore.
		6.TREATMENT
			Treatment is largely supportive and focused on improving their quality of life.  
				A.Disease modifying treatment – slows the progression of the disease
					i.Nusinersen – increase SMN protein, given intrathecally (into spinal cord)
					ii.Zolgensma – rejects DNA that codes for the SMN protein – given as a one-time injuection
					iii.Risdiplam – given orally
				B.Supportive therapy and symptom relief
					i.Pulmonary support
					ii.Gastrostomy tube
					iii.Physical and occupational therapy
						1.PROM → contracture
						2.Bracing → scoliosis
						3.ADL therapies

		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATONS
			1.Interventions should include.
				i.Positioning, pressure relief, power mobility, inspiratory muscle training
			2.Special considerations:
				ii.Frequent position changes for pressure relief/ulcer prevention for infants or children that lack independent mobility.
				iii.Use caution with prone positioning – monitor breathing very closely
