Peripheral Neuropathy (Q&A_PeripheralNeuropathy) (Ací la identació no esta clara; voe el video ¿?)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

"Pin-and-needles" sensation
	Peripheral neuropathy, ALS, and spinal muscle atrophy share similar symptoms of muscle weakness. However, peripheral neuropathy differs on that weakness, numbness, and pain usually occur on the hands and feet. Some patients describe the pain as a “pins and needles” sensation. This can be a result of an injury, infection, or exposure to toxins.  

Peripheral Neuropathy
	Injury to the spinal nerves leaving the spinal cord Γ(Cervical Plexus, Brachial Plexus, Lumbar PLexus, and Sacral & Coccygeal Plexuses) or brainstem Γ(Cranial Nerves)
		(Γ)Possible symptoms
			tingling
			burning
			pain
			numbness
			muscle weakness
			red patches in the skin
	Areas distal to the injury affected more injury leads to nerve degeneration
		(Γ)Es a dir:
			An injury in an "spinal nerves leaving the spinal cord" leads to nerve degeneration → This nerve degeneration are felt more in areas distal to the injury

Nerve Pathways ('Nerve Pathways')
	Nerve root → Designated Dermatomes
	(Γ)------------------------------------------------
	Nerve root (sensory nerve fibers) (from a single spinal nerve root) →
		Designated Dermatomes (specific areas of the skin) →
			Test through sensory perception on the skin (testing sensation)
	Nerve root (motor nerve fibers) (from a single spinal nerve root) →
		Designated Myotomes (specific muscle groups) →
			Test through MMT

Nerve roots → Peripheral Branches ('Nerve roots Peripheral Branches Traction.png')
	Overlap of symptoms from designated nerve root injury vs. peripheral nerve injury
		(Γ)"nerve root" injury vs. "peripheral nerve" injury
			Nerve root injury
				arises from conditions like a herniated disk or osteoarthritis in the spine
					-leading to symptoms of nerve root disorders (in the specific area supplied by the affected nerve root) (corresponding to the specific dermatome supplied by the affected nerve root)
						pain
						abnormal sensations
						muscle weakness
			Peripheral Nerve Injury (due to damage to peripheral nerves that control movement and sensation throughout the body)
	  			results from damage to nerves outside the spinal cord
					-lead to symptoms like (in various parts of the body) (fingers, feet, and other areas further from the spine)
						pain
						weakness
						numbness
			Overlap of Symptoms
	  			nerve root injuries → localized symptoms specific to the affected dermatome
				peripheral nerve injuries → cause more generalized symptoms affecting multiple areas of the body
	  			Overlap of symptoms (between "designated nerve root injury" and "peripheral nerve injury")
					pain
					numbness
					tingling sensations
					muscle weakness
					-----------------------------
					distribution and extent of these symptoms
						may vary based on the location and nature of the nerve damage

Peripheral Neuropathy sub-types
	Mononeuropathy
	Mononeuropathy multiplex
	Polyneuropathy

Peripheral Neuropathy ΓCauses
	Compression positional, mass, trauma, disc herniation 
	Transection
	Ischemia vasculitis, atherosclerosis 
	Radiation induced
	Inflammatory post-infectious 
	Toxins medications, alcohol, chemotherapy agents
	Degenerative autoimmune processes

Clinical Symptoms
	Motor deficits, Sensory deficits, or both 
	Lower motor neuron disease hyporeflexia, fasciculations, decreased muscle tone, atrophy 
	Sensory burning, dysesthesias, numbness/tingling, loss of sensation

Radiculopathy ('')
	Injury to the nerve root
	Radicular or radiating pain following dermatomal and myotome distribution
		---------------------------------------------------------------------
		Example: 
			L5 radiculopathy 
				Disc herniation/spondylosis 
				Lateral aspect of lower leg
				Toe extension weakness
	(Γ)----------------------------------------------------
	Radiculopathy
		nerve root affected → symptoms radiate along the path of the affected nerve (localized to the affected nerve root distribution)
	Peripheral Neuropathy
		damage to peripheral nerves distal (outside) of the spinal cord and brain
			anywhere along the length of the nerve
				from its origin at the spinal cord to its termination in the periphery
			stocking-and-glove distribution affecting the hands and feet symmetrically
			can affect multiple nerves simultaneously

Γ-------------------------------------↑ General part
Γ-------------------------------------↓ Specific part (specific diseases)

Mononeuropathies
	Carpal Tunnel Syndrome ('')
		Compression of the median nerve
		Symptoms first 3 fingers and radial side of 4th finger
		Atrophy of thenar eminence, weakness with grip
		Treatment conservative, splinting, surgical release
	Upper Extremity Mononeuropathies 
		Ulnar Nerve Compression
		Radial Nerve Compression
	ΓLower Extremity Mononeuropathy
		Sciatic Nerve
		Lateral Femoral Cutaneous Nerve
		Fibular Nerve 
			Location nerve crosses around the lateral aspect of the knee 
			Acute foot drop
			“Steppage gait” 
			----------------------------------
			Treatment-Conservative, supportive 
			Splinting, Passive ROM 
		Tarsal Tunnel Syndrome

Mononeuropathy Multiplex
	Polyneuropathy
		Causes: Toxic, metabolic, infectious, autoimmune or systemic illness
		Distal nerve involvement, ascending pattern
		Stocking & Glove distribution 
		Risk for recurrent trauma, ulcerations

		Axonal vs. Demyelinating Processes
			Axonalmuscle atrophy, decreased sensation, absent reflexes 
			Demyelinatinggeneralized weakness, Proprioception deficits

		Tx: Address underlying cause
	Plexopathies
		Brachial Plexus Injuries ('Nerve roots Peripheral Branches Traction.png')
			Nerve root C5-T1 
				Causes 
				Same as other neuropathies 
			Trauma
			Axonal Neuropathy
	Plexopathies
		Backpacker plexopathy
		Stinger/Burner
	ERB’s Palsy 
		C5-C6 Nerve Branch injury
		Infant's arm is extended, internally rotated and arm adducted 
		Hand, wrist spared
	Klumpske Palsy 
		C8-T1 nerve branch involvement
		Paralysis of the hand
		Hand appears claw-like
	Lumbrosacral Plexopathy
		Symptoms numbness, tingling weakness depending on nerves involved

----------------------------------------------

Diagnosis 
	EMG 
	MRI with and without gadolinium 
	Laboratory workup 
	Nerve Bx
	Skin Bx

Treatment 
	Conservative treatment
	Removal of compressive force
	Removal of toxin exposure 
	Repletion of Vitamin deficiency 
	Medications
	Tricyclic anti-depressants
	Neurontin 
	Physical Therapy

Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following are treatment options for peripheral neuropathy? (Click all that apply).
		Removing cause of nerve compression
		Thymoma
		Plasmapheresis
		Tricyclic antidepressants

		✓Removing cause of nerve compression
		Thymoma
		Plasmapheresis
		✓Tricyclic antidepressants

('Peripheral Neuropathy Study Guide (1).docx')
	PERIPHERAL NEUROPATHY
		1.DEFINITIONS
			-Injury to nerves after leaving the spinal canal or brainstem that results in the inability for afferent and efferent messaging. All areas distal to the injury are affected, including nerve branches, however distal areas of nerves are more susceptible to injury.
				Peripheral nerves:
					-Provide the action and interpretation of our environment. They send messages from the brain to muscle groups to move and relay information back to the brain about the environment, like pain, temperature, and proprioception.
				Wallerian degeneration:
					Immune mediated process of degeneration that occurs to the distal axon of an injured nerve.  
				Radiculopathy:
					Symptoms related to compression or injury of a nerve root, rather than a peripheral nerve
				Myotome:
					Muscles innervated by a spinal nerve root
				Dermatome:
					Area of skin supplied by a single spinal nerve
				Mononeuropathy:
					One nerve affected → compressive lesion or transection of nerve
				Mononeuropathy multiplex:
					-Mononeuropathies occurring in multiple locations that aren’t next to each other or in the same muscle group. Associated with ischemic or vascular process. Typically patients will develop a single mononeuropathy first and then over time complain of additional mononeuropathies.
				Polyneuropathy:
					Many nerves affected throughout the body. Ex: Diabetes
		2.CAUSES
			A.Compression
				i.Positional → pins and needles from “sleeping wrong”
				ii.Mass → tumor, hematoma
				iii.Trauma → dislocated fracture causing compression of nerve
				iv.Disc herniation → generally compresses associated nerve root(s)
			B.Transection
				i.Often related to trauma, the nerve is cut
			C.Ischemia
				i.Vasculitis
				ii.Atherosclerosis
			D.Radiation induced
			E.Inflammatory and post-infections
			F.Toxins
				i.Medications
				ii.Alcohol
				iii.Chemotherapy
			G.Degenerative autoimmune processes → demyelination syndrome
		3.CLINICAL MANIFESTATIONS
			-The clinical manifestations depend on the nerve root or peripheral nerve involved, the location of involvement (proximal or distal) and the degree of injury. Patients can present with solely motor symptoms, solely sensory symptoms, or both.
				A.Motor → Lower motor neuron disease
					i.Hyporeflexia
					ii.Fasciculations → muscle dancing or twitching
					iii.Decreased muscle tone
					iv.Atrophy
				B.Sensory
					i.Dysesthesias: burning, pruritis, tingling, stinging
					ii.Loss of sensation → can lead to secondary injury because patients can’t feel the pain that would normally make them alter behavior
				C.Polyneuropathy
					i.Bilateral ascending symptoms that begin distally and extend proximally.  
					ii.“Glove and stocking distribution”
		4.DIAGNOSIS 
			A.Electromyography (EMG) – help determine the level of the injury and differentiate between axonal and demyelinating injury → should be done before labwork
				B.MRI with and without contrast if concern for nerve root or spinal cord involvement
				C.Bloodwork → based on the type of peripheral neuropathy (axonal vs. demyelinating)
					i.Glucose
					ii.Vitamin B12
					iii.Toxic metals
		5.TREATMENT
			Treatment will depend on the location and cause of the peripheral neuropathy. Below is a list of general guidelines that can be applied to all patients
				A.Conservative: nonsteroidals (ibuprofen), steroids, splinting
				B.Removal of:
					i.Compressive force (tumor)
					ii.Toxin (alcohol)
					iii.Vitamin deficiency (give back the vitamin the patient is missing that is causing symptoms)
				C.Medications to help with nerve pain
					i.Tricyclic anti-depressants (amitriptyline)
					ii.Gabapentin
				D.Physical Therapy considerations
					i.Fall risk – balance training.
					ii.Positioning -splinting/orthotics
					iii.For sensory impairment: intervention should include education re: skin checks, footwear. 
					iv.Strengthening/Gait training

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Module 10 Introduction
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

For this module, we will be wrapping up by learning of several conditions and diseases that are not necessarily treated primarily with physical therapy, but that are necessary for physical therapists to be aware of because you will likely see patients with one or more of these diseases.   

Your knowledge of these diseases will enable you to develop appropriate physical therapy plans for patients coming to you for physical therapy who also have one or more of these diseases. You will be comparing acute and chronic disease, Crohn's disease with ulcerative colitis, diabetes mellitus type I and type II, and learning the difference between the types of anemias. 

You will also be listening to the final podcast episode of “What does it mean to know what I don't know?”

You will not be taking a pre-quiz or quiz so that you can have time to prepare for your final case study and the final simulation. 

As you go through your final module and on to the rest of your time as a DPT student and healthcare provider, remember the comic below. It has been a pleasure working with you. Good luck in all your future endeavors!

Learning Objectives
	By the end of this week, you will be able to: 
		Compare and contrast the clinical presentation, diagnostic workup, and management of acute versus chronic kidney disease. 
		Compare and contrast the clinical presentation, diagnostic workup, and management of Crohn’s disease and ulcerative colitis.  
		Explain the role diabetes mellitus plays in contributing to cardiovascular disease and other chronic diseases. 
		Differentiate between the types of anemias and their most common causes.

////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
10.1. Medical Terminology
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

Hyperglycemia  
Definition: Elevated glucose.
Etymology: Hyper – excessive, glycos/o - sugar/glucose, -emia – blood condition

Polyuria
Definition: Excessive urination.
Etymology: Poly – excessive, -uria – urinary conditions

Polydipsia 
Definition: Excessive thirst.
Etymology: Poly – excessive, -dipsia – condition of thirst

Albuminuria   
Definition: Albumin in urine.
Etymology: Albumin/o - protein –ura – urinary condition

Glycosuria    
Definition: Sugar in urine.
Etymology: Glycos/o - sugar or glucose

10.6. Module Summary
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

You have completed this module. Before moving on to the next module, remember to review these three key learnings: 

Diabetes is a common disease that carries significant long term health consequences. 
Chronic kidney disease and inflammatory bowel disease are manageable, but still affect a patient’s quality of life. 
In most cases, a patient’s symptoms, rather than a laboratory value, will dictate the management of anemia. 

Outcomes
	After completing this module, you can now: 
		Compare and contrast the clinical presentation, diagnostic workup, and management of acute versus chronic kidney disease. 
		Compare and contrast the clinical presentation, diagnostic workup, and management of Crohn’s disease and ulcerative colitis.  
		Explain the role diabetes mellitus plays in contributing to cardiovascular disease and other chronic diseases. 
		Differentiate between the types of anemias and their most common causes.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Acute Kidney Injury (Q&A_Acute Kidney Injury)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Difficulty Filtering Waste
	Acute kidney injury is a common condition that affects more than 200,000 Americans each year. This condition makes it difficult for the kidney to filter waste from the blood. Common causes for this are that there is lack of blood flow to the kidney, kidney damage, or blockage of urine. 

.Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following is NOT a criteria for acute kidney injury?
		Increased Cr>1.5x baseline in 7 days 
		Increased Cr>0.3 mg/dL in 48hrs 
		Increased pH<7.35 in 7 days
		Urine volume < 0.5ml/kg/hr x 6 hours

		Increased pH<7.35 in 7 days

('Acute Kidney Injury Study Guide.docx')
	ACUTE KIDNEY INJURY
		1.PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			A.Early mobility can facilitate discontinuation of foley catheter.
				i.Decrease risk of infection
				ii.Prioritize ambulation to bathroom in acute setting.


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Chronic Kidney Disease (Q&A_Chronic Kidney Disease)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.No Symptoms
	Unlike other diseases, chronic kidney disease often has no symptoms and for a patient to be diagnosed, they need to do a blood test. For patients that do show some symptoms, they develop slowly and over time. Without the proper medication, chronic kidney disease can be fatal.  

.Knowledge Checker
	Slows the rate of progression
		Smoking cessation
		Glycemic control
		Blood pressure control
		Protein restriction
	Treats secondary complications
		Diuretics
		Vitamin D supplements
		Erythropoietin

('Chronic Kidney Disease Study Guide.docx')
	CHRONIC KIDNEY DISEASE (CKD)
		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATONS
			1.CONNECTING THE DOTS
				A.Recall affects that cardiac issues can have on the kidneys and vice versa.
					i.Chronic kidney disease increases the risk of major cardiovascular events.
					ii.85% of patients on dialysis have abnormal left ventricular structure and mass.
					iii.Hypertension can be both a cause and consequence of CKD.
					iv.Patients may experience accelerated atherosclerosis with CKD.
					v.Heart failure is prevalent.
			2.DIALYSIS (HEMODIALYSIS OR PERITONEAL DIALYSIS)
				A.PT may treat if stable during dialysis.
					i.This is facility specific.
					ii.Compliance is highest during first 1-2 hours of dialysis.
					iii.Monitor vitals closely.
				B.Alternatively: Schedule PT around dialysis schedule
					i.Patients may experience increased fatigue following dialysis.
					ii.Monitor vitals closely when treating post dialysis. 
					iii.Monitor lab values prior to treatment: Hgb, Hct, glucose, potassium, calcium, creatinine, BUN, WBC, platelets.
			3.IMPACT OF CKD ON FUNCTION/EXERCISE TOLERANCE
				i.Patients with CKD have impaired exercise tolerance and decreased endurance.
				ii.Skeletal muscle fatigue/atrophy/weakness
				iii.Interventions should include aerobic and resistance training.
				iv.Avoid use of 1RM due to poor tolerance. 
				v.Utilize RPE
			4.POST-TRANSPLANT
				i.Expect increased fatigue and weakness initially.
				ii.Use of steroids may delay adaptation to resistance training.
				iii.Modify intensity and duration of exercise during periods of rejection. 


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Diabetes Mellitus (Q&A_Diabetes Mellitus)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Lack of Insulin
	Typically, doctors use the full name diabetes mellitus, instead of diabetes alone, to set this disorder apart from diabetes insipidus, a rare disorder that does not affect blood glucose levels. Diabetes mellitus is a disorder where insulin is ineffective in maintaining normal blood sugar.

.Knowledge Checker
	Which of the following patient groups qualify for diabetes mellitus screening? (select all that apply)
		Patients who have a BMI of 25 who are <45 years old
		Women with history of gestational diabetes 
		Patients who have previously been diagnosed with pre diabetes 
		Patients who are >45 years old who have not been screened in four years

		✗Patients who have a BMI of 25 who are <45 years old
		Women with history of gestational diabetes 
		Patients who have previously been diagnosed with pre diabetes 
		Patients who are >45 years old who have not been screened in four years

('Diabetes study guide.docx')
	DIABETES – PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
		1.HYPERGLYCEMIA
			A.Signs/Symptoms
				i.Frequent urination
				ii.Dry mouth
				iii.Increased thirst and hunger
				iv.Flu-like achiness
				v.Headache
		2.HYPOGLYCEMIA (GLUCOSE <70 MG/DL)
			A.Signs/Symptoms
				i.Shakiness
				ii.Dizziness
				iii.Sweating
				iv.Tachycardia
				v.Inability to concentrate
				vi.Confusion
				vii.Irritability
				viii.Weakness
				ix.Numbness or tingling of lips and tongue
				x.Nausea/vomiting
			B.CAUSES
				i.Too much insulin or oral agent
				ii.Too little carbohydrate intake
				iii.Missed meals.
				iv.Excessive or poorly planned exercise
			C.Exercise Considerations
				i.Regular exercise is important for diabetes management.
				1.Consistent time of day and frequency are ideal.
				ii.Ingest carbs if pre-exercise glucose level in <100 mg/dL in insulin treated individuals.
				iii.Monitor for signs of hypoglycemia during, immediately after, and up to 24 hours after
				iv.Exercise should be modified during times of stress and at peak insulin times.
				v.If glucose level prior to exercise is 250-300 mg/dL
					1.HOLD PT. 
					2.Ketones should be monitored.
					3.Exercise can further increase blood glucose at these levels.
				vi.Signs of Ketosis include.
					1.Distinct fruity smell of breath*
					2.Extreme thirst
					3.Frequent urination
					4.fatigue

Inflammatory Bowel Disease (Q&A_Inflammatory Bowel Disease)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Exact cause is unknown
	How does the image to the right relate to Inflammatory bowel disease? Click on image to zoom in.

	The exact cause of Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) is unknown. However, IBD is still the result of a defective immune system. Symptoms of IBD include persistent diarrhea, abdominal pain, rectal bleeding, weight loss, and fatigue.

.Knowledge Checker
	Ulcerative Colitis
		Oral glucocorticoids
		Oral 5-aminosalicylates
		20-30% of patients require colectomy
		Topical mesalamine or glucocorticoids
	Crohn's disease
		Oral 5-aminosalicylates
		Biologic therapy
		Oral glucocorticoids
		Immunomodulators

('Inflammatory Bowel Disease study Guide.docx')
	INFLAMMATORY BOWEL DISEASE
		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.REGULAR PHYSICAL ACTIVITY 
				A.Decreases stress
				B.Assists with bowel function
			2.EDUCATION
				A.Breathing and relaxation techniques if breath holding due to pain/discomfort


////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
Anemia (Q&A_Anemia)
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////

.Lack of healthy red blood cells
	A patient with anemia will typically feel tired, have shortness of breath, feel light-headed, and dizzy. Anemia results from a lack of healthy red blood cells that can carry adequate oxygen in the body. It is a very common condition that affects more than three million Americans every year.

.Knowledge Checker
	Some causes of anemia, such as chemotherapy, can affect multiple pathways in the pathogenesis of anemia.
		True
		False

		True

('Anemia Study Guide.docx')
	ANEMIA
		PHYSICAL THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS
			1.CLOSELY MONITOR HEMOGLOBIN AND HEMATOCRIT
				A.Hemoglobin (note: may vary slightly by facility)
					i.Hgb <10 g/dL modify PT 
					ii.Hgb < 8  g/dL hold PT 
						1.*exceptions occur - discussion with medical team
				B.Hematocrit (note: may vary slightly by facility)
					i.HCT < 30 % modify PT
					ii.HCT < 25 % hold PT
						1.*exceptions occur - discussion with medical team
				C.If transfusion planned, HOLD PT until transfusion complete
					i.Religious beliefs (i.e. Jehovah witness) may prohibit transfusion in which case you can proceed and monitor symptoms and vitals closely
				D.Exercise Implications for Anemia 
					i.Fatigue
					ii.Dyspnea
					iii.Tachycardia
					iv.Syncope or Dizziness (if severe or acute)
					v.Generalized weakness
					vi.Exercise requires increased cardiac output
						1.If demand is not met or if there is underlying cardiac disease, angina may be experienced – Stop PT 
				E.Monitor post-operative patients closely for signs/symptoms
			2.IN THE CASE OF HYPOPLASTIC OR APLASTIC ANEMIA:
					i.Stem cells in bone marrow do not produce enough blood cells (RBC, WBC, platelets)
						1.Decreased platelet production = increased risk for bleed/bruising
							a.Activity modifications may be warranted.
